# Nava-vraja-mahima. Vol. 2

## Dedication

Although I have written Nava-vraja-mahima for the pleasure of all Vaisnavas who either physically or mentally reside in Vrndavana, I dedicate this book to those whose hearts are devoted to Radha-Syamasundara and New Vraja-dhama. It is my hope that they will pass their time in this way:

*iha vatsan samacarayad*  
*iha nah svami jagau vamsim*  
*iti sasram gadato me*  
*yamuna-tire dinam yayat*

**“Here our Lord herded the calves, and here He played the flute. I pray that I may pass my days shedding tears as I speak these words on the Yamuna’s shore.” (Sri** ***Raghupati Upadhyaya)***

## Preface

The title of the book you are now reading, N*ava-vraja-mahima,* when rendered in English becomes “The Glories of New Vraja-dhama.” I originally conceived of it as a booklet, meant to guide devotees visiting the holy places of New Vraja-dhama, ISKCON’s rural community in Hungary.

As you must have noticed, the book has grown in size since its inception and would now likely require a small handcart for eager pilgrims to be able to transport it from one pastime place to another! Nonetheless, it still serves its original purpose of extolling New Vraja-dhama by praising its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara, and by narrating the Supreme Lord Krsna’s pastimes in His eternal abode, Sri Vrndavana-dhama.

Texts that glorify the holy abodes (*dhamas)* of the Lord are called *dhama-mahatmyas.* Two prime examples of this genre of devotional literature are Rupa Gosvami’s *Sri Mathura-mahatmya* and Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s *Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya.* Both of these books establish the sanctity of the *dhamas* they depict — Vrndavana and Navadvipa respectively — by citing Puranic praise of the *dhamas* themselves as well as the pastime places within their boundaries. There is, however, an interesting difference between the two. *Sri Mathura-mahatmya* is an unordered compendium of scriptural references to the pastime places it describes, while *Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya* chronicles the Lord’s pastimes and the places at which they occurred by following the sequence in which pilgrims visit those places as they circumambulate the *dhama.* Thakura Bhaktivinoda also recounts the personal spiritual reflections he gleans from circumambulating Navadvipa in an appendix to *Sri Navadvipa-dhama*-m*ahatmya* that he calls *Sri Navadvipa-bhava-taranga.*

Despite their stylistic differences, the transcendental benefit readers derive from these two types of *mahatmyas* is the same. They act as practical guidebooks to the *dhama,* and at the same time they aid pilgrims in immersing their thoughts in the Lord’s glories. For that reason, devotees who do not travel to the *dhama* can also derive transcendental benefit by circumambulating the holy places in their minds as they meditate on both the Lord’s pastimes and the *dhama's* glories as they are recounted in sacred texts of this nature.

*Nava-vraja-mahima* is a *mahatmyas* in the style of *Sri Navadvipa*- *dhama-mahatmya* in that it includes an account of my own personal circumambulation, *or parikrama,* of New Vraja-dhama and the realisations that came to me as a result. In fact, as I visit the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama — more commonly known to the people of Hungary as Krsna Valley — I sometimes reflect on similar *parikramas* I have undertaken in Bhauma Vrndavana, recount histories of how New Vraja-dhama was established and developed, or express my own devotional aspirations. This personal approach is primarily an attempt to please the devotees of New Vraja-dhama, who requested me to describe the *parikrama* not as a passive observer but as a participant who shares his adventure with the reader.

Amongst many possible readers, this personal adventure is especially intended to be shared with the residents of and visitors to New Vraja-dhama, for whom this book will naturally be of greatest interest. Those readers who are not a part of that particular audience may rightly question what New Vraja-dhama is and how its glorification can be relevant to persons who live outside Hungary or who may never visit.

As its name suggests, New Vraja-dhama is modelled after the original Vraja-dhama, Bhauma Vrndavana, Krsna’s eternal home as it appears on earth. The inspiration to establish New Vraja-dhama came from my spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the founder-acarya of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. In the 1960s in America, Srila Prabhupada established New Vrndavana and envisioned it to be a self-sufficient community, dependent upon agriculture and cow protection, as well as a place of pilgrimage in the West. New Vraja-dhama is therefore our humble attempt to fulfil the desire of His Divine Grace that such places of pilgrimage be established throughout the world.

In the introduction to this book and in the essay entitled “The Truths of the Dhama,” the reader will find a detailed history of how and why New Vraja-dhama came to be, along with a philosophical study of the spiritual principles that make it a holy place. I cannot overstate how important it is for the reader to carefully go through these parts of the book in order to clearly understand the spiritual foundations upon which New Vraja-dhama and *Nava-vraja-mahima* rest.

What benefit will those who do not visit New Vraja-dhama gain from reading this book? The answer is threefold.

*Nava-vraja-mahima* contains extensive and very detailed narrations of Krsna’s pastimes at all the major places in Vrndavana, as well as at many lesser-known places. Extensive research went into compiling—from the *Vedas*, *Upanisads, Puranas, Samhitas,* and other Vedic histories, as well as from the commentaries of our previous *acaryas* and other sources — the information it contains. Altogether, the text and the accompanying references offer the reader a unique opportunity to delve deeply into Krsna’s pastimes in Bhauma and Goloka Vrndavana and better get to know the Supreme Lord and His loving associates.

Second, although the topographical orientation, distances, and occasionally the sequence of New Vraja-dhama’s *parikrama* differ from the same in Bhauma Vrndavana, the book also serves as an in-depth guide for pilgrims to use in visiting the pastime places of Sri Krsna’s original earthly abode.

Third, through the glorification of New Vraja-dhama, this book testifies to the greatness of Srila Prabhupada’s achievement in spreading the potency of Vrndavana throughout the world.

Now, allow me to clarify a few points about certain expressions and literary devices used in the text, about the structure of the volumes, and about the actual *parikrama* route of New Vraja-dhama.

The expression “Krsna’s pastimes in New Vraja-dhama” is not meant to imply that Krsna manifests His eternal pastimes here in the way that He does, creation after creation, in Bhauma Vrndavana. Rather it means that the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama correspond to the original places in Bhauma Vrndavana or Goloka Vrndavana. Largely due to the uncommon format of the text (which I will discuss elsewhere in this preface) and because of the corresponddence between the pastime places in both *dhamas,* it was sometimes necessary to refer to the pastimes associated with those places in a way that may make it sound as if they happened in New Vraja-dhama.

If I say, for example, “At this place, called Aghavana, Krsna killed Aghasura,” it means “Krsna killed Aghasura in the Aghavana of Bhauma Vrndavana, which is commemorated by the place of the same name here in New Vraja-dhama.” Similarly, phrases such as “At this place Radha and Krsna enact Their *rasa* dance” should be taken to mean “At the Rasa-sthali of Goloka Vrndavana, the divine couple enact Their eternal *rasa* dance. The place called Rasa-sthali in New Vraja-dhama commemorates that original place.”

The reason I use such expressions, aside from literary necessity, is to emphasise the importance of New Vraja-dhama and its stature as a place of pilgrimage. Again, the reader will find the philosophical and spiritual reasoning and supporting scriptural evidence for employing this convention in the introduction and the essay on *dhama-tattva* that follow this preface.

Another literary device found in this book — as well as in other books describing the holy *dhama* — is that I sometimes speak about New Vraja-dhama as if I were directly seeing the unmanifest *(aprakata)* abode and pastimes of the Lord. These expressions are not declarations of my existential position; rather, they are poetic expressions of my deep feelings for the subject matter and the places themselves. I qualify such statements here to avoid causing misunderstandings that might blur the line between the manifest and unmanifest features of the *dhama,* merging them in a way that could be taken as contrary to authorised *dhama-tattva.*

When, in describing the *dhama,* authors weave between manifest and unmanifest realities in this way, it often causes the features of the *aprakata* realm or the pastimes Krsna is relishing there to seem as if they are part of the world visible to conditioned souls. But devotees well versed in the principles of *dhama-tattva* will have no difficulty following these transitions between the manifest and unmanifest realms, whereas others may either not notice them or find them confusing.

Our great *acaryas* may express themselves in such ways due to the overwhelming bliss they feel at seeing the *aprakata-dhama* and the eternal pastimes going on there. Or, in the case of those who are not on the fully realised platform of our great *acaryas*, authors may be making a conscious endeavour to emphasise the divinity that underlies the features of the *dhama* that conditioned souls are able to perceive.

In writing *Nava-vraja-mahima,* poetic inspiration would some- times impel me to describe the features of the *dhama* not in terms of what I was seeing with my eyes — for I cannot see that which is unmanifest — but in terms of the *aprakata* counter-whole as I have heard about it from scripture and as I contemplate it in relation to New Vraja-dhama. Readers who notice such expressions in the pages of this book should take them in this light, not as my personal realisation of the unmanifest *dhama* in the way of the great *acaryas.*

**\* \* \* \***

*Parikrama* is the traditional way of visiting Vrndavana. When Krsna sent Uddhava to Nandagrama with messages for the Vraja-vasis, He directed His cousin along a specific route and pointed out places to note along the journey. Thus Uddhava relished *parikrama* by travelling from Mathura to Nandagrama, as did Krsna by describing the journey to him.

On His only visit to Vrndavana, Caitanya Mahaprabhu spent the first month of His two-month stay touring through Vraja’s twelve forests. And even though Lord Caitanya is Krsna Himself, He requested the help of a guide and followed the established route of His time.

With the rediscovery of Krsna’s pastime places, the Vraja-mandala *parikrama* has evolved into what is currently called *caurasi-krosa parikrama,* or circumambulation of the one hundred and sixty-eight-mile *dhama,* the longest version of which takes almost two months to complete. Today, often during the holy month of Karttika, hundreds of devotees relish walking barefoot from place to place on ISKCON’s Vraja-mandala *parikrama,* living in the meadows or fields of Vraja, and absorbed in hearing about Krsna and chanting His names.

For Hungarian devotees who cannot go to Bhauma Vrndavana, who cannot stay there for thirty days or more, or who would like to go on *parikrama* every year but cannot afford to go to India so often, the Nava-vraja-mandala paricrama is for them. Annually, members of the Hungarian *yatra* visit selected places of New Vraja-dhama over the course of several days, and as the *dhama* is further developed and *parikrama* paths established, the guided tours will become more extensive. In addition, by using the separate book of maps provided with *Nava-vraja-mahima,* devotees may go on *parikrama* at any time they want and thus relish the nectar of hearing Krsna’s pastimes and serving His *dhama.*

In this way, *Nava-vraja-mahima* seeks to serve those devotees keen to visit Krsna’s abode, both in Hungary and in India. And for devotees who are neither residents of nor pilgrims to one *dhama* or the other, this work gives them the chance to visit Krsna’s holy *dhama* in their minds and thus bring the atmosphere of Vrndavana to wherever they reside.

Circumambulating the *dhama* is a means of offering respect. It includes visiting pastimes places, remembering pastimes, bowing down, taking the dust from pastime places on one’s head, bathing in sacred waters, and performing *nama-sankirtana.* Many major places within the *dhama* — Govardhana Hill, Vrndavana town, and Varsana amongst them — have their own *parikrama* routes. As far as possible, these places have been or will be represented in New Vraja-dhama. There is never a dearth of transcendental diversion for the devout pilgrim!

At the time of writing this book, the main pastime places of New Vraja-dhama have been established, whereas many *parikrama* paths and other secondary pastime places have been mapped out but not yet developed. These places — like the construction of a magnificent new temple for Radha-Syamasundara— remain part of future plans to develop New Vraja-dhama.

Let me now describe the book’s structure.

As already mentioned, this book is written in the form of my own *parikrama,* which is actually a compilation of multiple *parikramas* I made of New Vraja-dhama, some encompassing the entirety of the *dhama,* others only of places especially dear to me.

In the course of describing my *parikrama,* there were times at which deep philosophical topics or lengthy pastimes came up that required explanations so extensive that to describe them properly would have broken the flow of the narration. These explanations became essays that are now appendices at the end of each volume. I request my reader not to neglect these appendices — nor the endnotes that accompany each chapter, for that matter — as readers are sometimes wont to do. They comprise roughly one third of the book’s total content and are instrumental in bringing the reader to a balanced understanding of its message. Designated stanzas in the running text direct the reader to these appendices at the proper times.

In addition to the *parikrama* and appendices, the ten final chapters of *Nava-vraja-mahima* are dedicated to directly glorifying Radha- Syamasundara, the presiding Deities of New Vraja-dhama. In these chapters I exalt Their Lordships through the meditations that arise as I chant the Gayatri mantras and the *maha-mantra* and from my direct service to Them on the altar. This last section of the book therefore contains in-depth analyses of the Gaudiya Vaisnava *diksa-mantras* as well as the Hare Krsna *maha-mantra.* In this way the book has three parts: *parikrama,* appendices, and direct glorification of Radha-Syamasundara.

The running text that forms the bulk of the book, comprised of the *parikrama* and the praises of Radha-Syama, is written in three-line prose stanzas. It is pleasant to look at and easy to read, but it was a challenge to write, edit, and lay out on the page. This challenge led us to adopt certain conventions, such as mixing Sanskrit verses with English translations within a single stanza, or having longer verses straddle more than one stanza. However, we feel confident that the book’s format will enable you to relish the topic all the more.

From the outset I wanted *Nava-vraja-mahima* to be well illustrated to give windows into Krsna’s pastimes and to contribute to the overall purpose of the work — to increase the reader’s attachment to the Lord. I hope this has been achieved to your satisfaction. In *Sri Krsna-samhita* (9.16), Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “By seeing pictures of Krsna or by repeatedly hearing descriptions of Krsna from persons who have seen Him, one’s greed for attaining Krsna increases unlimitedly.”

To assist you in navigating through the unfamiliar names of pastime places and the variances in the locations of these places in New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, each chapter also includes a map or maps of the area it describes.

Altogether there are eight volumes of text. The set is complete with a ninth volume solely dedicated to maps of the various holy places of both New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, along with short descriptions of each pastime place. Devotees can take this map book with them to follow the *parikrama* routes in both *dhamas* and have a reminder of Krsna’s activities there.

*Nava-vraja-mahima* is meant to serve as a companion for devotees who want to take full advantage of the presence of Vrndavana in Hungary and to develop their relationship with its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara. Furthermore, it is meant to encourage devotees anywhere in the world to worship their local Deities with devotion and recognise the Vaikuntha nature of those Deities’ abodes. It is my hope as the author that this work will serve these purposes in some small way and thus bring pleasure to devotees, for my own access to the *dhama* will be possible only by the good wishes of the Vaisnavas.

In conclusion, I request the reader, wherever he or she may reside, to consider that in its essence this book praises the same *dhama* and the same Krsna that are there in their own hearts, and which are thus very near and dear to them. It is in this way that our journey around New Vraja-dhama through the pages of this book is synonymous with our own inner journey back to Godhead.

The journey through *Nava-vraja-mahima* is a long one. The book spans over five thousand pages and took five years to write. Therein I have tried my best to portray the truths of Vrndavana as they are recorded in scriptures and narrated by perfect devotees like Srila Prabhupada. Sadly, I remain a conditioned soul and thus am prone to make mistakes. If my readers find mistakes within the pages of this book, I ask them to tolerate such faults in the way they do the pebbles or thorns that prick their feet while on *parikrama* of the *dhama.*

Hare Krsna.  
Sivarama Swami  
New Vraja-dhama  
Gopastami, 2 November 2011Chapter Five - Kamyavana

I began my *parikrama* of Nandagrama from Arista-tila and completed it atop Nandisvara Hill, from where I again took in the view of New Vraja-dhama before continuing on to Kamyavana.

From the pavilion that represents Nanda-bhavana I looked south. The glass dome of Radha-Syamasundara’s temple was clearly visible, as were the housetops of Vrndavana village beyond that.

In Krsna Valley, Nandisvara Hill — like the hills of Varsana — was not part of the original topography. It was created by devotees as part of the development of the sacred places of New Vraja-dhama.

At one time all of this was nothing but grazing land. Now, scores of homes and thousands of trees make it easier to remember Vrndavana forest, complete with its residents’ *kutiras.*

To the west, cow pastures gradually slope down the valley past the primary school to the Yamuna, and then rise up on the other side towards Gokula and beyond it to wheat fields undulating in the wind.

To the northeast — my next destination — lie some of the most secluded parts of Syamasundara’s realm, where the ruins of a long-abandoned village are currently the only man-made structures.

I looked behind me, where further uphill sat beautiful Kamyavana. I would now make my way there on the *parikrama* path that rings the northern petal of Krsna Valley, worshipping Kamyavana as I went.

Some parts of New Vraja-dhama — its eastern petals — are not yet under devotee management; still, they are home to many pastime places, the most prominent of which I have recorded in this book.

I made my way down the hill and past Yasoda-kunda along the road leading to Barifa, which sits on the tip of a petal of the *dhama.* Like other places in this area, it is connected to Krsna’s eating pastimes.

As I walked towards Akrura-tirtha, I recalled other pastime places along the path, beginning with Harasukha, where Krsna took away *(hara)* the *gopis*’ distress (*asukha*) as they waited for Him.

Once, as Krsna was leaving Nandagrama with the *gopas* and the cows, and Radha and the *gopis* were departing for Yavata, they passed secret messages to one another, agreeing to meet in this little forest.

But leaving the company of the *gopas* without being noticed was not as easy as Krsna had expected it to be. The boys hadn’t seen Him all night so they were hovering around Him like bees on a honeycomb.

Wherever Krsna went, the cowherd boys followed. He lagged to the back of the herd, but the *gopas*’ eyes stayed fixed on Him, making it impossible for Krsna to slip away for even a moment.

Finally, with some clever help from Subala, at the first place the cows stopped to drink Krsna was able to escape. He rushed to Harasukha, even though He feared there would be a price to pay for being late.

He was right! The dynamics of His rendezvous with the gopis are in some ways predictable. Lovers are always exhilarated when on their way to a tryst, in the hope that the object of affection is already there.

A deserted meeting place, however, makes the lover feel let down. But this disappointment is soon overpowered by anticipation of the beloved’s imminent arrival. Joy still reigns at this stage.

But when the appointed time passes and the beloved has still not arrived, with each passing *danda* (twenty-four minutes), happiness invariably gives way to disappointment, sorrow, and then anger.

Fortunately for Krsna, He appeared at Harasukha just as the *gopis* were entering the stage of sorrow (*asukha*), sparing Him the consequences of having arrived after that sorrow had turned to anger.

Still, it was no easy task to pacify them. Taking help from His divine potencies, Krsna expanded Himself into as many forms as there were *gopis* waiting for Him and took each one into a separate secluded grove.

Harasukha is a small forest, but Krsna’s *yogamaya* arranged for each couple to be out of sight and mind of the others; consequently, He was able to console every *gopi* according to her nature.

This is one of Krsna’s wonders. He can reciprocate with each soul individually. And He does so not only with His dearmost *gopi* companions but also with inexperienced practitioners *(sadhakas).*

At the next place, Murara, the cowherd boys brought pleasure to a disconsolate Krsna by dancing and singing to the rhythm of His flute. It wasn’t easy, but their love for Him allowed them to succeed.

Krsna wasn’t always as successful at dispelling the *gopis*’ disappointment as He was at Harasukha, especially on those occasions that time had already transformed disappointment to anger.

Such anger, especially Radha’s, was a source of despondency for Krsna, for it kept His beloved locked in an impenetrable fortress, far from any sort of loving exchange, intensifying both His and Her grief.

The cowherd boys noted Krsna’s despair and tried to cheer Him with comedy, mime, and affection. But, like a crow pursues a hawk, Krsna’s sorrow would leave neither His heart nor His countenance.

The boys finally sat Krsna down on a tree stump at Murara and said, “Govinda! Play the most lively tune You can on Your flute and watch the new dance we have choreographed just for You.”

Unable to ignore the love of His friends, Krsna half-heartedly played His flute, until Subala whispered in His ear, “Hari! How many times has Radha been angry with You and then made up with You later?

“Don’t spoil Your day or the pleasure of Your friends. Have faith in My words. She will be Yours again. In the meantime, enjoy the *gopas’* performance, which is but an expression of their love for You.”

Between Murara and Akrura-tirtha are two pastime places related to each other, Moti and Khapana. Krsna once met Radharani at Moti and garlanded Her with a stunning necklace of priceless pearls (*moti).*

Srimati Radharani was extremely pleased to receive this gift, although it would later become a source of embarrassment, for Grandmother Mukhara would recognise it as one of Krsna’s own necklaces.

Luckily, Lalita was able to convince Mukhara by clever deception that the necklace was not actually Krsna’s but an identical one Mother Yasoda had given Radha for cooking Krsna’s breakfast.

After receiving the necklace from Krsna, Radharani walked with Him to a favoured *kadamba* tree, but Krsna suddenly vanished, as if He were made of mist dissipated by the rays of the Radha sun.

Where had Krsna gone? A demon had just entered Vrndavana. Krsna and His friends had raced off at once in order to kill the vicious interloper and to protect Maharaja Nanda’s *surabhi* cows.

Perplexed by Krsna’s mysterious disappearance, Radha asked Her friends, “What message *(khabar)* did Krsna receive to make Him leave so abruptly. Do you know?” Thus, this place is called Khapana.

**\* \* \* \***

I left the *gosala* behind me and entered a lesser-known and seldom frequented part of New Vraja-dhama, where chirping birds, rustling trees, and the occasional whirlwind are the only sounds.

Until we built the main road that runs parallel to the Yamuna and then veers uphill to the temple, this poorly maintained dirt road was the only access to the farm for all vehicular traffic.

It was not an easily travelled road. When the weather was dry, a fine dust would obstruct the drivers’ vision and clog carburettors. When it rained in summer or snowed in winter, it was completely impassable.

Now the road is rarely used, except by the few neighbours who still have vineyards on that land adjoining Krsna Valley. I walked through the peaceful atmosphere of the area, turning towards Sabala-grama.

During Krsna’s time the encroachment of the nearby desert dried up lakes in this area of Bhauma Vrndavana; consequently, trees and bushes grew only in the relatively more damp soil of the lakebeds.

The cowherd boys were unable to water their cows or to bathe when they passed this way. But the sight of lakebeds full of flowering blossoms, not water, was amusing to them, so they would chuckle.

Vrndavana is a fully conscious abode. It displays an endless storehouse of sublime qualities solely for the purpose of bringing tran- scendental pleasure to Lord Krsna and His friends.

Further along lies Ratu, or Rati-kunda, where in a nearby grove Krsna and Radharani acted as puppets in the hands of Cupid, displaying Their romantic mastery in pastimes of transcendental passion *(rati).*

The amorous love of this material world has its original, pure form in Vraja, where Radha and Krsna are the prototypes for all youthful lovers. Their pleasure lies exclusively in gratifying Their beloved.

We must never equate these amorous exchanges — or those of parenthood or friendship in Vraja — with their material counterparts, which are based only on the selfish desire to gratify one’s own senses.

In a grove at Rati-kunda, Radha and Krsna offered Themselves to each other body and soul with the same selfless, loving devotion with which a pure devotee offers a ghee lamp to the Deity of the Lord.

Let us, from a respectful distance, offer homage unto the crest jewel of pure love *(rati),* which the material mind and senses cannot know but which is carefully studied by those with full faith in its divinity.

As mentioned earlier, this petal of the *dhama* is full of pastime places related to eating the tasty foods of Vrndavana, especially during the winter month of Pausa when the fire of digestion is strongest.

Four such places are Barifa, Vatsavana, Sonkh, and Malapura. These holy sites are all near the *dhamas* boundary (sima) on the path one takes to circumambulate the *dhama (sima-parikrama).*

At Barifa the *gopas* overate, at Vatsavana they complained of hunger, at Sonkh the smell of the *gopis’* chaunce attracted Krsna, and at Malapura Krsna conjured up a variety of sumptuous dishes.

In their work of herding cows, playing games, and escaping from the demons who periodically come to kill Krsna, the cowherd boys expend an extraordinary amount of youthful energy.

The *gopas* replenish their energy by eating the fruits that grow abundantly throughout Vraja, the lunch packs they bring from home, and the yogurt and butter they raid from the Vraja-vasis’ storehouses.

Being from the families of cowherds, a significant portion of the *gopas*’ diet is made up of dairy products. Amongst their favourites are sweets like yogurt, *makhana, rabri,* and sweet rice.

But Krsna’s magical presence, which transforms any meal into an incomparable feast, is the most important ingredient in the *gopas*’ meals, be they in the forests of Vraja or in King Nanda’s dining hall.

Krsna’s captivating presence adds extra sweetness to all things sweet, enhances the flavour of each dish in a meal, and makes every meal a veritable “love feast” that nourishes body and soul alike.

Moreover, Krsna’s mystic powers make fruit ripen out of season, sweets appear out of thin air, bodies of water transform into lakes of yogurt, and small lunch packs manifest an endless supply of snacks.

On one such occasion, the cowherd boys arrived on the edge of Vraja at Barifa and sat in a large circle in the shade of a banyan tree. The cows also rested peacefully after the long journey from Vrndavana.

The boys had been snacking along the way and were worried that what was left of their lunch packs would not be enough to satisfy their appetites. “My tiffin is as empty as my stomach,” on*e gopa* said.

Another boy added, “I barely have enough to whet my appetite. Why bother eating at all?” In this way the boys complained to one another and then to Krsna, who said with a smile, “Friends! Do not lament.

“We should always accept whatever we receive as Lord Narayana’s mercy. If we do, we will never be disappointed. By His grace a morsel may satisfy any appetite. Without it, even a feast will not be enough.”

Pleased by Krsna’s words of wisdom, the cowherd boys sat to eat the remnants of their lunches with renewed vigour, only to find that as much as they ate, their tiffins were never emptied.

Again and again the boys scooped sweet rice and *makhana* into their mouths only to see it reappear in their tiffins. And when they had eaten their fill, they still had as much as when they had started.

The gopas were accustomed to Krsna’s miracles, and they praised Him profusely. But Krsna simply joined His palms and turned His lotus eyes to the sky as He said, “It is all the mercy of Lord Narayana.”

Next to Barifa is peaceful Vatsavana, also called Bacchavana, where the boys once arrived empty-handed and extremely hungry, having completely consumed everything in their lunch packs.

The cows were content to graze on the grasses growing there, but the *gopas* were disturbed by the growling of their stomachs. Try as they might to distract themselves, they could not forget their hunger.

Finally Sridama and others appealed to Krsna, “O brother! For lack of food we are too weak to eat, not to mention make our way back home. If You do not do something quickly, we will perish on the spot.”

Krsna felt great empathy for the plight of His friends. He played His flute in such a way that the sound conjured up pots of yogurt, *rabri,* and butter, appearing in clusters like small fields full of pots.

The ravenous *gopas* leaped on the snacks, just as tigers leap from behind bushes upon unsuspecting prey. As they sat together eating, they happily glorified the prowess of their friend Krsna and His flute.

Today various lakes decorate Vatsavana, lakes such as Rabri-kunda, where the *rabri* appeared, and Sankara-kunda, with its temple of Radha and Krsna. Bathing there washes one’s sins away.

**\* \* \* \***

Vatsavana is also where the cows and older *gopas* were overwhelmed by affection for their offspring, who were actually expansions of Krsna — the originals having been stolen by Brahma one year earlier.

After Lord Brahma kidnapped the cowherd boys and calves at far away Sei, Krsna expanded Himself into perfect replicas of the boys and calves and acted as the offspring of the elder *gopis* and the cows.

Krsna went undiscovered in His multiple roles until almost a year later, on a day when He and His friends were herding their calves on the edge of Vatsavana adjacent to Govardhana Hill.

The cows were pasturing on the top of Govardhana that day. As soon as they saw their calves from a distance, a special affection took hold of them and they spontaneously galloped down the hill.

Without caring for the roughness or steepness of the terrain, the cows ran to their calves, heads and tails raised, milk bags flowing with milk, their lowing filling the air with the sounds of affection.

Cows with younger calves never show such preferential love for their older offspring. But as highly unusual as it was, there they were, feeding their older calves milk and licking them with great delight.

The cowherd men were surprised by this sudden stampede, as the cows were usually well behaved. The men ran down the hill but at the sight of their sons, they too melted with affection.

The elder *gopas* warmly embraced their sons, lifted them in the air, and affectionately smelled their heads without a care for the calves that were nearby, greedily drinking all the cows’ milk.

It is difficult to say how long Yogamaya allowed these exchanges of extraordinary love to go on. Perhaps many days of the demigods went by. But finally the leader of the *gopas* got everyone’s attention.

With great difficulty the cowherds separated the cows from the calves and, to avoid the magnetic effect of the calves and boys, drove the cows around Govardhana Hill and out of sight.

During this unprecedented display of love, Lord Balarama— who stayed back to celebrate His birthday on the morning that Brahma had stolen the *gopas* and calves — carefully observed everything.

Amazed, Balarama thought to Himself, *kim etad adbhutam iva vasudeve ’khilatmani* / *vrajasya satmanas tokesv apurvam prema vardhate:* “What is this? What is this wonderful phenomenon?

“The affection of all the inhabitants of Vraja, including Me, towards these boys and calves is increasing as never before, just like our affec- tion for Lord Krsna, the Supersoul of all living entities.”

Lord Balarama at once deduced that He had been covered by some mystic power, which could only have been the illusory energy of Krsna. Who else could bewilder Balarama, the cause of creation?

Indeed, when Krsna had expanded Himself into the calves and *gopas,* He did not disclose this to Balarama because He feared that His brother would feel too much separation from friends like Sridama.

But didn’t Krsna feel such separation? Cakravarti Thakura explains that Krsna—in another form, like the one that went in search of the calves — stayed with the sleeping boys Brahma had stolen.

Now that the year was almost over, Krsna directed Yogamaya to lift the spell bewildering Balarama, who at first expressed amazement, then sensed a mystic power, and finally realised the actual situation.

The calves and *gopas* were now Krsna’s expansions. But where were the originals? Feeling guilty for having deceived Lord Balarama, Krsna disclosed everything, putting His brother at ease.

Because Lord Balarama was freed from the spell of Yogamaya at Vatsavana, practising devotees come here to free themselves from the spell of Mahamaya, which is nothing but Yogamaya’s expansion.

Another eating pastime took place in Malapura. There Krsna boasted to His friends that, as the supreme controller, He was able to do anything at anytime. “Just test me!” He challenged.

“By My will alone I create universes, and by My powers of illusion I subdue demigods and demons alike. Just because you defeated Me in play does not mean I am inferior. Go ahead! Test Me!”

Krsna’s boasting is a display of one of His four personality types, called *dhiroddhata,* a personality in which He shows transcendental envy, pride, anger, restlessness, and complacency.

The cowherd boys sat down facing Krsna as if they were about to enter a wrestling competition with Him and chattered amongst themselves to decide how Krsna could best prove Himself.

Recalling the event at Vatsavana, the *gopas* replied, “Manifest many *(mala)* enjoyable dishes such as *laddu, ksira,* and *sandesa.* But after we finish eating them, the dishes should reappear as they were before.”

Krsna smiled as if to say “Is that all?” and then instantly manifested innumerable sweet and salty preparations before the overjoyed *gopas,* who proceeded to methodically consume the entire feast.

But as soon as they had finished eating one tasty dish, it reappeared as it was before. When the boys could finally eat no more, they saw that the entire feast was still beckoning them as if untouched.

Laying upon their backs and rubbing their stomachs, the boys admitted defeat, casually declaring, “Krsna must truly be that Govinda whom the scriptures call the primeval cause of all causes.”

Amongst all the pastimes in this area related to eating, my favourite took place in Sonkh, where Srimati Radharani and Her friends ended up after an extended search for Krsna that began at Radha-kunda.

Although the *gopis* did not find Sri Krsna, they did notice that the area abounded in wild spinach, quality vegetables, and delectable roots. Soon they began to chit-chat about having a picnic.

When the *gopis’* talk reached Radha’s ears She teased them, saying, “How can we cook with no utensils, no ghee, and no spices? Whoever is hungry should be satisfied with the ripe fruit from the trees.”

But the majority of the *gopis* insisted that a cooked meal would be more appealing. At this, the land of Vraja, like a philosophers’ stone, manifested all the paraphernalia necessary for cooking.

In a small clearing amidst *kadamba* trees appeared pots, woks, bags of flour, a churn full of ghee, containers of spices, and all manner of cooking utensils. In short, everything found in a well-stocked kitchen.

The *gopis* looked at each other in wide-eyed amazement. Were they seeing a will-o’-the-wisp, the work of witchcraft, or a divine manifestation? Where had all these things come from?

After Srimati Radharani assured them that all these things were the blessings of Vrndavana and quite real, the girls at once busied themselves collecting firewood and their favourite vegetables.

Soon the forest clearing was crowded with cow dung fires and *gopis* busily stirring steaming pots as they melodiously sang songs praising Sri Krsna’s names, qualities, and pastimes.

As each preparation was almost ready, Srimati Radharani would prepare a tasty chaunce of selected spices fried in ghee. At just the right moment She would tip the chaunce into the pot and stir it.

Thus each dish would reach perfection. The *gopis* had a common but tacit desire they were too shy to voice: they wanted Krsna to appear and accept the feast they had cooked with only Him in mind.

Conscious of the heartfelt desire of the *gopis,* Vrndavana *dhama* deputed a meandering breeze to carry the aroma of their cooking to Krsna in order to attract that best of heroes to His eager heroines.

At that time Krsna was grazing cows on the far side of Govardhana Hill, smiling and joking with His friends. But He could not help but wonder, “Where are Radharani and the gopis now?”

With the cows settled in the shade to rest, Krsna entrusted the herd and the *gopas* to Balarama and, accompanied only by Madhumangala, strolled along the bank of the Yamuna singing Radha’s glories.

Suddenly Madhumangala froze. Eyes wide open, eyebrows raised, he grabbed Krsna’s arm and sniffed the breeze with the keenness of a wolf that had just found its prey by scent.

“Friend!” Madhu exclaimed. “Do you smell what I smell? Do you smell that heavenly aroma? That aroma has the culinary signature only one person in our land could be responsible for? Do you smell it?”

Krsna raised His nose and replied, “I do Bato! I do! And what you say is true. Only one person can cook like this. But how could Sri Radha and Her friends be cooking in the wilds of Vrndavana?”

“Dear Krsna!” Madhu replied. “The question ‘How could they be cooking?’ is irrelevant. The real question is ‘Who are they cooking for?’ And the self-evident truth is that they are cooking for You.

“Thus, my friend, ever eager to please the *brahmanas,* let’s follow this saintly breeze in the hopes it will simultaneously satisfy the yearning of our tongues and fill the emptiness of our bellies.”

Krsna needed no further prompting. He marched in the direction of that drifting bouquet of culinary aromas, all the while relishing Madhumangala's praise of each new scent that came their way.

As they drew nearer to their destination, the two friends stopped for a moment to relish the atmosphere of the forest garden called Ratu, where the *gopis* once displayed extraordinary affection for Krsna.

Once again following the scent of Radhika’s chaunce, Krsna and Madhumangala finally arrived at its source. But the *gopis,* though delighted at heart, showed indifference to their newly arrived guests.

One *gopi* said, “Well, that’s one drawback to open air cooking. It attracts even the most austere Vedic *brahmanas."* “Not to mention,” another gopi chimed in, “equally austere cowherds.”

A third *gopi* added, “We are preparing a feast for the sun god, but before we can even make the offering, these two are queuing up, plates in hand, showing the full extent of their religiosity.”

But neither Krsna nor Madhu were discouraged. They were used to the *gopis’* concealed affection. With kind words and signs of submission the two boys convinced the girls to invite them for lunch.

We may wonder why the *gopis* would not tell one another of their common desire to cook for Krsna, or why, after cooking, they acted as if they did not want to feed Him. Much could be said about this.

In brief, shyness keeps the *gopis* from showing their love, while love’s crooked nature makes them speak in contrary ways. The appendix entitled “The Concealed Meaning of the Gopis’ Talks” elaborates.

That clearing in the forest — where Radhika’s spicing attracted the two friends, where the *gopis* happily fed them, and where, after eating, Krsna and Radha retired to a *kunja*— is now known as Sonkh.

Several temples and lakes dot this area including Sonkh-kunda, where it is said that even today one can smell the aroma of the *gopis’* cooking, the scent of which lingers in the air.

**\* \* \* \***

A little distance beyond Sonkh, at a place called Kothara, the *parikrama* path in New Vraja-dhama turns abruptly towards Kamyavana along a line of trees that separate the two valleys.

This treeline cuts through New Vraja-dhama, separating the radiant petals of the *dhama* that lie to the east from the better known and more often frequented petals that lie to the west.

Kothara-kunda provides fresh drinking water for Krsna’s cows and a place for them to rest before returning home from this extreme corner of the *dhama.* A temple of Lord Narayana stands there today.

On the edge of Vatsavana is Malipura. The flowers that gardeners *(mail)* once cultivated there used to provide garlands for all of Vraja. This place is different from the nearby Malapura already mentioned.

The Vraja-vasis used huge quantities of flowers and garlands in worshipping their deities, ornamenting themselves, and decorating their homes — quantities that greatly increased on festival days.

Although women’s duties included tending to flower gardens and stringing garlands, the average household’s — not to mention the royal courts’ — demand for flowers exceeded their gardens’ yield.

To keep up with demand, communities of gardeners took up residence around the villages, cultivating extensive gardens that featured large varieties of flowers, as well as collecting flowers that grew in the wild.

Even today in Bhauma Vrndavana a large community of florists cultivates extensive gardens and strings garlands in the area along the *parikrama* path from Sakaroya to Dvadasaditya-tila.

In the mornings and afternoons, florists’ sons can be seen carrying large baskets of rose and jasmine garlands on their heads. They sell them to pilgrims who then offer them to the many deities in Vraja.

Malipura was once such a community of florists. They would always keep their best garlands aside for Krsna and Balarama, in case the king’s sons and Their friends brought the cows to this part of Vraja.

The brothers would graciously accept the florists’ offerings, saying, “Please give garlands to Our friends Sridama, Subala, and the others. That will please Us even more and make your offerings complete.”

In this way Krsna and Balarama taught the florists — and through them the world — that worship of the Supreme Lord is complete only when accompanied by worship of those dear to Him.

The pastures beyond Kothara are the cows’ favourites, so Krsna and Balarama would regularly bring them here while the demigods reverentially worshipped the divine brothers with perfect prayers.

From overhead the demigods and their wives would observe the carefree ways of the masters of creation, but they were unable to fully comprehend why Krsna and Balarama were herding cows.

Why were Lord Visnu and His snake-bed Ananta — who, served by countless perfected souls, float together upon the waves of the milk ocean — now walking barefoot in this remote countryside?

How did those two Supreme Souls, whom the personified *Vedas* and *Upanisads* constantly praise, become the butt of childish jokes told by simple cowherd boys not even purified by second birth?

What qualification did those *gopas* have to climb upon Krsna’s and Balarama’s shoulders, to steal food from Their plates, or to decorate Them with peacock feathers picked up off the ground?

The demigods and goddesses wondered in this way, and at the end of the day they returned to their own planets to discuss what they had seen; still, they could not understand it at all.

Sukadeva Gosvami says that the *gopas* were able to play with Krsna and Balarama as a result of many lives of pious activities. The ways in which they serve Krsna are far beyond the demigods’ grasp.

The demigods *(devas)* offered exalted *(sirsa)* prayers to Krsna and Balarama at Devasirsa. And the flowers the demigods and their wives showered upon the divine brothers became Devasirsa-kunda.

But the demigods continued to identify themselves with their uni- versal posts, so they could not approach the Lord. But some sages who gave up all false designations succeeded where demigods failed.

These sages (munis) attained the topmost *(sirsa)* perfection at nearby Munisirsa-sthana. It is said that they took a vow to stand on their heads *(sirsasana)* until Krsna, the goal of their austerities, appeared.

Can one attain the Lord simply by standing on one’s head? Of course not! But the sages’ heads were constantly touching the dust of Vrndavana, which gradually awoke in them the requisite devotion.

Through the eyes of that mature devotion, the sages saw — from a purified perspective of the world — Krsna and His friends merrily singing and dancing while driving their cows to Munisirsa-sthana.

Further along the *parikrama* path is Ghata, where the landscape of Vrndavana awakened in Krsna such a surge of affection for Srimati Radharani that Her absence made His life empty *(gata).*

Even the loving friends who surrounded Krsna, whose lives were completely devoted to entertaining and serving Him in countless ways, could not fill the place in His heart meant solely for Radha.

Most *gopas* took it that Krsna was tired from their long trek, but Subala could understand the intricacies of Krsna’s affection for the *gopis.* He took the Lord aside and affectionately said:

“My friend! You appear distracted. It seems to me that You long for a certain princess. What can I do to make You happy?” Krsna sighed deeply and replied, “Please bring Radha to Me, lest I die.”

Subala reassured his friend. Without hesitating for a moment he sent a few trusted messengers to find Radha and deliver news of Krsna’s great distress, and to tell Her that only She could relieve it.

Wouldn’t it have been faster for Krsna to find Radharani Himself? Of course! Except for one slight detail: His feelings of separation made His limbs so stiff that He was unable to walk.

Finding Radha was no easy task. And once found, it wasn’t easy to convince Her to come to Ghata. And once She was there, it was a challenge for Subala to persuade Her to meet Krsna in private.

But Subala’s dedication to Krsna empowered him to overcome all these obstacles, reconfirming his status as the leader of the *gopas,* especially of Krsna’s most intimate friends *(priya-narma-sakhas).*

The pastimes at Ghata stand as testimonials to the depth and intensity of Krsna’s love for His devotees and of His absorption in thoughts of them. He never forgets His surrendered servants.

Past Ghata is the forest of Pramodavana and the village of Pramodana, both of which are linked to the delight (*pramoda*) Krsna gave the *gopis* who came there in search of Him on two occasions.

Once, while looking for Krsna, the *gopis* arrived at Pramodana. From there they combed the countryside looking for a glimpse of their beloved, but wherever they looked, He was nowhere to be seen.

Gradually tears filled the *gopis’* eyes, tears that fell from their eyes to their cheeks, from their cheeks to their breasts, and from there to the ground, muddying it like a monsoon deluge.

The *gopis'* tears formed a rivulet that quickly became a stream that flowed to a forest some distance away, where Krsna just happened to be walking with Lord Balarama, His friends, and their cows.

Krsna was about to step over the stream when He noticed that the water seemed to be both blackish and crimson — and hot. Looking along the course of the creek, Hari noticed something else.

All the trees and creatures along the stream’s banks were crying. Instantly Krsna understood that the fountainhead of such a mysterious creek could only be the *gopis.* And He had to find them.

Without warning, Krsna simply disappeared from the company of His friends and reappeared in the midst of the *gopis,* who were blinded by their own tears and deafened by the sound of their own sobs.

So intense was the *gopis’* sorrow that Krsna struggled to keep His composure. To pacify them, He expanded into as many forms as there were *gopis* and gave Himself fully to each of them.

He took every *gopi* to a different forest grove. With the end of His soft upper cloth, Govinda dried their tears and, with words only His lips could make sweeter, assured them of His undying love.

After He had pacified the *gopis* and convinced them that what they were seeing was neither a dream nor a vision, Krsna gave them all the bliss of His companionship according to their individual desires.

Another time, the *gopis* went to Pramodana to collect water from a lake called Krsna-kunda. The reflection of dark clouds in the water sparked in the *gopis* such intense feelings for Krsna that they fainted.

By divine intuition Krsna came to know of the *gopis*’ condition, a tragic state only His personal presence could remedy. He rushed to Krsna-kunda and found the *gopis* lying on the ground like dry leaves.

By His touch and His voice Krsna revived the girls, who shyly hid their shame at having so overtly displayed their love. Still, they were unable to hide the joy they felt at having been rescued by Him.

A deity of Krsna’s friend Sudama is worshipped at Krsna-kunda. There is another lake in Pramodavana. This one is called Carana-kunda and it was blessed by Krsna’s feet (*carana*), as is all of Vraja.

Dear devotees! Somehow or another we decided to come to this material world to taste the bitter fruit of material enjoyment, and we have been suffering for our foolishness ever since.

If we recall the *gopis’* moods of separation in Pramodavana, a longing for Krsna may awaken within us, which if intensified will bring tears to our eyes that only the delight of Krsna’s presence can dry.

**\* \* \* \***

Two pastime places grace the area just before Kamyavana: Kanoyaro, the *asrama* of Kanva Muni, and Indroli, which is both the birthplace of Indulekha and the place where Indra atoned for his sins.

Kanoyaro is a small forest of *kadamba* trees. Its sweet water lakes, such as Panihari-kunda, attracted the great sage Kanva to set up his hermitage there in order to worship Lord Krsna.

Kanva Muni is a celebrated Vaisnava and son of the sage Medhatithi. By strictly following the principles of retired life *(vanaprastha*), Kanva was elevated to the assembly of heavenly King Indra.

Vedic culture recognises ten major brahminical lineages (gotras). According to the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* commentator Sridhara Svami, Krsna was in the line originating from Kanva Muni.

Kanva Muni is best known for raising the celestial daughter of Visvamitra Muni, Sakuntala, who was the queen of Maharaja Dusyanta and mother of the divinely empowered King Bharata.

*Srimad-Bhagavatam* narrates Bharata Maharaja’s birth, which is commonly accepted as having taken place in Kanva Muni’s Himalayan *asrama* on the bank of the river Malini.

Srila Prabhupada infers that due to Kanva Muni’s guidance, Bharata was a Vaisnava even as a child. As a result of the Muni’s influence, Bharata attained the preliminary state of transcendence in that life.

After Sakuntala and Bharata moved to Maharaja Dusyanta’s capital, Kanva Muni left his *asrama* in the Himalayas and moved to Kanoyaro in order to devote himself to the worship of Krsna in Vrndavana.

Kanva Muni would sit upon the banks of Krsna-kunda and blissfully recite his favourite names of Krsna, like Deva (“Lord”) and Cakra-pani (“He who holds the disc in His hand”).

Closer to Kamyavana is Indroli. The *gopa* Sagara and his wife Vela- devi gave birth to a contrary and hot-tempered little girl whom they called Indulekha because she was like a moon *(indu)* beam (*lekha*).

Born three days after Srimati Radharani as the sixth of the princess’s closest friends, Indulekha has a tan complexion and wears scarlet clothes the colour of a pomegranate flower.

As a child Indulekha learned gemology and the science of palmistry. She also learned how to decorate teeth, chant mantras, charm snakes, string necklaces, and weave cloth from the finest silk fibres.

In her village and the neighbouring area, Indulekha is the leader of her friends, such as Tungabhadra and Palindhika. Indulekha taught them to be skilled messengers in service to the divine couple.

By thus facilitating Radha’s and Krsna’s communications, Indulekha increases Their mutual love; consequently, cowherd girls throughout Vraja come to her for guidance in devotional service.

Indulekha engages these enthusiastic, pure-hearted *gopi* followers in fulfilling Radha’s and Krsna’s desires. She teaches them to make ornaments, sew garments, and oversee the divine couple’s treasury.

One night Radha and Krsna were playing a game of riddles. Krsna asked, “Who shows passionate redness when she appears, is very expert in various arts, and arouses amorous desires upon first sight?”

Pausing a moment, Srimati Radharani glanced at the faces of Her friends. As that glance fell on Indulekha, She pointed to Her friend and excitedly said, “It is My divine moonbeam, Indulekha.”

In his eternal identity as Rati-manjari, Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami acts as a maidservant of Indulekha-gopi. He prays to his superior, Indulekha, with these words of humble submission:

*Sphuran - mukta - gunja - mani - sumanasam hara - mcane mudendor lekha me racayatu tatha siksana - vidhim /yatha taih sanklptair dayita - sarasi madhya - sadane sphutam radha - krsnav ayam api jano bhusayati tau .*

**“May my guru Indulekha instruct me in the art of making beautiful garlands of pearls, jewels,** ***gunja,*** **and flowers, so that I can decorate Radha and Krsna in the temple at the centre of Radha-kunda.”**

Indra-kunda is another lake in Indroli. King Indra came here feeling humbled after Krsna had punished him for attacking the Vraja-vasis, whom the Lord had sheltered beneath Govardhana Hill.

Even though Indra had admitted his misconduct, he felt the need to reflect on the causes of his offence before subjecting himself to the humiliation that awaited him in his heavenly court.

On the banks of Indra-kunda he cast aside his celestial paraphernalia and absorbed himself in the life of a Vaisnava: chanting Krsna’s names, meditating on Krsna’s pastimes, and serving Krsna’s abode.

Indra wanted to be purified, but he was also aware that pride arising from opulence had contaminated his heart and could rear its ugly head again in the right circumstances. He was still a conditioned soul.

One day Indra approached the celestial cow Surabhi, disclosed his heart, and asked for advice on how to attain a transcendental position beyond the influence of intoxicating arrogance.

Surabhi suggested that they go to Navadvipa and worship Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, for the results Indra desired would be bestowed more quickly by Nadiya and Gaura than by Indroli.

The two celestials went to Godrumadvlpa. At Surabhi-kunja, where Thakura Bhaktivinoda would later build his home, they worshipped the Lord by incessantly chanting His name, Gauranga.

As Surabhi had predicted, they quickly attained the vision of handsome Caitanya Mahaprabhu, who addressed Indra with compassion and a voice choked with love. The Lord said:

“I know the inner core of your heart, your quest for perfect devotion. Soon I will appear in Nadiya. At that time, you too will take birth to serve Me and be freed from the grasp of illusion.”

This pastime shows that we can attain pure devotional service by approaching Vrndavana through Navadvipa, Krsna through Caitanya Mahaprabhu, and the *maha-mantra* through the Panca-tattva mantra.

Walking along the path between the line of trees that had been a main thoroughfare connecting the villages in Somogy County for centuries, I came to the peak of the hill just outside Kamyavana.

The word *kama* means “desire,” and because the fulfillment of every kind of desire is perfected in this forest of many charming *(kamya)* groves and lakes, it has always been known as Kamyavana.

Kamyavana forest fulfils not only the material desires of its visitors and the spiritual desires of Vaisnavas but also Krsna’s and the *gopis*’ hankerings for pure amorous loving exchanges, known also as *kama.*

Also called Kamavana, Kamyaka, and Kamyakavana, this fourth forest of Vraja qualifies visitors to relish the Lord’s abode by freeing them of sinful reactions and blessing them with pure devotion.

Kamyavana abounds with sacred places, of which the most prominent lakes, thrones, temples, and pillars each number eighty-four, although scriptures say that they total in the hundreds and thousands.

Kaman, the village of Mother Yasoda’s parents, lies at the heart of this forest. Nanda Maharaja lived in Kaman on his way to Nandagrama, as did the Pandavas for a part of their time in exile.

It is said that King Kamasena renovated many of the area’s sacred sites, which had mostly been lost to the influence of time or to man, as was the case for the lakes that were filled in for agricultural use.

Krsna is fond of Kamyavana for its beauty, for the variety of enjoyments it offers, and for the delicacies He and His friends are assured of tasting at the house of His maternal grandparents.

As the *gopas* arrive in Kamyavana, even the cows are excited to see the boys scramble up trees, pick the ripest and juiciest fruit, loudly taunt one another with jokes, or throw fruit at their friends.

The *gopas* flail their arms and open their eyes wide as they point out to Krsna the odd shapes in certain gardens, groves, or structures, which abound in the mysterious forest of Kamyavana.

Its lakes are full with every variety of blossoming lotus flower. Its gardens are just as bountiful with flowering vines, and the forest is rich with mysterious desire trees that glow with splendour.

The boys wander here and there. Finally they enter a branch of the Yamuna, form two groups, and enjoy colossal water fights that attract the cows and forest creatures as spectators.

Finally exhausted, the boys put on dry clothes, gather fruit, beg the local people for food, and, along with their lunch packs, enjoy a special picnic in the shade. After resting, they happily head home.

**\* \* \* \***

Caitanya Mahaprabhu came to Kamyavana from Govardhana. The Lord performed *parikrama* of the forest, bathed in all the lakes, and visited all the pastime places before proceed- ing to Nandagrama.

Jahnava-devi and Virabhadra Gosvami visited Kamyavana on separate occasions. Both followed the path taken by Caitanya Mahaprabhu, enjoying a tour through the sacred lakes and groves.

The eminence of Kamyavana as a pilgrimage place and a residence for renunciants has attracted many great personalities to its lakes and groves, all of which perpetually emanate spiritual mellows.

In ages long past the great Durvasa Muni would come here to reflect on the pastimes Krsna had performed in a previous day of Brahma, as well as those He would perform in the present one.

On one such visit during the Satya-yuga, as Durvasa Muni slept, a boy from heaven mischievously cut a few strands of the sage’s matted locks as a memento to show off to his friends.

The sage awoke and at once realised that he had been the butt of childish jokes. The Muni cursed the boy, “Since you crawl around to cause mischief, become an alligator for the duration of four ages.”

The boy begged forgiveness, and out of compassion the sage replied, “When the Lord of this forest, Krsna, appears in Navadvipa as the son of Saci, He will deliver you by His *nama-sankirtana.*

The boy then assumed the form of a crocodile and took up residence within a lake in Godrumadvipa. He terrorised animals and humans as he waited for his promised deliverance in the far distant future.

Four ages later, as predicted, Caitanya Mahaprabhu appeared in Mayapur. The Lord and His childhood friends once came to play near the lake, where they were warned of the presence of the crocodile.

The locals said, “No one bathes or drinks here out of fear of a giant crocodile.” At this, Nimai Pandita approached the lake, loudly chanting Krsna’s names, and at once attracted the creature.

The crocodile came out of the water and touched Lord Caitanya’s feet, at once regaining his original form. Falling before the Lord and offering prayers, the boy retold the story of how he had been cursed.

After offering obeisances and praising Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the youth returned to his heavenly abode to bid his parents farewell. As Durvasa had predicted, the boy then returned to Godhead.

Kamyavana is special in diverse ways. It was the last stop in Vraja for Vrndavana’s deities, like Govindaji, as they were being taken to safety in Rajasthan during Aurangzeb’s attacks on the *dhama.*

A unique feature of Kamyavana is the presence of Rupa Gosvami's deity of Vrnda-devi, who refused to leave Vraja. Since then she has been a source of inspiration for Vaisnava residents and pilgrims.

For me, an especially attractive feature of Kamyavana is that the Pandavas lived there for the twelfth year of their exile. They stayed in various places, including Kaman and its surrounding forests.

After King Yudhisthira gambled away his sovereignty over Indraprastha, the Kurus forced the Pandavas into exile for thirteen years, the last year of which they had to remain incognito.

Knowing that avoiding Duryodhana’s spies that last year would be extremely difficult without some special mercy, King Yudhisthira decided to come to Kamyavana to seek the blessings of the *dhama.*

Tribulations continued to beset them, even in Krsna’s land, but because the Pandavas were absorbed in talks of Krsna’s Vraja pastimes, their stay in Kamyavana was the best time of their lives.

Seated on the hilltop of Krsna Valley, I prayed to Vrnda-devi, who is always favourably disposed towards Krsna’s devotees, and fixed my mind on Vimala kunda, the starting place of Kamyavana’s *parikrama.*

Bathing without offence in this lake — the largest and most impressive in Kamyavana — is said to free one of all sinful reactions and enable one to return to Lord Krsna’s abode at the time of death.

Vimala-kuna's sanctity makes it attractive to sages and devotees like the Pandavas. At present, deities like Madana-mohana, Vimala-devi, and Nilakanthesvara Mahadeva are worshipped there.

While tending His cows in Kamyavana, Krsna waters them at this lake and sports in its pure waters with His friends. Due to its expansive size, Vimala-kunda is an endless playground for the *gopas.*

On the western side of the lake is Go-ghata, where Krsna’s cows drink. Countless temples, deities, and holy places grace this *kunda.* By bowing down once, respect is showed to them all.

Narada Muni once told King Bahulasva a pastime connected with Vimala-kunda. Hearing this pastime, in which Krsna housed ten million wives within its precincts, bestows all-auspiciousness.

When Lord Ramacandra brought His bride Sita to Ayodhya, the unmarried ladies of the city also desired to become His wives. For that purpose they underwent penance on the bank of the river Sarayu.

Lord Rama had taken a vow to accept only one wife; still, a voice assured those love-struck ladies that the son of Dasaratha would indeed accept them at a later date when He took birth in Vraja.

By the arrangement of the Lord, these female ascetics of Ayodhya were reborn in the country of Sindhu as the ten million beautiful daughters of the sonless King Vimala, ruler of Campaka City.

The pious king was distraught that he had no son to free him of his debt to the sages, forefathers, and demigods; that is, until one day the immaculate sage Yajnavalkya appeared in the king’s court.

The sage said, “O King! Give up your grief. You will give your daughters to Lord Krsna with an unprecedented dowry. You will please Him, free yourself of all obligations, and attain perfection.”

The king then enquired what Krsna would look like, whose son He would be, and where He would take birth. Yajnavalkya portrayed the Lord as the blackish four-armed son of Vasudeva, prince of Mathura.

But when King Vimala’s envoy reached Mathura, the locals informed him that the demonic king Kamsa had killed all the sons born to Vasudeva, and that the last offspring, a girl, had ascended to heaven.

Baffled, the envoy wandered along the bank of the Yamuna River as far as Vrndavana forest. There he saw a youth that at least partially fit the sage Yajnavalkya’s portrait of Lord Krsna.

He was handsome, dark as a monsoon cloud, had the sign of Srivatsa, and was decorated with a forest garland, but instead of administering a kingdom, He was herding cows — and He had two arms, not four.

The envoy returned to the king, relayed everything that he had seen, and asked for further directions. Should he return to Vrndavana and invite this two-handed youth to Campaka Puri?

At that very moment, all-knowing Bhisma, grandfather of the Kurus, arrived from Hastinapura. After King Vimala offered his distinguished guest an elaborate reception, he humbly enquired:

“Yajnavalkya Muni told me that Lord Hari will appear in Mathura as the son of Vasudeva and marry my daughters, yet Kamsa has killed all of Vaseneva's sons. How, then, will the sage’s words come to pass?”

Bhisma replied, “Vyasa told me that just after the Supreme Lord took birth in Mathura, Vasudeva secretly placed Him in the home of the cowherd king, Nanda, where Hari now sports as a young cowherd.

“Lord Ramacandra blessed some women of Ayodhya to take birth in your home. Marry your daughters to this Gopala, who in His eleventh year will leave Vraja and reveal His identity by killing Kamsa.”

King Vimala again sent his ambassador, who met Krsna in a secluded place in Vrndavana and conveyed to Him the king’s prayer: “O Lord! Please come and accept the hand of these girls who love You.”

Pleased by the king’s entreaty, Krsna called for Garuda. In a moment the Lord and the envoy traveled to Campaka City and appeared in the sky above a great sacrifice being conducted by King Vimala.

Tears streamed down Vimala’s face as he stood to receive the Lord. The king then fell at Krsna’s feet, first offering sweet words of praise, then his wealth, subjects, kingdom, and finally his host of daughters.

Krsna heartily accepted Campaka’s princesses and offered the king a boon. Vimala replied,

*mano me bhramari-bhutam sada tvat-pada-pankaje*  
*vasam-kuryad deva-deva nanyeccha me kadacana*

**“O Lord of lords! Please make my heart an eager bumblebee always humming at Your lotus feet. I shall never have any desire but this.”**

This was the only thought on the pure devotee, Sri Vimala’s mind.

Pleased to hear such words of unmotivated devotion, Krsna bestowed pure love upon His new in-laws, the king and his wives, and sent them directly to Vaikuntha on the back of Garuda.

The Lord then brought His beautiful wives to a splendid palace in the heart of Kamyavana forest. There the ladies served Him and passed the rest of their time playing games and singing His glories.

One moonlit night, Krsna expanded Himself into as many forms as there were wives and delighted their hearts by engaging them in the many facets of the *rasa* dance — dance, song, and love-sports.

King Vimala’s daughters, absorbed in unprecedented happiness, perspired profusely. The aromatic moisture from their spiritual bodies transformed into Vimala-kunda, the best of holy places.

By seeing, drinking from, bathing in, or worshipping Vimala-kunda, or by hearing about the perfection of the women of Ayodhya, one breaks a host of sins as great as Mount Meru and goes to Goloka.

**\* \* \* \***

Another of the Pandava’s pastimes linked to Vimala-kunda is the untimely arrival of Durvasa Muni and his famous demand that Queen Draupadi feed him and his sixty thousand disciples.

When the Pandavas were sent into exile, kindhearted Yudhisthira lamented that he could not properly feed the many *brahmanas,* attendants, and common citizens accompanying them.

In answer to the king’s prayers, the sun god gave him a mystical plate and explained, “Draupadi should place whatever she cooks on this serving dish. Until she eats, that food will be inexhaustible.”

Draupadi would cook a variety of delectable preparations and serve the many people accompanying her husbands as much as they desired. Only after everyone else was satisfied would she eat.

While the Pandavas were residing in Kamyavana, Duryodhana, wanting to harass Yudhisthira Maharaja even further, once brought his entourage and erected a tent city on the banks of Vimala-kunda.

Within sight and earshot of the Pandavas, Duryodhana and his friends entertained themselves in ways so outrageous that Indra became furious and sent his own commanders to arrest the fool.

The demigods made quick work of Duryodhana’s army and kidnapped Duryodhana. When King Yudhisthira came to know that his cousin-brother had been kidnapped, he asked Arjuna and Bhima to save him.

The two brothers were flabbergasted at being asked to rescue their tormentor, but Yudhisthira Maharaja explained an important principle that all Vaisnavas should bear in mind.

The righteous king said, “We may argue and even fight amongst one another as brothers, but when someone from outside our family attacks we must stand together, united against our common enemy.”

As requested, Arjuna and Bhima defeated the demigods and saved Duryodhana, who took Yudhisthira’s gesture of kindness as a sign of dishonour. He therefore became depressed and even more venomous.

So crestfallen was Duryodhana that he wanted to fast unto death. But his malicious friends and advisors convinced him to return to Hastinapura, where an unforeseen opportunity awaited.

Durvasa Muni and his legion of disciples had arrived at the palace unannounced, throwing even the royal Kuru household into confusion as to how and where to accommodate so many guests.

King Duryodhana, who could be a gracious host, received the sage and his retinue as if they were gods of heaven, winning the favour of the tempestuous and generally fearsome Durvasa.

Evil-hearted Duryodhana knew about the magic plate, as well as the time of day Draupadi normally finished her meal. He used these facts to hatch a plan that would make Durvasa Rsi curse the Pandavas.

As Durvasa prepared to leave Hastinapura, Duryodhana spoke highly of Draupadi’s cooking and Yudhisthira’s hospitality. Duryodhana humbly requested the sage to bless the Pandavas with his presence.

Durvasa Muni accepted this suggestion, departed for Kamyavana with disciples in tow, and arrived there as Duryodhana had planned, just after the Pandavas and Draupadi had finished their meals.

Durvasa left his disciples at Vimala-kunda and entered the Pandavas’ camp. After being received graciously, the sage ordered Yudhisthira Maharaja to prepare a meal for him and his retinue.

Yudhisthira Maharaja bowed to the Muni’s request, but after Durvasa had gone to bathe, the king raised the alarm, begging Draupadi to avert the disaster that threatened to devour the Pandavas.

Surrounded by her husbands, Draupadi said, “O King! I have not only eaten off the magic plate but also washed it. Until the rising of tomorrow’s sun, the plate will not expand the food I place on it.”

The king despaired for a moment, but then remembered his well- wishing cousin Lord Krsna. He replied, “*Devi!* Please call our worshipable Deity to rescue us. He is especially affectionate to you.”

With no other recourse, Draupadi prayed to Krsna just as she had at the gambling match in Hastinapura. True to His promise to protect His devotees, Krsna answered Draupadi’s prayers as He did before.

Krsna was in Dvaraka at the time, resting with Queen Rukmini. But as soon as He heard Draupadi’s prayer He at once rose from bed, left His palace, and appeared in Vrndavana within moments.

Indeed, Krsna left Dvaraka in such haste that He looked disheveled: not properly dressed, hair uncombed, and His forehead covered with perspiration from the exertion of hurrying to Kamyavana.

Yet throughout Draupadi’s disclosure of the Pandavas’ plight, Krsna’s consistent radiant smile reassured His devotees. After she had finished, the Lord replied, “Bring me your plate at once!”

Draupadi protested that the plate was clean, but Krsna insisted. Once the magic plate was in His hands He examined it and found, just on the outer edge, one grain of rice, which He quickly and greedily ate.

Krsna is the Soul of all souls. Because one grain of Draupadi’s devotional cooking fully satisfied Him, the sages bathing in Vimala-kunda also felt fully satiated, their hunger having disappeared.

The Lord then told Bhima to call Durvasa for his meal. Bhima was amazed by the order, but surrendered soul that he is, he at once went to Vimala-kunda, shaking the earth with his steps.

Now it was Durvasa and his followers who found themselves in an embarrassing situation. Feeling as if they had just eaten a complete feast, they could not even think of eating another morsel of food.

Durvasa saw Bhima approaching and took the only recourse he had. Along with his disciples he at once disappeared from the scene and returned to his own *asrama* to ponder what had happened.

That night Krsna and the Pandavas sat together and talked the whole night—first about the day’s events, then about His boyhood pastimes in Kamyavana. But at the break of dawn the Lord disappeared.

By this pastime Krsna taught the world three lessons: He is always ready to protect His devotees, He is satisfied with whatever one offers Him in pure devotion, and that everyone is satisfied when He is.

This third lesson is perhaps most relevant to those people today who live by the mistaken philosophy that a life of self-centered gratification will assure them of happiness and satisfaction.

Narada offers an example as evidence that just the opposite is true,

*yatha taror mula-nisecanena trpyanti tat-skandha-bhujopasakhah*  
*pranopaharac ca yathendriyanam tathaiva sarvarhanam acyutejya*

**“As pouring water on the root of a tree energises the trunk, branches, twigs, and everything else \[like leaves and flowers\], and as supplying food to the stomach enlivens the senses and limbs of the body —**

**“Simply worshipping the Supreme Personality of Godhead through devotional service automatically satisfies the demigods \[and all other living entities\], who are parts of that Supreme Personality.”**

About a month after the incident with Durvasa, another calamity befell the Pandavas and Draupadi. The brothers had gone away from camp, leaving their wife in the care of the *brahmanas.*

Coincidentally, Jayadratha was passing through Kamyavana on the way to his marriage in Salyadesa. He saw beautiful Draupadi collecting flowers and painting the forest with her dark complexion.

Jayadratha was unsuccessful in his bid to win Draupadi at her *svayamvara* ceremony, but he never gave up his attachment for her, or the sinister hope that by some means he could attain her.

Seeing Draupadi alone, Jayadratha seized the opportunity and sent a messenger with a proposition that she join him. Chaste Draupadi at once rejected his proposal, as well as his subsequent messages.

Finally Jayadratha personally came to kidnap Draupadi. As befitting a *ksatriya* woman, she resisted, but she was no match for his superior strength. Jayadratha took her onto his chariot by force.

The chief priest of the Pandavas, Dhaumya Muni, relayed the incident to Maharaja Yudhisthira. The five brothers at once sprang into action, attacking and defeating Jayadratha’s army.

Bhima finally caught and beat Jayadratha. He and Arjuna wanted to kill the rascal, but soft-hearted Draupadi requested them to let him go. Instead of killing him, they humiliated him with a ridiculous haircut.

Arjuna cut off all of Jayadratha’s facial hair and the hair on his head, leaving five small sikhas. They then forced Jayadratha to bow before Maharaja Yudhisthira as a slave.

**\* \* \* \***

According to some itineraries, the *parikrama* of Kamyavana starts before Vimala-kunda at nearby Dharma-kunda, where one of the most memorable pastimes of the Pandavas’ stay in Kaman took place.

As the Pandavas were chasing a deer that had run off with a *brahmana’s* ladle and *arani* sticks, thirst overcame them. Seeing Dharma-kunda in the distance, they sent Nakula to fetch water.

The lake was secluded, full of lotuses and lilies, and resounded with the muted cries of waterfowl. Its transparent waters propelled Nakula to quench his own thirst before filling his quiver with water.

As he approached the lake a voice rang out, “O child! Do not drink this water. This lake belongs to me. You cannot drink from it without having first answered my questions. Do not drink the water!”

Startled, Nakula looked around, but saw no one. He then impetu- ously drank the water and at once fell dead on the spot. After some time, Maharaja Yudhisthira sent Sahadeva to check on his brother.

Sahadeva arrived at the lake to find Nakula lifeless on the ground. Lamenting yet unable to control his thirst, he thought to drink. When the voice warned him, he too ignored it at the cost of his life.
