Arjuna and Bhima in turn followed their brothers to Dharma-kunda and, like their brothers, overcome by some mysterious force, they too ignored the celestial warning and died on the spot.

Finally, Maharaja Yudhisthira followed the path to the lake, the atmosphere surrounding which seemed heavenly, aside from the gruesome scene created by the dead bodies of his brothers.

Yudhisthira Maharaja ran to his brothers, examined each for a sign of life, but found none. Neither were there signs of a battle or of a foe who could have killed those four invincible warriors.

The king was perplexed. He too approached the water to drink, but, being wiser than his brothers, hesitated when warned. He asked, “Who are you?” A fearsome creature appeared and replied, “A Yaksa.”

The Yaksa continued, “I own this lake, but your brothers tried to take my water without answering my questions to satisfy me. For the act of stealing another’s property, all four died.”

Yudhisthira Maharaja replied, “I will not take what is yours. Ask me whatever you like, and I will answer.” At this submissive response, the Yaksa questioned the king about the principles of *dharma.*

The exchange between the mysterious Yaksa and Yudhisthira Maharaja is described in detail in the appendix entitled “Maharaja Yudhisthira’s Answers to Yamaraja’s Questions.”

Srila Prabhupada repeated this story often. He was especially fond of citing Yudhisthira Maharaja’s answer to one of the Yaksa’s last questions, “What is the most wonderful thing?” The king replied:

*Ahany ahani bhutani gacchantiha yamalayam*  
*sesah sthavaram icchanti kim ascaryam atah param*

**“Every moment people pass to the abode of death, but those who are alive think that they shall live forever.”**

After Yudhisthira Maharaja answered every question correctly, he received the boon of having one of his brothers brought back to life. The king surprised the Yaksa by asking him to spare Nakula’s life.

The Yaksa was taken aback because in the upcoming war, compared to Arjuna or Bhima, Nakula would be the least valuable. The Yaksa enquired, “Why have you asked for Nakula and not another?”

Yudhisthira Maharaja replied, “My father Pandu had two wives, Kunti and Madri, both of whom treated me equally. Is it right for me as Kunti’s son to be alive without either of Madri’s sons?”

The Yaksa challenged the king’s wisdom in light of the upcoming war and his likelihood of losing without either Arjuna or Bhima, the two most powerful. Yudhisthira Maharaja replied in no uncertain terms.

The king argued that more important than to act according to external results was to observe the principles of *dharma,* for one who conducts himself in this way is victorious whatever the outcome.

Yudhisthira explained why righteousness is the safest and most triumphant path in life with a saying that is now common, *dharmo raksati raksitah:* “Virtue (*dharma)* protects those who protect it.”

At these words the Yaksa, stirred by the king’s sense of truth and virtue, blessed him with the life of all four of his brothers, upon which they all woke up as if from a deep sleep, refreshed and free of thirst.

Yudhisthira Maharaja then asked the Yaksa to divulge his true identity. The Yaksa showed his original form as *dharma* personified — Yamaraja, the superintendent of death and the king’s father.

The Pandavas bowed to Yamaraja, who revealed that in the form of a deer he had stolen the *brahmanas* utensils and had then tested the brothers and the wisdom of his own son, the image of virtue.

Pleased with Yudhisthira Maharaja, Yamaraja returned the *brahmana’s* property and then gave the king a boon that in their upcoming year of hiding, no human would be able to recognise them.

Yamaraja continues to reside at Dharma-kunda, where devotees recall and get inspiration to apply the essential principles of *dharma* — such as strong *sadhana*, cleanliness, and sense control — in practical life.

The sublime stage of transcendence and the treasure of *vraja-bhakti* can only be attained by submission to guru and Krsna, by dedication to regulated devotion, and by elevating oneself to the mode of goodness.

In other words, only by embracing and patiently executing the purifying activities of Vaisnava *dharma* outlined for us by Srila Prabhupada do we enter the secrets of Vraja — in no other way.

As in Nandagrama, pilgrims sometimes visit pastime places within Kaman before taking Kamyavana’s outer *parikrama* path, which begins with a bath at either Vimala-kunda or Dharma-kunda.

Originally Kaman was surrounded by a wall and had seven gates in the directions of Dig, Setubandha-kunda, Carana-pahari, Vaisnavi- devi (in Punjab), Delhi, Nandagrama, and Mathura, via Varsana.

Inside the wall, pilgrims typically visit Dharma-kunda, around which lie other lakes such as Panca-pandava-kunda, where deities of the Pandavas were once worshipped in a now-collapsed temple.

Nearby is Pandava-kupa, a well from which the sons of Pandu would draw water and where Draupadi would do her washing. Like many holy sites in the area, these places too have been ploughed under.

In a *brahmanas* house some call Panca-pandava-tirtha, deities of Yudhisthira Maharaja and two of his brothers are served. The Pandavas are also said to have lived here for part of their stay.

The deities are black and very tall, and the deity of our worshipful hero Yudhisthira Maharaja exudes a regal and imposing devotional presence. One cannot help but bow down before him.

Just opposite the Pandavas’ house is the temple of Kamesvara Mahadeva, a deity originally worshipped by the Pandavas and later installed by Vajranabha as the guardian of this forest.

Foremost of demigods, Mahadeva invested this place with his potencies; thus a small lake beside the temple is known as Mani-karnika- ghata. A bath here grants liberation to the sincere person.

King Vrsabhanu and his wife, desiring offspring, made a pilgrimage to Kamyavana, offering heartfelt prayers to Kamesvara Mahadeva and bathing with great devotion in Mani-karnika-kunda.

Maharaja Vrsabhanu did not want ordinary children. He wanted children who would glorify his family’s devotion to the Supreme Lord. And Lord Mahadeva answered the king’s prayers.

Mother Kirtida proceeded to give birth to three divine children, one after the other: Sridama, Srimati Radharani, and Ananga-manjari, all of whom are intimately connected with Lord Krsna.

Of course, the family members of King Vrsabhanu are all eternally connected. They do not require the blessings of Lord Siva to be united, but Krsna’s associates follow earthly customs just as He does.

Some places in Kaman are linked to more recent history. A few of Vrndavana’s deities, after fleeing to avoid Aurangzeb’s atrocities, stayed in Kaman before moving on to Jaipur or back to Vrndavana.

Later, temples were built to house Deities—such as Radha-Vallabha, who stayed in Kaman until His return to Vrndavana — or to commemorate the stay of Deities like Radha-Govinda or Gopinatha.

In the temple of Radha-Govinda is an ancient deity of Vrnda-devi who was originally installed by Vajra at Brahma-kunda in Vrndavana, and was lost to the ravages of time, as were many other deities.

Vrnda-devi was rediscovered by Rupa Gosvami in Brahma-kunda, then moved to and installed in a small temple adjacent to the Radha-Govinda temple built by Raja Man Singh upon Sri Rupa’s request.

Vrnda-devi is large, with black eyes, a reddish complexion, and ten arms that are generally covered by her clothes. Her presence in Kamyavana is a sign of her compassion upon Krsna’s devotees.

When the time arrived for Vrndavana’s Deities to leave Kaman for Jaipur, Vrnda-devi instructed the priests of Govindaji, “I cannot leave Vraja. If I were to leave, then what would devotees think?

“How could devotees have faith in Vrndavana if Vrnda-devi herself were not present here. Krsna may come and go, but I can never leave. Therefore you must leave me here in Kamyavana.”

**\* \* \* \***

In the vicinity of Radha-Govinda’s temple is Carana- kunda. Once, while herding cows in Kamyavana, Krsna and Balarama decided to visit their in-laws in Kaman to taste their delicious cooking.

Having walked barefoot from Nandagrama, Their lotus feet were covered with the dust of Vraja, so before approaching their in-laws’ house the brothers washed Their hands and feet in this lake.

A throne famed as Visnu-simhasana is also nearby. It is the sitting place of Lord Krsna, but the Vraja-vasis also go there to celebrate the marriage of Lord Narayana and Laksmi in the month of Vaisakha.

Atop a large hill at the edge of Kaman sits Caurasi-stambh, an eighty-four (*caurasi)* pillar *(stambh)* palace. It resembles Nandaraja’s eighty *(assi)* pillar palace in Gokula called Assi-stambh.

The two buildings appear similar, but have different architectural floor plans: the one in Kamyavana is based around a large open courtyard, while the one in Gokula is an entirely closed structure.

It is said that this was the residence of Nanda Maharaja and his family when they lived in Kamyavana. A representative of its archeological department told me that the Pandavas also used it.

King Yudhisthira would sit upon a large stone throne, not far from which is a staircase that leads to an expansive balcony — above the throne and to the right — that served as Draupadi’s kitchen.

People from both within and outside Vraja knew that the pious king was living in Kamyavana, so they often came here for his infallible guidance and mediation skills in settling disputes regarding *dharma.*

Moreover, great sages like Vyasa regularly visited the Pandavas to give moral support and spiritual guidance to help them tolerate the injustices Duryodhana continued to heap on them during their exile.

In this regard Srila Prabhupada writes that the sage Brhadasva, one such advisor to the Pandavas, narrated the famous story of Nala and Damayanti as an example of how to tolerate the reversals of life.

The grandeur of Caurasi-stambh is a testament to the elevated status of the great men and women who joined Krsna to help Him enact the drama of His appearance and mission on earth.

But like most of the beautiful monuments in Vraja, it stands sorely neglected, pillars gradually falling into rubble. Cornices and stone slabs have already fallen, and with them the history of this place.

Behind Caurasi-stambh Hill is the temple of Radha-Gopinatha. *Pratibhu* expansions of those Deities are worshipped here, as in Vrndavana town. Their original forms reside in Jaipur City.

Prior to and during the time of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, Kamyavana, unlike Vrndavana forest, was still a thriving trade centre, a relatively populous area and well-known place of pilgrimage.

Vrndavana, on the other hand, especially what is now Vrndavana town, was an uninhabited jungle. Local profiteers of the time promoted Kamyavana as the “Vrndavana forest” of scriptures.

But when Lord Caitanya’s disciples came to Vraja, they showed by extensive research and preaching that the distinguishing landmarks and pastime places of Vrndavana were not actually in Kamyavana.

The discovery of Dvadasaditya-tila and Gomati-tila upstream from Mathura showed that pastimes like Kaliya-damana and Vamsivata could only have taken place on that portion of the Yamuna.

In this way Vrndavana forest was properly identified and developed according to its own heritage, and Kamyavana was extolled properly for its own unique places, not those of other forests in Vraja.

Now that we have heard some of the pastime places within Kaman, let us return to Vimala-kunda and follow the *parikrama* path that winds through Kamyavana like a snake hungry for Krsna’s glories.

Next to Vimala-kunda is charming Kamana-kunda. Its waters are composed of *kama,* the liquid essence of Kamyavana. A bath there can fulfil the spiritual desires of those pure in heart.

The *gopis,* knowing the secret of the lake, come there to worship the *kunda* and bathe in its waters with great devotion. Krsna then appears there and reciprocates their pure *kama.*

In a secluded grove behind Kamana-kunda is Narada-kunda. There Narada Muni fell into an ecstatic swoon while singing of Krsna’s pastimes, his tears creating a pond that still bears his name.

Yasoda-kunda and Nanda-kunda mark two neighbouring lakes where the king and queen of Vrndavana — and Krsna when He is awake and willing—bathe while visiting their relatives in Kaman.

At other times, when Krsna and Balarama are herding the cows in this direction, They enjoy resting in the shade of the trees surrounding the lakes, while the cows drink the clear water.

Pointing to the far side of Yasoda-kunda, Krsna once told His friends, “Even when we visit here and our in-laws are eager to provide the best food for us, mother insists on making butter for Me herself.

“Sometimes as we play in the water she churns the yogurt and when she is finished she washes the pots herself and insists on carrying the butter back while her servants carry the churn. Such is My mother!”

In the wooded area between these two lakes is the place Prabodhananda Sarasvati first performed *bhajana* upon arriving in Vrndavana following Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s visit to Sri Rangam.

Not long after the Thakura arrived in Kamyavana, his nephew Gopala Bhatta joined him. Gopala Bhatta, a teen of no more than thirteen or fourteen years, was a pure devotee of the Lord.

The saints lived between the two lakes and visited all the places in Kamyavana associated with Krsna, gradually making their way to Vrndavana where they met Lokanatha and Bhugarbha Gosvamis.

Kamyavana’s lakes rest in clusters so numerous that from the sky they appear like a treasure chest of turquoise necklaces dropped on earth by a careless attendant of Kuvera, treasurer of the *devas.*

Indeed, even the demigods are unable to count, not to mention bathe in, all the lakes scattered throughout Kamyavana. Of all the forests in Vraja-mandala, none is as opulent in lakes as this one.

The next lake, called Setubandha-kunda, or Lanka-kunda, represents the point of the ocean at Ramesvara from where Lord Ramacandra crossed to Lanka over a bridge (*setu*) of joined (*bandha)* boulders.

One summer day, accompanied by Srimati Radharani and Her friends, Krsna sat on the bank of this lake watching the monkeys jump from the trees into the water, frolicking as *gopas* would.

Occasionally a monkey would come, offer obeisances to Krsna, and then return to its companions and their mischief. Seeing this, Lalita- devi spoke to Visakha in a way meant to provoke Krsna.

“These monkeys’ friendship with Krsna reminds me of Lord Ramacandra’s friendship with the *vanaras* who built a bridge of stones across the ocean to save Sita from Ravana’s clutches.”

Krsna at once replied, “Lalita! Your words are divinely inspired. I am Raghunatha, the son of Maharaja Dasaratha, now born in the home of King Nanda.” The *gopis* giggled wildly at Krsna’s response.

Lalita replied in jest, “I believe that You are the leader of monkeys, but if You want us to believe that You are the jewel of the solar dynasty, then You will have to float a bridge of stones here.”

Krsna expanded His chest and accepted the *gopis*’ challenge. He called the monkeys to attention with His flute-song and with another melody instructed them to bring stones to His feet.

As the monkeys brought stone after stone, Krsna touched each one with His hand. Thrown into the lake by the monkeys, the stones then floated and then came together to form a bridge to the other side.

The monkeys, overjoyed at their achievement, ran back and forth across the bridge, leaping up and down, chattering, and jumping into the water, sometimes to the left and sometimes to the right.

Looking satisfied with Himself, Krsna raised His eyebrows as if to say, “Well, Lalita! Do you believe Me now?” The *gopis* accepted the obvious and sang, “Sri Rama! Jaya Rama! Jaya, jaya Rama!”

Basking in rare applause from the *gopis,* Krsna and the leader of His band of monkeys bowed to their audience. The *gopis* then left to collect flowers for a victor’s garland, and Krsna took Radha’s hand.

The divine couple then walked across the stone bridge to Lanka’s shore and on to Asokavana, where Ravana had once held sorrowful Sita-devi captive, and where Krsna now held blissful Radha captive.

**\* \* \* \***

After some distance the *parikrama* path enters a dense wood in which can be found sacred Luklukana-micali-kunda. Krsna and the cowherd girls play hide-and-seek in and around this lake.

When the *gopis* happen to be in Kamyavana at the same time as Krsna, they send Him a message to meet them. As soon as His friends doze off after their meal, Krsna sneaks away and comes to this lake.

The atmosphere of the lake garden inspires a sportive spirit. Krsna sometimes hides in the forest or even in the caves of the hill nearby, and the gopis fan out in all directions to find Him.

On one such occasion, Sri Krsna and the *gopis* devised a new version of hide-and-seek. They would all go underwater to see who could hold their breath the longest — first up was the loser, last up the winner.

Dressed in bathing clothes, Krsna and the cowherd girls entered the water until only their faces were visible — a shimmering blue lotus at the centre of a cluster of golden lotuses.

Filling the surrounding gardens with their laughter, they all agreed to submerge at the same time. Krsna counted, and on three all the lotuses went underwater, disappearing from sight all at once.

But the *gopis* had conspired to defeat Krsna. While He was diligently holding His breath underwater, they all surfaced, barely able to contain their glee at outwitting their unsuspecting lover.

The *gopis* remained above water until Krsna could no longer hold His breath. Just as His telltale topknot broke the surface, the girls all silently submerged, as if they had been underwater the entire time.

Krsna was astonished! He thought, “How could such frail girls hold their breath for so long?” He waited and waited. Finally the *gopis* surfaced one by one and cheerfully called out, “You lose! We win!”

Not one to be easily defeated, Krsna said, “Let’s do it again!” He counted to three, again they all ducked under water, again the *gopis* repeated their trick, and again Krsna lost. How was it possible?

By now Krsna suspected that there was deception behind the *gopis’* victories. The third time they played, He opened His eyes underwater and saw everything His girlfriends were up to.

Now Krsna was ready for a trick of His own. On the fourth time they submerged, He who is the vital air of all that lives stayed underwater, disappearing unseen to another part of the lake.

The *gopis* surfaced as they had done earlier and waited for the familiar sign that Krsna was surfacing, just as He had done before, but there was no familiar topknot, no burst of bubbles, no sign of Hari.

The *gopis* thought, “He must have taken a deep breath,” but after waiting another two, three, four minutes, they began to look at each other — at first in bewilderment, then in alarm.

It seemed to the *gopis’* that a millennium was passing while they waited for Krsna to emerge from the water. Unable to wait any longer, their minds full of anxiety, they began to search for Him.

The *gopis* looked for Krsna in the lake’s depths and shallows, beneath the overhanging boughs of trees, and within the clusters of water flowers, but He was nowhere to be found. Where had He gone?

The cowherd girls came out of the lake, their clothes and hair dripping water, and frantically searched for Him in the gardens and groves around the lake, but He was still nowhere to be found.

Repentant for having cheated Krsna, fearing that He had lost His life as a result of their pranks, the *gopis* finally gathered at the top of the *ghata* and spoke to one another in the delirium of ecstatic love.

One gopi said, “Alas! Has our Krsna drowned?” Another *gopi* added, “Why did we play hide-and-seek in the water?” A third exclaimed, “We should never have cheated Him just to win!”

Finally a *gopi* sobbed, “Have we lost our beloved, never to see Him again?” But just before the import of these words could transform play into tragedy, Krsna popped out of the water at the foot of the stairs.

Gasping for air, eyes red, hair disheveled, He acted as if he had stayed underwater all that time and was unaware of the *gopis*’ distress. He smiled playfully and called out to the girls, “You lose! I win!”

And though Krsna had won, the *gopis,* after enjoying the relief of seeing Him alive, quickly concluded He had cheated. In return for His “victory,” they made Him pay the price of their loving abuses.

On the side of the *parikrama* path towards Kaman is a necklace of lakes, each with a story to tell that could fill volumes. But let us at least hear about them briefly and offer them our deep respects.

At the lakes called Kasi, Prayaga, Puskara, and Gomati reside these holy places’ presiding deities, who are absorbed in remembering Krsna’s pastimes and pleased to be touched by Krsna’s devotees.

Vaisnavas make up the vast majority of Vrndavana’s residents and pilgrims. Such worshippers are of a different mood and calibre than those who visit these holy places in their different locations in India.

Residence in Kamyavana blesses these holy places with the rarely attained treasure of contact with Krsna’s devotees, a contact that accelerates their progress towards the goal they too seek in Vraja.

The affect of Vaisnava company is explained,

*aksnoh phalam tvadrs'a- darsanam hi tanoh phalam tvadrsa-gatra-sangah*  
*jihva-phalam tvadrsa-kirtanam hi sudurlabha bhagavata hi loke*

**“O devotee of the Lord!**

**“To see you is the perfection of the eyes, to touch you is the perfection of the sense of touch, and to glorify your qualities is the perfection of the tongue. It is rare to find a pure devotee like you.”**

Gaya personified resides at Gaya-kunda, along with the sage Agastya Muni. People who think that their forefathers’ progress to higher destinations is hindered confidently offer them water or *pinda* here.

Once, Nanda Maharaja, following family tradition, felt impelled to visit Gaya to offer oblations to his forefathers. He gave orders to his servants to make suitable preparations for the pilgrimage.

Krsna saw bullock carts being loaded for a long trip and asked His father, “O wise king! Where are you planning to go?” Nanda Maharaja replied, “We are going on pilgrimage to Gaya.

“While there we will offer *pinda-dana* to our forefathers, as Lord Ramacandra did, and free them and ourselves from sin. What’s more, we will see the footprints Lord Visnu left there in slaying Gayasura.”

Not at all happy to hear that He would have to leave Vrndavana and interrupt His play, Krsna proposed another solution. “Just as all holy places are present in Vraja,” He said with authority, “so is Gaya.

“O King! If you feel that you must offer oblations to your ancestors even though they were all sinless, then why take the long route to Magadha when the shorter one to Kamyavana is just as beneficial.”

Nandaraja offered to go to Kamyavana on their way back, but Krsna insisted that it was better to go there first. The caravan thus set out for Kaman, with a visit to Mother Yasoda’s relatives planned as well.

Once in Kamyavana, Krsna brought His father and uncles to Gaya- kunda. After they had made offerings to their ancestors the Vraja-vasi elders felt fully satisfied that their duty had been properly done.

We may wonder how Gaya-kunda can be equal to Gaya-tirtha without the presence of Visnu’s footprint. The answer is that the footprint of the Supreme Visnu, Krsna, is present on adjacent Candrasena Hill.

Krsna left His footprint there on a pleasant night at the junction of spring and summer. Following fragrant breezes, He and the *gopis* had been moving from one forest to another, *rasa* dancing all the while.

Walking through the many delightful groves of Kundavana, Krsna and the *gopis* finally came to Kamyavana. So enchanted was Krsna by the beauty of the forest that He desired to praise it with His flute.

Climbing to the summit of Candrasena Hill, Krsna played such a beguiling melody that the girls of Vraja with Him, and the demigod- desses in airplanes above Him, all fainted.

Influenced by that mysterious flute-song, all kinds of moving and nonmoving living entities were stunned. The rivers and streams turned solid, the hills and mountains slowly melted.

Krsna sank into the softened stone of this hill, leaving the imprint of His feet, which again hardened when He stopped playing the flute. Those prints are the most relishable sight for His devotees’ eyes.

Lord Caitanya, Lord Nityananda, Advaita Acarya, Madhavendra Puri, Madhvacarya, and the Gosvamis all came to have *darsana* of Krsna’s footprints here and by so doing tasted life’s perfection.

At Gaya-tirtha, Lord Visnu killed the powerful demon Gaya by stepping on him. The demon’s body was transformed into a series of hills, a holy place visited by untold pilgrims even today.

But Krsna created a Gaya even more sacred in Kamyavana with those same lotus feet, mixed not with chivalrous valour, but with the superior touch of amorous love and the flute-song’s caress.

**\* \* \* \***

In the area between Candrasena Hill and Kaman rests a lake called Dvaraka-kunda, where Krsna brought His wives from Dvaraka, stayed with them in a city of tents, and showed them His footprints.

The marks of Krsna’s footprints on the hill are called *carana-pahari.* One night, Krsna and the *gopis* were dancing in Kamyavana, and the girls became tired. As they rested, Krsna climbed Candrasena Hill.

To entice His beloveds to join Him, Krsna played His flute, the sound of which produced such divine enchantment that it not only melted the hill, but also the hearts of the *gopis* reclining nearby.

Krsna’s heart also melted, affected by His own flute playing as much as the hill was, and His mind, as bewildered as the minds of the *gopis,* prevented Him from seeing His beloveds from the top of the hill.

Intoxicated, Krsna staggered to a small lake at the bottom of Candrasena Hill. From there He could not tell whether to turn right, left, or go straight. He had no idea where he had left the *gopis.*

His senses of perception curtailed, Krsna sat down beside the lake and fixed His mind on the infallible compass — Srimati Radharani, who would surely guide Him in the right direction.

The Lord meditated upon Sri Radha’s features and Her witty talks. He remembered how They had just recently danced through the forests and enjoyed the beauty of groves, lakes, and gardens.

Since Krsna’s thoughts (*dhyana)* of Radha are not different from Her, they formed a pathway to Her lotus feet. And the lake where that pathway began, Krsna’s sitting place, is known as Dhyana-kunda.

As Krsna was climbing up Carana-pahari, Radha had retired to a lakeside garden to rest. That lake is now known as Vihvala-kunda, for there Krsna’s flute music totally bewildered (*vihvala*) Her mind.

As Her friends fanned Her with peacock feathers, Radharani lost Herself in exclusive meditation on Krsna’s lotus face and tender smile, a state in which She sometimes laughed and sometimes cried.

Even with Her eyes open Radha could not distinguish Her friends from the trees, nor tell whether it was day or night, nor understand the meaning of words spoken to or questions asked of Her.

Just as Radharani was in the depths of this divine trance, Krsna arrived. Taking Lalita’s advice He did not intrude on Radha’s ecstasy but went at a nearby lake called Syama-kunda to wait.

Gradually, by chanting Krsna’s names and massaging Radha’s body, the *gopis* were able to bring Her to external consciousness. They told Her that Krsna was waiting for them to resume their loving pastimes.

Radha submitted to the *gopis*’ will, and they quickly dressed Her in fresh clothes and flower ornaments. Her consciousness, however, remained linked to the subjects of Her previous internal trance.

When Her consciousness turned within itself, Krsna was with Her; when Her consciousness turned outward to Her surroundings, He was gone. And to Radha the two states were the same reality.

In Her bewilderment, then, Radha thought that Krsna was playing with Her, sometimes appearing and sometimes disappearing. Due to His apparent fickleness, jealous anger arose in Her mind.

Meanwhile, Krsna was waiting at Syama-kunda as Lalita had ordered. He kept looking down the path to catch a first glimpse of Radha, a glimpse that would dispel His loneliness and fill Him with pleasure.

He didn’t have long to wait. Radha and Her friends suddenly appeared. Krsna’s heart rejoiced, and He stood up to receive Her, but Radha kept walking right past Him without even slowing down.

Her lips in a pout, Her head held high, Her mind burning from the imagined injustice that He had toyed with Her, Radha ignored Krsna’s presence and showed Him only Her back as She walked away.

Krsna was dumfounded. What had He done? Still, He knew well the ways of Her love. He knew that Her anger needed no reason. And He knew that if He did not act quickly, Their pastimes would end.

Krsna caught up with the *gopis* and trailed alongside His beloved like a lost puppy. He offered profuse apologies, but was met with stony silence. He tried to hold Her hand, but was shooed away.

As Radha and the others kept walking, Lalita and Visakha stepped off the forest path and called Krsna over to them. Lalita said, “Don’t worry. Our friend’s love for You will never loosen its grip on Her.

“But just now that love deludes Her. She thinks You have repeatedly ignored Her. Only You have the expertise and ways to appease Her.” Visakha added, “And You have no time to waste.”

The Lord was especially pleased with Lalita and Visakha for divulging the cause of Radha’s anger and counseling Him how to pacify Her. The two lakes at that place thus bear these *gopis’* names.

Krsna ran ahead of Sri Radha and knelt in the middle of the forest path, leaving Her no room to pass on either side of Him. He looked up into Her fiery eyes and said in a pleading voice, “O Radha!

“How can You blame Me for falling victim to Your charm? Whenever I see Your flawless beauty I faint. How can You condemn Me for leaving You, then, when You are the one who makes Me senseless?”

Krsna could see Radha suppress a slight smile, so He continued, “O *devi*! To prevent Me from swooning to Your charms, please withdraw the sweetness of Your face and the loveliness of Your form.”

Krsna’s clever words dispelled Radha’s anger *(mana)* as quickly as it had been aroused. The lake next to Syama-kunda, where Krsna was able to breathe a sigh of relief is now known as Mana-sarovara.

Radha, now externally aware, took the lead from Krsna in Their joking affairs. She walked beneath a forest canopy and voiced Her eagerness to see a feminine beauty greater than Hers.

“I have heard that My beauty is not the ultimate. We have repeatedly heard speakers of the *Puranas* say that feminine charm reaches its summit in Mohini-murti, the enchantress of self-controlled Siva.”

Krsna assured Radha that Mohini-murti beauty could not compare to Hers, but Radha was not satisfied with words. She wanted more evidence. She wanted to see Mohini for Herself—and now!

Krsna and the *gopis* stopped in a clearing beside a crystalline lake shimmering in the night breeze. Radha persisted, “PaurnamasI-devi told us that You appeared as Mohini-murti from the ocean of milk.

“Please show us Your Mohini-rupa, and let My friends judge whose feminine wealth is greater.” Krsna looked to the *gopis*’ faces, all of which were aglow with expectation. They too were excited.

To prove that Sri Radhika was indeed the ultimate beauty, more beautiful than even His Mohini form, Krsna revealed the pastime incarnation of His beauty at the lake called Mohini-kunda.

Draped in a golden sari and ornamented with diamonds, Mohini-devi first stood alone beneath the moonlit sky and cast a momentary spell over the cowherd girls. Then She stood beside Srimati Radharani.

Krsna’s pastime incarnation appeared to be like Radhika’s shadow as the two beauties stood side by side. The *gopis* all agreed that the beauty of Mohini could never match that of Radharani.

After satisfying both Radha and the *gopis* with the vision of His Mohini form, Krsna resumed His original cowherd form. Confident of Radha’s favour, He then invited Her to continue Their pastimes.

As before, the divine couple and Their associates roamed from grove to grove. From Their pastime palette They painted the colours of *kama* onto the canvas of Kamyavana with the brushes of Their love.

**\* \* \* \***

The hill rising to the left of the *parikrama* path is called Indrasena-parvata. It is a special playground for the *gopas* and the battlefield upon which Krsna showed His heroism in killing the demon Vyoma.

A smooth, slippery depression in the stone called Picchalinl-sila extends down the slope of the hill, forming a perfect slide upon which the cowherd boys repeatedly ride.

Again and again, in great happiness Krsna, Balarama, and the *gopas* slide down this stone, then scamper up the hill only to slide down again, filling the forest below with the sounds of freewheeling joy.

When I visited Kamyavana I too slid down Picchalini-sila, both to share in the local children’s fun and as an act of worship by which I could remember Krsna’s and Balarama’s childhood pastimes here.

In so doing I made two observations. The first is that, like other things in Kali-yuga, Indrasena-parvata must be smaller now than it was in Krsna’s time, when Picchalini-sila must have been steeper.

Second, unless the *gopas* poured water down Picchalini-sila to polish the slide’s surface, the contact between stone and cloth was rough enough to suggest that the boys must have wore holes in their *dhotis.*

And while such wear and tear would be perfectly understandable to the *gopas’* mothers, I wondered how the *gopis* — who also played here when the boys were elsewhere — explained the holes in their skirts?

A little further along the hilltop is the Bhajana-sila. This stone, when tapped, can make musical sounds like a drum. The *gopas* take great pleasure in playing it to accompany their dancing.

Stimulated by the breathtaking view of Kamyavana from the hilltop, the boys would shout to hear their voices echo in the valley below and also beat the Bhajana-sila to hear its sound echo in the forest.

Although these kinds of games resemble those of ordinary children eager to enjoy themselves, they are actually selfless acts of love meant to please Krsna by playing in intimate friendship.

The endless varieties of sports enjoyed amongst boys is part of the spectrum of loving exchange Krsna eternally relishes, and the *gopas* are the perfect instruments in fulfilling such desires.

The *gopas* have no desire, or even ability, to enjoy playing without Krsna. What sustains them are not the acts of sliding, wrestling, or throwing balls but their success in bringing Krsna happiness.

When Krsna smiles at His friends they think their lives completely successful. The risks that being Krsna’s friend entail, like the constant threat of death by demons, are as insignificant as straw.

At the end and near the top of the hill is a cave where the demon Vyomasura, disguised as a *gopa,* entered Krsna’s play in the valley below to deviously kidnap and hide the cowherd boys, one by one.

The place *(sthana)* where Krsna and the cowherd boys were play- ing (*khela*) at the foot of Indrasena-parvata when they were stolen *(caurya)* by Vyomasura is called Caurya-khela-sthana.

As their cows grazed along the slopes of the hill, the *gopas* played a game of ‘stealing and hiding’ *(nilayana),* in which some boys took the roles of herdsmen, others sheep, and still others animal thieves.

The rules of the game were that the “thieves” stole the *gopas* who were sheep. When the herd disappeared, the “herdsmen” joined together to defeat the thieves and reclaim the abducted sheep.

Vyoma was the last demon to be sent by Kamsa. He appeared amongst the boys in the guise of a *gopa* and played the role of a thief, stealing most of the boys pretending to be sheep and hiding them in the cave.

Finally, only five “sheep” remained. This seemed natural to the *gopa* herdsmen, but to Krsna and Balarama, who understood everything, it was obvious that Their friends had actually been kidnapped.

Vyomasura was taking the boys one by one to the cave, sealing it each time with a large boulder. He reasoned that by stealing the cowherd boys Krsna would be disheartened and therefore easier to kill.

But knowing this would be His last day in Vraja, Krsna was already short-tempered. He had already killed Kesi earlier in the day, so He had little time or inclination to humour this demon.

Krsna grabbed Vyoma by the neck, just as the demon was carrying away another victim. Vyoma then resumed his gigantic original form, feared even by demigods, but failed to free himself from the Lord.

Throwing Vyomasura to the ground in retaliation for confining the cowherd boys in the suffocating space of the cave, Krsna strangled the demon, just as a ritualistic priest strangles a sacrificial animal.

As Krsna threw Vyomasura to the ground, the earth shook so much that the cows lost their footing. To steady the earth Balarama pushed down on a large rock, leaving an imprint of His feet there.

The infallible Lord then smashed the boulder blocking the cave’s entrance, led His friends to the safety of the pasture below, and embraced them with love as they embraced Him with love in return.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda explains that Vyoma represents the propensity to speak lies and to steal the property of others, both great obstacles to cultivating pure devotion and developing love for Krsna.

Vyoma was the son of the *brahmana* Maya. Krsna therefore thought it fitting to atone for killing the son of a *brahmana.* At Carana-pahari He performed a sacrifice with Madhumarigala’s guidance.

Who was Vyoma to have attained the good fortune of being liberated directly by the Lord? In his previous life, Vyoma had been Maharaja Bhimaratha, the charitable and devoted king of Varanasi.

When the king’s sons came of age, he retired to the Malaya Hills and performed austerity for one hundred thousand years. Unfortunately, he became extremely proud of his devotional and mystic attainments.

One day, Pulastya Rsi and his disciples visited the king’s asrama. The king, thinking himself equal to the sage in spiritual advancement, neither greeted his honourable guest nor showed him proper respect.

Pulastya Muni noted Bhimaratha’s mentality and said, “O King! Since you do not know right from wrong, and since your devotion is as fickle as the blowing wind, may you be reborn as a demon!”

The curse brought Bhimaratha to his senses. He at once begged for forgiveness and leniency. The sage assured him that even though born as a demon, he would die at the auspicious hands of Sri Krsna.

Indeed, it is said that upon his death, the soul of Vyoma entered into Krsna’s body just as “a lightning flash merges into a dark rain cloud.” In this way, Krsna liberated Vyoma from material existence.

We may wonder how, after renouncing everything and engaging in austerities for such a long time, a devotee like Bhimaratha could have strayed so far from the path of devotion as to ignore a great sage.

As we consider the answer, we may also remember the example of Bharata Maharaja, who, even at an advanced stage of devotion living alone in the forest, became attached to a deer.

Even if one practises devotional service for a very long time, if he does so alone, he is in danger. The safe path is to stay always in the company of devotees, for their guidance keeps us from going astray.

Moreover, there is always a risk that by performing austerities one’s heart will become hard, which is no asset for a devotee who wants to purify and soften his or her heart by attachment to Krsna.

Therefore, taking a lesson from King Bhimaratha’s example we must always stay in the company of devotees, avoid taking up austerities that are too severe, and chant Hare Krsna with a pure heart.

Lord Caitanya taught us that no matter how advanced we are, the company of devotees is compulsory,

*krsna-bhakti-janma-mula haya ‘sadhu-sanga krsna-prema janme, tenho punah mukhya anga*

**“The root cause of devotional service to Lord Krsna is association with advanced devotees. Even when one’s dormant love for Krsna awakens, association with devotees is still most essential.”**

**\* \* \* \***

If one looks out from the top of Indrasena Hill over beautiful Kamyavana, one will see untold gardens, groves, and lakes amongst which are scores of sitting places, such as the Visnu-simhasana.

Just as there are hundreds of lakes — and some say one thousand — in beautiful Kamyavana, so a hundred thrones *(simhasanas*), saturated with *kama,* fill the forest to serve Krsna’s various pastimes.

At the foot of the hill is Bhojana-sthali, also called Bhojana-thali. At this place *(sthali),* Krsna and the cowherd boys ate *(bhojana)* together. Krsna’s plates *(thali)* left their impressions in the stone.

After killing Vyomasura, Krsna bathed in the nearby lake called Ksira-sagara to purify Himself. At the request of His famished friends, He then sat with Balarama to share a meal at Bhojana-thali.

In the heat and confusion of being kidnapped, the *gopas’* lunch packs were either lost or spoiled. Hungry from their ordeal, the cowherd boys sat empty-handed and downhearted.

For Krsna, who was well aware that His uncle Akrura was already approaching Nandagrama, this would be His last meal with the cowherd boys. He wanted to make it a memorable one.

He invoked His potency of maintenance *(posana)* and manifested such a variety and quantity of food, especially milk products, that for a moment His famished friends were speechless.

True to form, the cowherd boys then served themselves huge helpings and joyfully dove into their feast, chatting amongst themselves about Krsna’s latest heroic conquest over the forces of evil.

In His excitement to eat, Lord Balarama spilled His condensed milk; to the amusement of the cowherd boys and the exasperation of the Lord, it flowed down the rock and into the lake known since as Ksira-sagara.

To this day, the Bhojana-thali stone bears the mark of that spilled milk as an indelible white streak, as well as the footprint of Lord Balarama caused by the heat of His anger at having lost His *ksira.*

That stone also bears the impressions of Krsna’s *thali*—a plate surrounded by several bowls. These impressions, left at an earlier time, often served as natural dishes whenever Krsna would eat there.

Hungry pilgrims who visit Bhojana-thali fill the impressions with varieties of sweets, which they offer to Krsna and Balarama, along with an *arati,* and then eat the remnants with delight.

The indentations from which Krsna ate on subsequent occasions must have happened on an earlier visit to Kamyavana because the meal after Vyomasura’s death was Krsna’s last in Vrndavana.

As the *gopas* were making much ado about their feast, joking and feeding one another, on the opposite side of the hill the *gopis* set about attracting Krsna’s attention with a music festival.

In a clearing within a forest called Kalavata, the *gopis* danced and sang with such artistic *(kalavat)* grace that the sound of their voices and ankle bells forcibly drew Krsna away from His friends.

Set amongst flowering trees, Kalavata invites us to do *bhajana.* By performing *nama-sankirtana* there in a humble and devotional state of mind, devotees may gain entrance into the secrets of this place.

Krsna was very pleased to have the *gopis’* company, but with the sun’s descent in the west came the reminder that it was time to return to Nandagrama. With a heavy heart He was forced to leave the girls.

Gathering His cowherd friends and cows together, Sri Krsna set out for Nandagrama on what should have been a joyous occasion full of laughter, singing, the lowing of cows, and the sound of the flute.

The same laughter, singing, and lowing was there, but the vibration of the flute was missing, for Krsna’s heart was so heavy with sorrow that He was unable to lift His flute, not to mention play happy tunes.

The cowherd boys put Krsna’s melancholy down to separation from His favourite *gopi,* but Balarama, knowing the real reason for His and Krsna’s sadness, kept a comforting hand on His brother’s shoulder.

My own visit to Bhojana-thali was a pleasant and memorable experience. The elderly caretakers of the place, a Vraja-vasi couple, greeted us and explained the pastimes in unusually intricate detail.

They described how the maidens of Vrndavana carry water pots, sing Vraja-vasi songs, and dance as they walk. And sometimes they pass by Bhojana-thali to attract Krsna’s attention while He is eating.

Intrigued, I asked the lady if she would show us how the *gopis* sang and danced. Our hosts were well into their seventies, but after some persuasion and with her husband’s permission, she agreed.

While her husband clapped she began to dance with such demure, charming steps, singing a song that often repeated the name Kanai, that we too clapped and imitated her dance moves.

The spontaneous *sankirtana* at Bhojana-thali was unique not only visually but also emotionally. It awakened in us affection for Krsna and His home. To use Srila Prabhupada’s word, it was sublime.

For me that little festival reconfirmed a most significant message: the essence of visiting Krsna’s holy land and of respecting the places of His divine pastimes is to be found in *nama-sankirtana.*

Further along the *parikrama* path is Kama-sarovara, a lake that resembles shimmering liquid gold. Radha and Krsna enjoy untold pastimes in the waters and surrounding gardens of this intimate place.

Kama-sarovara is the foremost amongst the many other lakes in this part of Kamyavana, which also includes Santanu-kunda, where the great king performed austerities before moving to Satoha.

On the bank of Kama-sarovara, the *gopi* Malati once saw Krsna kiss her rival Lalita. Turning away in anger, Malati addressed a nearby bumblebee with words actually meant for Madhusudana (Krsna).

“O debauched bumblebee (*madhusudana*)! How intelligent can you be to ignore this splendid blossoming *malati* creeper of Kamyavana forest in favour of kissing that mediocre *tumbi* flower!”

Raghava Acarya says, “Kamyavana has so many holy places that it is impossible to mention them all.” Nonethless, a small hill in the turn of the *parikrama* path is special enough to deserve a mention.

This hill is actually at the whorl of a lotus-shaped grove that teems with bumming bees. It is the exclusive resting place of Srimati Radharani. Even Lord Krsna needs permission to enter this *kunja.*

Once, as She rested here, the princess of Varsana noticed that the red lac *(alta)* had worn off Her foot soles. She requested Her friends to apply fresh lac, giving this place the namesake Alta-pahari.

As Radha rose to Her feet to leave, She walked over some stones, painting *(citra)* them with different *(vicitra)* foot marks. Since then those rocks have been worshipped as the Citravicitra-sila.

Who worships those rocks, upon which the marks of *alta* are still visible today? Krsna does, along with all living entities convinced that devotion to Srimati Radharani’s feet is the topmost perfection.

Dasa Gosvami says that our guru’s grace awakens the desire to see this *alta,* “O Queen of Vraja! Ever since Rupa-manjari filled my eyes with light I have yearned to see the red lac decorating Your lotus feet.”

Alta-pahari is a short distance from Dharma-kunda, where our *parikrama* of Kamyavana began and is now complete. May every sincere pilgrim complete his or her inner journey to Lord Krsna!

Let us offer our *parikrama* of Kamyavana to Kamyavana itself with the words of Dasa Gosvami,

*yatra kama-sarah saksad gopika-ramanam sarah*  
*radha-madhavayoh prestham tad-vanam kamyakam bhaje*

**“Let me worship the forest of Kamyavana, which is very dear to Radha and Madhava, and which contains the Kama-sarovara lake, where the** ***gopis*** **and Krsna enjoy many transcendental pastimes.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Now let us hear about three spiritually significant places that fall outside the *parikrama-marga* but are very near and closely connected to Kamyavana: Dhulauda-grama, Udha, and Atora.

At Dhulauda the dust (*dhuli*) raised by the combined movements of the cows and *gopas* created a cloud so high as to reach the heavens, where it blinded the demigods and made them call out in alarm.

The *gopas* usually graze the cows in the pastures and woods. In the grassy pastures they do not stir up much dust, and in the forest, whatever dust they do stir up is caught by the forest’s canopy.

But while travelling along roads or in open dusty spaces, the vast herd of cows and host of *gopas* kick up a large cloud of dust, a cloud that signals their location to everyone in Vrndavana.

Dhulauda is an especially arid patch of land suited neither for agriculture nor grazing. As the cows and *gopas* cross through it, Vraja-vasis and demigods alike can see the dust rising.

To reassure the Vraja-vasis, especially His parents, of His well-being, Krsna plays His flute as He passes through places in which dust isn’t raised, simultaneously sending romantic entreaties to the *gopis.*

A *gopi* once voiced how she and her friends yearn for these signs of Krsna: “That is not dust from the cows. It is a stream of smoke coming from hearth fires. That is not the sound of the flute in the forest.

“It is the sound of bamboo in the wind. O passionate one, look! The sun has not yet come to the western horizon. Don’t be so restless. Come, I will paint some colourful designs on your breasts.”

Udha, short for Uddhava, is the tranquil garden where Krsna’s cousin rested on his journey to Nandagrama to deliver to the *gopas* and *gopis* the messages Krsna had entrusted him with.

Udha is between Kaman and Nandagrama, which raises the question of how and why Uddhava drove beyond his destination, especially when Krsna gave Him an almost direct route leading to Nandagrama.

In *Uddhava-sandesa,* Rupa Gosvami details the road map Krsna gave Uddhava to follow from Mathura to Nandagrama, a road the Lord Himself describes as “beautiful, straight, and good.”

The route passed Gokarna, Ambikavana, Akrura-ghata, Bhatrola, Kotika, Chatikara, Nagendra-bhoga, Salmala, Sahara, Rahela, and Pritasara, finally reaching Nandagrama at Vidyutkari.

Sri Rupa does not mention how Uddhava got to Udha. Did he miss the turn at Sahara, continue straight past Varsana to Kaman, and turn back towards Nandagrama? What is sure is that he was at Udha.

Raghava Gosvami told Srinivasa Acarya and Thakura Narottama, “Everyone calls this next village Udha-grama. Uddhava stayed here on his way to the house of Nanda Maharaja.”

Tired from his journey, Uddhava, attracted by the beauty and solitude of this forest garden, rested here and pondered the things Krsna had instructed Him to say to the Vraja-vasis.

Uddhava’s mission was to pacify all the Vraja-vasis, but he did not fail to notice that Krsna, in describing the route, His memories, and in the messages themselves, was thinking mainly of the *gopis.*

Nestled among the roots of a large tree, Uddhava was aware that he was on a new adventure that would inextricably mix his own life with that of the Vraja-vasis, and amongst them, it seemed, the *gopis.*

The third and last place off the *parikrama* path in Kamyavana is Atora, where Krsna and His friends one day became so absorbed in play that they failed to notice that twenty-four hours had passed.

The *gopas* may have failed to notice the passing of time, but their parents certainly did not. When their sons had not returned with their herds at the scheduled time, they climbed to the rooftops.

But as much as they strained to see the familiar dust cloud on the horizon, and as much as they strained to hear Krsna’s flute-song, they could not. And so it was until darkness set in.

By that time Mother Yasoda had panicked, thinking that a demon had kidnapped or devoured her son and His friends. She set off into the darkness without even a torch to light her way.

Nanda Maharaja gently but firmly restrained his wife. He then organised well-armed *gopas* and soldiers to search the forests in two groups, one headed towards Kamyavana, the other towards Varsana.

Atora, however, is in a secluded forest between the roads leading to Kamyavana and Varsana. Neither search party could see or hear the boys, and so they both searched until dawn.

It was only after the sun had risen well into the sky that Nanda Maharaja followed the sounds of shouting boys to Atora, where his son was fully absorbed in playing with His friends.

Flanked by his soldiers, Nanda Maharaja watched Krsna and Balarama’s immersion in play, which had drawn not only Their friends but also the cows into it, like an inescapable whirlpool.

So deep was the divine brothers’ absorption that it took Nandaraja’s firm hands to stop the boys’ from moving. When Krsna and Balarama finally noticed Their father, They acted greatly surprised.

After offering obeisances, Krsna asked, “Father! Why have you come here?” Nanda Maharaja replied, “To bring You back home.” Balarama asked, “But why must we go, father. We just got here.”

Although twenty-four hours had passed, the *gopas* were so absorbed in play and the cows so absorbed in watching them that they all thought only a few moments had passed since they had arrived.

Nanda Maharaja told the boys how long they had been gone, at which they all showed great surprise. Amazed at his sons’ penchant for play, the king took his sons by the hand and brought all the boys home.

Though the Vraja-vasis were all wonderstruck at how playing with Krsna had mesmerised their children, they could not fathom the real truth — much more than twenty-four hours had actually passed.

Just as a night of Brahma had passed as Krsna was absorbed in dancing with the *gopis,* a second night of Brahma had gone by while Krsna was playing at Atora. And Krsna still wasn’t satiated.

In my *parikrama* of New Vraja-dhama, I was still seated at the hilltop near Carana-pahari although my mind had been at the holy places of Kamyavana, relishing the pastimes that are enacted there eternally.

Sri Krsna urges us to bring the mind under control and make it a friend. As a friend, the mind has the ability to meditate and enable us to remain rapt in thoughts of Krsna and to quickly attain Him.

One can see Krsna in one’s mind in proportion to the purity of his or her heart, and Krsna personally manifests Himself there in full when one is established on the transcendental platform.

The more I reflect on Kamyavana, the more my mind is drawn to the Pandavas, who lived there for some time. I am especially drawn to Arjuna, whose surrender has long been a source of inspiration for me.

I like to think of Arjuna dressed in simple white robes without ornaments, treading the paths of Kamyavana with the Gandiva bow in hand and the names of his confidential friend Krsna on his lips.

His shoulders are broad and impressively powerful, his facial features charmingly handsome — framed as they are by thick black curly hair. The perfection of his every limb commands natural respect.

Though he dwarfs others by his majestic stature and spiritual pres-ence, his mysterious moist eyes radiate compassion and his voice intense emotion, both of which stem from pure love for Krsna.

Of all places, the Pandavas chose Kamyavana as a place to spend part of their exile so they could be near the Vraja-vasis, who were not only Krsna’s family but also the Pandavas’ worshipful superiors.

By meeting the Vraja-vasis directly or by meditating upon them, Arjuna and the Pandavas aspired to taste a devotion to Krsna known only to Krsna’s kith and kin, especially the *gopi* followers of Radha.

Amongst the forests of Vraja, the specialty of Kamyavana is that it radiates the *kama* of the *gopis,* which is then easily assimilated by souls as pure-hearted and surrendered as Arjuna.

Even though duty called Arjuna and his brothers to leave Kamyavana, in subtle spiritual forms the Pandavas continue to taste *vraja-bhakti* in this forest, as they do elsewhere in Vraja.

It is said that by mystic power Arjuna and his brothers reside in five trees on the bank of Syama-kunda, continuing their *bhajana* in *samadhi* just as Uddhava does in creepers next to Kusuma-sarovara.

I offer my humble obeisances to Arjuna and His friend Krsna, of whom Sanjaya says, *yatra yogesvarah krsno yatra partho dhanur-dharah* / *tatra srir vijayo bhutir dhruva nitir matir mama:*

**“Wherever there is Krsna, the master of mystics, and wherever there is Arjuna, the supreme archer, there will also certainly be opulence, victory, extraordinary power, and morality. That is my opinion.”**

As I completed my *parikrama* of Kamyavana, I thought of this verse in the following way: “Wherever there is Krsna, the master of all mystics, and wherever there is Arjuna, the supreme archer—

“There will also be Srimati Radharani (sri), victory over the material energy *(vijaya),* the blessings of pure goodness (*bhuti*), and ultimate morality—spontaneous loving service to the Lord (niti).”

## Chapter Six - Terh-kadamba, Yavata, Sanketa, and Bahulavana

Two and a half hours southwest of the parliamentary capital of the land of the Hunas is its spiritual capital, New Vraja-dhama, an effulgent golden lotus within the murky pond of the material world.

At the whorl of that transcendental lotus stands an immaculately dressed cowherd girl, always glancing from the corners of Her eyes at an uncommonly handsome cowherd boy who always holds a flute.

Some people know this divine couple as the supreme powerful and His power, others as the divinities represented by the syllable om, and still others as two adolescent cowherds absorbed in loving pastimes.

Bestowing blessings upon all souls from Their altar, Radha-Syamasundara are that same daughter of Maharaja Vrsabhanu and son of Nanda Maharaja who revelled in Vrndavana five millennia ago.

To entice lost souls back to Godhead, Radha and Krsna display pastimes in which They sometimes wander along riverbanks, sometimes dance in forest clearings, and sometimes speak with birds.

At times They quarrel, at times They sing, and at other times They talk in riddles. But whatever They do, whatever They say, They are always the source of inexplicable tidal waves of bliss.

An untold number of young cowherd girls — *sakhis* and *manjaris* as beautiful, qualified, and witty as their mistress — enhance the bliss of Radha’s and Krsna’s amorous pastimes, endlessly varied as they are.

The nimble lotus feet of Radha’s maidservants are decorated with softly tinkling ankle bells, flashing jewelled toe rings, and splendorous red lac that reflects the divine passion in Their hearts.

Their hair is as soft as foam, as dark as night, and the parts in their hair are marked with vermilion. Garlands of tiny flowers are plaited into their beautiful braids with golden lotuses dangling from the ends.

The innocence in their angelic faces conceals the romantic schemes that loiter in their minds. And their bodies, blossoming with youth, conceal the ecstatic amorous emotions that frolic in their hearts.

The bliss these young girls relish comes not from direct contact with Sri Krsna but from their exclusive devotion to Sri Radha, which transmits to them the ecstasy that Radha and Krsna feel together.

The three lines that decorate the *manjaris’* soft golden necks and midriffs combine with their budding breasts and swelling thighs to proclaim to the world the innocent vanity of youth.

These maidservants are always dressed and ornamented with costly silks and priceless jewellery once worn by Radharani, remnants awarded to them by Radhika Herself for selfless service well done.

On one occasion the *manjaris* performed exceptional service of that sort by dressing Srimati Radharani as the queen of Vrndavana and Her closest girlfriends as the queen’s royal entourage.

This pastime took place in an enchanting forest called Kananbada, where Krsna and His friends disguised themselves so that they could personally witness the workings of Queen Radha’s court.

**\* \* \* \***

Kananbada is a complex of perpetually flowering groves. It has an outer ring of sixteen groves that encompass an inner ring of eight. A towering, majestic flower palace stands at the centre of the complex.

The entire enclosure is encircled by a thick hedge of flowering trees and bushes with only one opening, an archway of multi-coloured creepers; in effect, Kananbada is a flower-for tress.

One day, the *gopis* sought shelter from the midday sun in the groves of Kananbada. As they were resting, a group of Radha’s maidservants, always eager to glorify Her, proposed a plan.

The scheme was first presented to and approved by the older *sakhis,* and then Lalita-devi approached Sri Radha with the idea. Radhika readily accepted it, as She is always ready to please Her friends.

Vrnda-devi and Paurnamasi had set this pastime in motion earlier that morning, so all the things needed to dress Radha were already available and waiting for the *gopis* in just the right places.

The *gopis* adorned Srimati Radharani in a way befitting the reigning monarch of Vrndavana. They dressed each other as the queen’s ministers, attendants, bodyguards, and gatekeepers.

Wearing a flowing cape, golden slippers, and a regal crown, decorated with strands of necklaces and brilliant rings, Radha was coronated within the central grove of Kananbada and presented with a sceptre.

Lalita accepted the post of prime minister, another *gopi* was named minister of finance, and another, minister for agriculture. In this way the *gopis* assumed positions in Queen Radha’s court.

Political appointments, no matter how confidentially allotted, always become known to rival parties. How, then, could Srimati Radharani's coronation and female cabinet be kept a secret?

Through a parrot who had personally observed the proceedings at Kananbada, Krsna and His friends quickly came to know of the new left-wing female government takeover brewing near Kaman.

But Krsna was not concerned with the political influence the *gopi*-nominated queen and Her gopi-staffed administration would have on the residents of Vrndavana or the vested interests of the *gopas.*

He was more interested in observing the internal workings of the monarchy, just as the female attendants of the court were free to do. He was especially interested in seeing the monarch.

But such a study could only be done by female observers, so Krsna convinced a few prominent friends, like Subala and Madhu, to accompany Him disguised as cowherd girls.

When Krsna — dressed in a crimson sari, His hands tinkling with red bangles — smiled shyly at the cowherd boys, they wondered whether it was a disguise or if He had actually transformed Himself into a girl.

Placing this Mohini of Vraja within their midst, the bevy of *gopas* disguised as *gopis* set out for Kananbada, drawing perplexed stares along the way from people, cows, and forest creatures alike.

At the flowery gateway to Kananbada, stick-wielding gatekeepers stopped the procession and asked the boys, “Why have you come to the fortified capital of Vraja’s queen?”

Subala answered in a feminine voice, “We have come to speak with the queen.” Nodding in recognition, the head guard enquired further, “What do you wish to say to Her Majesty?”

Subala had not memorised his lines very well. He stammered, “Errr... we want... ummm... to...” But Madhu saved the day by cutting in: “We want to ask Her Majesty how She is faring.”

The doorkeepers had been distracted by the dark-complexioned girl at the centre of the group, but when they heard this dubious answer, they snapped to attention and blocked the entrance with their sticks.

The head guard replied, “I will consult the prime minister.” She then entered the compound and left the visitors smiling sheepishly at the guards. The *gopas* huddled together for a quick conference.

Madhu whispered, “The sticks those guards carry are huge.” Subala added, “And they are highly protective of their queen.” But Krsna insisted, “Let’s see what the prime minister has to say.”

“If I weren’t wearing this sari,” Sridama scoffed, “I could defeat these guards all at once...” “Friend!” Krsna cut him off. “It would not become you to fight with your sister’s bodyguards.”

While the boys were talking, the head guard informed Lalita that some suspicious-looking cowherd girls were at the gate asking for an audience. “Who (*ke*) are they (*uhara)?”* asked Radha.

The guard looked down and replied in an embarrassed voice, “I didn’t ask their names.” Lalita said firmly, “Ask these vassals to provide not only their names but also their village and their family names.”

The sentry returned to the gate, cleared her voice, and addressed the disguised boys according to the orders she had received: “The prime minister requests that you state your names, village, and families.”

Subala drew the veil over his face, Madhu looked at a bird that was flying by, but Ujjvala spoke up, “In this glorious kingdom, we, like all Vraja-vasis, wish only to be known as servants of the queen.”

These words were pleasant enough; still, the answer made the royal guard even more suspicious. Something wasn’t quite right about this band of unfamiliar *gopis* — if they were, in fact, *gopis* at all.

Sensing that the guards were becoming more and more uneasy, Subala herded the *gopas* down the road as he indignantly called back, “If being a lowly servant is not enough, what can be done?”

As the unusual band of *gopis* disappeared from sight, the queen’s gatekeepers looked at one another incredulously. Their captain again entered the compound to report on her suspicions.

But Sri Radha already had suspicions of Her own; from the moment Krsna had arrived at the gateway of Kananbada, She had felt a surge of loving emotions possible only in proximity to Her beloved.

The head guard’s report about the increasing eccentricities of their visitors only confirmed what Radha suspected: Krsna and His friends were playing some sort of prank, a prank She secretly welcomed.

“If those *gopis* come back,” Queen Radha instructed Her guards, “ask one of them to identify herself. Then bring them all here under a full escort. We will see exactly what it is they want to know.”

**\* \* \* \***

Meanwhile, Krsna and His friends had gathered in a *kunja* just off the main road. A few *gopas* wanted to return to tending cows and playing games, but most were determined to complete their mission.

“Let’s return to that fortress-grove without fear,” Krsna said. “I’ll gain access to the court by giving a false identity. Then we’ll be able to observe how the queen conducts the affairs of state.”

Madhu peered out of the *kunja* at the guards with their sticks and shook his head. Driven by love for Krsna, however, he took a deep breath and said, “Let’s go. My brahminical power will protect us.”

The boys adjusted their *gopi* dresses, touched up one another’s makeup, fixed each other’s hair, and set out once again for Kananbada, their path illuminated by Krsna’s sapphire effulgence.

The *gopi* gatekeepers again greeted their guests, but Krsna spoke up before they could ask Him any questions. He stepped forward and in the sweetest possible voice announced:

“Dear ladies! I am Syama-gopi of Nandagrama and these girls are My friends. We have come to offer our respects to the queen and to witness how She presides over Her subjects. Please take us to Her.”

This visitor’s confident new demeanour pleased the head guard. As Sri Radha had instructed, the guard escorted the guests into the compound and took them to the central *kunja* — the queen’s court.

The boys looked all around them, marvelling at the beauty of Kananbada. Slabs of precious gems paved the ground, and golden seats furnished with soft cushions were placed invitingly here and there.

Lotus swings hung from tree branches, and lapis archways stood along the pathways. Colourful flags strung together in festoons and garlands of aromatic flowers were visible wherever one looked.

Gorgeously dressed fair-faced *gopis* — some sitting, some moving about — performed a variety of services as they sang beautiful songs about Sri Radha and Her love for Syamasundara.

Finally ushered into the innermost *kunja,* the court of Queen Radha, the disguised boys saw that the ground, branches, flowers, leaves, and all the furnishings were bathed in a soft, comforting golden radiance.

Seated gracefully upon a jewelled throne facing the entrance, the source of that radiance, Srimati Radharani, increased the warm golden glow in the *kunja* with Her welcoming smile.

The boys were speechless to behold the splendour, majesty, and opulence of the court, qualities surpassed only by the regal bearing and extraordinary beauty of the queen. They all bowed to Her.

Krsna stepped forward and again offered His respects. He spoke in a voice made unsteady by awe, “Our respects to Srimati Radharani, the princess of Varsana and the queen of Vrndavana.

“We, Your servants, have come to enquire about Your welfare and to request a seat amongst Your attendants, so that we may observe how this court grants audience to its subjects and visitors.”

Prime minister Lalita leaned over and whispered something in the queen’s ear. “By the grace of the Supreme Lord,” Radha said, “this court — and I, its monarch — enjoy health, prosperity, and peace.

“We thank You for Your kind wishes. Until such times as confidential matters come before the court, You are welcome to sit amongst the peers and observe the general matters of state.”

Krsna bowed again, then He and His friends were escorted to a stall of seats. The *gopis* couldn’t help but laugh at their new guests. At close quarters it became clear that these “gopis” were actually *gopas.*

The affairs of court proceeded. Sri Radha welcomed guests, like Syamala-sakhl, conversed with ministers, like Visakha, and gave guidance on events, such as arrangements for an upcoming festival.

Meanwhile Krsna, riveted to His seat, basked in Queen Radha’s radiant presence, organisational skills, absolute wisdom, fair judgement, all-pervasive kindness, and affectionate dealings.

She was truly the undisputed monarch of Vrndavana; indeed, since Krsna could not wholeheartedly give Himself to anyone else, She was also the undisputed monarch of His heart.

Finally, just as Madhu let Krsna know that he was weak from hunger, Radha turned to Her guests and said, “O Syama-gopi! Now that You have seen the affairs of state, do You have any other requests?”

Palms joined, eyes brimming with tears, Krsna rose to His feet and said, “O Queen! I see that Your dominion over Your subjects is com- plete — You reign over their hearts as well as their bodies.

“Please know that Your perfect dealings have purchased Me forever. Now that I belong to You, heart and soul, I request that I too may reside at Your lotus feet like Your other maidservants.”

Radhika was silent. She extended Her right arm dramatically and waited for a moment. Suddenly, Krsna’s own parrot appeared, landed on Radha’s finger, and pressed its beak, like a kiss, against Her hand.

“To manage the affairs of state,” Lalita declared, “a monarch must have spies to inform Her of the activities of the citizens, as well as the movements of bordering kings — especially ill-motivated ones.”

Through subterfuge the parrot had betrayed its master and disclosed the boys’ identity. Madhu tugged on Krsna’s arm to signal that it was time they left, but Krsna restrained him. He was memerised.

Glancing at the parrot, Lalita continued, “Our spy has informed us that you are not *gopis,* but *gopas* in disguise.” Pointing to Madhu she added, “This is not a pregnant girl but a greedy *brahmana*.”

This was news to Radha’s subjects, so naturally there was outrage in the court. Attendants and peers grumbled, guards rattled their sticks, and ministers gathered together to consult amongst themselves.

Madhumangala looked at the angry cowherd girls and whispered to Subala, “Your adventurous friend has gotten us into this predicament. We will be lucky if we escape with our lives.”

As more and more gopis crowded into the court, the boys huddled protectively around Krsna, who was still spellbound by Queen Radha’s beauty, composure, and regal bearing.

Sri Radha gracefully raised Her left hand to silence the court. Then She spoke: “Although these guests have entered this court under false pretenses, they have not been privy to state secrets.

“Furthermore, since Syama-gopi is actually a prince in a friendly province, He and His entourage should be given a reception befitting royalty, regardless of how they managed to enter our midst.

“We should offer a seat of honour to prince Govinda and a feast to His intimate friends; in this way, we will have properly greeted our guests and significantly strengthened our political alliances.”

The boys were delighted to hear Radha’s proclamation. Leaving Krsna alone with the queen, they happily went with the minister of foreign affairs to a kunja adjoining the kitchen.

Krsna’s friends quickly dropped their disguises, washed their feet, hands, and mouths, and sat down at small tables. Campakalata and her friends served the gopas a feast, and then the boys rested.

Meanwhile, Krsna had also abandoned His gopi identity. The same maidservants who had dressed Radha dressed Him in a way just befitting the prince of Vrndavana — royal crown and all.

Krsna then sat beside Srimati Radharani to receive the formal worship of Her court. And finally, the monarchs of divine love retired to a solitary kunja while the gopis sang and the gopas slept.

In this way the capital of Kananbada abounded with the bliss of happy gopas, the sankirtana of festive gopis, and the loving exchanges of the divine couple. Relishing these scenes, I continued my parikrama.

Next I wanted to visit Yavata, the village Radha moved to after Her marriage. I decided to go there through Terh-kadamba, where Krsna counts His cows and where He first meets Radha in the morning.

**\* \* \* \***

The rays of the sun warmed my back as I happily walked through the waist-high grass in Krsna Valley, deeply inhaling aroma of the earth, one the distinctive hallmarks of New Vraja-dhama.

On my way west from Kananbada, I passed Bhajera, the birthplace of the famous sisters Rangadevi and Sudevi, sisters that are certainly within the circle of Krsna’s most intimate gopi friends.

Rangadevi is seven days younger than Srimati Radharani. She possesses a very sweet temperament, her complexion is the colour of a lotus filament, and her garments are the colour of a red rose.

Sudevi, like her sister, is very charming. And because her form, her way of dressing, and her personal qualities are so similar to those of Rangadevi, the two sisters are often mistaken for each other.

On my left I passed Kadamba-khandi, a tranquil kadamba forest where Krsna and the gopis come to dance and sing, as they did after Krsna entered Kananbada dressed as a gopi.

Near the dancing arena in Kadamba-khandi is a lake named Ratna-kunda because its waters abound with the various jewels of ecstatic love, jewels that are seen and touched by true saints.

A secluded grove called Nadola also graces Kadamba-khandi. In this grove the gopis arrange a flowerbed and put the divine couple to rest on it when They feel tired from Their festive sports.

Leaving the Lord’s pastime forest behind, I came to Dana, a grove of trees where peacocks like to gather and watch all-attractive Sri Krsna as He wanders over Vrndavana’s verdant pastures.

Krsna once hid from His friends during their play. Several different groups of boys looked everywhere for Him. One group finally arrived at Dana, where they found a peacock feather on the ground.

One boy held up the feather and proclaimed, “Krsna must have danced here with the peacocks. In His exuberance, His own feather must have fallen from His turban. We are on the right track!”

Indeed, they were on the right track. As the boys hurried north, they closed in on Krsna, who, having nowhere to hide in the open meadow, resorted to a bit of transcendental camouflage.

Beneath a solitary tree halfway between Dana and the parikrama path, Krsna manifested His form as four-armed Lord Narayana, replete with divine symbols in His hands, Kaustubha gem, and crown.

The boys spotted Krsna’s blackish effulgence from a distance and raced to be the first to touch Him. But when they arrived at the tree they stopped short, seeing before them the master of all creation.

The cowherd boys were struck with awe to see the towering form of four-armed Lord Narayana leaning casually upon His club and smiling at them fondly like a benevolent father.

Spontaneously the boys bowed before Lord Narayana. Then, still kneeling, full of wonder, palms joined in respect, the cowherd boys huddled together, not knowing what else to do.

Touched by His own love for His bewildered friends, Krsna could not restrain Himself. He quickly resumed His original form as a cowherd boy, called out, “Surprise!” and ran towards the grazing cows.

Brought to life again by Krsna’s clever prank, the boys jumped to their feet and ran excitedly after Him, calling out, “I knew it was Gopala!” and, “I will be the first one to catch Him!”

I worship the paths that lead to Hara-Narayana, where Krsna appeared as the Lord of Vaikuntha, His right foot before His left, a bamboo flute tucked under His belt, a turban hidden in a tree branch.

I pray to the Lord, “May the husband of Laksmi bless me with unflinching attachment to that playful youth whose heart overflows with love for His friends and who always tends cows in this land.”

**\* \* \* \***

As we tread between Dana and Terh-kadamba, or any distance between sacred places, while on parikrama we should absorb our minds in thoughts of the sacred pastimes enacted in the dhama.

At the heart of that kadamba forest is a crystalline lake where Krsna waters His surabhi cows. And at the edge of the forest is a large, bent (terh) kadamba tree upon which Krsna sometimes sits.

Did the tree bend from holding the weight of the universal Lord, the essence of concentrated bliss, or from witnessing His pastimes, like counting cows and teasing Radha? Only by revelation can we know.

Krsna’s ecstatic touch, His flute-song, and the narrations of His glories can melt ordinary stone and wood, what then to speak of the effects they have on sages who have taken the form of trees in Vraja.

After playing all day, Hari and His friends, colourfully decorated with forest flowers and mineral pigments, take their cows, calves, and buffaloes on the winding paths leading back to Nandagrama.

When they arrive at the forest near Nandagrama, Krsna plays various tunes on His Murali flute to stop the cows in their tracks, divide them into groups, and entice them to drink from clear lakes.

He then climbs onto a branch of this bent kadamba tree. From that elevated vantage point He carefully surveys His vast herd and counts every one of His cows, as well the cows of His friends.
