If any one of His innumerable cows go missing, Krsna calls them with a flute-song. As soon as the strays hear their names being called by the flute, they stop grazing and quickly rejoin their own groups.

Once satisfied that all the cows are accounted for, Krsna happily climbs down from the tree, drives the cows before Him, and along with His cheering friends makes a grand entrance into Nandagrama.

Another pastime that occurs regularly at Terh-kadamba is Radha’s and Krsna’s first meeting of the day, which takes place as Radha and Her friends walk from Yavata to Nandagrama to cook Krsna’s breakfast.

Just after rising but before bathing, Krsna and His friends go to the barns to milk the cows. They then bring some calves and cows to Terh-kadamba and while the animals graze, the boys wrestle.

As the gopas frolic in the early morning sunshine, the gopis intentionally pass by, just close enough to enable the divine couple to see each other and exchange knowing glances.

One summer morning the gopas brought their cows to Terh-kadamba. Krsna soon began the morning milking by the side of the road, where He could easily see Radha and She could easily see Him.

As the gopis approached, they could tell that Krsna was poised for some sort of mischief, but Radhika feigned indifference and proceeded on course, unwilling to allow Hari to waylay Her.

Krsna also feigned indifference. He sat with His back to the road and pretended to be absorbed in milking, supposedly unaware of the approaching bevy of increasingly exuberant gopis.

But just as Srimati Radharani was watching Krsna’s every move through an opening in Her veil, Sri Krsna was watching Radha’s approach through the corner of His right eye.

As Radha’s entourage neared, the jingling of the gopis’ ankle bells and the squish, squish of Krsna’s milking eclipsed the other sounds of Vraja — the wind in the trees, the cooing of doves, the lowing of cows.

The tension between Krsna and the gopis was palpable, even though both parties were trying to act naturally. Gripped by anticipation, all things were silent and still, waiting to see what would happen next.

When the gopis finally came abreast of Krsna — even though Radha was at the centre of the group and covered from head to toe — Hari sprayed milk from the cow’s teat straight into Radha’s face.

Srimati Radharani shrieked in surprise, the other gopis squealed in delight! Meanwhile, Krsna continued to work the teats of the wide-eyed cow, hosing down Sri Radha and Her friends with milk.

In their haste to avoid being showered with milk, the gopis scattered, leaving Radharani unprotected. Krsna seized the moment—He leapt to His feet, startling the cow and making it bolt forward.

For a moment the cow obscured Radha from the gopis’ view, so Krsna jumped to Her side and planted a kiss on Her cheek, a liberty only the approving parrots in the overhead branches could see.

The cow finally made its way through the confused mess of gopis, allowing Lalita to see that Radha was under Krsna’s control. Running to her friend’s rescue, Lalita yelled, “Let go of Her, You scoundrel!”

Although Krsna is feared by fear personified, He fears Lalita-devi’s anger. Boldly planting a final kiss on Radha s flushed cheek, Krsna ran to where the rowdy gopas were cheering Him on.

All glories, all glories to Terh-kadamba, where Krsna played the flute, counted cows, and teased the gopis. Hearing these pastimes cleanses dirty things from the heart and bestows transcendental love.

Seeing Krsna’s pastime places and hearing about His transcendental sports both nourish devotion. How this takes place is described in the appendix entitled “Parikrama as an Uddipana for Pure Devotion”.

Rupa Gosvami selected Terh-kadamba as his place of bhajana, confident that its peaceful sanctified atmosphere would inspire him to remember and record the truths of Krsna’s pastimes.

In this secluded place, undisturbed by anyone, Rupa Gosvami sat beneath a kadamba tree writing books on the science of devotional service and the love shared by the divine couple.

As he wrote of Radhika’s deep sorrow at being separated from Krsna, the kadamba tree would shed its leaves and flowers, just as a person sheds tears of sorrow in times of extreme lamentation.

As he wrote of Srimati Radharani’s happiness at again being united with Krsna, the tree would sprout new leaves and flowers, just as a person’s face lights up in times of great happiness.

In the company of Sri Rupa, an eternal companion of Krsna, the kadamba tree tasted moods of transcendental loving exchange unknown to great yogis, perfected demigods, even Lord Brahma.

One year during Rupa’s stay at Terh-kadamba, Sanatana Gosvami’s appearance day arrived. In his seclusion, without amenities, Sri Rupa was at a loss how to celebrate his guru’s Vyasa-puja.

So distressed was the Gosvami at being unable to properly worship his spiritual master that he could no longer write. He simply sat there, moistening his manuscripts with tears.

Srimati Radharani’s heart melted when She saw Her dear devotee in such distress. Without a second thought She appeared to him in the guise of a local girl carrying ingredients for a feast.

“Baba!” Radhika said. “The elders of our village are worried about you because you live here only on fruits and roots. They sent Me with these things so that you could eat cooked food and stay healthy.”

Overjoyed at this turn of events, Rupa Gosvami lost himself in cook- ing for his spiritual master, hardly noticing the unusual beauty of his visitor or the hypnotic effect of Her voice.

Sanatana Gosvami soon arrived from Pavana-sarovara for his daily visit to his brother, and Sri Rupa ceremoniously received and worshipped his elder brother, then fed him the freshly cooked feast.

Upon tasting Rupa’s cooking, Sanatana became so overwhelmed with spiritual bliss that he questioned his brother how, in that secluded place, he had gotten the ingredients for such a nectarean feast.

Sanatana Gosvami thought, “I have only once before tasted food that causes all eight ecstatic symptoms to appear in my body at one time, and that was the milk brought to me by Gopala.”

“Tell me!” Sanatana said, “Where did the ingredients for this feast come from?” Sanatana’s question suddenly made Rupa thoughtful. “A girl from the neighbouring village brought them to me,” he replied.

Sanatana Gosvami, who knew all the local residents, continued to question Rupa about the girl's clothes and appearance. Shaking his head, he finally said, “I know of no family who has such a daughter.”

Looking at Rupa with tears in his eyes, Sanatana concluded, “If She wasn't from this village, that girl you spoke to must have been from Varsana. She must have been the daughter of King Vrsabhanu.”

Sanatana gently reproached his brother Rupa, “Day and night we are trying to serve Srimati Radharani and unite Her with Krsna. But now you have made Her leave Him to serve us? This is not good.”

Although both brothers felt remorse for having been served by Sri Radha, their remorse soon gave way to ecstasy. Embracing each other, they laughed and wept and called out, “O Radha! O my queen!”

O gentle Radha, queen of Vrndavana, eternal object of Sri Krsna’s affection, I do not think it amazing that You appear to Your eternal servants, for you are tied to one another with the bonds of love.

But nothing in the three worlds save and except Your causeless mercy can explain why You sometimes appear in the minds of conditioned souls, like me, who have not even the slightest trace of love for you.

Some years back, early one spring morning on a trip to Bhauma Vrndavana, I visited Terh-kadamba. Tired after the long walk from Yavata, I fell asleep on a raised platform at the base of a kadamba tree.

In my sleep I saw an effulgent girl carrying a basket with vegetables, ghee, spices, and other ingredients. She was coming from the direction of Yavata, and as She passed I observed Her closely.

I recognised Her! It was Srimati Radharani in the dress of a village girl. Eager to return the love of Rupa and Sanatana, She had come to contribute to the worship of Sri Guru, Her personal emissary.

Sri Radha! Your affection towards maidservants like Rupa and Lavanga is so sweet that both Krsna and Balarama hanker for its taste. I hope that one day I too may know that sublime nectar.

**\* \* \* \***

I sat on a tree stump and chanted Krsna’s names, absorbed in remembering Terh-kadamba, until my attention was diverted by a group of guests in the distance touring Krsna Valley.

I waved to the fortunate visitors then headed in the direction of Yavata and the next lila-sthana, a forest in which Lord Siva resides as Asesvara, the master (Isvara) of the tranquil lake called Asa-kunda.

He is also known as Asiresvara, because he is the great master who bestows blessings (asis) upon those sincere souls who worship him, especially those who desire the highest blessing — pure devotion.

While performing austerity on the banks of Pavana-sarovara to obtain godly sons, Maharaja Parjanya regularly came to this forest, bathed in the lake, and prayed to Asesvara Mahadeva for his grace.

Asesvara Mahadeva is the controller of the three worlds. Covered in ashes, garlanded with skulls, ornamented with snakes, matted locks in a bunch atop his head, he holds a trident and dresses in tiger skin.

During my parikrama of Nandagrama I mentioned how Mother Yasoda once refused to let Lord Siva have Krsna’s darsana. It was to the banks of Asa-kunda that Lord Siva retired to lament.

Let me describe the pastime in detail: Mahadeva once appeared at the gates of Nandagrama hoping to see Krsna, his worshipable Lord. But Yasoda, beholding Siva’s fearsome appearance, denied his request.

Yasoda begged, “O great sage! Years of severe austerities have given you a frightful appearance. Were my pampered son to see you, He would be so frightened that He would be unable to sleep at night.

“Please return to your abode. You can come back and see Him after He has grown up.” And with that, Mother Yasoda discreetly closed the door on her unwanted guest. At least that was how she saw him.

Completely dejected, Mahadeva’s heart was broken. He had travelled all the way from Mount Kailasa to see Krsna, only to be barred from attaining his goal at the very last step of his journey.

Lord Siva is also celebrated throughout the three worlds as the master of all creatures (Pasupati); still, he knew full well that he had no authority over Krsna’s family members, especially His mother.

Pained by Yasoda’s dismissive words, Mahadeva stumbled down Nandisvara Hill, walking until he came to this forest, where the constant flow of his tears formed a tranquil, flower filled lake.

Day and night Lord Siva cried at having been rejected by the embodiment of transcendental maternal love. “What hope do I have of achieving vraja-bhakti now?” he murmured over and over again.

And while he despaired, all was not well in Nandagrama. Mother Yasoda was tormented by the thought that she had invited bad luck by turning away a saintly guest simply because of his appearance.

All-knowing Krsna was also aggrieved; He sulked, refused His meals, and ignored His playmates. Finally, Nanda Maharaja noticed the gloom that seemed to be consuming his palace. What was wrong?

Upon being asked, Yasoda told him what had happened, to which the alarmed king exclaimed, “Foolish woman! By offending an uninvited guest you have also unwittingly cursed your son and household.

“We must find that yogi at once and pacify him by any means.” Nanda Maharaja took Krsna by the hand and, followed by Yasoda and some senior gopas, set out in search of their offended guest.

The king and his search party combed the forests and gardens surrounding Nandisvara Hill until at last, by a lakeside garden southeast of Terh-kadamba, they found the fierce-looking ascetic.

The Vraja-vasis offered their respects to the yogi from a distance. Still holding the hand of his young son, who hid behind His father in fear, Nandaraja then gingerly approached Mahadeva. Nanda said:

“O great yogi! I have come to know that you were poorly received when you came to call at my home. Please tell me how we householders, whose duty it is to worship guests, can atone for this.”

Mortified that Krsna’s father was surrendering to him, Lord Siva disclosed the purpose of his visit: “I came from the Himalayas only to see your son and taste the remnants of His meals.

“You are the most pious amongst all cowherd kings. You are the father of a son whose blessings are sought by great demigods. Please put my heart at ease by fulfilling my desire to see your son.”

Nanda wasted no time. At once he placed his hands on Krsna’s shoulders and whispered instructions in his son’s ear. Krsna responded by joining His palms and bowing before the yogi.

After Mahadeva had gazed at the Lord of his heart for a long time, Krsna offered him a seat and tried to serve him opulent food brought from Nandagrama. The yogi, however, refused to eat.

With joined palms Nanda again enquired, “O great one! Why won’t you accept food cooked in my kitchen?” Mahadeva replied, “O King! Your cuisine doubtlessly surpasses the delicacies of heaven.

“But I have taken a vow to taste the many delicacies of your divine kitchen only after your son has eaten, and only alone, not with your family members and subjects present as they are now.”

Hearing this, Nanda Maharaja sent his associates back to Nandagrama. Then Lord Siva watched Nanda and Yasoda lovingly feed Krsna, who smiled bashfully at His guest as He ate.

After Krsna had finished eating, Nanda placed his son’s remnants on a large tray and respectfully placed the tray before Mahadeva, who offered prayers to the sublime remnants, his eyes filled with tears.

Nanda, Yasoda, and Krsna bowed all together, and as they turned to leave, Lord Siva said, “O King! So long as I remain in your kingdom, please send me the remnants of Krsna’s meals.”

“O Lord!” Nanda Maharaja answered. “May your mind be peaceful. Rest assured, so long as I am known as Vrndavana’s king, you will always receive a portion of the food my son leaves on His plate.”

From that time until the present day on top of Nandisvara Hill, a brahmana daily takes Krsna’s remnants to Nandisvara Mahadeva, and he accepts the Supreme Lord’s maha-prasada with great bliss.

Knowing that Lord Siva is very easily pleased, I bowed down and prayed that he might bestow upon me the gift of loving devotion, a gift that only great Vaisnavas such as he can give.

**\* \* \* \***

Going south, the old access road passes by Gendokhara-kunda, a lakeside meadow in Vraja where Krsna played ball, not with Balarama and the cowherd boys, but with Sri Radha and Her girlfriends.

Just behind Gendokhara-kunda is Kokilavana, where Krsna is known as Kokila-bihari. He and Radha once wandered there during the rasa dance, and there He inspired the cuckoos to sing in concert.

As spring gives way to summer, balmy nights bring relief from the oppressive heat of day. On one such night, Krsna took the gopis through all twelve forests of Vraja to enjoy rasa dancing.

In Nandisvara the divine couple hid from Sri Radha’s girlfriends in a banyan forest. From there They strolled through Bhadravana, Khadiravana, and Bilvavana, at last reaching Kokilavana.

The gopis followed the divine couple’s distinctive footprints until they finally arrived in Kokilavana, where their voices reached the ears of Radha and Krsna. Krsna then whispered to His beloved:

“O Radha! If Your friends find Us, Our private pastimes together will end. Quickly! Come with Me. There’s a dense forest nearby. The girls won’t find Us there, and We can be alone a little longer.”

Feeling somewhat proud that Her unique beauty, youth, intelligence, and good character were especially appealing to Krsna, Radha complained about the prospect of having to walk any further.

She said, “You herd Your father’s cows every day. Accustomed to wandering from forest to forest, You are very strong. But I am a delicate girl. I don’t have the energy to keep walking. Let’s stay here.”

Krsna fanned Radha with His yellow upper garment and, taking Her hand, He said, “It’s not far. Don’t worry, We won’t walk too fast.” But Radha would not rise; rather, She turned away from Her beloved.

Krsna then said, “If You can’t walk, then climb on My shoulders!” Just as Radhika stood to climb onto His shoulders, Krsna displayed His independent nature and disappeared, leaving His consort all alone.

Radha at once realised that Her pride had driven Krsna away. In distress She fell to the ground and cried pitifully, and the sound of Her weeping drew Her girlfriends to where She lay in disarray.

The depth of Radha’s grief was unfathomable. It drew the already sorrowful gopis into even deeper despair. Falling into one another’s arms, they filled Kokilavana with the sounds of their piteous weeping.

Gradually some gopis calmed themselves and showered Radha with a spray of sandal, aguru, and musk. In time, others followed suit, fanning Her with camaras or comforting Her with sweet words.

Finally Radha regained Her composure and gave the gopis a heart-rending account of how Krsna had deserted Her. Determined to win Him back, they sat together and sang His glories.

They sang words like these: “May Krsna, who enjoys glorious pastimes in the arena of the rasa dance, who brings life to the gopis’ eyes, and who in a secret place treats a girl roughly, appear before our eyes.”

As the gopis sang and sang, they saw His large lotus eyes, glowing at first like lanterns in the darkness. Then, suddenly, Krsna appeared before them completely, in His all-attractive blackish form.

In the same way that the senses become alert in contact with sense objects, the gopis at once stood up to show respect to Krsna, who instantly dissolved their pain with His smile, soothing like moonlight.

Hari then turned His attention to Radha and graciously requested Her to dance; thus, once again the gopis, indeed all of Vrndavana, became joyful with those ecstasies only the rasa dance can bring.

Kokilavana is a favourite haunt for a particular species of cuckoo. Black with brown wings, it is known for its unique call, which sounds like a repeated whistle that escalates in pitch and emotion.

Because Yavata-grama is near the forest of Kokilavana, its residents are accustomed to hearing this cuckoo’s unmistakable call mixed in with the other pastoral sounds of their idyllic cowherd lives.

One day, Sri Krsna, who resembles a black cuckoo and who can also perfectly imitate its call, secretly entered Kokilavana to solicit the help of these birds in freeing Radha from the prison of Her in-laws.

Hari called the birds into His presence and then played musical notes that sounded like the cuckoo’s call. The birds all joined Him in a chorus that spiralled upward and reverberated throughout Vraja.

As Krsna alternated between singing and playing the flute, He also danced, which excited the birds. The intensity of their singing and Krsna’s flute playing drew the residents of Yavata to their rooftops.

Looking out over the treetops, Jatila said to Visakha, “I have never heard cuckoos sing so loudly or so beautifully!” To which Visakha replied, “For some reason the birds of that forest must be rejoicing.”

Lost in the cuckoos’ concert, Jatila nodded, “Beautiful! So beautiful!” Clever Visakha, suspecting that Krsna was somehow behind the cuckoos’ festival, took advantage of Jatila’s wonderment and said:

“O mother! Could we girls go to see what has excited the birds so, and then quickly bring you a report?” Half-blind Jatila replied, “Go ahead dear and see them singing from close up. But come right back.”

Radhika was overjoyed to hear of Visakha’s plan. She dressed in Her finest garments and ornamented Herself with costly jewellery and then hurried to Kokilavana along with Her closest friends.

Although Radha said nothing about Krsna, the heating of Her heart, the steady rhythm of which could be heard above the cuckoos’ chorus, let Her friends know that Krsna was surely on Her mind.

Walking into Kokilavana was for the gopis like standing beneath a thundering waterfall — the overwhelming sound of the cuckoos’ singing completely drowned out all other sounds and sensations.

As they made their way deeper into the forest, the dense thicket of sound confused the gopis’ perception of where they were, so they pressed closer together to reorient themselves by the sense of touch.

At last they came to a palasa tree completely covered with blissfully singing cuckoos. Beneath the tree stood Krsna, His hands cupped to His mouth as He directed the birds in their uproarious serenade.

Seeing that the gopis had arrived, Krsna lowered His hands and stopped singing. The cuckoos, realising that their purpose had been fulfilled, also stopped singing, curious to see what would happen next.

Meanwhile in Yavata, the abrupt end to the cuckoos’ concert had startled Jatila, who exclaimed, “What a shame! The girls must have frightened the birds and made them stop singing.”

But the cuckoos had hardly been frightened. Quite the opposite, they were crowding the branches to witness Radha’s meeting with Kokila- Krsna in the forest that has since become known as Kokilavana.

All glories to the birds of Kokilavana, who joined together in a legendary concert to provide Srimati Radharani with an excuse to follow the call of an elusive and extraordinary black cuckoo.

**\* \* \* \***

My next stop was Yavata, a short distance from Kokilavana. Built by Maharaja Vrsabhanu for the pleasure of his daughter as a residence for Her in-laws, Yavata is in the district of Vidhuhrdya.

Spacious and aesthetically pleasing, with wide alleys and palatial dwellings, Yavata was a wedding gift for Sri Radha. But She was forced to wed someone other than Sri Krsna, so She saw it as a prison.

Every night, seated in the moon-tower of Her palace, Radha gazes towards Nandagrama, where unhappy Krsna is looking towards Yavata, and condemns Providence for allowing Her to marry Abhimanyu.

Krsna, however, is too clever to be deterred. He often meets Radha in Her husband’s village, or even in Her husband’s home. Assisted by Radha’s girlfriends, He boldly enacts pastimes in Yavata.

Srimati Radharani and Her friends, such as Citra, lived in Varsana until they were of marriageable age. After their weddings, however, they all moved to Yavata to their husbands’ homes.

Although the gopis live with their in-laws, Yogamaya deceives Abhimanyu and the other young men of Yavata, who never actually see their wives. The women they see are only shadows of them.

The gopis, who are expansions of Krsna’s own pleasure potency, could never be touched by any man other than Him, just as Sita was always safe from the hands of Ravana even though she was his captive.

Krsna is the true husband of the gopis; their marriage to others is an illusion created by Yogamaya to enrich His love-sports. Yogamaya also makes Him invisible to the Yavata-vasis during His escapades there.

By sowing doubt in the minds of Radha’s in-laws, Yogamaya intensifies the risks Krsna has to take to meet Her. But she also insures that distractions keep those doubts forever unconfirmed.

Dull-witted Abhimanyu counts his wealth in cows, thus he lives mostly in his barns, while distrustful Jatila and Kutila are always preoccupied with their many household chores.

The truths of Krsna’s Yavata pastimes are sublime and concealed from ordinary men. But by good fortune, pure Vaisnavas are able to relish such pastimes by speaking of, hearing about, or seeing them.

The area of Yavata is blessed with many banyan trees (vata). Under one such tree, Krsna painted Radha’s feet with red lac (yava). That tree is called Yavavata, from which the town’s name is derived.

Fifteen sacred kundas mark the major places of Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes in Yavata, although endless landmarks identify other sites at which the divine couple eternally enjoy each other’s company.

Walking to the centre of Yavata, I sat down in front of a friend’s cottage, now known as Kisori-kunja, and recalled some of the pastime places there, beginning with beautiful Kisori-kunda.

On Kisori-kunda’s southern bank is an isolated peninsula with tall grass at its centre and trees along its edge. Here the sounds of Krsna’s and the gopis' nocturnal love-sports echo across the waters.

When Krsna and the gopis hear the echoes of their own revelry, they mistake them for the joyful sounds of the celestials and place their forefingers to their lips. Wide-eyed, they whisper, “Shhh!”

By the end of night, Radha is tired and thirsty, Her clothes and hair in disarray, Her feet and ankles sore. At such times, Krsna lovingly serves Her within a hidden grove enclosed by a cluster of trees.

The divine couple then briefly rest until the cock crows at dawn, at which time They quickly rise and make Their way to Their respective homes, careful not to be seen by the night guards of Yavata.

This hidden (gudhali) garden is the playground of Cupid (Kandarpa), so it is known as Kandarpa-gudhali. It is a fitting name, for both the god of love, Cupid, and the transcendental Cupid, Krsna, sport there.

As with all the pastime places in Yavata, there are many wonderful pastimes associated with Kisori-kunda, one of which is retold in the appendix entitled “Kamsa Becomes a Gopi.”

I was seated in a garden adjacent to Kisori-kunja. In Bhauma Vrndavana this garden is in a very sacred kadamba forest known by the names Pibana-kunda, Piyala-kunda, and Padar-ganga.

Once, as Krsna and the gopis were playing there, in fun Lalita urged Krsna to kiss (pyar) Radha. Hari happily obliged, but when He drank (piba) the nectar of Radha’s lips, He became completely intoxicated.

Radha planted a Parijata tree on the bank of this lake and personally tended it until it blossomed. From these flowers She would make garlands to offer Krsna whenever He visited Yavata.

On another occasion, outside Kaman, the Pandavas were testing their skills when one of Arjuna's arrows strayed to this lake, where it struck ground and created a spring from which flowed the Ganges.

**\* \* \* \***

In the southwestern corner of the town, just beyond the impressive compound of Abhimanyu’s house, is a small but thickly wooded area known to the villagers of Yavata as Vatsa-khora.

On Gopastami day, Jatila once forbade Radha to perform Her customary puja at distant Surya-kunda, concerned that her daughter-in-law was spending too much time outside the home.

Thoroughly convinced that life had no meaning without Krsna, Radhika sat in Her room, soaking the floor with Her tears. Even Her most lighthearted friends were surrounded by clouds of despair.

Krsna had been hiding in Vatsa-khora, anticipating Sri Radha’s appearance at any moment, but the news that His lover was now confined to Her in-laws’ house turned His zeal to sorrow.

Unable to face the day without the sunbeams of Srimati Radharani’s company, Krsna turned to Subala and tearfully said, “O friend! I place My life in your hands. Bring Radha here, one way or another.”

Subala looked to the other boys for help. Receiving none, he replied with deep empathy, “Friend! How can I bring Her here in broad daylight? Wait until nightfall. Then Your desire will be fulfilled.”

Krsna shook His head, “How can I wait? Every moment is like the passing of a millennium. A few hours will certainly see old age bring My life to a bitter end. You must somehow bring Her to Me now.”

Subala understood the gravity of the situation. Signalling to the cowherd boys to stay low, he emerged from his hiding place and walked straight through the front gate of Abhimanyu’s house.

Subala’s family is intimately connected with Abhimanyu’s, so he was able to enter the house unannounced. As fate would have it, he proceeded to Radha’s quarters, also without being seen.

When cheerful Subala, who is full of love for both Radha and Krsna, opened the door, Sri Radha’s cowherd girlfriends thought that the end to their dear one’s misery had appeared before them in person.

The gopis knew Subala to be not only Krsna’s most intimate friend but also a surrendered servant of Srimati Radharani, so they welcomed him into their midst and explained the entire situation.

Lalita said, “Radha is so anxious to meet Krsna that we fear for Her health, indeed for Her life.” “My friend suffers from the same disease,” Subala replied. “And He may meet the same end.”

“What will we do?” Visakha asked. “We have to arrange for Radharani to escape from this dungeon,” Subala smiled, “and into the arms of my friend, who is in hiding hardly a stone’s throw from here.”

The gopis looked at each other and in unison whined, “But how?” Just then, as if in answer, a calf passed by the window, looking for its mother and bleating sadly. “Bring that calf here!” Subala exclaimed.

The gopis gave one another incredulous looks, but Lalita’s autocratic glance was enough to dispatch a few girls, who returned moments later both dragging and pushing the reluctant calf into the room.

Subala, who looks very much like Radharani, then revealed his plan: “I will trade clothes with Radha. Disguised as Subala, whose calf has run astray, Sri Radha will carry this calf out the front gate.”

The gopis were like wilted sunflowers brought back to life with the rising sun of Subala’s clever plan. And Radha was like a golden bee flying from flower to flower, spreading the nectar of that plan.

Subala is a twelve-and-a-half-year-old gopa, whose lustre, complexion, and facial features are male versions of Radha’s. What’s more, his blue clothing, assorted necklaces, and jewel earrings are also like Hers.

In short, Subala looks very much like Sri Radha, and She looks very much like him; still, to reassure Radharani and Her friends that his scheme was foolproof, Subala boldly spoke the following words:

“Friends! I will remain in the house and pretend to be a sick Radha. Meanwhile, She will leave to meet Krsna dressed as me, carrying this calf to conceal the only visible difference between us.”

The gopis laughed and clapped at Subala’s quick thinking. Without further ado Radha entered Her inner chambers, Subala went to another room, and with the gopis’ help they exchanged clothes.

Soon Radha and Subala emerged from their changing rooms, each one looking like a curious facsimile of the other. In perfect imitation of Radha’s voice, Subala said, “Lalita! I have such a headache.”

Radha also tested her new role. In Subala’s voice She said, “Today this rowdy calf has run amok!” Lifting the calf in Her arms, She walked out the door, down the hall, and into the open courtyard.

Jatila and Kutila, completely unaware Subala had ever entered the house, were surprised to see him on his way out, carrying a calf. But before they could speak, Radha said, “This rowdy calf has run amok!

“It strayed from the herd and ran into your house. To be safe I will tie it to its mother so that it can’t run away a second time, but I must hurry so that the herd of Nanda’s son can move on.”

At the mention of Krsna, Jatila’s attention shifted from Subala’s surprising presence to the dangers posed by Krsna’s being near her home and her daughter-in-law. Raising her hand in blessing, she said:

“Quickly, take your calf to its mother and let the herd continue to the pastures. The further a certain black cowherd is from this house, the more secure is the integrity of my family. Now go!”

Radha lowered Her head to hide Her irrepressible smile and answered, "As you order, mother. As you order.” Then She walked out the gate and towards the wood now called Vatsa-khora.

Meanwhile, in Srimati Radharani’s absence, Krsna was struggling to remain patient. He felt every moment to be a lifetime of agonising torture, as if He were sitting in a blazing fire.

As His close friends looked on with concern, Hari paced back and forth, chanted Srimati Radharani's name, looked repeatedly down the path, and wiped tears of longing from His eyes.

Suddenly, Subala reappeared. He held a restless calf to his chest and glowed with an uncharacteristic splendour. But where was Radharani? “Subala failed in his mission,” Krsna thought.

Syamasundara felt so dejected that He could not think straight enough to wonder why Subala appeared so special. Hopeless, He asked, “Where is Radhika? Why did you not bring Her?”

Srimati Radharani decided to test Krsna’s love. Parroting Subala’s voice, She answered, “Friend! I tried My best. But it was not possible to bring Radha here in broad daylight. Impossible!”

“Of what use is My life!” Krsna pleaded. “If I cannot see Radha, if I cannot hear Her voice or enjoy Her company, what will I do with My eyes, or My ears, indeed, with My entire being?”

Radha raised Her eyebrows and in a tone that was both conciliatory and suggestive, She replied, “Radha may not be available. But if You give the order I will bring Candravali to You in no time.”

Krsna did not hesitate to reject His beloved Radha’s suggestion. He shook His head disapprovingly, “No! No! You want to replace heavenly nectar with well-water. I am not interested.”

Faint from the pain of sorrow, His dark complexion devoid of lustre, Krsna concluded that thought in a whisper that eclipsed the last rays of hope, “I want My Radharani and no one else.”

Krsna’s words of desperation made Radhika feel guilty for having teased Him, but overwhelmed with bliss by His fidelity. She dropped the struggling calf and threw Her arms around Krsna’s neck.

Krsna was astonished at Subala’s behaviour. For a moment He could understand neither why His friend was acting the way he was, nor why Subala’s embrace was causing Him such special bliss.

Then, as Krsna looked into His friend’s eyes, He heard a girl’s voice say, “O moon of Vrndavana! Can’t You recognise Your maidservant, even as She holds You in Her arms?”

Radha’s voice ignited Krsna’s senses, just as a lightning bolt ignites a dry forest, and those blazing senses melted His sorrow, just as fire melts butter. Finally He could taste happiness again.

Krsna’s greedy eyes savoured the sight of Radha dressed as a gopa. Subala’s familiar turban, dhoti, wrapper, bhundi, rope, and stick took on special attraction when ornamenting Radha’s exquisite form.

Hari gazed upon Radhika’s pristine face, free of makeup, eyeliner, nose ring, and musk dot. The whirlpool of Her natural beauty drew Him even further into the depths of His attachment to Her.

Within that whirlpool, Radha’s laughing eyes and magnetic smile acted as lures to bring Krsna’s eyes under a hypnotic spell and then immersed His mind in the waters of loving confusion.

Finally, Radha led mystified Krsna to a beautifully prepared kunja. And the cowherd boys, satisfied that Krsna had achieved the happiness for which He had cried, left the area to play elsewhere.

Some hours later, Subala, still in the guise of an ailing Srimati Radharani, accompanied by gopis like Visakha and Rati, left for Vatsa-khora on the pretext of getting fresh air.

At Vatsa-khora, Radha and Subala again exchanged clothes, assuming their original appearances. Radha then reluctantly returned home, seeming much better for Her refreshing walk.

Krsna also reluctantly rejoined His friends and drove the cows back to Nandagrama, eager for nightfall when He could once again meet Radharani, this time in a grove by the Yamuna’s shore.

The nature of ever fresh spiritual love is that it never tires of the bliss it generates; thus, Krsna is never satisfied with revelling in pastimes, and devotees never tire of hearing and chanting of them.

Leaning against Kisori-kunja, I recalled how on Gopastami Sri Radha is dressed as a gopa with a calf in Her arms, and how pleased Syamasundara must be to see Her in such a state.

**\* \* \* \***

I then turned my attention to Abhimanyu’s house, a palatial mansion with a walled courtyard and a multistorey moon-tower (candra-salika), which in spite of its opulence is still a prison to his wife.

Behind the mansion are sundry barns, corrals, and pastures — home to Abhimanyu’s vast herd of cows. He treasures them more than the wife who, even in Her disdain, earned him such opulence.

To the north is Daharavana, to the east a thoroughfare leading through Yavata — a route Radhika avoids in favour of the path that circles the house and meets the back road to Nandagrama.

Yavata’s cowherds and families are all related to angry Abhimanyu, who is a good friend of Govardhana Malla — Candravali's husband, Kamsa’s loyal servant, and one of the strongest men in Vrndavana.

Only the wives of the cowherds — Radha’s childhood friends from Varsana — are close to Radha and favourable to Krsna. Is it any wonder that Radhika is somewhat averse to the other Yavata-vasis?

Every morning Radha walks with Her girlfriends to Nandagrama in order to cook for Krsna. Lowering Her eyes, covering Her face with Her veil, She keeps to the side road and avoids other pedestrians.

In the afternoons, Hari generally leads His cows through Yavata along the main road. Radha watches Him, either from the moon-tower or from the roadside along with the other townspeople.

But if Radha hides, or if Krsna is otherwise unable to see Her, then He herds the cows along the road that passes directly in front of Abhimanyu’s house and joins the main road again further north.

Within the courtyard of the mansion is a pilu tree whose trunk is close to the entrance of the house, but whose branches spread just outside the inner windows of Radharani’s quarters.

One day, suspecting that Her daughter-in-law had been familiar with Krsna, Jatila forbade Srimati Radharani to leave the house. The old lady then personally took up guard duty with a stick.

Undeterred, Krsna hatched a bold plan: He would speak to, and if possible, meet with Radha. That night, under the cover of darkness, He sneaked into the courtyard and quickly climbed up the tree.

Krsna went up the pilu tree to the highest branch that could hold Him. He then began to imitate the singing of a cuckoo in order to attract Srimati Radharani’s attention.

Indeed, Krsna’s sweet and persistent cuckoo-like calls did capture Radha’s attention; unfortunately, they also attracted Jatila’s. The wary old lady came to the foot of the tree brandishing a stick.

Cuckoos usually sing in the morning, midday, or afternoon, or any time in between, but they rarely if ever sing at night. Krsna’s untimely singing had made Jatila justifiably suspicious.

Half blind yet determined, Jatila called up the tree, “Who is there? Who is there?” Radhika, hiding from view, sent hand signals to Krsna as He lay upon a branch, silhouetted by the light from Her room.

Even Krsna’s going silent failed to convince Jatila that whoever was “cuckooing” had left. Waving her stick in the air, she repeatedly circled the tree while calling out to Radharani.

“Daughter! Turn out Your lights and go to sleep. I am keeping a very deep-voiced cuckoo from spoiling Your and our family’s reputation. Tomorrow I will hand Him over to the king’s men.”

Radha did as She was told and turned out the lights. But She stayed by Her window, exchanging gestures and whispers with Krsna while Jatila continued to tap her stick and pace about the courtyard.

Krsna was quite uncomfortable on His perch, increasingly so as the night wore on. But Radha’s presence, although just out of reach, and Her voice, although barely audible, made the discomfort worthwhile.

Finally Jatila sat down to rest and predictably dozed off. Making the most of His good luck Krsna spoke openly to Radha, but as the light of dawn approached He climbed down the tree and ran out the gate.

In the morning, Abhimanyu, after spending the night guarding cows, entered the house and saw his mother fast asleep in the courtyard. Rousing her, he asked “Mother! What are you doing here?”

Still drowsy, Jatila scanned the tree and said, “Someone is in the tree making cuckoo sounds!” Abhimanyu looked but saw nothing: “Mother, no one is up there, not even a cuckoo.”

“I am certain,” said Jatila emphatically, “that a black person climbed the tree to gain access to your new bride. He must have left as I rested.” Abhimanyu was doubtful, but replied: “If you fear such a danger, then from now on I will lock the main gate every night.” Dazed, Jatila nodded in agreement and took one last long look at the tree before going back into the house.

Krsna is usually tired in the morning from having stayed up at night with the cowherd girls, but this morning, having spent the night in a tree, He was more than tired — He was sore.

When Yasoda wakes Krsna, she notices scratches on Krsna made by the gopas and gopis during their play. But this morning, to Yasoda’s horror, the tree branches had scraped His soft body more than usual.

Yet these discomforts couldn’t deter Krsna. That day, all He thought of was meeting Radhika. And that night He again made His way to Abhimanyu’s house, only to find the gate bolted from the inside.

Circling the mansion, Krsna came to a cluster of beri trees on the edge of Daharavana and climbed into the one nearest Srimati Radharani's windows on the exterior wall of the house.

Again Krsna called Radha with His cuckoo-like voice. And again Jatila, suspecting foul play, came to the tree with a stick. Again she fell asleep, and again Krsna whispered to Radha of His unfailing love.

May the fortunate and pure-hearted souls who know what secrets Sri Krsna and Srimati Radharani share from Their respective hiding places on these moonless nights be merciful to us.

**\* \* \* \***

There are many other pastime places in and around Yavata, such as Larili-kunda, where Lalita arranged a meeting between Radha and Krsna after the two nights Krsna spent hiding in trees.

Then there is Mukta-kunda, where the gopis dressed Radha with pearl (mukta) necklaces, bracelets, and a headdress before She went on Her way to meet Krsna and liberated (mukta) Him from loneliness.

To avoid making this chapter too lengthy, I will not mention all of the lila-sthanas in this area, hut let us taste a bit more nectar by hearing of one pastime that ended inside Abhimanyu’s home.

Early one morning in Nandagrama, Krsna, instead of playing with His friends, was watching His beautiful mother, Yasoda, pack a large box full of ornaments, clothing, and cosmetics.

As usual, Krsna was nosy and kept pestering His mother with questions . “Mother! What are you doing?” He asked. Yasoda answered, “I am packing this box full of valuable things.”

Krsna kept on asking, “Why are you packing those thing into the box?” Yasoda again replied, “My dear little boy, this box is a gift-package for someone very, very special and dear to me.”

Krsna was relentless: “What kind of gifts are you packing?” Again Yasoda answered, but by now sounded somewhat impatient: “Why do You need to know? Just go outside and play with Your friends.”

But Krsna persisted, “I really want to know what you are doing. If you don’t tell Me, then I will keep asking you questions. I will never leave this room.” Full of love, Mother Yasoda replied reluctantly:

“All right! If You must know, then listen. Within this box, I am putting pollen of the camphor lotus, red kunkuma powder, sandalwood sticks, and musk, all for making scented ointments.

“And for making uncommon dresses, I am packing waist ornaments made of tiny tinkling bells, rare lapis lazuli gems, extremely valuable cloth, earrings, bangles, emeralds, and pearls.

“Are You satisfied? Now will You go outside and play with Your friends?” “No!” Krsna answered firmly. “I want to know whether this box is for Me or for My brother Balarama.”

Knowing that opposing Krsna would only increase His curiosity and determination, Yasoda relented: “I have already packed two much larger boxes, one for You and one for Your elder brother.”

Krsna raised both hands in wonder, “Who besides Dauji and I could be a dear object of your affection? Who else could be so special?” Mother Yasoda stopped packing and closed her eyes as she said:

“My beloved son! As a result of my past pious deeds and many austerities, Providence has bestowed upon me a son like You and a girl who is like a powerful tonic that sustains my life.

“This girl — the soothing camphor for my burning eyes — is the shelter of extraordinary feminine qualities, such as good behaviour, beauty, simplicity, and humility. No one in Vraja compares to Her.”

Yasoda went on, “Usually, girls who take shelter of these sublime feminine qualities become great. They take their place in history alongside other chaste women, such as Sita, Mandodari, and Kunti.

“But this girl is so extraordinary that womanly qualities take shelter of Her. And by doing so, these exalted qualities themselves become more glorious and more influential! Isn’t that amazing?

“And before You ask me the name of this girl for whom I have such natural love and affection, I will tell You, it is Sri Radha. And Her husband Abhimanyu is presently meeting with Your father nearby.”

When Krsna heard Radha’s name and qualities detailed by His own mother, His bodily hair stood on end in ecstatic delight, a symptom He at once hid by covering Himself with His upper cloth.

Filled with intense enthusiasm, Krsna again questioned Mother Yasoda. “Who is this girl? Whose daughter is She? Where does She live? How is it that you have such intense love for Her?”

Blissfully submerged in answering her son’s questions, Yasoda was interrupted by a gopi who told her that some jewellers were waiting outside to consult about a crown she had ordered for Krsna.

Before attending to the jewellers, Yasoda told Dhanistha, “Keep an eye on this box. Abhimanyu will come here and carry it to Radha as soon as he is finished speaking with our king.”

After His mother had gone, Krsna pondered her last words and devised a plan. Removing the contents of the box and entrusting them to Dhanistha, He climbed into the box and closed the lid.

Sometime later Mother Yasoda re-entered the house with Abhimanyu. After telling him whom the box was for and what it contained, she gave him the following message for Radharani:

“O fame of Kirtida! I have sent you a box full of wonders with which to daily ornament and dress Your enchanting body. May You be forever radiant, fortunate, and happy with these gifts.”

Thanking Mother Yasoda a hundred times and bidding her farewell, Abhimanyu placed the heavy box on his head with some difficulty, and set out on the long road back to Yavata.

Jatila’s son was beside himself with joy. But unknown to him, the extent of his joy was not from acquiring wealth. It was mostly due to being in direct contact with the form of concentrated bliss, Sri Krsna.

As Abhimanyu carried the box on his head, his body was studded with goose bumps, his eyes moist with tears, and his mind fixed on counting the cows his newfound wealth would purchase.

Hari could barely control His mischievous laughter as He relaxed in the padded box ceremoniously carried by Abhimanyu, the deliverer of Krsna to a rendezvous with Srimati Radharani.

Abhimanyu finally entered his home and called his mother, Jatila. He described the gifts Mother Yasoda had packed in the box. Breathless from the labour of his long trek, he declared:

“Mother! I must have left the house at an auspicious moment today, for I have been blessed with this box of untold wealth.” Jatila thought, “Now perhaps my daughter-in-law will be pleased with my son.”

Out loud Jatila said, “Son! Your beautiful young wife now sulks with Her friends in Her quarters. Take this heavy box up to Her. And may its contents make Her joyful once again.”

Abhimanyu blissfully carried the box upstairs, where he found that Srimati Radharani was indeed sulking. Why? Jatila had just ordered Her to stay in Her rooms for three days.

As soon as Abhimanyu opened the door — the chest tottering on his head — all the cowherd girls felt an upsurge of bliss. And Radha’s left side began to twitch uncontrollably.

The effect of the box’s presence on the gopis was like that of sunlight streaming into a dark room just as the shutters are opened, or like that of a tidal wave sweeping across a peaceful oceanfront.

Turning to Lalita, Radharani said, “Friend! My mother-in-law’s house is a place of never-ending misery. The goddess of auspiciousness never even glances at it. Why, then, does My left side tremble so?”

Lalita-devi replied, “Since I also feel bliss by seeing this box, I can only conclude that it contains some mind-enchanting ornaments that will bring You unparalleled good fortune.”

Radha added, “Just seeing this box brings Me great ecstasy. I cannot imagine what happiness I will enjoy when I open it and examine, not to mention wear, the jewelled ornaments inside.”

As Radha and Lalita were talking, Abhimanyu placed the box on a raised platform beside Radha’s bed. He repeated Yasoda’s message of love to his wife then left, closing the door behind him

**\* \* \* \***

All the cowherd girls, consumed by curiosity, chattered to each other as they eagerly surrounded the box and urged Radharani to put everything else aside and quickly open the lid.

Radha approached and placed Her hand on the box. A current of bliss instantly flowed through Her being as if She had been struck by lightning from a cloud of amorous desire.

Radha quickly drew back Her hand, but the current of ecstasy still coursed through Her body, awakening Her divine senses just as the rising sun awakens songbirds in the morning.

Lalita was suspicious. What kind of mystical thing was this? But as the other girls pressed in closer, clamouring to see what was inside, urging Radha to open the box, Radha threw open the latch and lifted the lid.

The instant the box opened, Krsna leapt to His feet, hands on His hips, eyebrows dancing. Pushed back like wheat bending in the wind, the gopis cried out: “Aha!” “What’s this?” “Oh my goodness!”

Extremely pleased with Himself, Krsna quickly kissed all the gopis surrounding Him and took full advantage of the rare opportunity of being in Radha’s private quarters to have a good look around.

While Krsna — the king of pranksters — stood within the box flouting His majesty, the alligator of enticement — His servant — quickly scurried about the room consuming the gopis’ bashfulness.

Some gopis shrieked in delight, others in embarrassment, still others in indignation. Barely containing her laughter, Lalita blurted out, “Friend! Yasoda, her message, and Your husband are all glorious.

“But Yasoda, Jatila, and Your husband have all ordered You to wear the ornament in the box. By honouring their request, Your submissive conduct will increase their glory all the more.”

Lalita’s comment made Radha blush, but elicited a round of cheers from the gopis and Krsna. Then, at the next moment, Radharani wagged Her finger at Krsna and replied:

“This thief stole the clothes and ornaments given to Me by Mother Yasoda, but before He could make a getaway, He was trapped in the box and brought here by My naive husband.

“Go right now! Explain this to My mother-in-law and bring her here.” Lalita adopted Radha’s mood and turned on Krsna, who was now standing with His arms crossed over His chest. Lalita said:

“Rascal! After stealing Radha’s gifts, You, who are dedicated to ridding the earth of all chaste women, ride here on Her husband’s head to entice Her in Her own apartment. Have you no shame!

“Now either return the cloth and jewels and other riches You stole from inside the box, or I will bring vicious Jatila here and expose the real truth of her son’s treasure-chest and its treasure.”

While Radha and Lalita were speaking, Krsna had been gingerly tapping His right foot on the box and shaking His head to and fro. When the girls finished, Krsna kicked the box for effect and replied:

“Lalita! Your friend is very clever in serving Her own needs. Now hear Me out. While playing at home I spotted this box and wanted to crawl into it, so I sent the contents here with Dhanistha.

“The box was so nicely padded that I decided to take a nap, and as I was innocently sleeping, Abhimanyu locked the top and forcibly brought Me here to be so absurdly accused by his wife.”

Krsna spoke like a skilled attorney from his box-podium addressing the gopi-jury: “Dear gopis! I place the allegations of King Vrsabhanu’s daughter before you for due consideration and a decision.

“If you find Radha guilty of entrapment, then I will punish Her accordingly; if you find Me guilty of theft and womanising, bind Me with the ropes of your arms and imprison Me here for three nights.”

The gopi-jury huddled together and quickly concluded that a proper decision could not be reached in the presence of the litigants; thus they retired behind the closed doors of another room.

Srimati Radharani and Sri Krsna were then left in the room all alone. They could either wait for the decision of the gopi-jury or reach a settlement that would be agreeable to Them both.

Whatever the final outcome, it would surely enrich the treasure house of Vrndavana’s loving pastimes and increase the transcendental bliss of Radha, Syama, and the gopis.

Seasoned with audacity and risk, this pastime glorifies the supreme prankster, who contrived to meet Radha in the house of Her in-laws by the unwitting collusion of His own mother and Radha’s husband.

I bow to the marital home of Sri Radha. The walls of that house kept Radha from meeting with Krsna, and so She considered it to be a dungeon whose every brick was a source of misery.

To again proclaim to the world, “My devotee never perishes” Krsna appeared in that dungeon and for three consecutive days and nights submerged Radha and Her girlfriends in loving pastimes.

The Supreme Lord is always self-satisfied (atmarama). He fulfils all His own desires (apta-kama). When He engages in such loving pastimes, then, He does so only to please His devotees.

He is the supreme potent being. Everything and everyone is a manifestation of His potencies. His pastimes of love are the natural reciprocation between the two constituents of the Absolute Truth.

Therefore Krsna can never be unlawful, immoral, untruthful, or lusty, for these traits cannot exist in Him. He is the all-perfect, all-just transcendence worshipped by every perfected being.

In Bhauma Vrndavana today, at the site of Abhimanyu’s house, is a temple in which the Deities of Radha-Radhakanta are worshipped. Forms of Jatila, Kutila, and Abhimanyu are kept in a side room.

The original tree Krsna climbed, and from which He tried to catch Sri Radha’s attention with the sounds of a cuckoo, still stands in the temple courtyard, where pilgrims worship it with devotion.

I decided not to follow the regular circuit of the dhama but to detour instead to Sanketa, from where I would follow the Nandagrama-Varsana road until the place where it meets the parikrama path.

As I walked through the field past Abhimanyu’s residence, I marvelled at the serene beauty of Krsna Valley lying open before me like a sacred book, recounting the places of Krsna’s glorious pastimes.

I pray that this book, Nava-vraja-mahima, represents to its readers the sweetness, grace, and purity of Radha’s and Krsna’s transcendental pastimes, just as New Vraja-dhama does to its residents.

**\* \* \* \***

Just before the main road and to my left is a place not many people know. It is named after a gopi who desired to known only as Srimati Radharani’s maidservant, Radha-nagari.

When the divine couple complete Their dawn pastimes and return to Their homes to rest, Radha’s associates also return to their homes to bathe, dress, and go out again to serve their mistress.

These gopis help wake Radha, remove Her nightclothes, dress Her in bathing clothes, massage, bathe, dress, and ornament Her, all the while sweetly chatting about Krsna’s wonderful qualities.

Early one morning, Radha-nagari’s in-laws caught her re-entering the house. Suspicious of their daughter’s whereabouts during the night, they forbade her to leave the house to serve Radha as usual.

Confined to her room, Radha’s friend cried, as did those gopis whose families once forbade them to follow Krsna’s flute-song into Vrndavana forest on the full moon night of autumn.

Radha-nagari’s resolute determination for Srimati Radharani’s service fulfilled her desire, just as those other gopis’ determination to have Krsna’s company had fulfilled theirs. Such is the power of pure desire.

As Radhika made Her way to Nandisvara, She was told of Her friend’s plight. Radha at once detoured to Radha-nagari’s home and gave clever excuses to her in-laws for her late arrival the night before.

Srimati Radharani explained that She had had a fever the previous day, and that Radha-nagari had stayed with Her all night. That was the reason she could only return home in the early morning.

As Radha-nagari’s in-laws saw Radha’s innocent face and listened to Her reasonable words, their hearts melted. They happily ordered their daughter to go with Vraja’s princess to Nandagrama.

From that day on, Radha’s friend and the place of Her friend’s residence have both been called Radha-nagari, a name associated with devotees who are the special objects of Radha’s affection.

To enter the circle of Radha’s maidservants is the rarest of perfections , hut to have Radha personally solicit a devotee’s service — a perfection attained by Radha-nagari—is rarer still.

I continued along the main road towards Nandagrama and eventually reached a dense forest that lies midway between the capitals of Nanda Maharaja and Vrsabhanu Maharaja.

This forest, Sanketavana, grew from the fertile soil of the divine couple’s meetings (sanketa). Visvanatha writes, “I think it \[Sanketa\] is the personification of the divine couple’s love \[sambhoga\].”

The trees, vines, flowers, and creepers growing at Sanketa correspond to the four types of meetings (sambhoga) between Radha and Krsna. The first happens just after Their love has been aroused.

The second happens after Srimati Radharani’s anger with Krsna has been quelled. The third happens after the divine couple have been separated by a short distance for a brief period of time.

And the fourth type of meeting takes place after Lord Krsna has returned from a distant place like Dvaraka, Mathura, or Hastinapura, where He has stayed for an extended period of time.

To help devotees better understand the divine couple’s pastimes in Sanketavana, a more detailed discussion on this topic is found in the appendix entitled “Meeting and Separation in Unwedded Love.”

The name Sanketa also denotes a meeting place set up by mirror signals (sanketa) sent by Radharani and Krsna from Their respective bedrooms in Varsana and Nandagrama.

When we come to Sanketavana it behoves us to drown our pride and false egoism in the ocean of our good fortune at being able to see firsthand the grove where Radha and Krsna secretly meet.

It also fitting for us to roll about in the dust and call out, “O Radha! Controller of my life, where are You? O Krsna! Lifter of Giriraja, where are You? O my master and mistress, where are You?”

Although every meeting of Radha and Syama is as fresh and exciting as if it were the first, the actual first meeting of the divine couple during Their pastimes on earth was unique.

After that first encounter, wherever Krsna was, He would regularly remember it. He would even describe that momentous occasion to confidantes such as Visakha-devi:

“Friend! When I joked with Radha at Our first meeting, She was so confused that She stuttered, creating with Her erratic words a stream of fresh nectar flowing by the shores of My ears.”

Even in Dvaraka, as Krsna was being served by thousands of queens, whenever He would recall the sweetness of His first meeting with Radha, the sorrow of separation would overwhelm Him.

**\* \* \* \***

That firstmeeting took place in the rainy season, when the inclement weather discouraged the Vraja-vasis from moving about, making it easier for Radha and Krsna to journey unnoticed to a meeting place.

In the nights preceding Their rendezvous, the intensity of Sri Radha’s attachment to Krsna would keep Her awake. She would pace up and down in Her quarters, engulfed in the amused gazes of Her girlfriends.

Srimati Radharani waited patiently, knowing that one day the force of Her love would push Her out the door, through the stormy night, and into the kunja where Krsna anxiously waited for Her.

Finally, on a night when the influence of loving passion completely overwhelmed Radha’s senses, mind, and intelligence, She submitted to the will of Her transcendental desire and resolved to meet Hari.

Just as a haunted person acts out of character, the daughter of King Vrsabhanu acted abnormally. Her speech and conduct, possessed by divine love, were unrecognisable even to Her most intimate friends.

Under the spell of one thought, “I will serve Krsna,” blissful Radhika smeared Her body with black musk, put on a blue sari, and quietly crossed the threshold of Her husband’s home.

As a powerful undertow sweeps a helpless swimmer out to sea, the all-consuming desire to please Krsna forcibly carried Radharani into the gathering darkness of a midsummer night.

Radha’s desire to meet Krsna was irrepressible; still, She was so intimidated by having to abandon the conduct of a chaste woman that the thought of turning hack constantly haunted Her.

Taking courage from Her friends, Radha ignored the sounds of the palace guards. As the rain clouds thundered applause at Her courageous, meritorious deed, She boldly entered the forest.

Govinda Dasa Thakura prays, “O Radha! Your glistening necklace, stifled bracelets, and embroidered bodice are all black; the lotus flowers, buzzing bees and all-encompassing night are blacker still.

“As You place one resolute step before the other, Your curly locks sway upon Your forehead as do the black waves upon the ocean of black deeds now lapping at the shore of Your family’s reputation.

“Since the sympathetic sunset has dressed You in a cloak of invisibility, I can only assume that You are able to run in the darkness because the pure love radiating from Your heart lights the way.”

Actually, Sri Radha could neither see the path nor the way, but love itself, like an internal compass, kept Her on track. And just as She was guided to Their meeting place, so was Sri Krsna.

As Radha moved forward with determination, suddenly She noticed a tamala tree. Deluded by ecstatic love, She mistook the tree to be Krsna with His arms outstretched. Stunned, She couldn’t move.

Lalita-devi patiently caressed Radha’s limbs, but even after Radha could go forward, Her mind remained bewildered. For as long as the tamala tree was visible, She concealed Herself amongst the gopis.

Finally, a message from Krsna arrived. Radha suddenly became contrary and replied, “Tell that king of rascals that I do not trust Him. I will meet Him only in the presence of My friends.”

As Radha and Her small entourage continued on their way, one gopi teased, “Since the sun has already set, I assume You are still heading in a westerly direction to serve the Krsna sun.”

Meanwhile, Syama arrived in Sanketa. After putting the final touches on the kunja, He paced about, unable to sit still because the raging fire of anticipation had burnt His patience to ashes.

Krsna’s mind raced out of control; He repeatedly looked down the path, imagined that Radharani had arrived, and finally, on the brink of fainting, sat down to repeat Her names with love.

As Krsna was chanting, a flash of lightning suddenly lit up the courtyard outside the grove, revealing the caped form of Srimati Radharani, flanked by Her devoted friends.

In the moments of deafening thunder that followed, Radha and Krsna were unable to greet each other with words, and so for those fleeting moments They exchanged timid, though eager, glances.

The goddess of bashfulness (Lajja-devi) then paralysed both hero and heroine with the imminence of the meeting for which They had both planned and prepared for Their entire lives.

Lalita pushed Radha forward, but when Radhika reached the entrance of the kunja Her body shook, Her mind lost all sense of direction, and Her legs went as stiff as stone pillars.

She tried to regain Her confidence by clutching Her friend’s hand, and as hot and cold tears streamed down Her cheeks, Radharani moved forward with faltering steps.

Then, just a few paces away from Krsna, the goddess of contrariness (Mana-devi) halted Sri Radha’s progress and persuaded Her to turn on Her heels and flee. At this, Krsna almost fainted!

Her gopi friends skilfully checked Radha’s escape. By gentle persuasion they urged Her to move closer to Krsna, and the closer She got to Him, the more Her resistance slackened.

Sri Radha inched forward warily — Her eyes fixed upon the effulgence emanating from the toenails of Krsna’s lotus feet — only to be stopped again by the goddess of love (Prema-devi).

Within Her heart Srimati Radharani desired to embrace Krsna, but love, in order to heighten Krsna’s yearning for Radha, forbade Her to move forward and enjoined Her to feign unwillingness.

Lalita-devi and the other gopis, although young, were learned and skilled in the arts of making the heroine (Radha) favourable to the hero (Krsna). Wasn’t that their eternal and constitutional service?

One gopi whispered in Radha’s ear, “Listen, friend! Today we request a favour of You. Give Krsna the pleasure of embracing You once, just once, and we will never again ask anything from You.”

Another gopi said, “O moon-faced one! Just look at this poor boy. He came alone through this dangerous jungle only for You. Be compassionate to Him. Relieve Him of His distress and anxiety.”

When Srimati Radharani heard Her companions’ sweet words, She glanced kindly at Hari. Recognising Her thoughts, some gopis gently nudged Her into the heart of the kunja.

Standing directly before the person whom sacred scriptures describe as the original male, the source of all beauty, and the embodiment of all mellows, Radhika was exceedingly happy.

As the gopis started to leave the kunja, Radha tried to join them, but Krsna reached for Her hand, and by His touch evaporated Her hesitation and fear and further fuelled Her desire to please Him.

**\* \* \* \***

Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu came to Sanketa on His parikrama of Vrndavana. Sitting upon a large rock, the Lord heard the various pastimes Radha and Krsna had enjoyed here.

As He heard of the divine couple’s meeting, Caitanya Mahaprabhu closed His eyes and savoured the emotions Srimati Radharani felt on those nights that Providence fulfilled Her lifelong desire.

Deeply absorbed in the mood of Srimati Radharani, Caitanya Mahaprabhu tasted Syamasundara's qualities through the unique nature and intensity of Radha’s love for Krsna.

Fully realising two opulences — Radha’s emotions and Krsna’s qualities— the Lord tasted the distinctive bliss that Radharani feels when She savours the sweetness of Krsna and Krsna’s love.

In Sanketa, Gopala Bhatta Gosvami lived for many years in an underground chamber next to the deity of Sanketa-devi, where he remained deeply absorbed in remembering the pastimes of this place.

Behind Sanketa-devi’s temple is a rasa-vedi, a platform commemorating Krsna’s rasa-lila here with the gopis. Some distance in front of the temple is the temple of Sanketa-bihari.

Other Gosvamis also visited or stayed at Sanketa to see Sanketa-kunja and other pastime places, such as the rasa-mandala and a nearby tree upon which the gopis swing the divine couple.

These great devotees would bow to this place, where Sri Krsna brought Srimati Radharani to lovingly decorate Her with flowers after They had left the gopis in the midst of a rasa dance.

Here Radha also made curls in Krsna’s black hair and painted pictures on His moonlike face. After decorating each other in diverse ways, the divine couple left for Kokilavana forest.

At Sanketa, Vaisnavas also regularly celebrate a mock wedding of the divine couple, a ceremony arranged by the gopis in their eagerness to heighten the loving exchanges between Radha and Krsna.

One such celebration took place during the time of the Gosvamis, when Syamananda Pandita accompanied his spiritual master, Hrdaya Caitanya, on a tour of Vrndavana’s twelve forests.

While at Sanketa, the devotee audience watched performers enact the rasa dance. While Krsna danced at their centre, some gopis danced around Him, some sang, and yet others played instruments.

Syamananda Pandita was so absorbed in the performance that he fell to the ground, unconscious, and after some time he began to roll in the dust, calling out the names of Radha and Krsna.

Absorbed in the identity of a gopi, Syamananda placed a cloth over his head and danced madly alongside the performers, drenching his clothes and body with the tears that streamed from his eyes.

At the sight of such an exuberant display of gopi-bhava, Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami — who shared the attitude of fraternal love of his guru, Gauridasa Pandita — was transcendentally offended.

Without saying a word, Hrdaya Caitanya abandoned the performance and retired for the night, thinking, “Syamananda is certainly a maidservant of Radha who has no place in my circle of gopas.”

In the morning, Syamananda offered obeisances to his guru, and Hrdaya Caitanya, still angry, burst out, saying, “Since you have discarded the devotional mood you received from me, you should go.”

Syamananda humbly replied, “Gauridasa Pandita is Krsna’s close friend Subala, but he always helps Krsna meet Radharani, and when they do, he engages in services to Them within the kunja.”

“That’s a lie!” roared Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami. “Pandita Thakura never spoke to me of such things. You must behave like a cowherd boy. Don’t even think of accepting the mood of a gopi”

Syamananda Pandita replied quietly, “Please forgive me. I cannot do that.” This answer so enraged Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami that he picked up a stick and struck his disciple repeatedly.

The other devotees appealed to the Gosvami, but Syamananda addressed them, saying, “Please do not try to correct my spiritual master. I am very happy that he has beaten me in this way.

“Today my spiritual master has acknowledged that I am his own property and therefore his merciful chastisement is like cooling sandalwood paste that soothes my body and mind.”

Finally Syamananda turned to Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami and said, “O my dear Prabhu! Please be merciful to me—an ignorant fool who has disobeyed your order. Please forgive me.”

The devotees were awestruck by Syamananda’s conduct. They glorified him throughout the day, even as they bathed, cooked, ate, and visited the groves and gardens around Sanketavana.

When night fell, the pilgrims rested beneath trees, and as Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami slept, Caitanya Mahaprabhu appeared to him in a dream — the Lord’s divine limbs marked with red welts.

Seeing Mahaprabhu’s injured body and blood-stained clothes, Hrdaya Caitanya gasped in horror, “O Lord, what has happened to You? What is the explanation for this terrible sight?”

“This is all your doing,” Caitanya Mahaprabhu replied. “You beat Syamananda, who is like My second self, so naturally all the injuries that you inflicted upon him have also come upon Me.”

Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami fell at the Lord’s lotus feet and sobbed, “I am lost! No one can save me from the hell that awaits me. O Lord! I had no understanding how dear Syamananda is to You.

“I have committed an unforgivable offence to a great soul, and thus I have also offended You. Since there is no forgiveness for such a sin, I shall commit suicide by entering into fire.”

Merciful Mahaprabhu was pleased by Hrdaya Caitanya’s penitent attitude, hut as atonement for his sin the Lord ordered his devotee to sponsor twelve festivals for the pleasure of the Vaisnavas.

At daybreak, Hrdaya Caitanya Gosvami called Syamananda Pandita and the other devotees and publicly narrated every detail of his dream, including the Lord’s order to hold twelve festivals.

The devotees, however, after having witnessed Hrdaya Caitanya’s brutality against a faultless disciple on the previous day, were reluctant to participate in any celebrations he might arrange.

But then Hrdaya Caitanya turned to Syamananda, exclaiming, “I punished you unjustly, but because you did not take offence you have saved your guru from sinful reaction and delivered him from hell.”

Placing Syamananda on his lap, the Gosvami declared, “From this day on, you are not my servant but my very life.” In humility, Syamananda fell at his guru’s feet while the devotees chanted, “Hari!”

Seeing Hrdaya Caitanya’s change of heart, the devotees’ attitude towards him softened and they gave their blessings for the inauguration of the twelve festivals ordained by Lord Gauranga.

The devotees sent Syamananda to Vrndavana, where he solicited Jiva Gosvami’s help in acquiring the ingredients for the festivals, which were later conducted with great pomp and success.

Remembering these events, I smeared the dust of Sanketa over my face and body. I prayed for unflinching faith in my spiritual master, my ultimate goal, faith like that of Syamananda Pandita.

“O meeting place of the divine couple!” I prayed. “In my service of representing my spiritual master, let me never think my students are anything other than the servants of Radha-Syamasundara.”

**\* \* \* \***

Adjoining Sanketavana, in the direction of Varsana, is the sequestered forest of Vihvalavana, in which Krsna was once overwhelmed (vihvala) by His impatience to meet Srimati Radharani.

Once, Krsna was waiting for Radha in Sanketa. The appointed time for Their meeting had long passed, and still Radha was nowhere in sight. Krsna worried, “Perhaps suspicious Jatila has locked Her up.”

Transcendental anxiety beset Krsna’s mind as He paced back and forth in the courtyard of Sanketa-kunja and enumerated to Madhumangala the possible reasons why Radha had not arrived.

Finally, with the hope of meeting up with Radhika, Krsna and Madhumangala set out down the lonely path towards Varsana. After walking some distance, however, She was still nowhere to be found.

Madhumangala placed an arm around Krsna’s shoulder and said, “Friend! Do not worry. My reading of the stars says that a golden campaka garland called Radha will soon hang about Your neck.”

Although Madhu’s reassurance was meant to bring Krsna happiness, the mention of Srimati Radharani's name had the opposite effect — it increased His unhappiness a hundredfold.

The winds of Radha’s absence had already rubbed together the bamboo of Krsna’s ecstasies and ignited the fire of sorrow in His heart, but Madhu’s utterance of “Radha” added fuel to that fire.

The cloud of Yogamaya, seeing that Krsna was being scorched by an inferno, released a torrential downpour that flooded His heart and overflowed the embankment of His eyes.

Krsna’s tears flowed into a hollow beside the footpath and formed a small pond — Vihvala-kunda— whose waters induce overwhelming feelings of separation in devotees who drink from or bathe in them.

A four-armed form of Yogamaya, Vihvala-devi, resides on the bank of the lake by the same name. Like many other temples in Vraja, this temple of the goddess is sorely neglected and falling into ruin.

I paid my respects to Vihvalavana and walked south, parallel to the main road, bowing to Candravalivana and Rithora, the place of Candravali's birth, both some distance to my right.

Rithora is the residence of the gopa Candrabhanu. He was blessed with a beautiful little girl whose submissive ways charmed the residents of his village and brought her girlfriends under her control.

Within the surrounding forest is a lake, Candravali-kunda. As a young girl, Candravali and her friends would play and bathe within its waters and rest within the shade of a kadamba tree on its banks.

Followers of Rupa Gosvami know that Candravali-devi, like all the Vraja gopis, is an expansion of Srimati Radharani's personal form. She displays the submissive aspect of Radha’s transcendental disposition.

Indeed, as Radha’s primary expansion, Candravali-devi is almost as beautiful, witty, and qualified as Srimati Radharani; still, Gaudiya Vaisnavas always offer her respect from a distance.

They do this because Candravali is Radha’s principal competitor, a role that does not sit well transcendentally with Radha’s followers, who want Krsna’s attention to be exclusively on their mistress.

The reader might ask, “Why would Radhika expand Herself into a gopi who is Her closest competitor?” The concise answer to this important question is that She does so for Krsna’s pleasure.

Without many consorts, and without these consorts being of a variety of sentiments, there is no rejoicing in rasa; therefore Radha, whose primary concern is to increase Krsna’s bliss, expands into many.

And to help Krsna taste all the sweetness of pastimes like the rasa dance, the expansions of Radha are grouped together according to the similarity of their sentiments, resulting in competing parties of gopis.

Srila Prabhupada writes, “The variety of innumerable mistresses is a source of relish for Sri Krsna, and therefore these expansions from Srimati Radharani are necessary for enhancing Krsna’s pleasure.”

In the lotus of Vraja, and within it the lotus of its pastimes, Radha eternally competes for Krsna’s attention; still, She remains the whorl of both of those lotuses as the cynosure of Krsna’s love.

Radha is the enchanter of Govinda, His be-all and end-all, the crest jewel of His consorts, the one who gives Him pleasure directly as the wife of Abhimanyu, and indirectly as the wife of Govardhana Malla.

Another gopi expansion of Srimati Radharani is Campakalata-devi, whose birthplace is in the village of Sunthera, on the opposite side of the main road in the direction of Kokilavana.

Unlike Candravali, Campakalata is Srimati Radharani’s ally. Campakalata possesses supreme love for the divine couple; thus, she is counted amongst the eight most prominent gopis.

These most prominent gopis sometimes display more love for Radha than they do for Krsna, or more love for Krsna than for Radha, but in their hearts they have equal love for Them both.

By nature, Campakalata-devi is more contrary than submissive. But her contrariness is not as intense as that of the foremost of Radha’s friends, Lalita-devi, whose anger puts the fear of God even in Krsna.

Her qualities are best compared to Visakha, who is like the shadow of Sri Radha. Unlike Visakha, however, and more so than any other gopi, Campakalata is extremely secretive about her activities.

Moreover, Campakalata is very clever. When rival gopis like Candravali try to lure Krsna away from Radha, Campakalata knows how to thwart their plans by skilful diplomacy and logical persuasion.

Two days younger than Srimati Radharani, Campakalata-devi has a complexion like a cluster of yellow campaka flowers and she meticulously dresses herself in clothes the colour of a cataka bird.

Having studied many books on culinary science, Campakalata is a master of cooking in all six flavours. But her specialty, which earned her the nickname “sweet hands” (mistahasta), is making candy.

What’s more, Campakalata serves the divine couple by offering Them necklaces and by fanning Them with a camara. She is also the leader of the gopis who care for the trees, creepers, and bushes of Vraja.

A skilled forester, Campakalata knows where every kind of fruit, flower, and root can be found in Vraja’s forests. She is also expert in decorating kunjas and pathways with these natural ingredients.
