Although Sunthera is her place of birth, Campakalata lives in Yavata. Her grove for service — where everything is the colour of molten gold — is on the bank of Radha’s lake.

Let us pray to Campakalata-devi, who sings Krsna’s names in a sweet voice as she collects flowers for decorating her kunja, which she does with artistry unmatched by even the Lakshmis of Vaikuntha.

Let us remember her as she artistically dresses in preparation for His arrival. Due to her deep absorption in thoughts of Him, she shines with a splendour that surpasses the effulgence of the sun.

Let us bow to Campakalata and to Raghava Gosvami, her counter- whole in Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. He always resides at the foot of Govardhana Hill in the cave called Manikandali.

Raghava Gosvami wrote a book entitled Bhakti-ratna-prakasa. He also took Narottama Dasa Thakura and Srinivasa Acarya on parikrama of Vrndavana, which is elaborately described in Bhakti-ratnakara.

Raghava Gosvami is sometimes mistaken for Raghava Pandita, who is a resident of Panihati in Bengal and Dhanistha gopi in Krsna’s pastimes. The fact is that there are two Raghavas.

**\* \* \* \***

As I approached Premavana I offered my respects to Dhanistha gopi and to her birthplace, the village of Dhanasimha, which is assigned to a place in the woods within the turn of the parikrama path.

One hundred million gopis, of whom eight hundred thousand are eternally perfect, served Krsna in the paramour mellow during His divine pastimes in Bhauma Vrndavana.

Of these eight hundred thousand eternally perfect gopis, Dhanistha is amongst the hundred most important. She stays in the home of Nanda Maharaja and assists in Krsna’s pastimes mostly from there.

Amongst Dhanistha’s assorted duties, she is especially expert at carrying milk, preparing food, and cleaning, decorating, and anointing Krsna’s home with aromatic substances.

Before Radha enters the kitchen in Nandagrama each morning, She hands Her veil, rings, and ornaments to Tulasi. Then She washes Her hands and feet with water brought by Dhanistha.

Dhanistha then helps serve breakfast. After the gopas retire to rest, she brings Srimati Radharani the remnants from Krsna’s plate, either on Mother Yasoda’s request or on her own initiative.

Radharani is too shy to eat in the presence of Her superiors, but affectionate Mother Yasoda insists on watching the girls eat. “When I see You eating,” Yasoda often says, “it soothes my eyes.”

Before Radha wed Abhimanyu, Yasoda would dream of the day Radha married her son. And even though Radha is married to someone other than Krsna, Yasoda still treats Radha like her daughter-in-law.

Yasoda’s affection is unlimited. On one particular day, as the gopis prepared to leave, she had Dhanistha pack a basket of gifts for Radha, gifts like betel leaves, sandal paste, vermilion, and new clothes.

While Yasoda was busy seeing the sakhis off with kisses, Visakha and Dhanistha exchanged a few pieces of clothes and some ornaments the divine couple mistakenly took from each other the night before.

Then, after the gopas took the cows out to the pastures, Mother Yasoda ordered Dhanistha and her maidservants to bring the boys different savouries Lalita and her friends had prepared earlier.

Krsna, exhausted from play, saw Dhanistha and happily enquired from her about His parents, “Are they happy? Have they bathed, performed worship, and eaten to their full satisfaction?”

Dhanistha replied, “After completing their worship, Your parents fed and gave charity to the brahmanasfate to their satisfaction, and sent me here with some delicious snacks and messages of love.”

Pleased that His parents were well and happy, Krsna then informed Dhanistha of His plan to visit Radha-kunda and requested her to persuade unpredictable Radhika to meet Him there.

Welcoming her assignment, Dhanistha hurried to Yavata, where Radha, after having completed Her morning duties, sat sulking, gripped by doubt that fate would allow Her to meet Krsna that day.

Radha enquired, “Dhanistha, why have you come here?” To which Dhanistha replied, “To tell You the news.” “News about what?” Radha asked. “About the moon of Vraja,” Dhanistha replied.

For Radha, any news about Krsna is always welcome. Curious, She asked, “How is Syamasundara?” Dhanistha paused dramatically before saying, “He is overpowered by His enemy, Cupid.”

Tears filled Radha’s eyes and a smile of satisfaction slowly decorated Her lips. Dhanistha continued, “Cupid, resentful that Krsna’s beauty had eclipsed his own, decided to punish our Gopala.

“As Hari sat in a grove by Your lake, unarmed and accompanied only by His shadow, Cupid, armed with an arsenal of terrible weapons and accompanied by many soldiers, ambushed Him.

“Cupid arrayed his ultimate military formation — the beauty of spring—and invoked sublime weapons like flower-arrows, the songs of honeybees, and the stimulant of spring breezes.

“This infallible army quickly captured Hari and imprisoned Him in a forest fortress by Your lake. He now waits for You to rescue Him, as He has rescued You many times in the past.”

Dhanistha squeezed Radha’s hand and urged Her with a sultry voice, “Radha! Only You can save Him from the forces of kama. Please do so quickly; otherwise You may be condemned for being ungrateful!

“Only when Krsna is with You does He shine like the enchanter of Cupid. And when You are not with Him, although He enchants the whole world, Cupid enchants Him.”

Dhanistha’s ambrosial words stirred Radha’s hope, a hope that induced signs of ecstasy in Radha’s limbs. Her entire being was paralysed, and that paralysis quickly spread to Her attendants.

With great difficulty, Radharani composed herself, releasing Her friends from the influence of Her ecstasy. Then, with a graceful nod of Her head, She signalled Her desire to meet Krsna.

Sri Radha, twirling a toy lotus in Her left hand, holding Kundavalli’s hand in Her right, left Her house with a rapid yet controlled gait, Her suppressed eagerness ornamenting Her with a unique splendour.

As planets surround the sun, the gopis surrounded Radharani: Tulasi and Dhanistha walked in front, Lalita and Visakha on either side, Rupa and her assistants behind, and other gopis all around.

But in Her intense eagerness to sec Her beloved, Srimati Radharani was so bewildered that She mistook a tamala tree for Krsna, the enchanter, and a golden yuthi vine for an enchanted Dhanistha.

Bitten by the snake of loving envy, Srimati Radharani mistakenly scolded Her friend: “O Dhanistha! What is this?” Confused, Dhanistha looked here and there and replied, “What is what?”

“Are your eyes closed!” snapped Radharani. “Look in front of you, where Krsna, the moon of all cheaters, dances.” Then, turning wide- eyed to Her other friends, Radha rambled on, as if mad:

“Friends! Look at these deceitful dancers, Krsna and Dhanistha. That womaniser has subdued Dhanistha and charmed her into dancing the chaladutya, also hoping to lure us into the snare of His arms.”

Dhanistha smiled and said, “Chaste girl! I am not dancing, but You are. And Your dancing teacher is not Krsna, but Your own love for Him. No doubt that love will also make Krsna dance.”

The smiling faces and laughter of the gopis sobered Srimati Radharani, who then saw that what She had taken to be Krsna and Dhanistha was actually a tamala tree embraced by a golden vine.

Sri Radha was embarrassed by Her display of ecstatic delusion, but the cowherd girls were unperturbed. They huddled around Her and jokingly but gently lead Her along the path to Radha-kunda.

Let us sincerely pray to Dhanistha gopi for the blessings by which our creepers of devotion will eventually yield the fruits of eternal engagement at Lord Krsna’s lotus feet.

**\* \* \* \***

In New Vraja-dhama, the western corner of the intersection of the parikrama path and the road that runs between Nandagrama and Varsana marks the love-saturated forest famous as Premavana.

At the heart of Premavana is a divine lake known as Prema-sarovara. This lake is filled with the liquid form of a rare and confidential type of transcendental love even the Vedas do not speak of.

The waters of this lake have gradually seeped through the cintamani soil of the area and nourished the grasses, creepers, and trees, saturating the forest with the pinnacle of love.

Prema-sarovara is octagonally shaped. There is a temple of Prema- bihari on its banks, as well as a platform that commemorates the rasa dance, and a swing where the divine couple is said to sport.

Once, in the company of Their cowherd girlfriends, Radha and Krsna were in Premavana, deeply absorbed in the ecstasies of loving pastimes. Suddenly a bumblebee entered the grove.

The divine couple’s maidservants had posted Madhumangala as doorkeeper. He had strict orders to keep out all intruders, but after consuming a basket of forest fruit he had dozed off.

When the bumblebee saw Sri Radha’s face, he mistook it for a golden lotus flower. Greedy for nectar, it hovered around Her mouth, thinking the aroma of Her breath was the sweetest honey.

Annoyed by the bee, Sri Radha tried to shoo it away with Her hand, then with Her veil. When all efforts failed She cupped Her hands over Her face and called out for help, rousing Madhumangala.

Krsna did nothing but watch Radha with amusement, but Madhumangala was quick to jump into action. Brandishing his staff, that king of brahmanas chased the bee out of the kunja.

Mission complete, Madhu returned and proudly announced, “Madhusudana (the bee) is now gone.” At this, Sri Radha slowly removed Her hands from Her face and stared past Her lover.

Radha misunderstood Madhu’s words to mean that Krsna (Madhusudana) had gone away. That misunderstanding churned (uddipana) the nectar of Her loving feelings (anuraga).

This churning precipitated a poison-like ecstasy called prema- vaicittya, in which the news of Krsna’s departure blinded Radha to the reality that Krsna was sitting right there beside Her.

Even though Krsna was close enough to embrace, Srimati Radharani’s intense absorption in love projected onto Her heart and mind extremely intense feelings of separation from Her beloved.

Feeling hopeless without Her lover, Radharani began to pine piteously for Krsna, calling out, “O Lord of My life! Why have You left Me alone? Where have You gone?”

Krsna was taken aback by Radha’s condition. But before He could do anything to pacify Her, He was overwhelmed by the influence of Her ecstasy and also called out, “O My dear! Where are You?”

Just as Radhika thought that Krsna had left Her, Krsna thought that She had left Him. He also lost sight of His beloved and was overcome by the intense agony of Her sudden departure.

The gopis were stunned by this sudden and unexpected turn of events. They stood motionless like statues, unable to say or do anything to console their master and mistress.

The tears streaming from their eyes were the only signs of life in the gopis. As those tears fell to the ground, they swelled the rushing stream already formed by Radha’s and Krsna’s own tears.

The united stream of loving sentiments flowed into a nearby kunda Krsna once dug as a water trough for His cows, which everyone now knows as Prema-sarovara, the “lake of transcendental love.”

Unable to tolerate the pain of separation any longer, Radha and Krsna both fainted. Seeing this, the gopis also fainted. Only a panicked Madhu was left to bring them all back to consciousness.

Fortunately, the resourceful parrot couple, Suka and Sari, were on hand to help. As Madhu massaged Krsna’s lotus feet and hands, Suka loudly chanted Krsna’s name and Sari chanted Radha’s.

The sounds “Radha” and “Krsna” acted as antidotes to cure the effects of prema-vaicittya raging within the divine couple. They both came back to external consciousness and into each other’s sight.

Radha and Syama looked longingly at each other. As Their sorrow subsided, the gopis also regained consciousness. Seeing the divine couple happy again, they began to sing and dance in ecstatic bliss.

In time, Madhumangala told all the Vraja-vasis of the incident at Prema-sarovara, how it had become a reservoir of the liquefied form of the most intense type of transcendental love.

Suka and Sari also spread the news amongst the animals of Vrndavana, and Prema-sarovara soon became a regular place of pilgrimage for both humans and animals to relish bathing in.

Lord Caitanya speaks of the intense opposing emotions caused by prema: “If one develops love of Krsna, all the bitter and sweet influences of this love will manifest in one’s heart in two ways.

“The poisonous effects of love of Godhead defeat the severe and fresh poison of the serpent. Yet there is simultaneously transcendental bliss, which pours down and defeats the pride of nectar.

“In other words, love of Krsna is so powerful that it simultaneously defeats the poisonous effects of a snake, as well as the happiness derived from pouring nectar on one’s head.”

In summary, love of Krsna is perceived as doubly effective — simultaneously poisonous and nectarean. At Prema-sarovara devotees hear that pastime in which the poisonous effect of love is dominant.

The gopas and gopis would joyfully play Holi in Premavana, sprinkling that forest with the nectarean effect of meeting. In doing so, they counteracted any poisonous effect of love that lingered there.

Following the emotional exchanges at Prema-sarovara, Krsna — who is always fully submerged in His pastimes — was sometimes distracted by certain thoughts, thoughts that He would eventually act upon —

Thoughts that contributed to a momentous decision that would shape His later pastimes: the decision to leave Vraja for Mathura and Dvaraka, from where He would return at times, but never stay.

Krsna thought, “I have appeared in Vrndavana to give My fully surrendered devotees, the cowherd men and women — especially Srimati Radharani — the transcendental pleasure of My company.

“But the incident at Prema-sarovara shows that My presence some' times gives Radharani great pain, something I do not want to do not to mention see, and yet I seem constantly obliged to do just that.

“Sometimes when I am standing right before Srimati Radharani, Mother Yasoda, or Sridama, they lose sight of Me out of intense apprehension of losing My company and fall into a delirium.

“Just the other day, I was conversing with Radharani when suddenly the flames of separation burning from the fire of Her intense love for Me melted Her mind, causing Her to see Me as a gopi.

“Placing a straw between Her teeth Srimati Radharani then begged, ‘O friend! Please show Me where My lover Krsna is.’ My heart was completely shattered by witnessing this incredible behaviour.

“And I was equally devastated when at Prema-sarovara, tearful Radharani beseeched Madhumangala: ‘When the gopas were frightened of the forest fire, Krsna responded to their call.

‘“Why does He not respond to My calls? Have I offended Him in some way? Has some other pretty cowherd girl called Him to a secluded place? Has lotus-eyed Krsna now abandoned Me in this forest?’

“At that time, I was unable to relieve Srimati Radharani’s pain. What’s more, I was also drawn into Her irrepressible mood. I suffered doubly—for Radha’s unhappiness, as well as for My own.

“But when I am away from My dear ones, absence from Me so heightens their love that a tamala tree reminds them of Me, or they even visualise (sphurti) Me standing before them.

“Once, when Srimati Radharani saw bandhuka flowers, lotus flowers, and a colony of bees surrounding a tamala tree, She imagined the combination of these things to be Me.

“Another time, after Radha saw a picture that Visakha drew of Me, She said, ‘As I was looking at this picture, a young man, as splendid as a sapphire and decorated with a peacock feather, emerged from it.

“‘He stood before Me with moving eyebrows, making Me so mad with love for Him that I laughed without reason and felt the moon to be as hot as fire, and fire to be as cooling as the moon.’

“By remembering Me, and especially by perceiving Me in these ways, My companions enjoy great happiness, their hearts become peaceful, and I too find the happiness for which My heart always yearns.

“From the unveiling of these mysterious pastimes, it seems that I can make My parents, friends and gopi beloveds, and even the cows happier when I am away from them than when I am with them.

“This transcendental contradiction is a great wonder, but being a firsthand witness to the pastimes of Vrndavana, I have come to the conclusion that this is the way of My devotees’ love.”

Thinking like this, Krsna’s decision to leave Vrndavana was further strengthened, a decision that matured into action when He accepted Kamsa’s invitation to attend the wrestling match in Mathura.

**\* \* \* \***

The next forest along the parikrama path after Premavana is Bahulavana. Some Vaisnavas consider it the most sacred forest of all, for it includes the most sacred place of all — Radha-kunda.

When Caitanya Mahaprabhu came here on pilgrimage, He recalled how the cowherd boys bring their cows to this tranquil forest and how the gopas rest while the animals drink from Bahulavana’s lakes.

As the Lord was thinking like that, the cows grazing in Bahulavana were spontaneously attracted to Him. Surrounding Him, they licked His body and gazed at Him with affection.

Absorbed in ecstatic love, Caitanya Mahaprabhu caressed the cows, which pressed around Him closer and closer, just as Nanda Maharaja’s cows had pressed around Krsna thousands of years ago.

As the Lord walked through the groves and gardens of Bahulavana, forest animals like deer and rabbits fearlessly approached Him, and birds like doves and cuckoos repeatedly circled over His head.

Indeed, as Lord Caitanya approached, the trees burst into bloom, and as He passed beneath them, they showered Him with their petals as the entire forest echoed with cries of “Jaya! Jaya!”

The people of the area were astounded to see how the cows, animals, and forest greeted the Lord. Consulting amongst themselves, they concluded, “This sannyasi must be a fair-complexioned Sri Krsna.”

Caitanya Mahaprabhu smiled at the residents of Bahulavana and showered them with loving glances as He sweetly chanted Krsna’s names, filling their hearts to the brim with the topmost pleasure.

Within Bahulavana are Sankarsana-kunda and Krsna-kunda. One who bathes in these lakes during the month of Caitra can see the forms of Laksmi-Narayana that eternally reside in this forest.

It is also said that persons desiring wealth, beauty, and good progeny can fulfil such desires by bathing in these lakes. Pure Vaisnavas, however, bathe only out of respect or out of love for the Lord.

On the bank of Krsna-kunda are deities of a cow, its calf, a tiger, a brahmana, and of Krsna, all of which remind pilgrims of the narration in which the cow Bahula took shelter of Sri Krsna.

Once the cow Bahula was peacefully grazing within a forest pasture when a tiger attacked her. The cow requested the tiger to allow her to return to her calf, feed it, and bid it farewell before he ate her.

The tiger agreed to the fair proposal and sat down to await the return of the cow. In the meantime, the cow went to her master, a brahmana, and to her calf in order to tell them what had happened.

When the calf and the brahmana heard Bahula’s pitiful story, they were so moved by her plight that they both selflessly volunteered to take her place as the tiger’s rightful meal.

Bahula declined their selfless offer, but in the end all three — cow, calf, and brahmana— returned to offer themselves to the tiger, who was delighted by this exceedingly favourable turn of events.

As the tiger contemplated which victim to eat first, who should appear but Lord Krsna, the presiding Deity of religious principles, like truthfulness, and the guardian of Vraja’s residents, like the cows.

Holding His cakra in one hand, Krsna convinced the tiger that he would earn everlasting fame if he were to show leniency to the cow and its kin for their truthful and honourable conduct.

The tiger then released Bahula from its vow and retreated into the forest. The calf and the brahmana rejoiced that their mother had been spared and that Krsna had mercifully intervened.

Lord Hari was so pleased with Bahula’s truthfulness and integrity that He blessed her to become His consort; thus, in her next birth Bahula became one of the wives of Lord Krsna.

Bahula eternally resides in the forest that bears her name. Pilgrims and Vraja-vasis alike remember her as an emblem of truthfulness, the quality that protects one from the influence of Kali.

Bahula’s adherence to truth purchased the Lord, for truth is the most fundamental of religious principles and the very essence of His being. In this way Bahula’s glory is known throughout the world.

Indeed, the appearance day of Vrndavana’s most sacred holy place, Radha-kunda, is celebrated on Bahulastami; thus, Bahula’s name is forever linked with that of Srimati Radharani.

Another pastime connected to Bahulavana took place during Krsna’s twelve-forest rasa dance festival mentioned earlier when we visited Kamyavana, Kokilavana, and Madhuvana. Here are some details:

As Krsna and the cowherd girls observed the beauty of Vrndavana forest, they beheld a mysterious effulgence spreading in all directions from the matchless banks of the River Yamuna.

Curious about the source of that radiance, Krsna led His entourage of loving gopis north to the flowering gardens of Bahulavana, which were aglow with the purity of Bahula’s vow.

As Krsna narrated the story of Bahula, He noticed that the gopis were tired from trekking through Vraja. Raising His flute to His lips, He played a melody that invokes clouds (megha-mallara raga).

A garland of rain clouds at once gathered overhead, accompanied by a fragrant breeze, and sprinkled fine drops of water over the forest, relieving the gopis’ discomfort and making them happy.

On another occasion Krsna was late for a meeting with Srimati Radharani, which She took as a sign of His neglect. To show Her displeasure, She disappeared into a grove of Bahulavana to sulk.

Distressed at the loss of Radhika’s company, Krsna recruited the gopis to help Him find Her. After searching every hiding place in Vrndavana, they finally found Her on the bank of Krsna-kunda.

As Krsna was to quickly discover, however, finding Radha was easier than persuading Her to give up Her unfounded anger. Again, with the help of Her friends, He was able to win Radha over.

Since the time Radha sulked by the banks of Krsna-kunda, the lake has also been known as Mana-sarovara. And because it is the place where Bahula attained perfection, it is also known as Bahula-kunda.

Great devotees such as Jahnava-devi have bathed in this lake, knowing that their visit to Bahulavana would otherwise be as incomplete as a visit to Haridvara without bathing in the Ganges.

**\* \* \* \***

With the strong conviction that the dhama’s grace can bestow all perfection, I left Bahulavana for Chatikara, which in Vraja is on the Delhi-Mathura road — in Krsna Valley, on Prabhupada Walk.

After Krsna toppled the twin arjuna trees, the cowherd men began to consider whether or not the forest of Mahavana was too dangerous a place to live peacefully and safely raise children.

Krsna had also developed a desire to play in the soft sands of Vrndavana. He entered the hearts of the village elders and inspired them to convene a meeting about changing their place of residence.

Upananda spoke up in the council: “O King of Vraja! Since the birth of your all-auspicious son, dangers have beset our community in the form of terrible demons like Putana and Trnavarta.

“Our conclusion is that Mahavana must be the cause of these difficulties. We recommend that our people move without delay to the forest of Vrndavana, where the goddess of fortune always resides.

“Vrndavana is pleasing in every season. It is opulent with sweet grasses for our cows. And for anyone who lives there, the wealth of the worlds becomes as insignificant as a clump of straw.”

Nanda Maharaja was attached to residing in Brhadvana, but for the sake of his subjects, who were obviously unhappy, he accepted their proposal and replied, “I agree! Let us move to Vrndavana at once!”

In the days that followed, the cowherd men and women gathered up all their household paraphernalia and other family possessions and loaded them onto nicely ornamented bullock carts.

On an auspicious day, they herded together their cows and other livestock and began the two-day journey to Vrndavana. Crossing the Yamuna at its shallowest point, they set up camp at Chatikara.

The cowherd men formed a semicircular (chatikara) boundary wall with their carts. This “wall” extended from Kaliya-ghata to Govardhana and almost reached as far as Nandagrama to the north.

Since Nandagrama had not yet manifest itself as the capital of Nanda’s kingdom, and since Mahavana had now been abandoned, the opulence of those two places entered the area of Chatikara.

When Krsna and Balarama saw Vrndavana forest, the Yamuna River and Govardhana Hill—manifestations of Their own internal potencies — They were greatly pleased.

And when They expressed Their pleasure with smiling faces, playful gestures, and the broken language of small childen, They gave even greater pleasure to Their parents and the other Vraja-vasis.

As Krsna entered boyhood, He showed more interest in playing with His friends than in being with His parents. With only playing games in mind, He begged His parents to allow Him to tend the calves.

Mother Yasoda berated her husband, “How can your son, still on His mother’s breast, accept such painful work?” Pulling on His mother’s apron, His voice full of magic, Krsna interrupted:

“Mother! Why do you object to My desire to tend the calves with My friends. I will not listen to any arguments you use against this great fun. Please, just give Me permission to perform My duty!”

Mother Yasoda surprised her husband by accepting Krsna’s plea, and so, on an auspicious day selected by brahmanas, Vrajaraja took Krsna, Balarama, and Their contemporary friends to the barn.

Nanda Maharaja selected some calves for Krsna and some for Balarama. He then handed his son a red stick and showed Him how to control the calves and what orders to give them.

Jumping up and down, restless Krsna replied, “We know how to tend the calves.” His doting parents said, “We know that. But still, do not wander too far away today, and come home early.”

The young cowherds showed off their skill at controlling the calves, and when they had convinced their parents of their competence, they wandered into the pastures surrounding Chatikara.

Day by day, Krsna wandered further and further afield with His calves and friends, exploring the forests and meadows of Vraja. Revelling in His newfound freedom, Krsna’s ecstasy grew.

His bluish bodily effulgence tinged Vrndavana forest, the golden radiance of His sparkling smile warmed the forest creatures, and His red-tinted sidelong glance enlivened His friends.

Vrndavana forest, which naturally resounded with the singing of birds and humming of bees, was further embellished with the lowing of calves, blowing of buffalo horns, and the sounds of joyous laughter.

Above all, as Krsna became more adept at music, His flute-song became an integral part of Vrndavana’s collage of sounds as it echoed across the valleys, forests, and waters of Vraja.

While the calves grazed, Krsna and Balarama played with Their friends in various ways. They threw stones at the fruit in the trees and when the fruits fell, they would either kick or throw them.

They imitated the fighting of bulls. Some boys would cover themselves with blankets, and those “bulls” would loudly roar and charge at each other, as the other boys goaded them on.

The demigods fell unconscious in bliss as they watched the delightful pastimes of Krsna and Balarama, and the Vraja-vasis, especially the boys’ parents, were suffused with ever-increasing joy.

Before sunrise, Yasoda would tenderly wake Krsna, cleanse His mouth, rub Him with oil, and bathe Him. Then she would apply kajjala to His eyes, dress Him, and decorate Him with valuable ornaments.

Ushering her son into the dining hall, she would feed Him many tasty, nourishing dishes and then follow Him into the pastures saying, “I will walk You to the edge of Vrndavana forest.”

Time and again, Mother Yasoda would turn back towards the village, only to return to feed Krsna sweets, straighten His clothes, and make sure that He had everything He needed, as only mothers can do.

But after a while, Krsna would lovingly urge His mother to return home to prepare for His return in the evening. Free from their parents, the boys would explode into unrestrained, high-spirited play.

When the playful calves saw the free spirit of their guardians, they would jump around as only calves can do, further surcharging the atmosphere with their joyful exuberance.

Just as the boys began to tire, Mother Yasoda’s servants would arrive with a morning snack of tasty dishes which the cowherd boys would eagerly munch on, laughing and joking all the while.

Srila Prabhupada once said,

**“Krsna and Balarama would have opulent preparations along with puns and kachoris, while the cowherd boys would have simple foods with chapatis.**

**“After all, Krsna was the son of a king, His friends simple cowherds. But when they sat together to eat, out of loving affection the boys would eat Krsna’s puris, and He would eat their chapatis.”**

Invigorated by their meal, the boys went on playing, and as they did Krsna’s ankle-bells filled the forest with enchanting jingling sounds that made the songbirds hide their heads in shame.

In the late afternoon Krsna would return to the waiting arms of His love-struck mother, who would personally bathe, dress, and feed Him, and then tuck Him in for the night on a jewelled bed.

In this way Krsna passed His boyhood in the company of loving friends, and under the watchful eyes of His parents. And as Kamsa dispatched demons to Vraja, Krsna killed them one after another.

While speaking to Uddhava, Krsna described a flower-filled forest in the area of Chatikara, or Sattikara, which was the capital city of the king of bees. Krsna stayed there for twelve days.

In Krsna Valley, a children’s playground marks the location of that forest. In Bhauma Vrndavana, a temple of Garuda-Govinda testifies to Sri Krsna’s supremacy as the Personality of Godhead.

During Krsna’s appearance as Lord Ramacandra, Indrajit bound the Lord with a snake-noose in the battle with Ravana. Then the task fell to Garuda — Visnu’s carrier—to free King Rama.

But Garuda could not understand that Ramacandra, in His pastime as a human, would almost always depend upon the services of His associates like Hanuman. Garuda then doubted the Lord’s divinity.

Garuda thought, “If, as others say, Ramacandra is indeed the Supreme Personality of Godhead, then why did I have to rescue Him from Indrajit’s weapon? Why could He not free Himself?”

This doubt stayed with Garuda even after Lord Rama concluded His pastimes on earth. When the heavenly planets were abuzz with news of the Lord’s appearance as a gopa, Garuda flew to Vrndavana.

Hiding behind the clouds, Garuda watched how carefree Krsna was in losing a wrestling match to Sridama. Then He saw the Lord kill invincible demons like Aghasura, whom all the demigods feared.

By the effect of living in the dhama, Garuda became enlightened about the truths of the Lord’s humanlike pastimes. Garuda could then understand that Lord Rama, just like Krsna, had hidden His divinity.

Garuda repented for his past offence. This penitence allowed him to perceive that rural Vrndavana was filled with hidden majesties and potencies that eclipsed even those of Vaikuntha.

Realising that a curtain of deluding maya — which even he could not penetrate — covers the pastimes of Lord Rama and Lord Krsna, Garuda fell prostrated before Krsna in Chatikara forest.

Govinda consoled Garuda by stroking his head. The Lord then climbed on His carrier’s shoulders and blessed him: “From now on, your name will always be spoken before Mine — Garuda-Govinda.”

Some days after Garuda had returned to the heavenly planets, the cowherd boys were arguing amongst themselves about who was seated upon Garuda, whom they had seen flying over Chatikara.

Some boys were insistent that it was Krsna, who was really the Supreme Narayana. Others argued that Krsna was just a cowherd boy, and so it must have been Lord Visnu on Garuda, visiting Vraja.

To settle the dispute, Krsna manifested a four-armed form and climbed upon Sridama’s shoulders, who then transformed himself into Garuda. Krsna waved as they flew in circles above the boys.

Krsna’s final pastime at Chatikara took place along the roadside, as He passed by on His way to Mathura. By smiling at the waving villagers, Krsna prompted a charming argument amongst the young girls.

One girl said to her companion, “That dark youth on the chariot is looking at me.” Another maiden objected, “You are wrong; He is looking at me.” A third girl giggled, “Actually, He is looking at me.”

In this way the girls debated amongst themselves, “At me!” “At me!” “At me!” Their contradictory claims created a symphony that resembled the sound of waves crashing against one another.

These simple words of affection broadened Krsna’s smile, who as the Supersoul watches every living entity simultaneously, especially a few village girls on the outskirts of Vrndavana.

Amongst the unlimited places of pastimes in Vraja, Varsana is one of the divine couple's favourites. And in Varsana, Mana-garh is the best due to its seclusion, ambience, and unparalleled view of the area.

## Chapter Seven - Varsana

At the end of the golden age the creator, Lord Brahma, performed austerities with a desire to become a hill upon which Krsna and the gopis could perform their pastimes in Vrndavana.

That would allow Brahma not only to witness the pastimes of Krsna and His dearest devotees hut also to receive the most coveted treasure: the touch of the soft pink lotus feet of Radha and the gopis.

By this desire — to serve Sri Radha as the hill upon which She would reside — Brahma showed a special fondness for Radharani, a fondness Lord Siva did not display when he took the form of Nandisvara Hill.

Lord Brahma, having been directly initiated by the sound of Krsna’s flute, is the original guru of the Gaudiya Vaisnavas, who therefore welcome Brahma’s transcendental favouritism towards Sri Radha.

Because the four heads of Lord Brahma appear as the four peaks of this hill, his incarnation in Vraja was known as Brhat-sanu (“The Great Peaks”) and also as Brahma-sarin (“The Heads of Brahma”).

Later, when King Vrsabhanu settled his family into a palace atop this hill, it became known as Vrsabhanu-pura, the capital of King Vrsabhanu. And today it is known as Varsana.

Sri Krsna is a rain cloud of sublime emotions, and Sri Radha is a rainmaker who seeds that cloud with Her matchless love, inundating the world with ecstasies of the transcendental amorous mellow.

Because Srimati Radharani causes this downpour (varsa) of bliss, She is called Varsana. And for this reason the place of Her childhood residence was named Varsana after Her.

Some persons assert that Radha’s birthplace is in Raval, while others contend that it is in Varsana. Both opinions are correct: in this millennium She appeared in Raval, and in another, Varsana.

No one disputes, however, that the lakes, gardens, groves, and stones of Varsana’s hills are saturated with the unparalleled sweetness radiating from Sri Radha’s form, qualities, pastimes, and entourage.

Eager to see these pastime places and to relish their sweetness, I hurried up the hill towards Radha-Syama’s temple in New Vraja-dhama and turned left at Konai to offer my obeisances to Yogamaya.

“O devi riding a lion!” I prayed. “You use the ropes of pious and impious deeds, and the chains of their inevitable reactions, to keep those averse to Sri Krsna bound to this world of birth and death.

“Although I am but one such poor conditioned soul, the influence of New Vraja-dhama has awakened in my heart a desire to enter the transcendental pastimes you orchestrate in this land of bliss.

“Please remove the obstacles to the fulfillment of this desire and qualify me with the kind of selfless loving devotion needed to serve the associates of Radha and Syamasundara.”

My prayer complete, I rose and with joined palms gazed at the beautiful form of Yogamaya. The mere sight of her can drive away all inauspicious thoughts and make one’s heart swell with joy.

Yogamaya’s temple lies on the edge of a jungle called Pipasavana by some and Piyaso by others. There Krsna became thirsty (pipasa) while herding cows, and Balarama brought Him a drink from Pyasa-kunda.

Notably, a mysterious ascetic who rarely shows himself resides here. He has long matted hair and is popularly known amongst the present- day Vraja-vasis as Jhariwale Baba, or the “holy man of the thicket.”

This ascetic is actually the son of Dronacarya — Asvatthama. He constantly chants Sri Krsna’s names in order to regain the Lord’s mercy, which he lost when he offended Queen Draupadi.

For one who offends the Lord, the only hope of salvation is the constant chanting of Krsna’s names — names more merciful than the Lord Himself. They can plunge anyone into an ocean of bliss.

Asvatthama is said to live on the bank of Jhariwale-baba-kunda. Being very remote it is popular with ascetics, and it also has a rasa-vedi on its banks to commemorate the fact that Krsna danced there.

To follow the complete parikrama of Varsana, I walked from Piyaso counter-clockwise to Gendokhara, a level field in between the foot of Dana-garh and Unchagaon where Radha would play ball.

In their childhood, the gopis would meet here to play, laughing in the way only young girls can. And in their youth, the gopis’ play attracted a new audience — the roving bumblebees of Sri Krsna’s eyes.

Radha and Krsna had known each other as infants, but He began to show interest in girls only towards the end of His boyhood. And that interest fully blossomed only after He had entered eternal youth.

Krsna’s increased interest in girls was due in part to youth’s awarding Cupid greater access to Him, and in part to youth’s bestowing upon the gopis irresistibly fresh and sweet new qualities.

These qualities, found in the bodies of the gopis, are youthfulness, beauty, sweetness, and delicacy, features that give impetus for Hari’s ever-increasing transcendental pleasure.

The youthful qualities of the Vraja gopis’ spiritual forms are elaborately described in the appendix entitled “The Qualities In the Gopis’ Forms That Invoke Ecstatic Love In Krsna.”

Gendokhara is one of the areas in which Krsna especially enjoyed the beauty of the gopis in many affectionate pastimes, pastimes that made His youth successful and revealed the wonder of His amorous life.

Sri Rupa narrates how Krsna once made Srimati Radharani close Her eyes in shame by publicly relating, in the assembly of Radha’s friends, the details of His intimate activities with Her on the previous night.

Krsna then showed the utmost cleverness by drawing pictures of dolphins on Radha’s breasts. By these pastimes in the groves of Vraja with Radha and the gopis, Krsna fulfilled the purpose of His youth.

Some three hundred years ago, Varsana was but a small settlement connected to Unchagaon. Over the years, however, Varsana flourished to become what it is today, while Unchagaon has fallen into obscurity.

Unchagaon is also known as Sri Lalita-grama because Radha’s best friend, Lalita-devi, grew up there and because many pastimes, including the marriage of Lalita-devi and Sri Krsna, took place there.

Please allow me to first praise Lalita-devi’s innumerable and purifying glories, glories surpassed only by those of Srimati Radharani, the brilliant desire-vine growing in King Vrsabhanu's dynasty.

**\* \* \* \***

Lalita's golden complexion is like gorocana. Her face is as round and brilliantly shining as the full moon. Her graceful form — displaying perfection in shape and proportion — is incomparable.

Her eyes are restless, like those of a startled doe, yet they are also brazen and fearless, revealing her contrary and hot-tempered nature. For this she is feared by He who is feared by fear personified.

Lalita’s dress and bodice resemble the multicoloured feathers of a peacock. Costly rings, bracelets, necklaces, and a tiara decorate her body, but her life’s treasure is Sri Radha, who is always by her side.

Born twenty-seven days before Sri Radha, Lalita is the eldest amongst Radha’s group, as well as its leader and its guru. She is the exemplar who shows the left-wing heroines how to conduct themselves.

At times Lalita thinks that Radha gives Herself to Krsna too easily: “Firmly reject this shrewd son of Nanda. O immoral girl! Don’t be weak! I’m saying this for Your own good. Don’t submit to Him!”

Once, Lalita was convinced that Krsna had been unfaithful to Radharani. Even though He had fallen at Radha’s feet to proclaim His innocence, Lalita would not permit Her to even look at Him.

Srimati Radharani sat in Her kunja, Lalita-devi sat in hers, and Sri Krsna paced worriedly back and forth between the two groves, repeatedly trying to break Radha’s pique, to no avail.

But with time Radhika had a change of heart. Her stance towards Krsna softened. Nonetheless, out of fear of Lalita’s strict order, Radharani would not allow Krsna into Her presence.

Thus Srimati Radharani was just as unhappy at being unable to meet Krsna as Krsna was at being unable to meet Her. And without Lalita’s consent, the situation appeared hopeless.

As Krsna paced back and forth contemplating His fate, a ray of hope suddenly appeared just outside Radharani’s kunja. There sat young Rati-manjari, ready to fulfil even the least of her mistress’s desires.

Krsna knew how dear Rati was to Radha, and so He approached her to intercede. With palms joined, He begged, “O Rati! Please request Radha to abandon Her anger. I can no longer bear this neglect.”

Rati agreed to do her best to help, but as she took a step in Radha’s direction she balked. One more look at Krsna’s pleading face, however, was enough to embolden her, so she entered the kunja.

“O Syama-pyari!” said Rati-manjari, “Your beloved Syamasundara is desperate for an audience with You. Can’t You give up this senseless anger and at least toss a wisp of a kindly glance His way?”

Radhika simply gave Rati a helpless look. Her voice burdened by resignation, She replied, “What can I do? Lalita has forbidden Me to speak with or even look at Hari. I cannot disobey her.”

Rati now understood that Lalita held the keys to Radha’s and Krsna’s happiness. She walked out of the kunja, right past anxious Krsna, and entered Lalita’s grove, where she found her talking with some gopis.

Lalita looked up at the new arrival and asked, “Rati! Why have you come here?” Looking at the other girls for encouragement, Rati fell at Lalita devi’s feet and with a trembling voice replied:

“Devi! I cannot tolerate seeing Radha’s and Krsna’s sadness at being separated from each other. They are both eager to reunite. But because of your order, Radha keeps Her beloved at bay.”

Lalita-devi didn’t hesitate a moment. She sent Rati back with a message that made Radha blossom with happiness and sealed Krsna’s eternal debt to Rati. Such is Lalita’s influence on the divine couple!

In response to Krsna’s conduct — and sometimes even at His suggestion— Lalita-devi often displays a contrary mood that at times leads to extreme anger or outrageous insolence.

Lalita may seem to impede Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes by such extreme ways, but in truth she enriches them. The residents of Vraja all know that her love for the divine couple is without equal.

Lalita-devi is expert at everything. In particular she performs feats of magic, composes and deciphers riddles, and fashions all manner of things from flowers, like awnings, couches, and ornaments.

Of all flower ornaments, best are her five-pointed flower crowns called puspapara, which are made with five colours of buds and flowers. Radha taught Lalita the art of making these floral tiaras.

But perhaps Lalita’s greatest service lies in her effort to inspire every girl in Vraja to devote her body and soul to the service of Sri Radha, by joining Radha’s entourage of gopi friends and maidservants.

Lalita-devi’s chief assistant is Rupa-manjari, who in the form of Rupa Gosvami promotes Lalita’s mission — which is identical to that of Krsna’s — amongst the conditioned souls of this world.

Only by the mercy of Lalita-devi, then, do both conditioned and liberated souls attain the good fortune by which they can qualify themselves for Radharani’s service — and in no other way.

I offer my obeisances to Radha’s dearmost friend, whose every desire was fulfilled when she saw a certain dark-complexioned boy blocking the path upon which Radha was walking near Govardhana Hill.

The pastime places of Lalita-devi’s childhood are marked by two hills: the village of Unchagaon itself and nearby Sakhigiri, famed as a playground of little Lalita and her friends, of whom Radha was chief.

On the crest of Unchagaon is the marriage platform known as lalita-vivaha-mandapa, a testament to Lalita’s intimate relationship with Radha and Krsna where deities of all three are served today.

After Nanda Maharaja had moved his family and subjects to Nandagrama, which is not far from Varsana, Krsna was able to meet Srimati Radharani and Her girlfriends with ease.

One day, not long after relocating to Nandagrama, Krsna and the young gopis, who were still in their girlhood, were conversing in a very sweet way within a garden on the top of Sakhigiri.

Radha was the centre of Krsna’s attention, but to broadcast Lalita’s unique place in the divine couple’s heart and her special role amongst the gopis, Radharani wanted to fix that attention on Lalita instead.

As Radha sat at Krsna’s left and Lalita to His right, Visakha and some other gopis hid behind Them. Then, by a pre-arranged plan, Krsna’s shawl was tied to a corner of Lalita-devi’s veil.

Much to the wonder of suspicious Lalita, the twins Rangadevi and Sudevi sang wedding songs, while the scholar amongst cowherd girls, Tungavidya, recited wedding mantras.

As the gopis showered flower petals on Lalita and Krsna, Lalita quickly tried to move away. But because she was tied to Krsna, to the amusement of all, she only managed to pull Him closer to her.

Irate Lalita was glowing red, but before she could vent her anger on those who had conspired against her, Radharani embraced her and whispered some reassuring words in Lalita’s ear.

Finally, Lalita relented. She submitted to the will of Sri Radha and Her friends and joined the other gopis present in a grand wedding festival held at the hill now known as Lalita-vivaha-sthala.

At Unchagaon one also finds Lalita’s ancestral home, where she was born and where she lived with her parents, Visoka and Saradi-devi, until her marriage to Bhairava-gopa in Yavata-grama.

Some locals call this place Atora-parvata because the remnants of Lalita’s balcony (atari) can still be seen, a balcony from which she would gaze in the direction of either Varsana or Nandagrama.

On Sakhigiri hill are many pastime places connected with Lalita and her companions, like the stone slide (picchalini-sila) on which the girls would slide down the hill, just like the boys did in Kamyavana.

Sakhigiri is also a carana-pahari, a hill of footprints. But the footprints there are not Krsna’s. They belong to the young gopis, most notably Sri Radha, whose footprints can be seen by careful scrutiny.

There are also stones upon which the young gopis drew pictures (citra-silas), stones coloured with dye from playing Holi (citravicitra-silas), and stones marked by the lac from the gopis' feet (alta-silas).

A lake named Gopi-puskarini graces this place, along with Sakhi-kupa, a well the gopis dug themselves. As young girls they played water sports in the lake, and when thirsty drew water from the well.

Nearby is Triveni-kupa, a well Lalita would bathe in every day just after dawn. While visiting His friend Sridama, Balarama once bathed there before the sun rose and before any girls arrived.

One winter day, Krsna dug the well with the vibration of His flute in response to the gopis’ desire to bathe at the Triveni-sangama — the confluence of the Ganges, Yamuna, and Sarasvati rivers.

The waters of these three rivers responded to Krsna’s call and entered the well. Thus all those who bathe there, or even touch the soil surrounding the well, get the benefit of having bathed in the Triveni.

In truth, the auspicious waters of Triveni-kupa were made more transcendentally potent by being touched by Sri Krsna’s Vrndavana pastimes and blessed by the nectar of the gopis’ love for Him.

To commemorate Lord Balarama’s pastimes here, Narayana Bhatta Gosvami established a temple of Dauji beside Triveni-kupa. The Gosvami’s samadhi is situated on the other side of the well.

On the far side of Unchagaon is a confidential pastime place called Deha-kunda. Here the gopis unconditionally gave themselves body (deha) and soul to Sri Krsna, the exclusive beloved of their hearts.

Another time, Krsna and the gopis were sporting on the banks of this lake while a marriage festival was going on nearby. A poor brahmana approached and spoke to them in a voice filled with distress.

“Fortunate is that wealthy merchant who can give his daughter to a good man and celebrate the happy occasion with such a grand festival. But alas, I cannot give my daughter such happiness!

“My daughter is of marriageable age, but I have no money. I cannot offer her to a suitable husband.” His eyes full of tears, the impoverished brahmana implored Krsna, “Dear sir, please help me!”

In all circumstances, Krsna is equally kind towards brahmanas and cows, yet in this secluded place He had neither gold, nor jewels, nor even cows to bestow upon this poor vipra. What could He do?

Krsna looked to the gopis for inspiration. His eyes fell on Srimati Radharani and with a faltering voice, He said, “O saintly brahmana ! My entire wealth and fortune is Sri Radha. Please accept Her!”

Krsna’s words fell like lightning bolts on the heads of Radha, the gopis, and the brahmana. At the words “Accept Her!” Radha’s eyes filled with tears, Her mind reeled, and She swooned.

“No! No!” the gopis cried, surrounding Radharani. The brahmana held his head and wailed, “I cannot marry off one daughter. What will I do with two? You have simply added to my miseries.”

Krsna thought, “Without gold, even Radha — the greatest treasure— will be a burden for this brahmana.” He then tossed His rope over a branch and created a seat by tying a stick to one end.

On the other end He hung a cow blanket tied up like a sack. Then, placing Radha on the seat, He proceeded to fill the sack with His and the gopis’ jewellery until the two sides were equally balanced.

Krsna considered that giving Radha’s weight in gold would fulfil His vow to give Her in charity, so He dragged the treasure to the brahmana’s feet and said, “Take this gold, equal in weight to Radha.”

The brahmana was weak and emaciated because of poverty, but due to excitement over his new-found wealth and his contact with Sri Krsna, the source of strength, he easily shouldered his daksina.

The brahmana repeatedly thanked and blessed both Krsna and the gopis and then happily departed for his village, visions of his daughter’s opulent marriage festival dancing in his mind.

That fortunate brahmana was unable to understand that his true wealth was not in a sack of gold but in his encounter with Krsna and the gopis — an encounter rarely granted, even to great yogis.

But the impression of that meeting with the Lord remained in the brahmana's heart. With time that remembrance quelled his material attachments and aroused a taste for loving devotional service.

One day, not unlike little Dhruva, the brahmana lamented that upon achieving the perfection of life — seeing Krsna face to face — he had asked for the trivial boon of material wealth.

Following this incident, the gopis installed a deity of Mahadeva called Venisankara on the banks of Deha-kunda. Bathing there and seeing Lord Siva surpasses bathing at the confluence at Prayaga.

**\* \* \* \***

From Gendokhara I began my parikrama of Varsana. First I stopped at the lake Pili-pokhara. Named after the pilu trees on its banks, it was once turned yellow by the turmeric from Radha’s lotus hands.

Because these trees bear fruit year round, children come here to snack on pilu. During Their childhood pastimes, the divine couple would do the same as a pretext to enjoy each other’s company.

How Radha’s touch changed the waters of Pili-pokhara, and how those waters affected Krsna when He bathed there, are both described in the appendix entitled “The Pastime of Pili-pokhara.”

To the northeast, but still under the shelter of Varsana Hill, is one of Nanda Maharaja’s granaries, Bhadavali, which is surrounded by lush pastures where the cows of King Vrsabhanu graze.

And further east of Pili-pokhara is Kirti-kunda, famous as the bathing place of Sri Radha’s mother, Kirtida. Any creature that dies upon the banks or within the waters of Kirti-kunda returns to Goloka.

Like Yasoda, the name Kirtida means “that which distributes fame.” The most famous person is Yasoda’s son, Sri Krsna, but the potency that grants universal fame to Krsna is Kirtida’s daughter, Sri Radha.

Thus the two queens Kirtida and Yasoda, by playing their roles as mothers to Radha and Krsna respectively, give to the world “fame personified” (kirti) and the “most famous person” (yasas).

Walking a little further to the eastern border of Varsana, I came upon Bhanu-sarovara, also known as Vrsabhanu-kunda. In the expansive waters of this lake, Maharaja Vrsabhanu takes his morning bath.

Some authorities say that Srimati Radharani appeared in Raval, others say that She appeared in Varsana from a lotus within the waters of this very lake. Both views are no doubt correct.

During Krsna’s most recent advent on earth, Radha appeared in Raval. In a previous day of Brahma, however, King Vrsabhanu found Her one morning as She was playing within a lotus in Bhanu-kunda.

Just as Nanda Maharaja took his clan from Gokula to Chatikara, and from there to Nandagrama, King Vrsabhanu moved his followers from Raval to Vasati, and from there to Varsana.

And just as Krsna successively displayed His childhood, boyhood, and youth in those three kingdoms of Nandaraja, Radha enjoyed Her childhood in Raval, girlhood in Vasati, and Her youth in Varsana.

In the material world, as in Goloka, Krsna originally, eternally enjoys pastimes as a youth, manifesting also as a child and as a boy to please His parents and His friends through further pastimes.

This principle holds true for Krsna’s associates also; while in Her youth Radha lived in Varsana, then later in Yavata where She manifests Her original pastimes, features, and qualities.

Bhanu-sarovara is surrounded on all sides by trees that not only give shade to visitors but also screen one part of the lake from the other, enabling both men and women to bathe here in privacy.

Sometimes, Sri Radha and Her friends bathe on one side of the lake, while Krsna and His friends bathe on the other. From there Krsna swims secretly underwater to where the gopis are.

There, unseen by the elders also present, Krsna startles Srimati Radharani or another gopi by grabbing her feet before swimming back to the company of His amused cowherd friends.

Sometimes Sri Radha and Her gopi friends bathe alone in the pure waters of Vrsabhanu-kunda. At other times, when no one else but Radharani and the gopis are there, Krsna appears.

Intoxicated by Their youth, Radha and Krsna enjoy a variety of water sports, like playing hide-and-seek amongst the clusters of blue and golden lotus flowers that decorate the surface of the lake.

Bhanu-sarovara is a picture of tranquility in which flowers of many colours reflect the variety of the divine couple’s water sports, and the placid rippling waves reflect the happiness of Their carefree youth.

When the Deities of Larili-Lal—first established by Vajranabha — were under threat, They took shelter in the waters of Bhanu-sarovara and were later discovered and reinstalled in Their temple.

For the protection of his subjects, King Vrsabhanu installed the deity of Vrajesvara Mahadeva on a bank of this lake. Although there have been efforts made to move the Lord, he refuses to budge.

He is offered this prayer, vrajesvaraya te tubhyam maharudraya te namah / vrajaukasam sivarthaya namas te sivarupine: “Obeisances to Vrajesvara, Maharudra, who resides here in his auspicious form.”

All glories to Bhanu-kunda, which appears as the liquid form of King Vrsabhanu’s love for his eternal daughter, a girl so mysterious, so sublime, that even the Vedas hesitate to speak Her name.

Chanting the holy names of Krsna, I walked in the shadow of Vrsabhanu-pura, along Varsana’s parikrama path, until the village of Karala, Lalita-devi’s birthplace, came into view.

Here Krsna repeatedly enjoys rasa dancing, during which the gopis make different graceful gestures with their hands. Because of this waving (hilna) of hands (kara), this place is also called Karhela.

Candravali’s grandmother lives here, so Candravali regularly visits Karala with her husband Govardhana Malla. And because this is Lalita’s birthplace, Lalita also visits here when Radha visits Varsana.

A rasa-mandala pays tribute to Krsna and the cowherd girls having danced there. On the banks of a tranquil lake called Lalita-kunda sits a temple devoted to the worship of Lalita and Krsna.

Just as all married women regularly visit their parents and their parental home, Srimati Radharani, after Her marriage to Abhimanyu, would travel from Her in-laws’ home in Yavata to visit Varsana.

Along with Her close friends, Srimati Radharani comes to Varsana for the spring festival, Her own birthday, and Durga-puja. Sometimes She stays for a few days, sometimes for a few months.

When Radha visits Her parents, Her girlfriends also visit theirs, and because the gopis are out from under the watchful eyes of their inlaws, they are freer to move about and meet Krsna.

These opportunities resemble those the gopis had to meet Krsna when the elder residents of Vraja were on pilgrimage to the holy places. At those times Krsna dressed Himself in a variety of feminine disguises.

For instance, in the guise of a woman learned in astrology, yoga, mantra, and tantra, Krsna once appeared on the footpath before Radharani and engaged Her in a lively banter.

Radha saw through Krsna’s disguise, but to humour Him She asked, “O knower of all things! What place of residence gives supreme bliss?” To which Krsna replied, “The cowherd pastures of Krsna.”

Radha continued, “What is the essence of life for all beings?” Krsna replied, “Singing songs about Krsna.” Radha: “What bestows incomparable auspiciousness?” Krsna: “The desire to obtain Krsna.”

Radha: “What is the most enjoyable thing?” Krsna: “The beauty of Krsna.” Radha: “What is the ultimate attainment in this creation?” Krsna: “The company of Sri Krsna.”

On another occasion, Krsna appeared at Radha’s doorstep disguised as a female doctor. In a melodious voice that hypnotised the gopis, He offered a complimentary consultation for Varsana’s princess.

Radha happened to be suffering from a high fever caused by the anguish of separation from Krsna, and so the doctor was led to Her inner chambers where He greeted His patient by touching Her feet.

The doctor was told that Radha was suffering from a lover’s heart-ache, and so He proceeded to take Her pulse. Meanwhile, He sent the gopis to bring items like damp towels and sandal paste.

While the gopis were scurrying about, attending to their errands, they heard Radha call out in alarm, “O friends! This doctor should be taking My pulse to prescribe medicine for My love-torn heart.

“But alas! Not only is she touching My wrist, she is touching all My other limbs as well.” The gopis quickly rushed in to Radha’s room to find their mistress completely cured by the new doctor’s treatment.

All glories to Sri Krsna, who ingeniously overcomes every obstacle His own pastime potency places between Him and His meeting with Radha. In this way His cleverness showers the world with bliss.

**\* \* \* \***

Walking west a short distance, I came to where the four-peaked hill of Brahma joins another mountain, Visnu-parvata, to form at that juncture a narrow pass everyone calls Sankari-khor.

Atop the hill to the north is Mora-kutira, and on the hill to the south is Vilasa-garh. These two pastime places are like the two parts of a jewelled chest that houses the treasure of dana-lila.

In dana-lila, Krsna enjoys extracting tolls from the gopis in many parts of Vraja, including Dana-gati at Govardhana, Dana-garh at Varsana, and Sankari-khor between Varsana and Visnu-parvata.

Sankari-khor is the passage between the villages of Varsana and Citrasali, the route Radha and Her friends often take—instead of the shorter, easterly path — to descend from Vrsabhanu-pura.

Sri Radha’s purpose in taking this circuitous route is to give Krsna ample opportunity to fulfil His unlimited desires by meeting the gopis in the secluded gardens, vales, and hills of Brahma-giri.

The pass at Sankari-khor is so narrow that only one person can walk through at a time. Here Krsna sets up His tollgate to obstruct the gopis as they carry an assortment of milk products to Varsana village.

On one such occasion Krsna stood just above His tollbooth on Visnu-parvata. When the gopis went by, carrying pots of milk, He called out to them in an authoritative voice, filling Radha with rapture.

“O bold girls! From where did you get such audacity? You just ignored My toll station! You know better than to pass through it without paying the proper tax! Who do you think you are?”

The mere sight of Krsna made Radha thrill with goosebumps and shivers of emotion, but She carefully concealed Her ecstatic symptoms and walked on, completely ignoring Krsna’s challenge.

Not to be outdone, Krsna leapt across the gully, landed just behind Radha on the path, and before She could react, knocked the pot of milk off Her head, spilling its contents onto the ground.

Radharani’s and Krsna’s intense loving affection melted the rock upon which They stood. Thus even today Their footprints, along with stains from the spilled milk, can be seen on that rock.

This kind of loving exchange takes place repeatedly at Sankari-khor: the gopis carry milk, butter, and yogurt on their heads, and the cowherd boys haughtily demand a share of their goods.

And if the gopis would still refuse to pay the tax, even after a heated debate, Krsna would lead the gopas in plundering their goods, enjoying the spoils unabashed even as the girls looked on.

One day the gopis met to plan their retaliation against Krsna’s daily harassment, never once considering that they could avoid it simply by taking the public road from Vrsabhanu-pura to Varsana.

The girls hatched a plan that they would carry out a few days after Srimati Radharani’s birthday celebration, a plan kept so secret that neither Krsna nor His friends would ever suspect anything.

Early on the assigned morning, Lalita directed hundreds of gopis to hide in the caves and lay in wait in the groves on both sides of the narrow pathway, while Radha and a few gopis walked along as usual.

And as usual, Krsna and His friends harassed Radharani’s entourage, plundered their wares, and then gorged themselves on the spoils, unaware that they had fallen headfirst into a trap.

As the boys gloated over their own cleverness, Radhika signalled to the gopis in hiding, and the hills were suddenly swarming with cowherd girls shouting, “Catch them!” and “Get them!”

At first the boys froze, stolen yogurt dripping from their shaking fingers, but when Subala yelled, “Run for your lives!” they jumped to their feet and scattered in all directions.

But there was no escape for Krsna and His friends. Wherever they ran, scores of gopis blocked their way, captured them, tied their hands with flower garlands, and forcibly dragged them back to the tollbooth.

Then the gopis tied the boys’ sikhas to tree branches, and while some girls ridiculed them, others lovingly slapped the boys’ cheeks until their faces glowed red from both embarrassment and pain.

As Lalita repeatedly slapped Madhumangala demanding, “Will you continue to harass innocent girls, or will you stop this nonsense!" Madhu begged for mercy, pleading his case with palms joined.

“Spare me! Spare me! I was famished, and being a simple brahmana boy I fell under the influence of the unruly son of Vraja’s king. But if you release me I shall never associate with Him again. I promise!”

Sankari-khor swarmed with groups of gopis, each crowded around a single captive gopa. It resounded with the irate voices of gopi-interrogators and the chastened pleas of their gopa-prisoners.

The gopis’ anger and superior numbers were forces so overwhelming that the captive cowherd boys could do nothing but submit to the humiliation that Radharani’s friends subjected them to.

Krsna was not to be spared the retribution meted out to His chums; in fact, as their ringleader, special punishment was reserved for Him, to be carried out personally by Radha, Visakha, and their helpers.

Krsna’s hands were bound to a small tree and His sikha to a branch, His upraised, unprotected face a beautiful target for the gopis’ reddish palms as they all took turns slapping Him.

The cowherd girls then dressed Krsna in a blouse, skirt, and veil, rubbed sindura in the part of His hair, decorated His arms with bangles, and placed anklets on His feet.

Then, while Krsna was still bound to the tree, Srimati Radharani balanced a pot of yogurt on His head, and to the delight of Her friends, covered the lower part of His face with the veil.

Then all the cowherd girls abandoned their gopa prisoners and converged en masse around Hari, taunting Him with words like, “Pretty girl! Pay us the tax for the yogurt You are carrying.”

The gopis ridiculed Krsna until Lalita-devi’s voice rang out over the din of *gopi*-hilarity. “Out of the way!” Lalita commanded. “This Syama-devi is about to get a taste of Her own medicine.”

Standing at the same place on Visnu-parvata where Krsna usually stood, Lalita picked up a stone. Taking careful aim she threw it at the pot of yogurt precariously balanced on Krsna’s head.

The pot exploded with a clang, and its contents flowed down Krsna’s face, shoulders, and chest, the sight of which sent the gopis into hysterics as they laughed, clapped, and even danced around.

Krsna tried to hang His head in shame, but Lalita-devi held His chin firmly in her hand and shook His head from side to side. She asked, “Will you ever demand tax on our yogurt again?”

Krsna murmured a lacklustre and barely audible no. Unconvinced, Lalita continued to shake His head and said, “Hold Your ears with Your hands and take a vow: ‘I will never again tax the gopis’ yogurt.’”

Seeing no way to escape the embarrassment into which He had so deeply sunk, Krsna repeated the vow three times, after which Lalita-devi and the cowherd girls freed Him and His friends.

In light of the eternal nature of His pastimes, how Krsna can keep an infallible vow to His devotees — such as this vow made at Sankari-khor— can only be resolved by His inconceivable potencies.

Yet to this very day, this enchanting pastime called burhi-lila and the pronouncement of Krsna’s vow are re-enacted annually by local villagers and pilgrims at Sankari-khor a few days after Radhastami.

Passing through Sankari-khor I paid my respects to Mayura-vana, a forest covering the hill to my right, up to a clearing on the peak that devotees call Mayura-kutira or Mora-garh.

The most prominent pastime place here is a dance platform marking the place where Radha and Krsna once danced as a peahen and a peacock, along with the resident peahens and peacocks of the area.

One year during the rainy season, when peacocks are especially lively, Radha and Krsna climbed to the top of this hill, accompanied by some gopis, and were greeted by clouds and peacocks alike.

The beauty of Radha and Krsna appeared like a rain cloud run aground, flashing lightning here and there. They surpassed, by a thousand times, the beauty of the clouds and lightning in the sky.

Outdone by Radha’s and Krsna’s unrivalled splendour, the rain clouds and lightning felt embarrassed by the divine couple’s display of beauty. Forced to search for a place to hide, they could find none.

Desperate, the clouds cried and turned pale. They were in ecstasy to shelter Radha and Krsna with their shade, cool Them with their tears, and glorify Them with their thunder.

The peacocks were already thrilled by this vision of Radha and Krsna, but when the clouds began to rumble, they could no longer contain themselves, and so they began to dance, as peacocks are wont to do.

Surrounded on all sides by a pride of blissfully dancing peacocks and spurred on by the rumbling clouds, Radha’s and Krsna’s own ecstasy overflowed, and They also began to dance.

The divine couple performs this peacock dance in various places, such as Govardhana, Mayura, and Varsana, just as They perform the rasa dance in many places throughout the forests of Vrndavana.

And just as the gopis play fitting music to accompany Radha’s and Krsna’s rasa dance, they accompany the divine couple’s peacock-dance with appropriate songs in the mallara and megha ragas.

It is said that Srimati Radharani once requested a blind Vaisnava residing in a cottage upon Mora-garh to paint a picture of Her dancing with Her beloved amongst the peacocks of Mayura-vana.

At first the Vaisnava humbly protested: “How can a blind man paint a picture?” But Srimati Radharani knew that he had the spiritual vision to be able to see Their pastimes from within.

She replied, “Have faith in My words. To inspire all practising devotees now and in the future, the same potency that guides your inner meditation will also guide your hands.”

By the divine mercy of Sri Radha, not only was the humble devotee able to beautifully portray the peacock-dance of the divine couple, but his eyesight was restored — he could see what he had painted!

At Mora-garh Krsna also had pastimes with His gopa friends. Once the boys climbed this hill to revel in the beauty of the surrounding area. Exhausted from the climb, they became very hungry.

In response to His friends’ insistent requests, Krsna made baskets of laddus appear. As those tasty apparitions materialised, the gopas ran from one basket to the other greedily devouring their contents.

It was not long before the boys had eaten their fill, but baskets of sweets kept popping out of thin air. In their merriment the gopas began to toss laddus at each other with gleeful abandon.

When the boys were finally exhausted by their play and all of Mora-garh was littered with broken sweets, the gopas lay in the shade of the trees and lovingly joked about their wonderful friend Gopala.

Across from Mora-garh is the small mountain Visnu-parvata, and the flower garden Vilasa-garh, where, just prior to Her youth, Srimati Radharani and Her girlfriends would frolic (vilasa).

On such occasions, Krsna would sometimes arrive uninvited and on some pretext lead Radha away in order to enjoy the innocence of Her budding youth in a solitary, flower-filled tamala-kunja.

It is said that once when Krsna tried to see Radha, Her friends would not allow it. As the *gopis* stood blocking His path, a strong wind stirred up a dust storm, clouding the entire Varsana area.

The air became so thick with sand that the *gopis* had to cover their eyes with their hands, giving Krsna the ideal opportunity to slip past them and enter Radharani’s kunja to embrace Her.

On other occasions, unfortunate Krsna waits all day for Her to come, but Sri Radha never arrives. Because of these daytime vigils at Vilasa-garh it is also known as one of His sitting places (Krsna-baithaka).

All glories to Sri Vraja-dhama, the everlasting playground where Sri Krsna relishes pastimes with the Vraja-vasis in the boundless bliss of spontaneous reciprocal love, vraja-bhava.

**\* \* \* \***

The village of Ciksoli lies on the far side of Sankari-khor. As the birthplace of the gopi Citra, it was originally called Citrasali. But at one time it was also called Citrasala — Radha’s picture gallery.

In a secluded part of this village, at the edge of Gahvaravana (“dense forest”) is a beautiful tamala-kunja where Radha waited for Krsna while He mistakenly waited for Her at Visnu-parvata.

Some clever gopis ascertained that Krsna had gone to the wrong meeting place. After a lengthy search they found Him, and undetected by the villagers, brought Him to the tamala-kunja.

Radha’s friends were extremely eager for Krsna to see the brightly colourful (citra) way in which Srimati Radharani had dressed, decorated, and ornamented Herself, solely for His pleasure.

Thus, at sunset, when Sri Krsna finally entered the tamala-kunja and beheld Radha’s exquisite beauty, He stopped in His tracks, able only to stare at Her and say, “Amazing (citra)! Simply amazing!”

On another occasion at Ciksoli, Radharani was simultaneously afflicted by three different types of attachment to Krsna, only to be delivered from Her loving bewilderment by Lalita-devi.

Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu heard this pastime when Srila Rupa Gosvami introduced his drama Vidagdha-madhava to an assembly of senior Vaisnavas, including Ramananda Raya, at Jagannatha Puri.

Dressed in red garments, despondent Radha had hidden Herself in a grove of vetasi trees. Lalita and Visakha found Her lamenting and were heartbroken to see their friend’s pitiful condition.

When Visakha-devi enquired from Radha about the cause of Her sorrow, Radhika reproached Her friend, saying indignantly, “Aren’t you ashamed to question Me after having caused My misery?”

Visakha and Lalita were taken aback by Radha’s aggressive tone. Visakha tried to defend herself, but Radha replied, “Crazy girl! You drew a picture of a boy who turned out to be a mystic and a rake.

“When I was alone, gazing at your drawing, that beautiful boy came out of the portrait and ignited an inferno in My heart with His smile and touch. And now you want to plead your innocence? Liar!”

Lalita asked, “Did this happen in a dream?” Radha replied, “Whether I was dreaming or awake — I don’t know. All I know is that My burning heart now longs for that handsome boy’s touch.”

Overcome by longing, Radha drifted off into the realm of Her own thoughts, murmuring to Herself, “Wicked heart! Monkey! Are you not ashamed to love three persons simultaneously?

“You love some boy whose name is Krsna, you also love a cowherd who plays the flute, and now you love this boy in the picture. O heart! When I kill My body, only then will I be free of you.”

Radha’s two friends glanced at each other, eyebrows raised. Trying to make light of Radha’s infatuation, Lalita joked, “Friend! It is springtime, none of us are immune from the attacks of Kamadeva.”

Unmoved by Lalita’s attempt to levity, mortified by Her own predicament, Radha revealed Her desire to die, “Let the sandalwood breezes play, the artful cuckoos sing, the humming bees fly about.

“These envoys of Cupid will only move Me to hasten an end to My troubles.” Hearing this, Lalita and Visakha began to weep saying, “We cannot understand Your sufferings, or Your heart’s longings.”

Immersed in Her dilemma Radha replied, “Friends! The palpitations My heart is going through are extremely difficult to cure. Even if medical treatment were applied, it would only end in defamation.

“If you truly want to cure Me,” continued Srimati Radharani, pointing to a nearby creeper, “then wrap this flowering vine about My neck like a noose and hang Me!”

Lalita and Visakha covered their ears in horror at Radharani’s reply. As tears burst from their eyes, they sobbed, “If you continue to say such horrible things, we will be the ones to die.”

Radha looked at Her friends with affection but continued to speak in an unsettlingly dispassionate tone. “Only because you are unaware of what truly lurks in My heart do you expect My life to continue.”

Lalita-devi enquired cautiously, “Have You divulged everything to us?” Srimati Radharani lowered Her head and answered, “No, I have not. My great shame prevents Me from doing so.”

With great compassion Lalita replied in a comforting voice, “We can see how spotless and pure You are.” Visakha-devi added, “How, then, can there be anything shameful about You?”

Radha sighed in resignation and began, “Let Me disclose the truth of the matter, and then you will understand My dilemma. Since I first heard the name of a person called Krsna, I have lost all good sense.

“Then, after I heard the flute-song played by some unknown cowherd, an intense madness arose in My heart. And now that I have seen the boy in this picture, My mind has become attached to Him as well.

“I am condemned, for I have become simultaneously attached to three persons. Since such wantonness is unpardonable for a chaste woman, it would be better for Me to die and be freed from My sins.”

Lalita’s face lit up with a smile. Wiping the tears from her own cheeks, she said happily, “O friend! Even the least reputable of the beautiful girls of Gokula could never love any man other than Krsna.

“How then could You, the dazzling tiara of Vraja’s gopis, be in love with three different men? Listen! The three men — the one named “Krsna,” the flute-player, and the boy in the picture — are the same.

“Simply, Your heart is drawn to three features of Krsna: His name, pastimes, and form. Thus Your love is supremely chaste, and You may now give up Your shame, as well as Your plans to end Your own life.”

Hope now dawned in Radha’s eyes. She looked up at Lalita and then Visakha. Not knowing how to hide the smile blooming upon Her lips, She again looked down at the ground and breathed a sigh of relief.

Srimati Radharani thought to Herself, “Heart, be peaceful! Be still! The man You seek to attain is none other than the refuge of all beings, the controller of all worlds, and the Soul of all souls.

“And yet,” Radha continued to think, “What good am I? Of what use are My silken cloth, scented veil, golden ornaments, and perfumed limbs if the one for whom they are meant does not want them?”

Lalita and Visakha rephrased Radha’s thoughts out loud, “Even if a flowering creeper anoints all directions with waves of fragrance, it lives in vain without the honey bee to drink its nectar.”

Sri Radha exchanged a confidential glance with Her friends. Alone, She would be unable to realise Her fervent quest, but by the aid of devoted friends like these, Her desire could be realised — and it was.

**\* \* \* \***

Citra-devi is one of Srimati Radharani's closest friends, and her fortunate parents are Carcika-devi and Catura. After marrying Pithara, like the other young gopis, Citra also moved to Yavata.

Citra’s complexion is golden with a soft saffron glow, and her clothes are the colour of crystal. Born almost a month after Radha, she is the most mild-mannered amongst Radha’s eight closest girlfriends.
