Citra-devi is a master of both astrological and botanical sciences. From her father, Catura, she learned many languages and the art of composing riddles and speaking with double meanings.

Once, when Krsna and the gopis were marvelling at the natural beauty of Vraja’s gardens, Citra pointed to a swarm of bees flocking to a particular bandhujiva flower within an entire cluster and said:

“These bees have the same lustre as Krsna, they sing on the fifth note, like His flute, and they collect the honey of only one of many flowers, just as Krsna favours the love of only one of many gopis — Sri Radha.”

The hidden meaning of Citra’s statement was this: Like the bees, the cowherd girls have abandoned their husbands to accept the essence of life (prema) from the supreme husband (Krsna).

Once, following a duel of words between the divine couple, Krsna posed a riddle about Citra. Krsna: “Radha! Your qualities eclipse those of the goddess of fortune, thus she has entered Your form.”

Radha: “But Laksmi is Your wife...” Krsna: “...And therefore You are that Sri.” Radha dismissed Him with a wave of Her hand. Radha: “How can a gopi become the goddess of fortune?”

Krsna: “If I am the husband of the gopis, and I am the husband of Laksmi, then you must all be goddesses of fortune.” Radha: “And She gave up Her divine powers just to be a human girl?”

Krsna: “The goddess of fortune, attracted to the sweet simplicity of My form as a cowherd boy, assumed Your human-like form just to become My wife. Isn’t that Her prerogative?”

Radha and Krsna continued posing riddles to each other until Krsna finally asked, “Who is expert in making ornaments and pictures (citra), is very mild, does not tolerate pride, and gives Us joy?”

Rising slightly from Her seat, Radha happily answered, “Citra!” Radha and Krsna cast loving glances at Their beloved girlfriend who, true to character, shyly lowered her head without saying a word.

Srimati Radharani is partial to Citra-devi for her artistry. Citra uses natural pigments to paint lifelike pictures of Krsna’s pastimes on silk, and also draws beautiful designs upon Krsna’s cloud-like body.

These body paintings are like carefully woven nets spread out surreptitiously by Cupid, the relentless fowler, to capture Sri Radhika’s restless wagtail eyes.

In addition to being a gifted artist, Citra-devi is a skilled gourmet who can understand the taste of foods merely by glancing at them and, like all the gopis, she is an accomplished musician.

Her favourite instrument is the vina, but she can also play music by striking pots filled with different amounts of water. And sometimes she recites mantras in perfect rhythm with Krsna’s dancing.

Her specialty, though, is gardening. Eight maidservants, headed by Rasalika, help Citra grow herbs and medicinal creepers. From that garden produce she makes various kinds of nectarean beverages.

Mother Yasoda knows about Citra’s magic potions, and so she has Citra come along with Radharani every morning to prepare these drinks and serve them at breakfast to Krsna and His friends.

Although Citra is mild-mannered, sometimes she becomes annoyed, even with Krsna. On one such occasion, she urged Radha not to be so tolerant of Hari, whose deceitful antics had upset Citra’s friend.

Citra said, “My friend with a crooked heart! O Radha! You cast loving glances at Krsna and pretend to glorify Him. Give up this politesse and show Your true feelings. Show Him Your anger!’’

On other occasions, Citra displays excessive tolerance for Krsna’s double-dealings. This open-heartedness at times provokes a stern rebuke from the likes of Lalita-devi:

Once Krsna failed to arrive for a scheduled meeting with Radha. Greatly distressed, She was lamenting to Her friends when Citra began to glorify Him, further infuriating an already irate Lalita.

“Fool!” snapped Lalita, “Have you become hypnotised by the mantras of Krsna — that king of rogues and cheaters? Can’t you see how He tortures Radha? And you glorify Him with flowery words? Be quiet!”

To mundane eyes, the gopis’ exchanges may reflect the competitive and even jealous nature of female dealings, but to devotional eyes these are delightful varieties in the exchanges of divine love.

Gopis like Citra are Krsna’s eternal companions. Their every word and deed enrich His Vrndavana pastimes, especially His romantic pursuits with Radha, who is the treasure of both the gopis and Krsna.

One day, as Radha and Citra strolled through Vrndavana forest, a creeper rubbed against Radharani’s neck, breaking Her necklace. It was Krsna’s favourite, and so She called out to Citra:

“Friend! Come help Me pick up these scattered pearls.” As they both collected the beads, Srimati Radharani saw Krsna’s flute lying beneath the foliage on a pathway leading up Govardhana Hill.

Radha exclaimed, “Pretty-faced friend! Look! It appears Krsna has accidentally dropped His flute while passing this way. Good fortune has smiled upon us. Quick! Let’s pick it up and hide it.”

All glories to Citra-devi! Her heart overflows with a variety (citra) of compassionate moods. She always dresses Srimati Radharani in clothes that perfectly suit Radhika’s desire to please Krsna.

**\* \* \* \***

From my vantage point at Ciksoli in New Vraja-dhama, I offered respects to six holy places that stand west of Varsana: Kamai, Dabhararo, Surya-kunda, Mukta-kunda, Dohani-kunda, and Rankoli.

Kamai, the birthplace of Visakha-devi, can be found here just across the parikrama path from Chatikara. Simply by remembering this village or by saying its name, a devotee receives the mercy of Radha.

O gentle Vaisnavas! Let us take shelter of Visakha, who was born at the very same moment as Radharani, and whose features and qualities are also nearly identical to the princess of Varsana.

Visakha’s graceful limbs, which glisten like lightning, are draped in exquisite cloth embroidered with many stars. Overflowing with love for Radha and Krsna, she maintains Their clothing and ornaments.

Dasa Gosvami prays, *ksanam api tava sangam na tyajed eva devi tvam asi samavayastvan narma-bhumir yad asyah / iti sumukhi visakhe darsayitva mad-isam mama viraha-hatayah prana-raksam kurusva*:

“O Visakha! O beautiful girl, realm of Radha’s playful, joking pastimes! Because you are the same age as Radha, She will not leave your company for even a moment. I offer my obeisances unto you.”

On the banks of Ravar-kunda, Visakha and her friends would make statues of Krsna from mud and decorate them with flowers and twigs. Today, a temple of Gopala and a temple of Dauji stand at that site.

North of Kamai is Dabhararo, said to be the birthplace of either Rangadevi or Tungavidya. In either case, because it is the home of one of Radha’s dear friends, Radha comes here to play with Her dolls.

One day while Krsna was herding His father’s cows in the area of Varsana, Subala told Him that Srimati Radharani was playing nearby with Her friends, in the place now called Dabhararo.

Hari followed His friend to a cluster of trees on the outskirts of the village, from where He could see the young gopis playing ball, their dresses billowing in the wind, their laughter warming the sunshine.

The milkmaids of Vrndavana looked like carefree goddesses of fortune, but one of them, Srimati Radharani, stood out like a sparkling diamond set amongst other valuable gems.

When Krsna saw Radha’s lithesome movements and heard Her honeyed laughter, His eyes flooded (dubu) with tears. From that day on, the locals have called their village Dabhararo.

Between Dabhararo and Ciksoli is a lake known as Surya-kunda. On the shores of this lake Radha and Her friends regularly worship the sun god with offerings of flowers and incense.

Radha worships Surya at various places around Vraja. Thus at Chatravana, Govardhana, Kotaravana, Nandagrama, Satoya, Sasculi, and Chota-bharana, lakes can be found that bear the sun god’s name.

Further north from Dabhararo is Mukta-kunda, also known as Ratna-kunda. The famous book Mukta-carita elaborately describes this lake’s glories. Here is but a brief account of the pastimes recorded therein:

One day, near Radha-kunda, the gopis offended Krsna by not granting His request to give Him a few pearl necklaces with which to decorate His favourite cows for the upcoming Dipavali festival.

To teach the gopis a lesson, Krsna sowed an entire field with pearls at Moti-kunda near Nandagrama. Within a few days He was harvesting baskets of beautiful pearls and decorating His cows with them.

The gopis thought they could also increase their families’ wealth by following in Krsna’s footsteps, and so they took all the pearls from their homes and planted them at Mukta-kunda near Varsana.

But Krsna’s pastime potency had something different in mind for the gopis. Instead of harvesting beautiful pearl plants as expected, the gopis found their garden filled with nothing but thorn bushes.

When Radha and Her friends met at Mukta-kunda, they realised that they had not only failed to increase their families’ wealth, but because their pearls were now underground, what wealth they had was lost.

After consulting amongst themselves, the cowherd girls reluctantly concluded that the only way they could regain their lost wealth was to ask their rival, Krsna, for a portion of His newfound riches.

The bartering for pearls between the gopis and Krsna is truly nectar for the ears of all Vaisnavas. And by the grace of the holy dhama, those negotiations at Moti-kunda can still be heard today.

East of Mukta-kunda is Dohani-kunda, after which the entire area is named. There are the barns and corrals of King Vrsabhanu, where he keeps and waters his vast herd of cows and bulls.

Srimati Radharani often goes there with Her girlfriends to watch the cows being milked, and sometimes — because they are all cowherd girls — they also milk the cows themselves.

On one such occasion, while Radha was milking a cow, Krsna appeared on the scene and teased, “Friend! You are the daughter of a cowherd king, and You don’t even know how to properly milk a cow?”

Radha looked up at Hari, unable to hide the fire of affection in Her eyes, and replied, “You are the son of a cowherd king. If You are so adept, then why don’t You show Me how to do it properly?”

Krsna pulled up another stool and sat down beside Radharani, elated to be so close to His sweetheart. And as He began to milk the cow, He said, “Just watch carefully, and do as I do.”

Srimati Radharani watched intently as Krsna worked two of the cow’s teats. She followed His every move, all the while wondering what it was that He was doing differently than Her.

Suddenly, just as She was about to tell Him that His milking technique was no better than Hers, Krsna squirted Her in the face with streams of milk directly from the cow’s udder.

Flabbergasted, Radharani wiped the milk from Her face, prepared to berate Krsna. But when She saw the sweetness of His smiling face, Her anger melted, and She could only look at Him shyly.

Finally, I came to Rankoli. On the nights when the gopis visit their parents in and around Varsana, Krsna calls them to this place with His flute to enjoy rasa dancing. Thus it is also called Rasoli.

Following their dancing, Krsna and the gopis bathed in a lake there called Rasa-kunda and, in the peaceful silence of the night, they all enjoyed a picnic underneath a banyan tree on the banks of the lake.

Some authorities say that Rankoli, not Dabhararo, is the village of Tungavidya. Others say that it is the birthplace of Indulekha. Devotees offer respects to both gopis, for they are very dear to Radha.

Offering my obeisances to all of Radha’s eight dearmost friends, I entered the dense, conch-shaped forest of Gahvaravana, which covers Varsana’s western side, including Dana-, Mana-, and Mora-garhs.

**\* \* \* \***

Cahvaravana appears as mysterious as the Vedas; its closely-knit trees as hidden as Vedic mantras; its caves as enigmatic as *Vedanta*'s codes; the songs of its birds as oblique as the purports of the Upanisads.

The sruti section of the Vedic literature describes in cryptic language the elusive truth, a truth that remains a vague mystery to all but the purest devotee philosophers like Lord Brahma or Narada Muni.

That Absolute Truth is actually a rowdy cowherd boy, who, surrounded by equally rowdy cowherd friends, tends His cows in the pastures of Vrndavana and dallies in groves with beautiful gopis.

The goal of human life is no secret in Vraja. There the wind passing through the trees hums, “All Vedic knowledge searches after Him!” and the birds perched on branches sing, “You are His.”

The cows in the pastures low, “He is the Lord and controller of everyone,” and the peacocks that dance to the rumbling clouds cry their own song, “The Supreme is unlimited truth and knowledge.”

The meanings of these statements may bewilder Mayavadi philosophers, but they are self-evident truths to Vaisnavas who walk along the path through Gahvaravana leading to Gahvara-kunda.

Because Krsna enacts so many pastimes upon its banks, Gahvara-kunda is also called Krsna-kunda, and because its waters are cool even in the summer months it is also called Sitala-kunda.

Madhumangala, highly offended at being tied to a tree by his sikha in the burhi-lila at Sankari-khor, demanded that the cowherd girls compensate him for the humiliation he was forced to endure.

Thus while Radha and Krsna retired to a grove, Lalita sat Madhumangala upon a throne overlooking Gahvara-kunda and, assisted by the gopis, fed him a mountain of Manohara-laddus.

By the foot of Mana-garh is a cultivated field where an old Vraja-vasi woman grew chickpeas, to which Krsna and His friends regularly helped themselves whenever they tended their cows in the area.

Once, this woman ran after the boys, who easily evaded her by running through the paths of Gahvaravana to Gahvara-kunda. Near the lake, Krsna and His friends roasted their ill-gotten gains.

While the cowherd boys were eating the roasted chickpeas and having a hearty laugh, the old gopi arrived on the spot, intending to teach Krsna and His thieving friends a good lesson.

But seeing and sincerely appreciating Krsna’s unparalleled beauty, that elderly gopi had a change of heart. Her anger subsided and loving attraction to the Lord awakened within her heart.

In the mood of parental love, that simple-hearted woman began not only to skin and roast the chickpeas herself, but also to personally feed Krsna, the Supreme Lord, as if He were her own son.

Such are the pastimes that take place around Gahvara-kunda, where carefree Krsna sports with His friends, teases the gopis, and with Radharani dives deep into the ocean of amorous love.

From Gahvara-kunda I made my way to Mana-garh and sat down at the arena of the divine couple’s rasa-lila to chant the maha-mantra and meditate upon the pastimes Radha and Krsna enjoy there.

Amongst the unlimited places of pastimes in Vraja, Varsana is one of the divine couple’s favourites. And in Varsana, Mana-garh is the best due to its seclusion, ambience, and unparalleled view of the area.

At Mana-garh, Radha and Krsna bless the creator by dancing upon his peak-like head, a favour enjoyed only rarely by the likes of Kaliya, upon whose hundred heads Krsna sports with the gopis.

The sounds of dancing footsteps, jingling ankle bells, clapping hands, and blissful voices descended upon the villages surrounding Mana-garh like thick fog flows down a mountainside into a valley.

Stirred from sleep by this soft melodious festival, the Vraja-vasis think, “The demigods in heaven must be celebrating Krsna’s wonderful deeds,” and then they go back to their dreams of Hari.

The residents of Vrndavana are fully absorbed in perfect loving devotion to Krsna. Unlike conditioned souls, their state of consciousness is always the same, whether awake or asleep.

Krsna likes to swing with Radha, for when the swing goes very high, She slides closer to Krsna and in fear embraces His neck. Knowing this, Vrnda astutely places swings in many places throughout Vraja.

Some swings hang from the branches of large trees, others hang from purpose-built ornate arches, like the gold and jewelled arch at Mana-garh, from which is suspended a well-padded swing.

When this swing is pushed forward it flies out over the northern slope of Mana-garh, over villages like Rankoli, just as an eagle soars out from its nest across the valley below.

Radha is always reluctant to sit upon this swing, but after Krsna’s persuasive assurance that They will only sway gently by the force of the wind, She gives in to His will, only to be tricked once again.

Srimati Radharani pleads with Krsna, but He simply laughs and swings higher and higher, making His beloved shriek with fright as They soar high over the rugged mountainside below.

Wrapping Her arms around His neck, Radharani repeatedly begs Krsna to stop, which He finally does, but only after seeing the terrified look on Her face and the hot tears streaming from Her eyes.

Mana-garh is most remembered by the pastime in which Radharani sulked when She came to know that, on His way to meet Her at an appointed time, Krsna had dallied with Candravali.

**\* \* \* \***

Once, Krsna sent a message to Radha expressing His great eagerness to be with Her. He even indicated the exact time of night and the specific place in Vrndavana where They should meet.

Sure enough, as the time for the rendezvous approached, Krsna climbed down from His bedroom balcony and, chanting Radha’s name under His breath, made His way towards Varsana.

Illuminated by a full moon, the night was bright. In His haste to get to Radha, Krsna recklessly took the main road. Of course, He was easily seen and intercepted by Padma, Candravali’s closest friend.

Krsna’s mind was far from thoughts of Candravali, but she had been observing a nightlong vigil, worshipping Durga in the hope that her fortune would change, and the goddess would draw Krsna to her.

Candravali's dearest Padma appeared to have become goddess Durga’s envoy. She had successfully found Krsna hurrying down the road to a destination Padma suspected she knew all too well.

In loyal service to her mistress, Padma stopped Krsna and cleverly said, “Fate has led me to You just in time. Candravali is dying in a nearby bower, her heart broken from not seeing You.”

To conceal the deep love He felt for Sri Radha, Krsna replied, “O Padma! Candravali always stays in the abode of My heart, which stands always at her beck and call. What would you have Me do?”

Smiling, Padma replied, “You are, no doubt, on an important errand. But please divert Your course for a moment to Gauri-kunda and reassure my friend of Your unfailing affection for her.”

Krsna was in a tight spot. He wanted to meet Radha, yet how could He refuse the request of a surrendered devotee? In an instant He collected His thoughts, “I will pacify Candravali with sweet words.

“I’ll convince her of the urgency of My errand and continue on My way to Radharani. I’ll only be a few moments late.” With feigned enthusiasm, He replied, “Take me to Candra at once!”

But Krsna’s plan was stalled by the affectionate reception and kindly comforts with which Candravali greeted Him. What started out to be a few moments ended up being a few hours.

Candravali's submissive love was enough to distract Krsna from His mission. Engaged in sweet talks and intimate exchanges, He somehow forgot that some distance away Radharani was waiting.

Indeed, long after the appointed time of Her meeting with Krsna had passed, Radha still paced anxiously outside Her grove near Gahvara-kunda, wondering what could have happened to Her sweetheart.

As if in answer to Her thoughts, one of Lalita’s trained parrots arrived. It had been spying on Candravali's kunja. The parrot gave Radha an exact report of what it had secretly seen and heard.

Radhika was heartbroken. Krsna had personally requested a meeting, set its time and place, and then had detoured to see another, just as He was at Radha’s doorstep. This was simply too much!

In the sort of angry tone only the gopis dare express, Radha mocked Hari: “How could He be so crooked, that crest-jewel of miscreants, that incorrigible cheat, shameless demon, king of debauchees?

“Krsna is a black serpent with nothing better to do than attack saintly girls in order to destroy their religious principles. Then, after shaming them, He casts them aside like used dolls.

“This highwayman who lurks on the roads of Govardhana, the shores of the Yamuna, and the paths of Vrndavana, is a traitorous black-hearted reprobate, a pitcher of ignorance best forgotten.”

Touched by the tamarind of divine sorrow, the milk of Radha’s love transformed into the yogurt of proud anger and then, churned by Her own indignant comments, into the liquefied butter of divine wrath.

Radha, whose flaming heart furiously consumed that liquefied butter, was convinced that She no longer wanted anything to do with Krsna, or with anything that might remind Her of Him, like —

The black musk dot on Her chin, or the blue sari She wore, the sapphire bangles on Her wrists, the priceless necklace He had given Her, the dark collyrium on Her eyelids, or even Her own black hair.

With a determination so fierce that it frightened all but Her most hot-tempered friends, Radha rid Herself of all decorations, ornaments, and dress, and to avoid seeing Her own hair, shattered Her mirror.

But prema, ornamented by these displays of deviousness and arrogance, nourishes Radha’s affection for Krsna. And beyond that, the fear such wrath invokes in Him nourishes His love for Her.

The divine couple’s love for each other is further enriched by the intense rivalry for Krsna’s attentions that gopis like Candravali stir up, gopis who, in truth, are expansions of Sri Radha.

Therefore, although the multitude of gopis in Vraja are different persons, in different forms, appearing in different roles, but with a single heart, they live and breathe only to please Radha and Krsna.

Meanwhile, Krsna, who was intoxicated by Candravali’s indulgences, sobered up like a drunkard who inevitably comes to His senses, and remembered that Sri Radha was waiting for Him.

The moon had hidden behind the treetops in fear of Radha’s anger, and so the Kaustubha jewel around Krsna’s neck was the only illumination He had to find His way to Gahvara-kunda.

However, by the time He had arrived at the kunja — once beautifully decorated — it had been abandoned, its lamps burnt out, its pictures shattered, its ornate bed of flowers unused.

Krsna took in the scene: at the entrance of the kunja that had filled His mind all day, He noticed Radha’s distinctive *kunkuma*-coloured footprints leading in the direction of a nearby hill.

Krsna thought, “Radha becomes angry either for good reason, or for no reason at all. My dalliance with Candra and My being late for Our meeting are both very good reasons for Her to be angry with Me.

“When Radha is angry for no reason, She can be calmed by a beautiful place, the passing of time, or the sound of My flute. But when She has good reason to be angry, I must pacify Her Myself.

“I will find Her and speak sweet words to Her. Then I will tell Her some witty jokes. And if that doesn’t work, I will worship Her with incense and a ghee lamp, and I will bow down to Her lotus feet.”

His mind teeming with such thoughts, His tongue constantly chanting Radha’s name, Krsna set off. His feet often slipped on the steep, rocky path to Mana-garh, but His resolve never flagged.

The moon emerged from its hiding place, shining above the clouds to light up the rocky path. Hari looked up at the sky and thought, “As a goal, this moon may be easier to attain than My angry Radhika.”

Krsna finally reached the mountaintop, which had a diameter of one hundred cubits. He was met there by a gopi who guarded the entrance to a corridor of trees, dimly lit by an occasional lamp.

Further along the corridor, Krsna could see a luminous grove. There sat Radha with Her friends, silhouettes before the fire of Her relentless anger. Krsna thought, “Lalita must also be here.”

As Krsna tried to enter the kunja, He was stopped abruptly by the guard. He bribed her, however, with a valuable pearl necklace, and with effort He was able to persuade her to deliver a message to Radha.

The message said, “Dearly beloved! You have put faith in a forest-parrot that mechanically repeats the words of a cruel-hearted gopi who delights only in fomenting disagreements between Us.

“Please do not immerse Your heart in jealous anger. It causes Me too much distress. Be merciful to Me, Your humble servant.” Srimati Radharani, however, was in no mood for conciliatory messages.

She knitted Her eyebrows and replied to the guard, “You are only a minute late, yet Your parched lips suggest that the cold forest winds have made You ill. Please return to the warmth of Your home.”

Not easily deterred, Krsna again appealed to Radha, “O beautiful one! I realise that I have offended You grievously. Still, please know that You remain the only object of My most ardent love.”

This second message softened Radha’s heart. In the presence of Her friends She bowed Her face, trying to hide the streams of tears flowing from Her eyes that moistened Her upper cloth.

Although momentarily quelled, the force of Radha’s anger again flared up. She turned to Her messenger and snapped, “Tell Him to offer His fickle love to the moon of His own heart.”

Krsna was crestfallen at Radha’s brusque and unequivocal answer. He had no choice but to go. He turned away from Radha’s kunja and walked slowly down the same steps He had just ascended.

The gopi-messenger despaired to see Krsna’s state: shoulders slumped, head hanging forward, eyes flooded with tears. But she knew that to intercede on His behalf would be to suffer the same fate as His.

Lonely, rejected, and lamenting the circumstances that had robbed Him of Radha’s favour, Krsna left and made His way to the bottom of the hill. From there He walked aimlessly, having no idea where to go.

Divine fate, however, could not tolerate seeing Krsna’s intense sorrow. As He walked beneath trees that nearly blocked out the moonlight, fate arranged that He cross paths with Visakha-devi.

When Visakha saw Hari alone on such a romantic night, she questioned Him about the course of events that had ended in mishap, and listened as He remorsefully explained everything to her.

In a voice that could cheer a sinner on his way to Yamaraja, resourceful Visakha devised a scheme by which Krsna could regain access to Radha and contain the raging fire of Her divine wrath.

Krsna followed Visakha to a nearby kunja that was— like many of liters— replete with every item necessary to serve Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes. There He allowed her to dress Him as a gopi.

Before long Krsna reappeared — accompanied by Visakha-devi — dressed in a sari, His hair braided, His limbs decorated. Plucking a vina, He hastened to Mana-garh with renewed confidence.

When the two girls arrived at Sri Radha’s door, the *gopi*-guard, amazed at the delicate beauty of Visakha’s companion, thought, “How has such a beauty gone unnoticed for so long in Vraja-mandala?”

And as she watched Syama-gopi walk along the tree-lined entrance to the kunja, she could not help but think: “This new girl’s gait, presence, and smile seem strikingly familiar!”

Visakha entered the kunja unannounced and boldly spoke out: “Manini! I have brought you a famous singer to lift your spirits. Look! Here is Syama, who will certainly bring a smile to Your lips.”

Radha cast an indifferent glance at Her guest, but when Their eyes met, Radha’s attention was spontaneously drawn to Her guest’s dress, features, and demeanour. Radha thought, “Who is this girl?”

Radharani remained tongue-tied while Visakha-devi introduced Syama-gopi as a gifted musician — like the celestials — capable of driving away the sorrow of the most broken-hearted of lovers.

And since Radha did not object, Visakha sat Syama close to Radha. Visakha then asked Syama to sing a sweet song that would soothe the princess’s nerves, relieve Her love-worn heart, and make Her happy.

Great poets can describe the voice, melody, and music of the song with which *gopi*-disguised Krsna pacified His beloved. Bereft of such talent, however, I cannot even try to do so without being impudent.

Yet I can say that Syama’s recital affected Srimati Radharani so deeply and so completely that She instinctively and tearfully embraced this cloud-like minstrel with Her soft vine-like arms.

Krsna’s song had already softened Radha’s heart — made hard by the effects of wrath — and now His compelling touch had not only evaporated all trace of Her anger, but also disclosed His true identity.

Recoiling from Syama, Radha cast a mildly reproachful glance at Visakha while smiling apologetically at the other gopis, who by that time had entered a fit of irrepressible laughter.

Radhika repeatedly pushed Krsna until He was perched precariously at edge of His seat, all the while struggling to subdue Her own laughter, which was fuelling the hilarity of Her friends.

On the verge of being shoved off the settee, just as the shawl slipped off His head, Krsna dropped to His knees, stared submissively at Radha’s flushed face, and joined His palms in supplication.

Krsna’s beguiling eyes — beautified by tears — arrested Radha’s glance, captured Her mind, and checked Her movements. Her gaze transfixed on Him, all sense of shyness flew away like a flock of birds.

Radha’s love for Krsna made Her forget His misbehaviour. But the sound of the gopis’ whispers reminded Her that They were not alone. After an eternal moment She looked away — speechless.

Krsna took Radha’s hand and said, “My dearest! A full moon night such as this would be wasted — an offence to the goddess Ratri — were it not to resound with the merriment of Our loving pastimes.

“Look, Your gopi friends are visibly eager to enjoy the pleasant atmosphere of this mountain peak. Why should You — their true friend — leave their commendable desire unfulfilled?

“I am but a servant of the milkmaids of Vraja. Please tell Me, then, how may I fulfil the purpose for which You and Your friends have taken so much trouble to climb to this remote mountain top.”

Radha openly expressed Her pleasure by patting the bench beside Her, and cast an alluring smile at Him. Krsna quickly cast off His female disguise and, with the gopis’ consent, sat down beside Radha.

Ecstatic at the favourable turn of events, the gopis celebrated by offering arati to the divine couple, singing Their glories, and playing musical instruments. And as bliss overwhelmed them, they danced.

The gopis’ ecstasy was contagious, forcing Radha and Krsna to Their feet. As the moon happily looked on, They began to dance in the centre of that circle of jubilant gopis.

The divine couple’s nocturnal festival had been enriched by Krsna’s conquest of Radharani’s anger, a towering mountain surrounded by the foothills of Her friend’s inflammatory words.

Thus, in addition to rasa dancing, the divine couple enjoyed the heightened bliss of other pastimes like decorating each other with flowers, swinging, picnicking, and finally resting within a kunja.

Because of Krsna’s success in breaking Radha’s belligerent anger (mana) on the northwestern peak of Varsana, that mount became known to the gopis as Mana-garh, a name it retains to this day.

This pastime also etched the name Mana-garh into the minds of Candravali and her friends to remind them of the place where Krsna apologised to Radha for having shown affection to an inferior rival.

**\* \* \* \***

From Mana-garh, a pleasant walk east through flowering gardens brings us to Varsana’s next peak, Dana-garh, stretching north to south. Finally it adjoins Mora-garh, where it overlooks Sankari-khor.

Dana-garh is so named because it overlooks a place where Krsna regularly demands a toll (dana) from the cowherd girls. On the northern part of this hill is a temple called Dana-mandira.

In Bhauma Vrndavana devotees can see an ancient set of Radha-Krsna Deities that were installed in Dana-mandira six thousand years ago. These Deities are called Radha-Dana-bihari.

Some scholars argue that worship of Krsna, especially alongside Radha, began less than five hundred years ago, but the presence of the Deities Radha and Dana-bihari disproves this theory.

According to the temple pujaris, these Deities were being worshipped even while Radha and Krsna enacted Their pastimes in Varsana, having been installed before Krsna’s appearance.

Let us offer our obeisances to the humorous words exchanged between the divine couple, who manifest Themselves in Deity forms just to make known the eternal beauty of Their playful quarrels.

Atop Dana-garh, beautiful gardens abound that reflect Radha’s colourful youth, the fragrance of which bewilders the Krsna-bee, a bee incessantly addicted to the nectar of Radha’s qualities.

Great devotees in the mood of Radha’s girlfriends or maidservants are as eager to serve these gardens as they are to serve Radha Herself, for they know that here the divine couple enjoy great bliss.

I looked in the direction of King Vrsabhanu’s palace and recalled how, from childhood to youth, Radha and Her girlfriends would play in the gardens, groves, and lakes along this mountain ridge.

As babies, often naked, the girls played in the dirt within the safety of the palace compound, but as they entered childhood, they played games like kho-kho, venturing further afield.

And when the girls entered youth, Radha would lead the gopis on excursions throughout Varsana, along paths seldom used and past darkened groves, with the hope of meeting Krsna.

Eager to fulfil Radha’s desire as much as His own, Krsna would take His boyfriends to Dana-garh and confront Radha and the gopis in the brash and haughty ways typical of teenage boys.

With His friends flanking Him on both sides, Krsna would block the pathway and impersonate a toll-collector, demanding, “To where arc you girls transporting the beautiful artefacts decorating your bodies?

“Don’t you know the eternal King Cupid demands a tax on the transport of all luxury goods? I, his obedient collector, am here to evaluate and tax your ornaments, as well as the limbs they decorate.”

When Krsna would speak like this and begin to move threateningly towards Radharani, Lalita would step in between Them and ward Him off with a piercing glance and warning shout.

“Stay where You are!” Lalita would say, “We’ve heard Your tales of Cupid kings, tax collectors, and dutiable goods, but we have no time for fantasy. We’re on our way to worship the sun god.”

Krsna would fall back when challenged by Lalita, but from within the protective circle of His friends He would continue to demand compensation for the girls’ yogurt, ornaments, or beauty.

Everyone present — Lalita and Radha included — wanted to fulfil Krsna’s desires. And so after much argument, joking, and laughter, Radha would retire with Krsna to negotiate Her dues in private.

Let us worship Dana-garh, glorious with gardens, lakes, and valleys. It echoes with the sounds of Krsna’s impudent demands, bringing great bliss to those young gopis whose very life is Sri Radha.

As I arrived at the capital of King Vrsabhanu, my ears yearned to hear the clamour of the market, the sound of churning rods, the lowing of cows, and the chatter of its residents and pilgrims.

My eyes longed to see the beautiful palace citadel, its walls surrounded by the cowherds’ regal homes, neatly planted orchards, verdant vegetable gardens, and wide protective moat.

I yearned to behold the towering gates that give entrance to the outer compound. In this protected garden, Radha, Sridama, and Ananga, free from royal dress and obligations, play with their friends.

Beyond this is the thirty-room royal residence, divided into quadrants by two corridors: the first extending from the main entrance to the rear, the second joining it at the halfway point on both sides.

The joyous, unparalleled atmosphere of this palace makes the marble walls soft, the golden doors more effulgent, the pearl-laced curtains more delicate, and the inlaid jewelled decors more intriguing.

The palace of King Vrsabhanu is famed not only for its beauty and grandeur but also for its cheerful ambience, which visitors eagerly absorb through all of their senses, swaying like intoxicated elephants.

The eternal home of Srimati Radharani is inaccessible even to the servants of Lord Narayana in Vaikuntha, what to speak of great demigods, the administrators of universal affairs.

Permeated by Radha’s sweet youthfulness, which is untouched by awe-filled majesty and the modes of material nature, Vrsabhanu-pura can be influenced by neither Hari-dhama nor Devi-dhama.

Unlike Yavata, where Radha is always sombre, Varsana resounds with Her carefree laughter, which is like a spring breeze carrying the aroma of blossoming flowers and the sound of chirping birds.

Radha’s laughter inspires even the eldest of King Vrsabhanu’s guests with youthful exuberance and fills even the palace servants with a kind of bliss unknown to the inhabitants of Nandagrama.

The concert of Radha’s laughter, mixed with the tinkling of Her anklets and the song of Her vina, shames the warbling of Brahma’s celebrated swan and defeats even the vibration of Krsna’s flute.

Those devotees who have been made fortunate by the grace of guru and the holy name hear these divine sounds, which wash away, like waves of transcendental sweetness, all impurities from the heart.

Bowing down reverentially before Vrsabhanu-pura, I completed my parikrama of Varsana and turned towards Govardhana, eager to visit its holy places, which are famous for fulfilling the sadhakas' desires.

Dear reader!

May the words describing Sri Radha's coronation protect your ears, lips, and tongue, and give you devotion to Radha and Krsna.

## Chapter Eight - Umrao

Eager to see Giriraja, king of mountains, I left Varsana. I hoped to drink from the fount of His nectarean company, so I rejoined the main parikrama path at a little-known place called Rahela.

Krsna sent His cousin Uddhava to Vraja with letters for His forlorn kinsmen and instructed him to enter Nanda Maharaja’s kingdom at Rahela, a small forest thick with flowering trees.

The woods of Rahela are especially dear to Krsna because they remind Him of the unfettered nature of the gopis’ love for Him. The moment they hear the call of Krsna’s flute, they rush to meet Him.

One evening, while most Vraja-vasis were bathing and dressing for supper, Krsna hid in Rahela and played sublimely alluring melodies on His flute — melodies so mysterious that only the gopis could hear.

Some of the young gopis were still bathing. Others had finished bathing but were not fully dressed. And those who had finished getting dressed were in the midst of applying cosmetics to their faces.

When the sound of Krsna’s flute fell upon their ears, each gopi not only heard Krsna call her by name, but also felt drawn by His intense loving desire — the flute-song aroused kama in the gopis’ hearts.

Until that moment they had been peaceful, but the call of the flute agitated them. In their haste to join Krsna, the gopis abandoned home and family, neglecting even their appearance.

Those who had been bathing left home with skirts on their upper bodies and sashes wrapped around their legs. Even those who were already dressing ran out into the night with their clothes in disarray.

The gopis who had been busy decorating their faces left home with their makeup incomplete or misapplied — with kajjala on only one eye, with lipstick on their cheeks, or with kajjala on their lips.

The gopis ran out of their homes with the swift, unpredictable force of the wind, the sound of Krsna’s flute dragging them through the forests of Vraja until they arrived at the lotus feet of the Lord.

Boys prefer to see their sweethearts attractively dressed, but Krsna relished the tousled sight of the gopis. It was a sign of their love for Him, and He showed them extraordinary kindness in return.

Face to face with the object of their desire, and He so affectionately disposed to them, the gopis were overcome by shyness. With their toes, they drew patterns in the dust of Vrndavana.

It is said that with pure devotion and the blessings of the dhama one can see those patterns — even today. And with those same blessings one can aspire to overhear Krsna’s affectionate words to the gopis.

**\* \* \* \***

Just south of Rahela, sheltered by a weeping willow, lies Umrao. In this place Sri Radha was crowned queen of Vrndavana, and Radha-vinoda appeared to Lokanatha Gosvami.

Lokanatha arrived at this sacred spot on his travels through the forests of Vraja. He stayed for some time in a grove near the banks of Kisori-kunda, a lake that commemorates Radha’s coronation bath.

The Gosvami had a long-cherished desire to serve a Deity of Krsna, but had not disclosed to anyone this yearning of his heart. One day, holding a Deity of Himself, Krsna appeared before His devotee.

The merciful Lord said, “You may worship this Deity of Me, known as Radha-vinoda.” Krsna carefully placed His own enchanting blackish form in Lokanatha’s trembling hands, and then disappeared.

But the Gosvami was perplexed. He had not even a hut in which to live. How could he properly care for a Deity? In response to Lokanatha’s anxiety, Radha-vinoda tenderly smiled and said:

“First you call on Me to accept your worship, then when I come you complain. Don’t worry. Like you, I reside in this forest of Umrao. Just make some arrangement to feed Me — but quickly! I’m hungry!”

Lokanatha was floating on waves of prema, but somehow he quickly prepared rice and offered it to the Lord on a fresh banana leaf. As Krsna ate, Lokanatha gazed at Radha-vinoda’s breathtaking beauty.

When the local villagers came to know that Lokanatha’s life was now the property of Radha-vinoda, they offered to construct a simple cottage for the Lord and His devotee. But the Gosvami refused.

Both the devotee and the object of his devotion continued to live in their woodland refuge. Radha-vinoda’s temple became a simple cloth bag that Lokanatha lovingly wore around his neck.

Lokanatha Gosvami continued to relish this life of extreme renunciation until the day he departed from this world, but he was never a great distance from the company of pure Vaisnavas.

He would regularly seek out the Six Gosvamis of Vrndavana. He was also a frequent companion of Bhugarbha Gosvami, who had long given up sleep in favour of meditating upon the divine couple.

Lokanatha Gosvami’s Deity is called Radha-vinoda because Radha is always a source of pleasure (vinoda) for Krsna. This is especially true of the time She was crowned the undisputed queen of Vrndavana.

Srimati Radharani’s coronation is mentioned in the Puranas and in the works of the Gosvamis, but Srila Jiva Gosvami has described it most elaborately in his Madhava-mahotsava.

The name Umrao derives both from the name Unmatta-radha-sthali (“the place where Radha became maddened with happiness”) and from the word umaraha, which means a “rich man or prince.”

With the following prayer Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami invokes the blessings of both Unmatta-radha-sthali and the glorious pastime performed there,

*sardham manasa-jahnavim upanadi-vargaih—sa-rangotkaraih savitry-adi-suri-kulais ca nitaram akasa-vanya vidhoh*  
*vrndaranya-varenya-rajya-visaye sri-paurnamasi muda radham yatra siseca sincatu sukham sonmatta-radha-sthali*

**“Ordered by the voice of Brahma from the sky, Paurnamasi crowned Srimati Radharani queen of the beautiful forest of Vrndavana by jubilantly sprinkling the waters of the Manasa-ganga —**

**“And other sacred rivers upon Her head in the company of Savim and other goddesses. I pray that the sacred place known as Unmatta-radha-sthali will sprinkle some transcendental happiness upon me.”**

Radha’s coronation was performed twice: first, as play by the gopis, and second, with all due pomp by the Vraja-vasis. Both events happened after the gopas had crowned Krsna in Chattravana.

All three events were part of one greater pastime, inspired by Krsna through the agency of Yogamaya. He desired to establish Radha’s sovereignty over Himself, as well as over Vraja and the Vraja-vasis.

The pastime begins with the gopas tending their cows in Chattravana. The boys were discussing how they might glorify Krsna in a unique way. Sridama suggested, “Let’s coronate Him king of Vraja!”

With one heart, the boys embraced Sridama’s splendid proposal. At once they busied themselves with arrangements for a grand yet pastoral ceremony that would culminate in the coronation of Krsna.

After much ado, the gopas sat Krsna on a regal stone beneath a canopy of flowers and crowned Him with a wreath of leaves and fresh buds. In various ministerial roles the boys attended to their new king.

Krsna readily assumed the role of monarch. To assert His dominion over Vraja, He appointed Madhumangala as the leader of a band of gopas, with orders to widely broadcast Krsna’s pre-eminence.

Intoxicated by love for his friend and bolstered by his gang of town criers, Madhumangala wandered from forest to forest, calling out, “O residents of Vrndavana, just hear this decree.

“The only authority here is the son of Nanda Maharaja, Krsna, the crowned sovereign of this land! Anyone found unlawfully picking flowers or fruit shall be brought before Him and severely punished.

“Flaunt the king’s authority in even the slightest way, and you shall be dealt with strictly and mercilessly! Therefore listen carefully, O residents of Vrndavana: Krsna is your unrivalled king.”

Madhumangala addressed herds of deer, flocks of birds, a muster of peacocks, a pride of lions, even a school of fish, and his words were agreeable to all who heard them — except for one special group.

A group of gopis collecting wild flowers nearby heard the news. They excitedly repeated Madhu’s decree to Lalita-devi while she and her attendants were lovingly adorning Sri Radha within a palatial grove.

Radharani, seated upon a majestic throne, was dressed in elegant silk robes, decorated with flower ornaments and garlands, and bedecked with jewels exceeding the value of a hundred royal treasuries.

As soon as the news that Krsna had usurped Vraja’s sovereignty entered Lalita’s ears, her face flushed and her hands shook. As hard as she tried, she could not fix a flower crown upon Radha’s head.

Amused by Her dearmost friend’s tempestuous reaction, Radha arched Her perfect eyebrows to stoke Lalita’s ire, as if to say, “O loyal friend! What will you do about this treasonous audacity?”

Lalita clutched the crown as she replied to Radha’s silent query: “Instigated by a group of foolish cowherds and one deviant brahmana, one impudent vassal dares to assume the post of Vraja’s ruler?

“While Radharani sits upon Her throne as the queen of Vrndavana, does some cowherd playing make-believe king foolishly think that we can be subjugated as easily as dumb forest animals?”

Lalita held the crown ceremoniously above Radha’s head and announced, “Let this crown, made of the forest’s treasures, proclaim Vraja’s real monarch!” Then she placed it on Sri Radha’s head.

As the gopis cheered, Lalita ordered Vrnda, “O friend! At once assemble a full battalion of gopis to capture those town criers, the rest of their insurgency, and that blackish so-called king.”

Radha, a faint smile decorating Her face, added, “Those who falsely claim authority over My kingdom should be challenged, defeated, bound hand and foot, and brought before Me for punishment.”

Vrnda armed thousands of gopis with flower-batons and deployed them to Chattravana. On the way, they soon encountered Subala, Madhu, and other gopas, exuberantly proclaiming Krsna’s glories.

Suddenly the boys found themselves surrounded by thousands of militant gopis. As they fled in fear, Madhumangala, less agile than the others, was captured and bound with a flower garland.

Madhu was brought cowering in fear before the throne of Sri Radha, his bravado deflated. After surveying with a regal glance the pudgy brahmana prisoner, Radharani leaned forward and said:

“I am the real authority over this land, but you promote an imposter as ruler of My kingdom. For that offence you must be punished. And soon your friends and your rascal leader will also be punished.”

Madhu hung his head low but whispered defiantly, “Make my punishment be such that my belly is full.” Radha frowned, clapped Her hands twice, and ordered Her gopi-guards, “Release him!

“This fake brahmana is only interested in filling his belly. Send him away with a message for his would-be king: ‘Capitulate Your false title, lands, and symbols at once, or pay the price!’”

**\* \* \* \***

Hands still bound behind his back, Madhumangala scurried off to Chattravana, where Krsna was still smarting from the indignity of having His gopas trounced by a gopi-army.

Krsna’s loss of face plummeted even further seeing His brahmana advisor before Him, bound and shamed. Krsna rose from His throne, embraced Madhu, and enquired, “How could this have happened?”

Madhu replied, “This is the result of Your pronouncing Yourself king of Vraja. Cupid — the emperor You claim to represent — trembles before the true monarch of this land, Srimati Radharani.

“You have challenged Radha’s authority, and unless You surrender to Her, I fear that You too will meet with an ignoble fate. Friend, I implore You! Please abandon Your impudence at once.”

Krsna considered the situation. Binding a king’s advisor could be seen as a declaration of war. “Then again,” He thought, “surrendering to Her might prove to be more thrilling than defying Her.”

While Subala untied him, Madhu spun in an eddy of love for Krsna and brazenly continued, “Put aside for a moment my disgrace at the hands of those forest fairies and accept the good counsel I proffer.”

Madhu then took Krsna by the hand and requested the gopas, “Our friend will now have to humble Himself before a mighty queen. Your presence would only embarrass Him even more. Please wait here!”

With a smile, Krsna assented to Madhu’s command. The two of them then set out on the path to Umrao, where Radha’s parrot-spies had already informed the queen of Krsna’s intention to capitulate.

The news that Syamasundara was approaching, ready to surrender, pleasantly assailed Radharani with loving ecstasies: Her face turned pale, Her limbs trembled, and tears began to well in Her eyes.

And when Radha could see from the elevated vantage point of Her queenly throne what appeared to be a sapphire glow coming down the path to Umrao, Her entire body became rigid and motionless.

Lalita placed a reassuring hand on Radha’s forearm and said, “O Umaraha! Be steady. To accept surrender from a defeated rival, You must display the regal composure befitting a victorious monarch!”

Srimati Radharani felt strength from Lalita’s words. She took a deep breath, subdued Her ecstasy, and entreated Her mind, “O friend! Please be calm. Your desires will be fulfilled soon enough.”

Krsna was blinded by a golden radiance as He approached Radha’s stately kunja. But as He chanted Radha’s name, the glare faded to reveal a magnificently dressed monarch seated upon a jewelled throne.

Surrounding Sri Radha were thousands of gopis, richly dressed and armed with flower-weapons. Krsna thought, “I did well not to test My ragtag gopa army against such a superior military force!”

The ecstasies Krsna had fought to subdue as He approached Umrao suddenly erupted. He turned pale, His limbs stiffened, and He froze in His tracks as streams of tears shot from His eyes.

Madhumangala whispered in Krsna’s ear, “My love-struck friend! Don’t let a few morsels of happiness deprive You of the feast of bliss awaiting You at Radha’s side. Control Your heart and keep walking.”

As Krsna came close enough for the gopis to hear His footsteps, wide-eyed Radha thought, “I am most fortunate. Not only can I see Krsna’s enchanting form, but I can also hear the tinkling of His ankle bells.”

Hari stopped at the foot of the dais and let His eyes drift slowly from Radha’s feet to Her face. He was as tongue-tied to behold Her unique feminine beauty as She was to see His Cupid-like elegance.

Lalita and Madhu exchanged notable glances. Holding Krsna’s hand, Madhumangala cleared his throat and addressed Sri Radha, “O all powerful queen of Vrndavana! Vrndavanesvari!

“I have advised my friend Krsna, the prince of Nandagrama, to submit to Your royal authority. Following my wise counsel, He has come here to surrender Himself to You — unconditionally.”

Madhu looked at Krsna, then at Radha, before continuing, ‘‘As a token of His subservience, Krsna will offer You His most valuable possession, His priceless embrace. Please graciously accept it.”

His words made the gopis giggle and Radha flush with embarrassment. Madhu, with typical melodrama, then stood Krsna next to His beloved, and stepped back to revel in the sight of the divine couple.

Again, Madhu stepped forward. He took the wreath from Krsna’s head and placed it at Radha’s feet. Then Krsna lay His flute on Radha’s footstool and knelt before Her with folded hands.

By these gestures the boys proclaimed Radha to be Vraja’s queen — and Krsna’s queen as well. Radha assented by motioning for Krsna to sit beside Her. Meanwhile, Madhu danced about in exultation.

Lalita was pleased with Madhu. To show her gratitude she placed one plump laddu in his mouth. Madhu munched and mumbled, “You have committed a grave offence by shackling a brahmana.

“If you feed me one hundred thousand laddus, then perhaps you will be freed from your sins.” Amused, Lalita ordered that an overly large tray of sweets be placed in Madhumangala’s outstretched hands.

Elated and salivating, Madhu blushed at the divine couple and stammered, “Please excuse me. I have something important to attend to.” He then retreated to a private spot to devour his bounty.

Radha and Krsna were thrilled to be so close to each other, and that happiness made profuse ecstatic symptoms appear in Their bodies. Seeing this, the gopis also felt blissful euphoria.

After Krsna’s ecstatic tears had subsided, He beheld Srimati Radharani’s regal beauty, carefully studying Her flowing dress, flower tiara, ornate jewellery, floral decorations, and elegant makeup.

Krsna revelled in Sri Radha’s majestic appearance and regal bearing and thought, “Isn’t it a pity that everyone cannot have access to this enchanting vision of Vrndavanesvari, queen of Vrndavana.

“If, however, My pastime potency, Yogamaya, were to make a fitting arrangement, the Vraja-vasis’ eyes could relish the beauty I am now beholding, and their hearts could feel the joy I am tasting.”

All-knowing Yogamaya instantly knew Krsna’s thoughts. At once, even as Krsna spoke with Radha about Their eternal love, Yogamaya began making arrangements to fulfil Her master’s heartfelt desire.

Ironically, Yogamaya’s plan was set in motion the moment Candravali heard about Srimati Radharani’s coronation. Everyone knows that young girls cannot tolerate being outdone by a rival.

And when Candravali heard that Krsna had conceded to Radha’s sovereignty — including sovereignty over Him — she was incensed. Through hot tears, she looked to her friend Padma.

Clever Padma quickly pacified her friend with a bold plan: she would convince Krsna to coronate Candravali instead — not in play, but for real, and not in some distant grove, but before all the Vraja-vasis.

**\* \* \* \***

In the days that followed Radharani’s ascent to the throne of Krsna’s heart, He wandered about in a daze, seeing Radha everywhere, seeing Radha in everything, unable to think of anything but Radha.

One day, while still in this state of divine confusion, Krsna sat surrounded by His friends on the edge of a forest, watching the cows graze nearby. Just then, Padma and her maidservants approached.

After politely greeting the cowherd boys, Padma turned her attention to Krsna and addressed Him in a very pleasing voice. “O Govinda! Have you heard the latest talk in the villages of Vrndavana?”

Deeply immersed in thoughts of Srimati Radharani, Krsna barely heard Padma’s question. But she had awakened the curiosity of His cowherd boyfriends, and so she was able to unfurl her scheme.

“People are calling for the selection of one monarch to unite the two great and the other lesser rulers of Vraja. They say that choosing a queen rather than a king could avoid future enmity amongst them.

“Since You are the son of Nandaraja, one of Vrndavana’s kings, the Vraja-vasis would most certainly embrace the queen You nominate, as well as any demands You might set for her coronation.”

The naive cowherd hoys welcomed Padma’s suggestion, seeing in it the possibility of a future adventure and a feast. In their excitement, the gopas began to chatter like a flock of birds.

As the distracted boys gossiped amongst themselves, Padma took the opportunity to corner Krsna. She said, “A desire arose in my heart as I was performing the Katyayani vow. You may now fulfil this desire:

“May the beautiful and qualified gopi whose name begins with ‘Candra’ be the queen of Vrndavana and its many residents. May that gopi, and none other, also be the queen of Sri Krsna’s heart.”

Govinda paused, deep in thought. The name Candra reminded Him of Radha’s partial expansion, Candrakanti, and assuming Padma must be speaking of Radha, He let out a sigh, declaring, “Let it be so!”

It appeared that Krsna had now given His public consent to Candravali’s coronation. In no time, that news reached Radha’s beautiful ears and stirred up the brew of divine envy in Her heart.

After surrendering to and worshipping Radha as Vrndavana’s queen, how could Krsna be so two-faced as to coronate Candravali at a public function? The mere thought of it infuriated Sri Radha.

But the gopis in Radha’s inner circle were even more enraged. They began to discuss how they could capture Krsna, and what they might do to teach Him a good lesson for shaming their faultless queen.

Between Radha’s incessant wrath and the gopis’ vengeful conspiracy, the atmosphere in Yavata became so caustic that it took the form of a red mist, hanging ominously over the residence of Abhimanyu.

And Krsna, who had spies everywhere, quickly came to know about the mysterious haze in Yavata and its worrisome origin — a palace engulfed in anger, boiling over with livid cowherd girls.

Krsna’s heart also burned, not with anger but with pain. How could fate misrepresent Him so? He was absorbed in devotion to Radha, but now the Vraja-vasis were saying that He was eager to deify Candra!

Krsna pondered the depth of His predicament and decided to meet with Vrnda-devi. He explained everything to Her, pleading, “Devi! My life is in your hands. If you do not save Me, I shall perish!”

Vrndavana’s able custodian replied, “Your careless decision to coronate Candra has deeply wounded Radha. Routine appeasements won’t work. This time You’ll have to do something extraordinary.”

His anguished cloud-blue lotus face tilted upward, His restless eyes wide open, His two pinkish palms facing the sky, Krsna volunteered: “I’m prepared to do anything. Just tell Me what to do.”

Vrnda thought for a moment then replied, “News of Candravali’s coronation is being spread by childish rumour. It does not sound like the sort of official decree that inaugurates a new monarchy.

“In my opinion, the best way to fight fire is with fire. To extinguish the fire of Candra’s coronation, light the fire of a new coronation for Radha, but do it publicly, and have the gods broadcast the event.

“Govinda, to eradicate all trivial rumours about Candravali, the creator himself must order the residents of Vrndavana to arrange for Srimati Radharani’s coronation. Who will question his authority?”

Krsna responded enthusiastically to Vrnda’s suggestion, yet He remained perplexed as to how and why the chief of the demigods would champion His cause. Knitting His eyebrows, Krsna asked:

“How could such a thing come to pass?” As Vrnda-devi set off towards the north, she cast a knowing glance over her shoulder and replied, “I will request Paurnamasi to give us her wise counsel.”

But before Paurnamasi could help, she first had to recover from the fit of ecstatic laughter she had gone into when she heard about the events leading up to the predicament she was being asked to resolve!

In a secluded grove near the banks of Vrnda-kunda, Paurnamasi outlined for Vrnda-devi a strategy that would make all of Vrndavana clamour for Srimati Radharani’s coronation bath.

Paurnamasi also reassured Vrnda that when Radha heard of Her imminent coronation, She would do more than simply give up Her anger at Krsna, She would become exceedingly pleased with Him.

For if Krsna were to preside over Radha’s coronation ceremony, He would be making known to all of the Vraja gopis — Candravali included — that He and Radha were like husband and wife.

Taking her cue from the elderly brahmani, Vrnda climbed to the top of a nearby mountain, and in a celestial voice, gave directions that gladdened the hearts of the Vraja-vasis — especially the women.

That “voice from the sky” first commanded Paurnamasi, “O great yogini ! Without delay make due arrangements for the coronation and bathing ceremony of Vraja’s Laksmi-moon, Srimati Radharani.

“By seating Radha upon a jewelled throne and performing Her royal abhiseka, you will spread Her glory, not only throughout Gokula but also throughout the entire earth, and thus the universe shall rejoice.

“This royal bath will proclaim the love for Radha cherished by the Vraja-vasis — and especially by Krsna — therefore it should be performed on the full moon day of this month, the month of Madhu.”

The voice then spoke to Radha: “O Radha! Do not be shy to accept such public worship. Because this ceremony bestows peace, prosperity, and happiness to all, You should embrace it as service to the world.”

Again that heavenly voice spoke to Paurnamasi and ordered her to start the auspicious ceremony at once by observing the introductory rites called adhivasa. The celestial voice then fell silent.

All the Vraja-vasis were amazed to hear these orders descend from the heavens. Candravali and her friends suffered utter disappointment, while all others felt indescribable bliss.

Paurnamasi and Vrnda fainted in ecstasy, Radha went numb, unable to speak, Her girlfriends embraced one another, Nandaraja entered a state of disbelief, and King Vrsabhanu proudly distributed gifts.

Kirtida planned how she would bathe her daughter, Mukhara sang wildly, Yasoda impatiently called for the festival to begin, the gopas danced, and Krsna wandered about Varsana clutching Subala’s hand.

After the shock of the initial ecstasy had worn off somewhat, the Vraja-vasis busied themselves with arrangements for the scheduled ceremony. Craftsmen constructed platforms, thrones, and canopies.

Women placed water-pots in auspicious places, young gopis fashioned decorative ornaments, children distributed incense everywhere, and servants garlanded the entranceways with strings of kadamba flowers.

Meanwhile, skilled bards assiduously recorded the events, including whatever was being said and done. But they unanimously agreed that Radharani’s moods were too mysterious to document. Why?

Because if Radha’s transcendental emotions bemused even Krsna, if even He were puzzled by the bliss that they gave Him, then how could mere poets or historians access Her confidential passions?

Only Srimati Radharani’s girlfriends, and those favoured by them, know the secrets of Her moods and deeds. Thus, knowledge of the coronation festival’s inner spirit belonged only to the gopis.

From the moment Radha heard the celestial decree, She fully accepted it as Krsna’s desire. She knew that this pastime would bring Him great pleasure, and pleasing Krsna was Her reason for living.

If it pleased Krsna that She walk in procession through Vrndavana, that She be bathed by demigoddesses, that She be dressed as royalty—then she would wholeheartedly and joyfully consent.

If it pleased Krsna that She sit on the throne of Vraja as its monarch, that She be crowned in His favourite forest, that She appoint the members of Her parliament — then She would agree to that as well.

But how things still more wonderful — like being bathed by Krsna’s love — could come to pass, that She didn’t know. Regardless, She had faith that Paurnamasi could realise all things difficult to accomplish.

Thereafter, according to heavenly command, the adhivasa ceremony began with pomp, and elder ladies of Vrndavana like Yasoda, Rohini, and Kirtida gave their wholehearted blessings to Sri Radha.

As was the local custom, some excited elderly women spattered the crowd with butter and yogurt, while others playfully splashed fragrant mixtures of turmeric water over the jubilant assembly.

Love-intoxicated women accompanied Radha to Umrao, where only a few days before, She had playfully assumed sovereignty over Vraja, and where the arena for Her real abhiseka was now being prepared.

Radha searched for Krsna’s footprints in the sands of Umrao and then stood riveted, gazing upon them. The Vraja-vasis, spellbound by Her intensity, greedily drank in the sight of Her devoted face.

As they lost themselves in Radha’s transcendental features, the Vraja-vasis’ own bodies displayed eight kinds of ecstasy, their minds fully immersed in thoughts of Sri Radha’s soul-stirring qualities.

Her gopi friends were amazed that the mere sight of Radharani could reawaken the long dormant Krsna consciousness in others, transporting them safely to the eternal realm of transcendence.

Radha prayed to the dust of Umrao, hopeful that this event would bring Her at least a glimpse of Krsna. The minds of all those present, from their contact with Radha, were spontaneously drawn to Hari.

Finally, Radharani lifted Her gaze from Sri Krsna’s footprints. The Vraja-vasis thought, “This ceremony has already caused us so much bliss. What kind of ecstasies will the actual coronation bring?”

**\* \* \* \***

But the joy of Radha’s coronation would not be easily won; Padma was adamant, still, that Krsna should be true to His promise and crown Candravali, not Radharani, as His queen.

Padma assured Candravali that she would sabotage Sri Radha’s coronation. On the day following the adhivasa, Padma hurried to Yavata, where she successfully poisoned Jatila’s mind with gossip.

“Foolish woman!” hissed Padma, “Did you not hear the celestial voice say that Krsna — who is so fond of flirting with chaste house-wives— would also be present at Srimati Radharani’s coronation?

“How then can you send your spotless daughter-in-law into the inexorable jaws of infamy? Open your eyes!” Jatila, who was half-blind and half-crazy, easily fell into Padma’s clever trap.

Jatila locked Radha in the house and sent fools like Govardhana Malla to obstruct the ceremony. But ever-resourceful Paurnamasi quickly quelled Jatila’s fears, dismissed Jatila’s bodyguards, and freed Radha.

Then, with the blessings of Kirtida, Sri Radha, suppressing a smile of relief, left Yavata, accompanied by a bevy of noisy girlfriends, to travel to the ornately decorated bathing platform being prepared at Umrao.

As the gopi procession moved across the fields of Vraja like a multicoloured victory banner fluttering in the wind, Mother Earth quivered expectantly at the thought of personally bathing Radha.

Musicians led the procession, followed by women showering flowers, brahmanis chanting mantras, and gopis singing and dancing. Young girls trailed behind, carrying ingredients for the sacrifice.

And Radha’s entourage of most intimate friends formed the heart of that blissful caravan of women. They guarded their princess so carefully that even demigoddesses could not catch a glimpse of Her.

Vrndavana’s residents had eagerly discussed the festival throughout the night, but with the rays of the rising sun, the cries of peacocks at daybreak heralded the end of the ecstatic suspense.

As Sri Radha passed the Vraja-vasis’ homes, they envisioned Her as the sum total of all auspicious things one could hope to see at the commencement of a journey, an undertaking, or a sacrifice.

They saw the ornaments on Her ears as flowering creepers in bloom, Her braid as the tail of a peacock, Her face, a fully opened lotus, Her eyes as flitting hummingbirds, and Her smile as a pure white swan.

As She left the villages and entered the forest, Radha remembered the pastimes Krsna had enjoyed in each place, and that remembrance revealed facets of Her unmatched beauty no one had seen before.

Even Radharani’s most intimate friends were mesmerised by Her stunningly exquisite bearing. Each of them thought, “I must have never properly studied Sri Radha’s beauty before today.”

To savour Radha’s unique splendour, the gopis crowded around and offered Her a variety of personal services: on Radha’s right, Lalita fanned Her; on Her left, Visakha held a picture of Sri Krsna.

In front, Citra carried sandalwood pulp to refresh Radha’s faded tilaka; behind Visakha, Campakalata carried a jewelled chest meant for Krsna; and behind Campakalata, Tungavidya sang Krsna’s glories.

In front and to the right, Indulekha carried Srimati Radharani’s tambula; and to Indulekha’s left, Rangadevi accompanied herself with a vina as she sang enchanting songs about the divine couple.

Amongst the inner group of gopis, Kundalata praised Radhika with clever words, and after Kundalata came Sudevi, absorbed in Radha’s thoughts, serving Radha’s emotions through meditation.

The wives of the demigods were also eager to soothe their eyes with a glimpse of Sri Radha, but because they were unable to see Her from above, they assumed the forms of gopis and joined the procession.

As this jubilant parade approached the arena of the abhiseka, the Lord’s pastime potency inspired Yasoda and Kirtida to return to their homes, stopping first to place Radha in the care of Paurnamasi.

Both mothers repeatedly embraced Sri Radha, bathing Her with their tears. After some time they walked away, repeatedly turning back to have one last look at Radharani’s affectionately smiling face.

In the same way that the surge of an ocean wave washes driftwood ashore, a surge of the waves of divine love brought Srimati Radharani’s colourful procession to rest in the forest of Umrao.

Within a very special grove, diligently prepared by Vrnda-devi, the arriving guests beheld a bathing platform, coronation throne, dignitaries’ scats, observation arenas, and all requisite paraphernalia.

Seated beneath a large bakula tree, the astonished entourage beheld their new surroundings. Meanwhile, Vrnda-devi escorted Sri Radha through each section of the grove, describing their various purposes.

A palatial central chamber surrounded by six smaller yet impressive sub-chambers, all together formed a large square, which could be accessed from the four directions through four gigantic doors.

All the chambers — with distinctive courtyards, floors, arches, trees, gardens, vines, flowers, and canopies — were so remarkable that even Lord Brahma was unable to adequately describe them.

Of course Sri Radha appreciated Vrnda-devi’s handiwork, but without Krsna, the vast, opulent arena held no attraction for Her. Externally She stayed calm, even as She disclosed Her inner turmoil.

“O friend!” said Radha. “Without Syama and His artful loving pastimes, this matrix of groves is like a beautifully decorated body from which the life-force has long since passed. I am not happy here.”

Vrnda squeezed Radha’s hand reassuringly, “Be patient! Just as a bejewelled chalice signals that nectar will shortly be served, this charming grove signals that a divine pastime is about to unfold.”

Enlivened by Vrnda’s words, Srimati Radharani soon noticed a beautiful throne at the centre of the easterly chamber. As effulgent as the sun, it was clearly the seat of Vrndavana’s monarch-to-be.

**\* \* \* \***

Thetwo girls then proceeded to the northerly chamber where they found Paurnamasi and some brahmacaris lighting a sacrificial fire on an elaborately decorated square dais called the Sarvabhauma-grha.

As the Vraja-vasis were circumambulating the sacred fire, the young cowherd girls relocated themselves to the southerly chamber in order to observe the proceedings from beneath a splendid canopy.

Accomplished mystic that she was, Paurnamasi poured oblations onto the sacrificial fire and chanted sacred mantras, causing the wives of Siva, Surya, Yama, Agni, and other demigods to appear.

These celestial ladies praised Radharani and then at Paurnamasi’s behest joined in making arrangements for the coronation. Who could contest Sri Radha’s supremacy in the presence of such witnesses!

Yet Radharani was anxious. Neither the women of Vrndavana nor the demigoddesses of far-flung realms could substitute for Sri Krsna. And a kingdom without Krsna was of no interest to Radha.

Paurnamasi saw the concern on Sri Radha’s face and read Her mind. She turned to Vrnda-devi and whispered, “Now is the time to bring the sapphire of Vrndavana out of hiding. Go quickly!’’

Rather than speak openly to Radha, Paurnamasi pacified Her with a glance that said, “All Your desires will soon he fulfilled.” Then the guru of the Vraja-vasis turned her attention to the proceedings.

Festive music inspired the guests to dance with abandon. The ensemble’s thunderous concert rent the skies and caused flowers to fall from heaven, signalling the beginning of the bathing ceremony.

At that very instant, sacred waters sprang from the right hands of Mothers Ganges, Yamuna, and Sarasvati. That holy water mixed with the perspiration of Paurnamasi to form a beautiful lake nearby.

Into the waters of that lake entered the potency of every sacred place in Vrndavana and the most hallowed tirthas on earth. Colourful birds sang from trees overhanging its playful waves and blooming lotuses.

Prompted by these auspicious omens, Radha arose from Her place. As Her closest friends helped Durga-devi to instal nine sacred pots around the Sarvabhauma-grha, Sri Radha ascended the throne.

Krsna had been watching intently from a secluded grove. The sight of Srimati Radharani assuming the throne made Him tremble and weep. Stunned, He finally collapsed unconscious into Subala’s arms.

Thus, when Vrnda arrived to bring Krsna to the bathing arena, she first had to assist Subala in reviving Him. When He regained consciousness, He began madly glorifying Sri Radha’s regal beauty.

Meanwhile, the wife of the sun, Chaya-devi, offered arati to Radharani, meditatively waving a five-pronged ghee lamp before Radha’s butter-soft limbs and the soothing glow of Her moonlike face.

The throngs of Vraja-vasi onlookers in their reverie felt as if the sun — desiring relief from the scorching effects of its own rays — was seeking shelter in the cooling effulgence of the moon.

While the crowd was lost in thought, Vrnda brought Krsna there, unseen, and hid Him behind the seat of Paurnamasi. That mystic yogini then addressed Bhavani, the mistress of the material energy:

“Is this the proper moment for hopeful beings like me to realise our desire?” Parvati answered, “In Vraja, the grace of Krsna makes every moment the perfect occasion to fulfil the innate desire of every *jiva*.”

Her face decorated with a mischievous smile, Paurnamasi clapped thrice and announced, “Behold my mystic power!” To the Vraja-vasis’ amazement, she then pulled Krsna out of His hiding place.

Krsna’s head was bowed, but His self-satisfied smile was plain for all to see. He suddenly found Himself standing next to Radharani, His mind unsteady, hands quivering, eyes moist with tears.

The arrangement of divine providence had now placed Radha and Krsna in a familiar dilemma: In the presence of elders, how could They restrain Themselves, especially from exchanging glances?

The solution lay in the hypnotic effect the divine couple had upon the assembled gopas and their wives, an effect that enabled Radha and Krsna to exchange unnoticed but mutually satisfying glances.

In that exchange, Krsna’s glance was like a begging bowl extended by the pauper of love, and Radha’s glance was like coins of gold bestowed by the monarch of divine emotions and self-satisfied beauty.

The Vraja-vasis were bewitched by the spell of Krsna’s transcendental qualities and features, while their minds were like patchwork quilts woven from thoughts of Krsna’s divine form and qualities.

Some saw Krsna’s silken complexion as a downy cloud suffused with lightning. Others saw the feather in His topknot as a dancing peacock eager to enjoy the rain of Srimati Radharani’s coronation bath.

Others saw in His shark-shaped earrings evidence of the charms that melted their hearts, and in His broad chest they saw the night sky revelling in the twenty-four-and-a-half moons of Sri Radha’s form.

And still others saw in His rolling eyes an intoxicated bull-elephant who had entered the foaming river of Vrndavana’s most beautiful women to cool the scorching effects of lordly virility.

With the sweet music of His flute, Krsna broke the Vraja-vasis’ trance and signalled the beginning of the coronation bath, enticing everyone to sing and dance, each to his or her own heart’s content.

The forest animals also accepted Krsna’s invitation to join in the kirtana. Cuckoos sang in tune with the singers, bees hummed along with the vinas, and peacocks stepped in time with the dancers.

Uma began the bathing ceremony by chanting Vedic mantras and offering flowers, durva grass, puffed rice, and white mustard seeds. She then gently poured arghya over Radharani’s head.

Uma then washed Radha’s feet with water mixed with rose petals. Radha folded Her hands in respect, and Uma whispered, “Don’t offer me respect, offer me friendship when You play in the Yamuna.”

As goddess Uma offered acamana, madhuparka, a second acamana, and finally a second arati, she meditated constantly on her ambition to one day become a cowherd girlfriend of Sri Radha in Vrndavana.

Then, as some Gandharva girls sang the glories of Radha and Krsna, Lalita and the most prominent cowherd girls poured water over Radha and sprinkled Her with eight auspicious kinds of earth.

That earth was made even more auspicious and fragrant by its contact with the feet of the one who wanders throughout Vrndavana tending cows, searching for young *gopis*, and playing with His friends.

The fragrance of Krsna’s feet comforted Radha and exhilarated the senses of the Vraja-vasis. The gopis then bathed Radha in ??n??-gavya, eight more varieties of earth, honey, and finally kusa grass.

Young brahmanis came forward and sprinkled Radha with fragrant kunkuma, and then, using a pot with a thousand holes, bathed Her with water blessed by herbs, scents, seeds, flowers, and jewels.

Krsna tried to steal a glance at Radha’s figure — which was now just slightly revealed through Her water-soaked clothes — but His eyes were intercepted by the sweetness of Her kunkuma-stained face.

Not since the joyous festivities of Holi had Krsna seen this rare portrait of sacred beauty — streams of red liquid flowing down Srimati Radharani’s forehead, nose, cheeks, and chin.

Even though Krsna’s heart was eager to taste the nectarean vision of Her silhouette, His eyes held fast, refusing to focus on anything but Radha’s slightly downturned face and bashful, laughing eyes.
