Paurnamasi prepared for the next part of the abhiseka by touching Srimati Radharani’s head and throat, placing a kusa grass ring on Her finger, and soaking Her hands in gorocana.

Thereafter, Radha was bathed nine more times. Each bath was poured from a pot made of a different jewel: coral, ruby, pearl, diamond, cat’s-eye, emerald, yellow sapphire, blue sapphire, and gomeda.

Throughout these baths, Radha and Krsna, fearing that the Vraja-vasis would detect Their mutual attraction, suppressed Their loving ecstasies, hardly acknowledging each other’s presence.

Yogamaya had arranged the entire abhiseka for Their pleasure, but the divine couple, wary of recklessly exposing Their innermost feelings, would not even dare to look at each other.

All this was thoroughly amusing to Their intimate companions, especially to gopis like Sudevi and gopas like Vasanta, who treasured the opportunity to serve their masters with sweetly crooked words.

**\* \* \* \***

The first bath — pure water poured from a ruby pot—appeared like fresh nectar descended from the full moon flowing over golden creeper, its tendrils draped with strings of fine pearls.

Stoka-krsna joked, “Friend! You needn’t feel shy to steal glances at Radha. And why bother to suppress Your love? Everyone is so absorbed in watching Her that no one will even notice You.”

One gopi coached Radha, “Krsna is staring at You, but don’t look back at Him, even by mistake. In times like these, You can stoke His loving affection for You by acting as though You’re indifferent.”

Goddess Uma and her associates administered the second bath. They poured golden water from a sun-red coral pot over Radha’s dense, cloud-like hair and it flowed down onto Her moon-like face.

Still, that water could not conceal Srimati Radharani’s golden effulgence, which gradually spread throughout Umrao, touching everyone and everything with its hue and warmth.

Indeed, while everyone was enveloped in Radha’s spiritual radiance, Vasanta said, “Hari! Why be so shy? Everyone is blinded by Radha’s brilliance. Your bumblebee eyes can safely rest on Her lotus face.”

Samjna and Chaya performed the third bath using a pot made of pearls. The water from that bath appeared like rivers of grace flowing into the ocean of lustre, youth, and beauty — Sri Radha.

A contingent of ladies from the sun planet — angels, fairies, and society girls — assisted the two wives of Vivasvan by ringing bells, banging gongs, chanting mantras, and blowing conch shells.

In the midst of this din, Ujjvala teased Krsna, “Radha’s girlfriends have interpreted Your reluctance to gaze upon their mistress as a sign of fear, and so they are laughing at You. How intolerable!”

When the gopis saw that they had been able to ruffle Krsna and His friends, they tried to use gossip to provoke Radha as well, but She remained unperturbed; rather, She embraced Krsna with a glance.

By this time, the entire bathing arena was flooded, and although the spectators’ fish-like eyes were eager to play in the water that had washed Radha’s transcendental form, their desires were unfulfilled.

The Vaivasvata Manu-like Paurnamasi cast the Matsya-avatara-like eyes of Krsna into the ocean of Radha’s caranamrta. Those eyes grew so large that they left no room for other aquatics.

The personified rivers headed by Yamuna-devi inaugurated the fourth bath using a diamond-studded pot. The rays of light reflecting off the pot haloed Radha, and its waters praised Her with these words:

“O Radha! Krsna is the master of the three worlds and the treasure of Vaikuntha, but He serves You as His greatest wealth. Therefore You are our supreme empress and our ultimate goal. Glories to You!”

Meanwhile, Gandharva-gopa leaned over and said, “Hari! Queen Radha is concentrating on Her subjects. Be careful not to distract Her with Your flute-song, bodily fragrance, or sidelong glance.”

Krsna took the hint and doubled the influence of His potencies. As Sri Radha flushed with passion, Paurnamasi jokingly chided the rivers for making Radha turn red from the force of their waters.

Rebuked by Yogamaya, Yamuna gave way to neutral gopis like Syamala, who bathed Radharani with a pot made of cat’s-eye. As she poured water, she inflamed Radha’s prema with these words:

“May this fifth bath, the liquid form of the fifth mellow, bestow upon You the fifth perfection. By that perfection may You realise Your innermost desire to have Krsna as an obedient, submissive lover.”

The gopa Vidagdha heard these words and gave contrary instructions to Krsna: “Syamasundara! If the sight of Radharani causes You to tremble as if You were cold, then why do You look at Her?”

Syamala understood Vidagdha’s intent and spoke to Radha: “Krsna is but an arm’s length away, and yet He ignores You. Rebuke such mean-spiritedness. Pierce His heart with a callous glance.”

Before the gopas and gopis could provoke an argument, Vrnda, the keeper of Vrndavana forest, lifted an emerald pot high in the air and whispered beguiling words into Radharani’s receptive ears.

Vrnda-devi said, “With this sixth abhiseka, I shall bathe Your six limbs and request that, with the six qualities of a monarch, You protect this transcendental forest of six seasons.”

The water falling from Vrnda’s pot turned yellow-black like honey as it flowed over Radha’s hair, white like milk as it touched Her face, and it foamed like a waterfall as it cascaded over Her limbs.

Radha gazed eagerly at Krsna. She could understand that His uncontrollable trembling was a sign of His transcendental longing to be able to serve Her in the unique way Vrnda was doing.

The gopa Kinkini also noticed Krsna’s ecstasy and complained, “Your trembling causes Your waist bells and ankle bells to ring so loudly that I, Your little bell, have become redundant. Should I go away?”

Radha’s face gradually became free of water and Her blue-lotus glance caught Krsna’s eyes, transforming His cold trembling into a statuesque rigidity, eliciting peals of laughter from Krsna’s friends.

This sweet exchange between the divine couple soon came to an end, however, when the youngest amongst the groups of gopis began to bathe Radharani with water from a yellow-sapphire pot.

**\* \* \* \***

Gopis like Kundalata and Dhanistha competed amongst themselves to perform the seventh auspicious bath, the sight of which made the women from the seven upper planets turn pale from ecstasy.

Radha’s dominion extends to all corners of the universe, and so residents throughout the fourteen planetary systems felt as if the water splashing over Radha was nectar pouring over their own limbs.

And because Krsna is within everyone’s heart, He felt the bliss of each of these residents to be His own. Lost in the divine rapture of the moment, Krsna hardly heard Madhumangala remark:

“Friend! You are warmly dressed. Why then do You tremble in the same way as Radha, who is covered in wet bathing clothes in the open air? Her shivering is understandable, but I don’t know about Yours!”

The eighth bath was offered by gopis who first blessed Radha with the words, “May this abhiseka protect Vraja, conquer the pride of Candravali, and broadcast Your glories throughout the universe.”

This eighth abhiseka was poured from a shimmering blue sapphire pot by gopis like Kasturi, who had renounced all personal enjoyment — even the pleasure of Krsna’s embrace—just to make Radha happy.

These girls imbued the bathing waters with the love of Radha’s eight closest friends in the hopes that it would enhance Her bliss with the eight existential ecstasies like crying, horripilation, and paling.

These gopis shared a secret desire known only to Srimati Radharani: they wanted to proclaim Radha’s supremacy over Laksmi by demonstrating the supremacy of Radha’s maidservants over Laksmi’s.

Knowing this, Radha secretly mounted the chariot of these gopis’ hearts, and with the charioteer of Her beauty, rode to Vaikuntha to conquer with rasa the Laksmi-servants of Narayana.

Krsna witnessed Radha’s divine conquest, glorifying it with a honey-soaked flute-song. That melody nested so deeply in Radha’s ears that it could not be flushed out with any amount of water.

Her ears blocked, Radha lost Her emotional balance. No longer able to control Her love for Krsna, Her radiance flared up, silhouetting Her form more clearly, even through Her thick bathing clothes.

When Krsna beheld the singular beauty of Srimati Radharani’s form, the hairs on His body stood on end and tears sprang from His eyes, disclosing to the amused gopas exactly what was on His mind.

Unable to restrain himself, Subala joked, “By irrigating Your bodily hairs with tears, You have assumed the appearance of a field of thorn trees, awakening the curiosity of the Vraja-vasis. Better be discreet.”

Krsna accepted Subala’s advice and adjusted His wrapper, but not before the keen-eyed gopis noticed His excitement and revealed its cause to Radha, who then admonished Him with a sobering frown.

For the ninth and final abhiseka, Radha’s dear friends, like Madhurika and others, brought water in a rare and valuable pot decorated with golden and brilliantly shining gomeda gems.

Everyone present, including Radha, became aware that the unprecedented event was about to draw to a close. Thus their self-restraint washed away in the water cascading over Radha’s lotus feet.

Under the influence of prema, Lalita and Visakha were stunned. They stood frozen in place until prema prompted them to once more resume their service to Srimati Radharani.

Prema also exerted a unique effect on the other Vraja-vasis. Even though their eyes were fixed upon Radharani, they remained oblivious to the blatant facial expressions She directed at Krsna.

The heedless glances and raised eyebrows Radha cast Krsna’s way so agitated Him that He was unable to hold Her gaze. Shifting His eyes to Her feet, Krsna took on the appearance of boyish timidity.

The cowherd boys found Krsna’s love-struck display highly entertaining. In their jollity they embellished the abhiseka’s mantras, songs, and music with jokes, chuckling, and mimicry.

During the divine couple’s loving exchange, the nine opulences of Vrndavana forest transformed into nectar, and the gopis used that nectar to bathe Radha, endowing Her with unrivalled splendours.

These nine opulences are beyond words. To list them can only hint at their wonder: the joy of the monsoon season, the pleasure of autumn, the satisfaction of winter, and the happiness of the dewy season.

Then follows the brilliance of spring, the auspiciousness of summer, the satisfaction of autumn-winter, the splendour of dewy-spring, and finally the bliss of a summer monsoon.

When the distilled and liquefied essence of these nine qualities — themselves expansions of Sri Radha’s form — was imbued with the Vraja-vasis’ love for Radharani, it gave off an ineffable potency.

Yet as great as it was, when this glorious liquid cascaded over Radha’s delicate features, Her unmatched touch imparted to it a fresh, effervescent virtue that caused it to bubble and fizz with ecstasy.

The Vraja-vasis watched the ninth bath, amazed. Subala looked at Krsna’s face, only to find Hari being bathed by His own tears, with Radha reflected in His eyes! What abhiseka could be so wonderful!

Arjuna teased Krsna, but his words had no more effect than flowers tossed into the wind. Spellbound by the silent incantations of Radharani’s eyes and eyebrows, Krsna could not hear a thing.

The gopis’ romantic counsel also fell on deaf ears. By bathing Her with His gaze, Krsna had completely captured Radharani’s consciousness, which was now totally merged in thoughts of Him.

During the grand proceedings, Srimati Radharani’s braided hair had become loose, the painted motifs on Her skin worn away, Her garlands had fallen apart, and Her bathing clothes clung to Her body.

Radha knew that by Yogamaya’s arrangement no one but Krsna and Her girlfriends could see Her bodily beauty in drenched clothes, but when She noticed it Herself, out of modesty She lowered Her head.

The bathing ceremony complete, the gopis, goddesses, Gandharvas, and womenfolk of Vrndavana — not to mention Krsna — eagerly anticipated seeing Radha freshly dressed and ornamented.

Krsna thought, “What wonders will the rest of this coronation hold? What marvels will Radha’s ceremonial dress and royal crown reveal? How will She appear when finally seated on Her throne?”

**\* \* \* \***

The gopis guided Radha from the bathing throne to another magnificent seat. There they dried Her water-soaked body and clothed Her in a dress prepared especially for a monarch.

Radha’s girlfriends surrounded Her, forming a curtain to screen Her from sight. Their bodily effulgence lit up that enclosure so that Radharani’s maidservants could attend to their duties.

Some girls dried Radha’s limbs and others dabbed dry Her hair, while those who formed the curtain marvelled at Her unadorned beauty, which, they felt, easily eclipsed the beauty of Her awaiting garments.

So as not to disappoint them, Radha invested Her full allure in the dress She had been offered. Its embroidered swans immediately came to life and cooed in concert with the tinkling of Her ankle bells.

Radha then accepted a delicate black bodice scented with perfumes like musk. That bodice, like Her complexion, gave off a soft reddish glow that revealed Her heart’s passion and radiated love for Krsna.

Krsna and the Vraja-vasis were not able to see Radha, but they could see, radiating from behind the gopi-screen, that golden effulgence, tinged with red, which pleased and pacified their eyes and hearts.

The gopis had finished dressing Radharani, but because the royal jewels, sceptre, and crown were to be offered later as part of the coronation ceremony, they had not yet ornamented Her.

So that She would not be completely unadorned, the gopis decorated Radha with flower ornaments. Thus She appeared like a floral goddess of Vrndavana, making the nearby trees shudder in bliss.

Radha’s dressing complete, the gopis stood aside in one smooth motion, causing the onlookers to erupt in joyous applause. Meanwhile, Radha and Krsna gazed at each other without restraint.

The queens of heaven offered arati to Radha before She was led to yet another throne. Once there She was shown Her reflection in a bowl of fresh ghee that represented the molten love of Her followers.

Radha then called for gifts to distribute to Her subjects. The first gift was a box made of flower buds engraved with Radharani’s name and filled with flower petals. She gave this special box to Paurnamasi.

She also gave baskets of jewels to the brahmanas who were supervising the ceremony. When She asked Madhumangala what sort of gift He would like, Madhu respectfully joined his palms and said:

“Please give me Your most cherished treasure!” All the gopis laughed when they heard this answer, but, bound by Her promise, Radha held out a flower garland and somberly replied, “O Bato! Take your gift.”

Madhumangala understood that Radha had given him Krsna. Overjoyed, he embraced Krsna by the neck and began to dance about in such a way that it appeared Krsna was dancing with Madhu.

But this unsettled Krsna. He had made so much effort to unite with Radha, and She had given Him to another? Sensing Krsna’s concern, Ujjvala joked, “Friend, You look pale and thin. Better sit down.”

The cowherd girls were so amused by Krsna’s despondency that they let their veils fall from their heads. Within full view of troubled Krsna, they freely laughed along with Krsna’s friends.

It was now time for Radha to receive blessings. Paurnamasi began by saying, “Enjoy the kingdom of Vrndavana!” Then other elders bestowed a variety of benedictions befitting a reigning monarch.

When all blessings had been bestowed, Radha made Her first decree: “Let all bound creatures be liberated!” This was good news for those creatures still bound by the modes of material nature.

But the same words struck fear in the hearts of the perfected gopis and gopas, who were also bound — bound to Krsna by the ropes of love. Only a reassuring glance from Radha could dispel their fear.

Merely by sitting on the throne of Vraja, Radha increased the bounty of the twelve forests — the rivers flowed with nectar, the mountains erupted in gems, the trees oozed honey, and the cows dripped milk.

Krsna’s presence in Vrndavana makes it exceptionally beautiful, but now that Srimati Radharani was about to be crowned, Vrndavana’s beauty had increased a thousand-fold. Krsna was overjoyed.

To increase Krsna’s pleasure further, the moment arrived for Radha’s ceremonial dressing, worship, and ascension to the throne. All this was to take place within the central chamber, beneath madhavi vines.

Paurnamasi organised a procession that placed Krsna, His friends, and the goddesses in front. Radha’s girlfriends encircled Her in the middle, and the assembly of satisfied guests followed behind.

By having Krsna lead Radha from one chamber of the grove to another, Paurnamasi broadcast the inner purpose of the coronation: to confirm that Radha was Krsna’s and that Krsna was Radha’s.

When the assembly reached the central chamber, Radha was seated on an ivory throne. All the Vraja-vasis sat facing Her — the boys to Her left, the elders in front, and the goddesses to Her right.

As Yamuna and Manasa-ganga waved camaras at either side of Radharani, the Vraja-vasis silently watched as Her golden effulgence mingled with Her beguiling scent to form a wispy golden mist.

The Vraja-vasis also witnessed that the gopis who were preparing to dress and decorate Radha had themselves become dressed and decorated by the radiance of Radha’s sweetness, beauty, and aroma.

Once again the gopi-curtain encircled Radha, screening Her from the eyes of Her subjects, but not from the all-pervasive mind of Krsna, the master of yoga, for He was absorbed in trance upon Her.

Samjna untied Radha’s cloud-like hair and let it fall to Her hips. The unbinding of Radha’s tresses released a fragrance that assailed Krsna’s sense of smell, and its allure caused His breathing to flutter.

After rubbing Radharani’s hair with a cloth, Samjna parted it in front and then braided it. Finally it resembled a black vine decorated with tiny flowers, a variety of gems, and Krsna’s bee-like mind.

She placed a peacock feather at the end of the braid and asked Chaya to add scented oils, tiny garlands of delicate flowers, and sindura to the part — staining red Krsna’s already passionate mind.

Chaya finished dressing Srimati Radharani’s hair by setting a dazzling jewel in the part and placing an ornate crown on Her head, which then resembled a star-filled sky surrounding the full moon.

When the gopis beheld the goddess’s handiwork, they trembled and paled from ecstasy. But their desire to dress Radharani overcame these divine obstacles, and they set about their own services.

One gopi began by painting patterns on Srimati Radharani’s face: budding creepers on Her forehead, flowering creepers on Her cheeks, and a bumblebee-like spot of kasturi on Her chin.

Another gopi drew a line of kajjala behind Radha’s eyelashes, hung a pearl on the tip of Her nose, and sparkling jewels on Her ears, jewels that threw reflections from Her pink cheeks to Krsna’s roving mind.

Just as a third gopi was about to redden Radha’s lips, she noticed how rosy Radha’s smile already was. Thinking that lipstick would only hide the natural beauty of these lips, she put down her brush.

The same girl then fastened a choker engraved with Krsna’s name around Radha’s conch shell neck, strings of pearls and gems upon Her black bodice, and valuable rings on all ten of Her fingers.

Meanwhile, the swan of Krsna’s mind played within the lake of Sri Radha’s form: Her arms resembled waving lotus stems, Her navel, feet, hands, face, and eyes were like gently swaying lotus flowers.

But when a very young gopi laid gunja necklaces upon Radha’s soft cakravaki breasts, Krsna’s mind could no longer stay afloat. It drowned within the waters of that lake — to be seen no more.

Only after Campakalata placed garlands of lotus, asoka, mango, and jasmine flowers on the Radha-vine did that deceased Krsna-swan come back to life, but this time as a honey-starved bumblebee.

Krsna’s bumblebee mind buzzed playfully about Radha’s flower garlands until it was drawn to taste the sweet jingling coming from a belt of tiny bells that circled Radha’s full, round hips.

Radha’s dressing was complete when the gopis fitted Her with toe rings. They had also planned to lacquer Her soles, but Her feet turned red just by touching the ground, so the lac was deemed unnecessary.

To what can Radha’s fully ornamented beauty be compared? A golden vine fully blossomed? Mount Sumeru glistening in the sunshine? Hardly! Her indescribable glory can only be compared to itself.

The cowherd girls, also unable to answer this question, again stepped aside to allow the Vraja-vasis to judge for themselves whether any beauty in the creation could compare to Sri Radha’s.

But the vision of the regal queen of Vrndavana so amazed Krsna and the assembled onlookers that they were unable to truly evaluate Radha’s beauty. They could only agree that it was incomparable.

Taking advantage of the transfixed silence of the crowd, Parvati-devi initiated a loving exchange that she felt certain would disclose Radha’s and Krsna’s eternal companionship as the divine couple.

**\* \* \* \***

Parvati took a garland of blue lotuses that Yamuna-devi had gathered from her waters and fashioned with her own hands, and, without asking whom it was for, offered it to Sri Krsna.

Yamuna at once understood Parvati’s plan yet feigned surprise, chiding her, “Devi! Offering Krsna a garland means that you are His bride.” Parvati replied, laughing, “I’m always making mistakes.”

The goddess then removed the garland from Krsna’s neck, along with a gunja necklace. She then slid both over Radha’s head and said with an impish smile, “I am happily married, therefore these are for You.”

Yamuna took her cue from Parvati and removed the strand of gunja berries from Radha’s neck and gave it back to Krsna, saying, “Mother Parvati mistakenly took this necklace from You. Here!”

In Vedic culture, if a boy offers his garland to a girl, it is a request for her hand in marriage. If the same girl offers her garland or necklace to him in return, it is a sign that She has accepted his proposal.

It was not surprising, therefore, that Srimati Radharani blushed at the goddesses’ contrivance. Yet She did not protest, for it made explicit Her hidden desire: complete and perfect union with Krsna.

As a result, Radharani’s irrepressible happiness radiated through Her smile, glance, and complexion. She appeared like a single golden lotus rising above the cluster of Her blue lotus garland.

To more clearly affirm the union of the divine couple, Parvati respectfully approached Krsna and took some musk-paste from His chest, which she carefully placed on Radha’s forehead as tilaka.

Yamuna’s and Parvati’s humorous exchanges made the Vraja-vasi assembly laugh, inspired the demigods to shower flowers, and caused the trees to sway back and forth in a delightful swoon.

Just then, with a false hope to succeed where the gopis, devis, and Vraja-vasis had failed in fully glorifying Srimati Radharani, many accomplished poets stepped forward to offer Her prayers.

And although the poets’ hopes were not fulfilled, they did please Radha by their efforts. In return She offered them untold wealth, which the bards — who aspired for prema alone — respectfully refused.

Once the poets were silent, their recitations complete, Paurnamasi indicated that the time was nigh for Radha’s ascension to the throne and that the musicians and minstrels should make merry.

But before the musicians could raise their instruments or the minstrels could open their mouths, Krsna, in His undying eagerness to glorify Radha, played a beautiful melody on His flute.

As arriving goddesses showered flowers from their vimanas overhead, the Vraja-vasis chanted “Jaya!” and formed two lines that wound throughout the chambers to the Sarvabhauma-grha.

As Radha passed between admiring Vraja-vasis, She showered Her kinsmen with love-laden glances, but to the demigoddesses overhead She revealed only a limited vision of Her divine grace.

Behind Radharani, Krsna led a sankirtana party of singing and dancing cowherd boys. Beside Radha, Lalita and other loyal girlfriends carried the royal paraphernalia, such as a white parasol.

When the entire retinue arrived at the entrance to the Sarvabhauma-grha, they stopped for a moment to admire the opulent and expansive arena prepared by Vrnda-devi for Radha’s coronation.

The entire floor of the chamber was paved with gems and gold, its sides were marked with marble pillars joined by arches, and the dome above was an intricate lattice-work of twigs and flowering branches.

At the centre of the arena stood a tall bakula tree, luxuriant with fruits and flowers, in whose branches lived many wise birds who perpetually conversed about the innumerable wonders of divine love.

Beautiful jasmine creepers hung from the branches of the tree and rested on its trunk. Intoxicated bees swarmed around its flowers, and a soft breeze led a kirtana of its gently rustling leaves.

This tree appeared to change shape, at times displaying its original form, sometimes taking the shape of a giant lotus, and at others assuming a form as effulgent as it was incomprehensible.

At the base of the tree stood a special throne, said to be the king of thrones. A footstool covered in tiger skin, along with royal paraphernalia all fashioned from gold, accented this throne.

Above the throne was a white parasol, above that a canopy of flowers. To either side were white camaras fixed upon embossed stands. Behind the throne hung a latticework of tiny white jasmine flowers.

The sight of the throne of Vrndavana electrified Radha’s mind and stimulated novel ecstasies throughout Her body. She thought, “When I sit upon that throne, all of Krsna’s desires will be fulfilled.”

Radha’s ecstasy simultaneously induced a debilitating euphoria in Her closest girlfriends, but Lalita managed to subdue the effects of that bliss and lead Radha to the throne, where Vrnda-devi offered arati.

Then, as all the people, animals, and plants shed tears of joy, the demigoddesses showered flowers, and Krsna lost Himself to astonishment as Srimati Radharani placed Her foot on the footstool.

Holding Lalita’s hand, Radha ascended the throne and gracefully assumed Her seat. Brahmanas chanted the appropriate mantras as She raised Her right palm in benediction, blessing Her subjects.

Paurnamasi offered a second arati, during which Vrnda-devi and her forest goddesses offered Radharani flowers and obeisances as symbols of their fealty to Vrndavana’s newly crowned queen.

Radha then noticed that Her superiors were still standing and requested that they be given seats. One of those seats was a dazzling throne meant for Krsna, next to but not adjoining Her own.

Each guest perceived Krsna’s seat differently, according to his or her mood. Those whose hearts were steeped in amorous love thought they saw Krsna seated right beside Radha — on Her own throne.

Those guests who were in a parental mood — although they were aware that Radha and Krsna were attracted to each other — saw the divine couple seated on separate thrones.

Once the seating formalities were complete, Paurnamasi caught everyone’s attention and, carrying an embroidered cushion that bore a crown and a sceptre, solemnly approached Sri Radha’s throne.

Amidst the Vraja-vasis constant chanting of “Jaya! Jaya!” Paurnamasi bestowed upon Radha the emblems of Vraja’s sovereignty, loudly proclaiming Her to be the queen of Vraja — Vrndavanesvari.

As Radha accepted the insignia of Vraja, everyone in that majestic kunja gasped as they witnessed a miracle: the throne, sceptre, and crown — already as brilliant as the sun — became twice as effulgent.

This marvel served as conclusive proof that Radha had not just then been empowered to become the queen of Vrndavana, as the source of power behind its royal emblems, She was eternally Vraja’s queen.

Sri Radha’s sovereignty firmly established, the time came for Her to appoint a royal cabinet, but because She was shy in the presence of Her superiors, She looked tentatively at Paurnamasi.

The all-knowing brahmani pacified Radharani with a nod and a wave of her hand and requested Krsna to make the parliamentary appointments, an honour He gladly accepted.

Lalita was the natural choice for Prime Minister, Visakha for Personal Secretary, and Vrnda for Forestry Minister. Similarly, all other *gopis* were appointed to offices perfectly befitting their natures.

After Krsna had filled every government post, dancers, musicians, and orators gathered by a stage to the left of the throne in readiness to perform for the pleasure of Queen Radha and Her august assembly.

First, the dancers portrayed Radharani’s qualities and pastimes in movement and gesture. Then the singers glorified Her with lyrical poetry set to music that left even Krsna enraptured.

As excellent as the performances were, however, Radharani’s mind was elsewhere. Even in that royal court, overflowing with guests, She yearned to be alone with Krsna in a secluded place.

Sri Radha looked to the sky and prayed, “O creator! You protected Me from being consumed by My own anger, from being humiliated by My competitor, and from being confined by My in-laws.

“O great lord! After having saved Me thrice, will you not protect Me once more? Without your mercy, I fear that I will drown in a deluge of desire for Krsna’s enveloping embrace.”

Lalita and Visakha understood Radharani’s longing. Together they thought, “If Radha and Krsna could be alone, without Their subjects, Their sublime sweetness would reach its summit.”

Wise Paurnamasi could read the gopis’ minds, and so she immediately invited the goddesses to return to Svarga. The ladies of heaven collected their belongings and blessed Radharani with these words:

“O Radha! May You attain the good fortune of Your lover’s company, and may this majestic grove be known as ‘Unmada Radhika,’ the place where Radhika becomes intoxicated by love.”

After being properly respected by all those present, the demigoddesses and their heavenly assistants mysteriously disappeared — no one could ascertain how they had left or where they had gone.

Paurnamasi then asked the Vraja-vasi elders to return to their respective homes and sent a contingent of senior women to Mother Yasoda’s house with the message, “Radha’s coronation is completed.

“Since She is now the queen of Vrndavana forest, it is only befitting that Radharani spend the first night of Her reign in Her kingdom’s capital, Umrao. She will be safe under my supervision.”

Thereafter, Paurnamasi requested the performers to broadcast the news of the coronation throughout Vraja, and she tactfully reminded the gopas that it was time to herd their cows back to their barns.

Smiling at the various groups of young gopis, Yogamaya instructed them to resume their household chores, but warned that they must be ready to return and serve their queen at a moment’s notice.

Finally, Paurnamasi turned to Krsna and said, “Hari! I must personally narrate the details of today’s festival to the monarchs of Varsana. Until I return, stay here and protect Radharani.”

Krsna thought to excuse Himself and go in search of His cows, but Paurnamasi suddenly vanished. He was left standing in that palatial grove with Radha, Vrnda, and a few other intimate friends.

**\* \* \* \***

How quickly things had changed! Only moments before there had been countless people and tumultuous noise, now there were only a handful of gopis and utter silence. Finally Radha’s desire was fulfilled!

Paurnamasi having gone, Krsna casually looked around and then confidently approached Srimati Radharani’s glittering throne, engulfing it in the aura of His soothing monsoon-cloud complexion.

But Radha was not so easily eclipsed by Krsna’s billowing sensuality. She pierced His darkness with lightning-flashes of love, and arose from His dominance as the moon emerges from behind a cloud.

For a moment Radha and Krsna gazed into each other’s faces, and then, in the same way that the moon sinks into the ocean, They submerged Themselves in the waves of each other’s ambrosial beauty.

The divine couple drank deeply from the bliss of amorous love until, without warning, Krsna let loose at Radharani the sharp arrow of a fickle glance, piercing Her heart and sending Her reeling.

A seasoned warrior in the manoeuvres of love, Radha quickly recovered from Krsna’s ambush and returned His glance. Shot from the taut bow of Her eyebrows, it penetrated Krsna’s very being.

Amazingly enough, Krsna felt the force of Srimati Radharani’s red- hot glance, but it did not weaken Him in the least. Quite the opposite, it invigorated His mind and senses a thousand times over.

Not unlike the way a lion leaps on its prey, Hari stepped on the footstool to the surprise of the gopis. Without taking His eyes off Radha for a moment, Krsna seated Himself beside Her on the throne.

The cowherd girls were outraged by Krsna’s audacity. Suddenly the air was filled with high-pitched voices shouting: “Cunning knave!” “How dare You?” “Who do You think You are, cowherd!”

Pleased with Himself, Krsna raised His eyebrows and said matter-of-factly, “Silly girls! Did you not see that this whole day has been orchestrated to crown Radha as My queen! Where have you been?”

Lalita was so incensed that Krsna could be so impudent that she was momentarily speechless. Visakha stamped her foot on the ground, and the other gopis threw their hands in the air in exasperation.

Thereafter, a lively quarrel ensued with arguments and counterarguments echoing throughout the vast complex, bringing the loving exchanges of Radha and Krsna to fever pitch.

Just as Lalita was on the verge of physically accosting Krsna, Vrnda, amused, held back Lalita’s hand. With humble gestures she kept the gopis from further provoking Krsna. “Friends! Please stop shouting!”

Vrnda declared, “Radha is queen, the peerless monarch. By Her own power She subdues upstarts who dare challenge Her sovereignty. Very soon She will effortlessly subdue this forest tribal, Krsna.”

The gopis laughed heartily, but Krsna took Vrnda’s words as His cue and addressed Radha, sweetly smiling, “O beloved! Let’s make the most of this happy moment!” He then drew Her to His chest.

Srimati Radharani glanced to the heavens as if to thank the creator for fulfilling Her every desire. Wrapped in Krsna’s divine embrace, She simultaneously cried and laughed in bliss.

Afraid that Her crying might soak Her clothing, ornaments, and body, Krsna gently draped His left arm over Radha’s shoulders and with His right hand wiped the tears from Her lotus eyes.

By the magic of Krsna’s touch, the mountain of Radha’s sovereignty sank into the ocean of amorous love, and that noble, mighty queen, who had been crowned that very day, once again became a milkmaid.

For a moment, neither the cowherd girls nor Krsna could recognise the sweet gopi who sat demurely on the throne. Was She the same Vrndavanesvari who had just been regally installed at the abhiseka?

Indeed! Radha was the unchallenged queen of both Vraja and Krsna, but She now eagerly discarded Her dominion over the kingdom in exchange for the happiness of serving the dominant Hari.

Many familiar symptoms of ecstasy ornamented the divine couple, but when Their complexions began to glow an unusual blackish-gold, the gopis could appreciate that a special mellow was unfolding.

Apart from the boldness He had shown in assuming a seat on the throne of Vrndavana, throughout the entire day’s proceedings Krsna had maintained the mood of a submissive lover.

In contrast, even though Radha had renounced all sovereignty in Her subjects’ absence, during the day’s events divine love had dictated that Krsna would be most pleased if She assumed a dominant mood.

Therefore, casting an impish smile in the direction of Her gopi friends, Srimati Radharani clasped Her hands around Krsna’s neck, and with one fluid movement slid onto His lap.

So intense was this expression of love that it caused Cupid to faint. As a result, his ambassadors — Radha and Krsna — entered a state of loving ecstasy that was as difficult to behold as it is to describe.

Tears poured from Their eyes, perspiration flowed from Their pores, goosebumps appeared on Their skin, and Their paralysed limbs trembled like leaves tossed by a faint spring breeze.

With choked voices, Radha and Krsna tried to express Their emotions, but They repeatedly fainted into each other’s arms, like trees swept up against one another during a tropical storm.

The gopis panicked, fearing that Radha and Krsna would fall from Their seat onto the ground, but because the girls were stunned by ecstasy, they were unable to move or to do anything about it.

Thus concluded Madhava’s festival of love, in which Radha agreed to ascend the throne prepared for Her by Krsna’s servants, and to accept dominion over Vrndavana forest and its inhabitants.

Even while the Vraja-vasis were present, Krsna wanted it known that Radha was as much His ruler as She was theirs. And when the Vraja-vasis departed, Krsna affirmed this truth by sharing Her throne.

By accepting Krsna’s lap as Her throne, and by treating Him as Her property, Radha reciprocated Krsna’s desire to belong to Her. Thus Their exchange of love was complete and perfect.

Both Radha and Krsna, the personifications of beauty, the temples of youthfulness, the kingdoms of all good qualities, the treasuries of all kinds of bliss, and the substances of the sixty-four arts —

The heavenly flowers scented by Cupid’s own fragrance, the monsoon showers of amorous love, the pinnacles of divine charm, and the loving shelters of Their devotees, accepted lordship over Vraja.

The astonished gopis were immobilised by seeing Radha and Krsna in a state of supreme bliss. But their irrepressible desire for service subdued their ecstatic symptoms, and soon they were able to move.

Revived themselves, the gopis then attempted to revive the divine couple in different ways, by fanning Them, by massaging Their feet, and by chanting the names of Their beloveds into Their ears.

Krsna was first to regain consciousness. Once He did, He helped the others soothe Radharani’s intense ecstasies. And when She again became tranquil, He placed tambula into Her mouth.

The image of Radha seated upon Krsna’s lap, surrounded by gopi friends engaged in a variety of services to Them, is the image of perfection that devotees may contemplate while visiting Umrao.

Having witnessed this event, the gopis thought, “Our friend Radharani has overcome all types of difficulty and inconvenience in order to fulfil Krsna’s desire to coronate Her as queen of Vrndavana.

“During the ceremony She revealed new facets of love for Krsna. Now She has artfully brought Him and Vraja under Her control. O heart! Having seen this, do you have any other desire to be fulfilled?”

**\* \* \* \***

Dear devotees! As I was preparing to describe Giriraja, my heart was captured by Radha’s maha-abhiseka at Umrao, and I felt compelled to narrate the pastime as it was originally told by Jiva Gosvami.

Krsna ordered that this festival be called Madhava-mahotsava; otherwise— He reasoned — naming it after Radha would cause Her embarrassment, whereas Madhava already implies Her name.

It may seem like I have described this pastime in detail, yet what I have written is merely a fragment of the divine emotions and delightful events that eternally take place at Umrao.

May those Vaisnavas who sit at the base of the willow tree in Krsna Valley and respectfully hear or describe the pastime of Madhava- mahotsava become the recipients of the following blessings of Sri Jiva:

**“O my ears, lips, and tongue! May the words describing the abhiseka of the queen of Vrndavana protect you birth after birth, and give you devotion to Radha and Krsna.**

**“O nostrils! May the fragrance of Radha’s divine abhiseka protect you! O eyes and hands! May the beauty and the happiness of service to the place of this abhiseka pastime nourish you!”**

## Appendix 7

**The Concealed Meaning of the Gopis’ Talks**

(This appendix follows from the narration of Krsna\`s pastimes at Sonkh with Madhumangala and the gopis, as described in chapter five.)

In order to reach a deeper understanding of the confidential meanings of the gopis’ talks, the following appendix addresses the subjects of “direct meaning” and “indirect meaning,” and the ways in which the two relate to the “crooked” nature of divine love. The first section discusses the philosophical reason for concluding that the gopis’ statements should be understood indirectly and not directly. The second section, which follows logically from the first, discusses the reason the gopis speak indirectly even amongst themselves: generally because they are too shy to openly express their true feelings towards Krsna, even to one another. The third section deals with the meaning of the “crooked” characteristics themselves; in short, if Krsna’s lover expresses a desire to do something for her own enjoyment, Krsna is happier to fulfil her desire than He is to accept something for His own pleasure.

**\* \* \* \***

The gopis, like all the Vraja-vasis, have not even the slightest trace of desire for their own personal enjoyment. They want only Krsna’s happiness and Krsna’s enjoyment, at any cost.

Thus when the gopis express the desire to prepare a feast, they do so not because they themselves want to relish tasty dishes, but because they want Krsna to enjoy tasty dishes. This desire for Krsna’s happiness is the sole impetus for anything and everything the gopis do, even when they seem to profess the opposite.

During the pastime in Sonkh, to which this appendix is attached, when the gopis’ say, “We want to enjoy a feast cooked with these fresh, wild vegetables,” it should be understood that they mean, “We want Krsna to enjoy a feast cooked with these fresh, wild vegetables.” To accept the direct meaning of the gopis’ words would be to accept a meaning incompatible with the very definition of pure devotion given by Srila Rupa Gosvami: “When first-class devotional service develops, one must be devoid of all material desires, knowledge obtained by monistic philosophy, and fruitive action. The devotee must constantly serve Krsna favourably, as Krsna desires.” Since the direct meaning of the gopis’ words is contrary to the very essence of the pure devotion that fuels their every thought, word, and deed, the indirect meaning given above is the only sensible interpretation.

Indeed, the gopis’ often express themselves through indirect meanings, hiding their true intent, much like the great sages and the Vedas sometimes do. In this way they all bring pleasure to Krsna, who favours such esoteric language.

In speaking to Uddhava Krsna says:

*veda brahmatma-visayas tri-kanda-visaya ime*  
*paroksa-vada rsayah paroksam mama ca priyam*

**“The Vedas, divided into three divisions, ultimately reveal the living entity as pure spirit soul. The Vedic seers and mantras, however, deal in esoteric terms (paroksa-vadah), and I also am pleased by such confidential descriptions.”**

Krsna explains the reason that sages and scriptures use indirect meanings: “I am never manifest to the foolish and unintelligent. For them I am covered by My internal potency, and therefore they do not know that I am unhorn and infallible.” In other words, the Supreme Lord does not allow Himself to be taken cheaply. He cannot be understood directly by superficial or inimical people; rather, by gradual purification of the heart, the statements of scriptures become self-evident, statements, for example, such as *apani-pado,* “He has no hands and feet.” The intended meaning of this statement is that “He has no material hands and feet. They are spiritual.” Only a person in purified consciousness can know the intended meaning of such statements.

**\* \* \* \***

The second point dealt with in this appendix is closely related to the first. The following section examines why the gopis would communicate their feelings in such an indirect way. The gopis concealed the fact that they were actually preparing the feast for Krsna’s pleasure because they were too shy to reveal their true feelings to one another. Therefore they expressed themselves indirectly, saying instead that they wanted to cook a feast for themselves. This concealment shows that the nature of amorous love is so confidential that the gopis, in their daily conduct, conceal that love even from one another.

In the Venu-gita chapter of Srimad-Bhagavatam, Sukadeva Gosvami confirms this confidential nature of amorous love. When the gopis first heard Krsna’s flute-song, they confided in their most intimate friends but hid their true feelings, not openly speaking with one another. Sukadeva said: “When the young ladies in the cowherd village of Vraja heard the song of Krsna’s flute, which arouses the influence of Cupid, some of them privately (paroksam) began describing Krsna’s qualities to their intimate friends.” Commentators on the Bhagavatam attest to the fact that the gopis are indeed reluctant to reveal their love of Krsna, even to one another.

This raises some interesting questions. If the gopis felt the need to reveal their hearts to some intimate friends without openly speaking with one another, in whom did they confide at such times, from whom did they conceal their feelings, and why?

To properly understand the answers to these questions, we must first acquaint ourselves with the variety of loving moods that the various kinds of heroines in Vraja have towards Krsna. In his Sri Ujjvala-nilamani, Rupa Gosvami analyses this subject in detail. In summary, those gopis who have similar moods are the friends who confide in each other, and those whose moods are sufficiently dissimilar do not share their intimate thoughts.

Later, however, when compelled by the overwhelming force of Krsna’s flute-song, a few gopis began to speak more openly about the flute’s player. In this atmosphere, the other gopis were able to admit that they too shared the same ecstatic love within their hearts. Overwhelmed by love for Krsna, and hence for one another, the gopis embraced each other. Sukadeva Gosvami describes the scene: “O King, when the young ladies in Vraja heard the sound of Krsna’s flute, which captivates the minds of all living beings, they all embraced one another and began describing it.”

In the pastime under discussion, the gopis who were most desirous of cooking a feast in Vatsavana could recognise that their mood of love was distinct from that of some of their companions; still, they had cooking to do. Thus they hid their feelings from one another.

The gopis’ shyness and discretion in their dealings with each other are a part of the culture in Vraja, a tradition broken only under special circumstances, such as an overwhelming flood of loving emotions, or Krsna’s special desire. An example of the former has been given above, when the gopis embraced one another after being overwhelmed by the sound of Krsna’s flute. Rupa Gosvami gives an example of the latter:

**“Lord Krsna made Srimati Radharani close Her eyes in shame before Her friends by His words relating Their amorous activities on the previous night. Then He showed the highest limit of cleverness in drawing pictures of dolphins in various playful sports on Her breasts. In this way Lord Hari made His youth successful by performing pastimes in the bushes with Sri Radha and Her friends.”**

We can respectfully apply the principle of speaking indirectly the glories of the Lord in our preaching. Devotee preachers discreetly avoid discussing in public the gopis’ love for Krsna, because in principle by discussing confidential topics indiscriminately to unqualified persons one commits the ninth offense to the holy name of the Lord:

**“It is an offense to preach the glories of the holy name among persons who have no intelligence or no faith in the subject matter. Such people should be given the chance to hear the chanting of the Hare Krsna mantra, but in the beginning they should not be instructed about the glories of the spiritual significance of the holy name. By constant hearing of the holy name, their hearts will be purified, and then they will be able to understand the transcendental position of the holy name.”**

By nature, amorous love is confidential; and when such amorous love is exchanged between paramours, secrecy is its very essence. This paramour love characterises the gopis’ relationship with Krsna. By the very fact that it is forbidden, such love surpasses the pleasures of nuptial bliss. Since the very purpose of love on the spiritual platform is to increase Krsna’s pleasure, this paramour love with the gopis is more pleasing to Krsna than any other kind of love.

Another aspect of spiritual love is that it seeks to conceal itself, masquerading as something other than what it truly is. For this reason the gopis love is generally called kama or transcendental lust. For example, the gopis sometimes say that they want to enjoy Krsna’s embrace. This appears to be lust. But such a statement should be seen in the same light as their expressed desire to enjoy a feast: their true desire is for Krsna to enjoy their embrace. But in both instances the gopis’ only desire, under different pretexts, is to give pleasure to Krsna. In much the same way, Krsna will sometimes behave like a lusty boy by showing fidelity to a particular girl, and then by enjoying one flower-like gopi after another. But that behaviour is only apparent, for Krsna’s true intent is to reciprocate the love of His devotees through the exchange of amorous pastimes.

The secretive and deceptive nature of amorous love can be described with one word: “crooked”. Rupa Gosvami likens this crookedness to the movements of a snake:

*aher iva gatih premnah svabhava-kutila bhavet*  
*ato hetor ahetos ca yunor mana udancati*

**“The progress of loving affairs between a young boy and a young girl is like the movement of a snake. On account of this, two types of anger arise between a young boy and girl — anger with cause and anger without cause.”**

It should not be difficult to understand why Krsna prefers crooked, deceptive dealings: they add mystery and wonder to His pastimes. Indeed, these two qualities fuel loving exchanges and contribute to the ever-expanding nature of divine love.

Furthermore, although not every action of Krsna and His loving associates is beset with crookedness, when crooked dealings do occur new avenues of loving exchange open up, avenues that are inaccessible when relationships are limited by highly predictable, unwavering respect. After all, Krsna reciprocates in a unique way with an unlimited number of living entities. Once crookedness enters the repertoire of loving exchanges, the variety in loving dealings expands without limit.

**\* \* \* \***

The third and final point in our analysis deals with the “crooked” dealings themselves, and how such indirect dealings between Krsna and the gopis reveal the selfless nature of divine love.

The gopis falsely say that they want to enjoy a feast for this reason: if they express themselves in an indirect way, Krsna will be more inclined to appear and share in the feast than if they admit that it has been prepared for His pleasure.

True love’s nature is that the lover always wants to please the beloved. Thus whatever they may say, the gopis’ desire is only to please Krsna, and whatever Krsna may say, His only desire is to please the gopis. Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami describes this selfless nature of love: “The desire to gratify one’s own senses is kama \[lust\], but the desire to please the senses of Lord Krsna is prema \[love\].” In the same way, Krsna displays the symptoms of love when He desires to please the senses of His devotees.

Thus, when Krsna hears that the gopis are having a feast, and that His presence is required to please them, He becomes very eager to accommodate His beloveds. If, however, the Lord hears that the gopis are intent on cooking a feast for Him, Krsna’s eagerness to appear is not so strong.

Knowing this, the gopis—who have already given up everything for Krsna’s sake — surely will not hesitate to adopt crooked means and deceptive words for the same reason they have given up so many other things: to serve Krsna.

This kind of devotion, in which the lover literally lives for the happiness of the beloved, is very difficult for conditioned souls to understand because the nature of material conditioning is to imbue the heart with selfishness. Such all-pervading selfishness makes it extremely difficult for the hedonist to appreciate altruism in its most perfect and absolute form, the gopis' selfless love for Krsna.

How is it possible that the gopis' love for Krsna is devoid of the slightest taint of lust—that every aspect of their relationship with Krsna is only for the sake of His enjoyment? How is it that the gopis do not care for their own pleasures or pains — that all their activities, physical and mental, are directed only toward offering enjoyment to the Lord?

Such questions may invoke respect in the egotistic soul, but they are unanswerable by mundane intelligence. The gopis’ devotion, therefore, can be understood and realised only by souls who are completely free from material contamination.

Krsna explained to Arjuna the reason the Vraja-devis groomed and took care of their bodies: solely because their bodies gave pleasure to Krsna. Such is the purity and strength of the gopis love for Him. Krsna says, “O Arjuna, there are no greater receptacles of deep love for Me than the gopis, who cleanse and decorate their bodies because they consider them Mine.”

The gopis carefully attend to their personal appearance, but not for any selfish purpose. The young damsels of Vraja dress and ornament themselves as beautifully as possible only to make Krsna happy when He sees and touches them. As mentioned above, the gopis have already dedicated everything they possess to the service of Sri Krsna. Thus they take it for granted that their bodies are also meant for His enjoyment.

From whatever angle one examines the love between the gopis and Krsna, one can find not a trace of lust. “Krsna has obtained so much pleasure by seeing me,” the gopis think. And with that thought their faces and bodies increase in fullness and beauty. The joy they feel from such fullness in turn increases Krsna’s own beauty and sweet' ness, making His joy expand. As Krsna’s beauty and sweetness swell, the gopis’ beauty increases yet again, and in this way a competition takes place in which no one acknowledges defeat. In fact, it is the joy of the gopis that nourishes the joy of Lord Krsna, and for that reason the fault of lust is not present in their love.

The gopis’ love for Krsna is so pure that they know joy only when Krsna is happy. Moreover, the gopis condemn even their own happiness if the ecstasies resulting from that happiness interfere with their service to Krsna. Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami writes:

**“Whenever there is unselfish love, that is its style. The reservoir of love derives pleasure when the lovable object is pleased. When the pleasure of love interferes with the service of Lord Krsna, the devotee becomes angry toward such ecstasy.”**

In his purport to the above verses, Srila Prabhupada makes two points relevant to the discussion at hand: First, the gopis’ feelings of happiness are “indirect,” in that they depend on Krsna being pleased. Srila Prabhupada’s use of the word “indirect” here serves to clarify the pure, dependent nature of the gopis’ love for Krsna; and to emphasise the point, Srila Prabhupada says, “Causeless love of Godhead is always so.” Since devotees condemn even happiness that prevents them from serving Krsna, it is self-evident that they would never forsake Krsna’s service for personal pleasure, not to mention liberation. And Krsna, who is bound by His vow to reciprocate the love of His devotees, also goes out of His way to give pleasure to the gopis and to fulfil their desires for serving Him.

In summary, the gopis’ indirect talks should be understood in the context of their unparalleled excellence in serving Krsna, which is based upon their exclusive understanding of His heart’s desire; no one else knows Krsna as well as they do. Therefore, one can trust that whatever they say or do — be it directly or indirectly — gives Krsna the greatest pleasure. The gopis know Krsna’s desires, and therefore they know how to render perfect loving service for His enjoyment and satisfaction.

As Krsna Himself says to Arjuna:

*man-mahatmyam mat-saparyam*  
*mac-chraddham man-mano-gatam*  
*jananti gopikah partha*  
*nanye jananti tattvatah*

**“O Partha, the gopis know My greatness, My loving service, respect for Me, and My mentality. Others cannot really know these.”**

## Appendix 8

**Maharaja Yudhisthira’s Answers to Yamaraja’s Questions**

(From Krsna Dharma’s Retelling of the Mahabharata)

(This appendix follows from the narration of Maharaja Yudhisthira’s meeting with the Yaksa at Dharma-kunda found in chapter five. It contains the questions posed by the Yaksa and the answers given by Maharaja Yudhisthira. This appendix is a reproduction of “Appendix 4” of Mahabharata, The Greatest Spiritual Epic of All Time, retold by Krsna Dharma Dasa.)

What makes one learned? How does one attain an exalted status? What is one’s second self and by what does one become wise?”

“By study of the Vedas one becomes learned. Asceticism raises one to an exalted status. Intelligence is his second self, and serving elders makes one wise.”

“What is the brahmanas’ divine attribute? What is their virtuous practise resembling that of the pious, what is their human attribute, and what practise of theirs resembles that of the impious?”

“Study of the Vedas is the brahmanas’ divine attribute. Their asceticism is like the virtuous practise of the pious, death is their human attribute, and slander by them is their impiety.”

“What is the divine attribute of ksatriyas, what is their piety and impiety, and what is their human failing?”

“Arrows and weapons constitute their divinity, sacrifice is their piety, and abandoning the distressed their impiety. Fear is their human Failing.”

“What is the Sama of sacrifice, what is its Yajur, and what can it not do without?”

“Life is the sacrificial Sama, mind is its Yajur, and it is the Rg which it cannot do without.”

Yudhisthira understood his actual meaning. By “sacrifice” he meant the spiritual sacrifice for obtaining pure knowledge. In the objective sacrifice which one normally performs with fire and mantras, the three Vedas — Sama, Yajur, and Rg — are required. In the subjective, spiritual sacrifice, the acquisition of true knowledge, along with life and mind, are as necessary as the mantras of the three Vedas are for the objective sacrifice. Particularly, spiritual sacrifice depends upon prayer, which is represented by the Rg mantras.

The Yaksa went on without pause. “What is of greatest value to cultivators, to those that sow, to those wishing for prosperity, and to those who bring forth?”

“Rain is the most valuable thing for cultivators, for sowers it is the seed, for those desiring prosperity it is the cow, and for those who bring forth it is the son.”

“What person, although breathing, endowed with intelligence, respected by the world and enjoying sensual pleasures, is nevertheless said to be not alive?”

“The person who does not satisfy the gods, guests, servants, ancestors and his own self with offerings of sanctified food is said to be dead even though breathing.”

“What is weightier than the earth? What is higher than the sky? What is fleeter than the wind? And what is more numerous than grass?”

“The mother is weightier than the earth. The father is higher than the sky. Mind is fleeter than the wind and thoughts are more numerous than \[the blades of\] grass.”

Yudhisthira understood that by serving the mother one was said to obtain the earth and by serving the father one could rise to heaven.

The Yaksa continued, “What does not close its eyes when sleeping ? What does not move after birth? What has no heart, and what swells with its own force?”

“Fish do not close their eyes when sleeping. Eggs do not move after birth. A stone has no heart and a river swells with its own force.”

“Who is the friend of an exile, of a householder, of a sick person, and of a dying man?”

“An exile’s friend is his companion, that of a householder is his wife, the physician is a sick person’s friend, and charity is a dying man’s friend.”

“Who is the guest of all creatures? What is the eternal religion? O king of kings, what is life-giving nectar, and what pervades this entire universe?”

“Agni is the guest of all creatures. Cows’ milk is life-giving nectar. Offering ghee into fire sacrifices made to the Lord is the eternal religion, and this entire universe is pervaded by air.”

“What is it that wanders alone? What is born again after its birth? What is the antidote to cold, and what is the largest field?”

“The sun wanders alone. The moon is repeatedly born. Agni is cold’s antidote, and the earth is the largest field.”

“What is the highest refuge of virtue? What of fame? What of heaven, and what of happiness?”

“Liberality is virtue’s highest refuge, of fame it is charity, of heaven it is truth, and of happiness the highest refuge is good conduct.”

“What is the soul of a man? Who is the friend given to him by destiny? What is his chief support, and what is his chief refuge?”

“The soul of a man is his son, his wife is the friend given by destiny, clouds are his principal support, and charity his best refuge.”

“What is the best of all laudable objects, of all sorts of wealth, and of all kinds of happiness? And what is the most important of all gains?”

“Skilfulness is the most laudable object. Knowledge is the greatest wealth. Health is the greatest gain, and contentment is the highest happiness.”

“What is the greatest virtue in the world? What religion always bears fruits? What is it that which if controlled never leads men to misery? And with whom does friendship never break?”

“Abstention from harming any creature is the greatest virtue. The religion of the three Vedas is always fruitful. The mind if controlled never leads to misery, and friendship with the righteous never breaks.”

“What is it that, by renouncing, makes a man dear to others? What is it which if given up never leads to misery? What is it which if renounced leads to wealth, and what is it which if renounced leads to happiness.”

“Giving up pride makes one dear. Abandoning anger never leads to misery. Desire, if renounced, makes one wealthy, and abandonment of avarice leads to happiness.”

“For what does one give charity to brahmanas, to dancers, to servants, and to kings?”

“One gives to brahmanas for religious merit, to dancers for renown, to servants for their support, and one gives to kings for freedom from fear.”

“What is it that envelops the world? What prevents a thing from discovering itself? Why are friends forsaken, and what prevents one from going to heaven?”

“The world is enveloped with darkness. Spiritual ignorance prevents self-discovery. Friends are forsaken due to avarice, and connection with the world bars one from heaven.”

“For what is a person regarded as dead? What causes a kingdom to be seen as dead, and what makes a sacrifice dead?”

“A poor person, although living, is considered as good as dead. A kingdom without a king is considered dead, and a sacrifice without charity is dead.”

“What is the path one should follow? What is spoken of as water, as food, and as poison? What is the proper time for a sraddha?”

“Following in the footsteps of the righteous constitutes the proper path. Space is spoken of as water in the Vedic texts on cosmogony. The cow is considered as food, as it is from milk that ghee is obtained; ghee is then used in sacrifices, and thanks to sacrifices there is rainfall, from which we get food grains. A request is poison. The proper time for a sraddha is whenever a qualified brahmana is available.”

Yudhisthira was unsure if his answers were satisfying the Yaksa. He looked at him quizzically. “What is your opinion, O Yaksa?” But the Yaksa simply went on placing more questions.

“What is the characteristic of true asceticism? What of self-control? What constitutes forgiveness and shame?”

“Following one’s religious duties is asceticism. Self-control means keeping the mind fixed in remembrance of the Lord. Forgiveness consists of tolerating enmity, and freedom from shame means abstaining from all vile acts.”

“O King, what is said to be knowledge? What is tranquility? What is known as the greatest kindness, and what is simplicity?”

“Understanding Brahman is true knowledge. A peaceful heart is tranquility. Kindness consists of a desire for the welfare of all creatures, and simplicity means equanimity of mind.”

“What is man’s invincible enemy? What is his incurable disease? What man is regarded as honest, and what as dishonest?”

“Anger is the invincible enemy. Covetousness is the incurable disease. A man who is friendly to all creatures is honest, and the cruel man is dishonest.”

“What, O great monarch, is known as ignorance? What is spoken of as pride? What is understood to be idleness? And what is called grief?” “Not knowing one’s religious duties is ignorance. Pride means thinking oneself to be the doer of acts in this world, without recognizing that there is a supreme power in control of everything. Idleness is not performing one’s religious duty, and ignorance is grief.”

“What is known by the rsis as steadiness, and what as patience? What is said to be the best ablution, and what is spoken of as charity?”

“Steadiness means adhering firmly to one’s religious duties. Patience is controlling the senses. The highest ablution is to cleanse the mind of all impurities, and charity means to protect all creatures.” “Who is considered learned? Who is an atheist? Who is ignorant? What is spoken of as desire, and what as envy?”

“One who knows his duties is learned. An ignorant man is an atheist, and thus is an atheist ignorant. Desire means longing for worldly things, and envy is nothing more than grief of the heart.” “What is hypocrisy? What is the grace of the gods? What is called wickedness?”

“Falsely posing as a religious man is called hypocrisy. The grace of the gods is the result of charity. Wickedness means slandering others.” “Virtue, profit, and pleasure are opposed to one another. How then can these three co-exist?”

“When a husband and wife are happily united for the purposes of performing religious duties, then these three can exist together harmoniously.”

“Who, O best of the Bharatas, is doomed to eternal damnation? Speedily answer this question of mine.”

“One who summons a brahmana for alms, but then gives nothing, is condemned to everlasting hell. He also goes to unending hell who denies the truth of the Vedas, the brahmanas, the gods and the religion of his forefathers. Also that man who although wealthy refuses to give charity must suffer everlasting damnation.”

“O King, tell me with certainty what makes one a brahmana? Is it birth, good character, learning, or study of the Vedas?”

“Hear, O Yaksa, O worshipable one, what are the true characteristics of a brahmana. It is by behaviour alone that he is recognised. Birth and learning, even knowledge of all the Vedas, are useless if there is no good character. He alone is a brahmana who performs his religious duties, offering sacrifices and keeping his senses under control. Otherwise he must be considered no better than a sudra.”

“What is gained by agreeable speech? What is gained by he who acts only after careful thought? What does the man with many friends gain? And what does he gain who is given to virtue?”

“One who speaks agreeably becomes dear to all. One who acts with care obtains whatever he seeks. The man with many friends lives happily in this life, and the virtuous man obtains happiness in the next life.”

“What makes the soul rise out of his entanglement in matter? Who keeps him company, who is his guide on that spiritual journey, and on what is he established?”

“It is knowledge of the Supreme Lord which makes the soul rise. Godly qualities are his companions, dharma is his guide, and he is established on truth.”

“What makes one learned? How does one attain to that which is most exalted? How does one acquire a second self, and by what, O King, does one become wise?”

“One becomes learned by studying the Vedas. By asceticism one attains what is most exalted. Intelligence is like a second self, and serving one’s elders makes one wise.”

The Yaksa then asked about all kinds of subjects, ranging from worldly wisdom to knowledge of religion to spiritual matters. Yudhisthira answered them all without hesitation. Finally the Yaksa said, “I am satisfied. Answer my last four questions and I will restore one of your brothers to life. Who in this world is happy? What is the most wonderful thing? What are the tidings of this world, and how can one find the eternal path of religion?”

With folded palms, Yudhisthira replied, “He who is neither in debt nor exiled and who lives simply, eating simple food in his own home, is happy. The most wonderful thing is that although every day innumerable creatures go to the abode of death, still a man thinks he is immortal. The tidings are that in this world — which is like a cauldron with the sun as its fire, days and nights as its fuel, and months and seasons as its wooden ladle — all creatures are being cooked by time. The eternal religious path is found only in the heart of great mystics.”

The Yaksa smiled. “You have rightly answered every question.”

## Appendix 9

**Parikrama as an Uddipana for Pure Devotion**

(This appendix follows from the description of Terh-kadamba and Krsna’s pastimes there found in chapter six. It describes how visiting Krsna’s pastime places and hearing about the pastimes He enjoyed there both nourish devotion.)

Any devotional activity is more successful and more effective when executed with knowledge of its methodology (sadhana), desired goal (sadhya), and the chain of cause and effect that connects the two (hetu). This includes undertaking parikrama of Krsna’s holy dhama, physically or even mentally. Therefore, devotees may rightfully wonder how hearing about or visiting the places of Krsna’s pastimes can increase their devotion to Krsna. This appendix will address that question.

To circumambulate — literally, “to walk all the way around” — the abode of the Lord and visit the places where Krsna enacted His many pastimes is synonymous with the process of devotional service called pada-sevanam (“worshipping the lotus feet”). This process naturally includes, and hence is enriched by, hearing the Lord’s names, pastimes, and glories (sravanam), chanting His names and glories (kirtanam), remembering Him (smaranam), worshipping His Deity form (arcanam), and praying to Him (vandanam). In order to be truly effective, these five additional devotional practises must be accompanied by a sense of being the eternal servant of the Lord (dasyam). When spontaneous attachment to Krsna awakens the sense of servitude is also accompanied by feelings of friendship (sakhyam) and complete self-surrender (atma-nivedanam). Thus when performed properly, parikrama encompasses all the above nine types of devotional service enumerated by Prahlada Maharaja in Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Pada-sevanam generally refers to serving the lotus feet of the Lord according to the circumstances, but its essence is intense attachment to thinking of Krsna’s lotus feet while performing that service. Srila Prabhupada confirms: “Being intensely attached to thinking of the Lord’s lotus feet is called pada-sevanam.” Visiting sacred places like Jagannatha Puri, Sridhama Mayapur, and Vrndavana, or bathing in rivers like the Ganges or Yamuna, involves remembering Krsna with ever-increasing intensity and frequency until, not unlike Krsna’s intimate associates, one can never forget Him. This is also pada-sevanam.

The quintessential purpose of parikrama, therefore, is to learn how to remember Krsna always. The gopis showed the perfection of such remembrance in their talks with Uddhava:

**“When Krsna was here in the company of Sankarsana, He enjoyed all these rivers, hills, forests, cows, and flute sounds. All these remind us constantly of Nanda’s son. Indeed, because we see Krsna’s footprints, which are marked with divine symbols, we can never forget Him. O Uddhava! How can we forget Him when our hearts have been stolen away by the charming way He walks, His generous smile and playful glances, and His honey-like words?”**

While circumambulating the dhama, devotees see the places of Krsna’s pastimes, touch the dust that touched Krsna’s feet, bathe in lakes that embraced His form, see His footprints melted into rocks, and listen to narrations of His divine pastimes. By performing these devotional activities with faith, devotees surely remember Krsna, and their hearts become purified. This purification of heart is what makes parikrama successful.

**Remembering Krsna**

In the gopis’ aforementioned comments to Uddhava, the cowherd girls describe two aspects of remembering the Lord: constant remembrance of Krsna and the inability to forget Krsna. Although constant remembrance may be achieved by the regulated effort of a purified mind, such remembrance is not the last word in thinking of Krsna. Recollection of the Lord is more mature and exalted when one’s mind is spontaneously attracted to Krsna, remembering Him always, without separate endeavour. This is the extraordinary attainment of not being able to forget Krsna for even a moment.

In addition to the types of remembrance introduced by the gopis, Nanda Maharaja describes two more stages:

*smaratam krsna-viryani lilapanga-niriksitam*  
*hasitam bhasitam canga sarva nah sithilah kriyah*  
*saric-chaila-vanoddesan mukunda-pada-bhusitan*  
*akridan iksyamananam mano yati tad-atmatam*

**“As we remember the wonderful deeds Krsna performed, His playful sidelong glances, His smiles and His words, O Uddhava, we forget all our material engagements. When we see the places where Mukunda enjoyed His sporting pastimes — the rivers, hills, and forests He decorated with His feet — our minds become totally absorbed in Him.”**

The two subsequent stages of remembrance described above are total absorption in thoughts of Krsna and forgetting all material engagements. When devotees cannot forget Krsna, remembrance of Him further matures to the point of total absorption, culminating in the cessation of all external awareness. Caitanya Mahaprabhu demonstrated this rare and glorious state when, mistaking the ocean at Puri for the Yamuna, He ran into the waves and fainted in ecstasy, floating unconscious upon them for most of the night.

Our acaryas list five different stages of remembrance, namely hearing, acceptance, remembering, spiritual attainment, and ultimate perfection. The stage known as remembering is further divided into five stages: recollection, absorption, meditation, constant remembrance, and trance. The cultivation of these stages is summarised by Srila Prabhupada and described in more detail by Bhaktivinoda Thakura. However, knowledge of the many subtle nuances involved in the different stages of remembrance is not a prerequisite to successful parikrama, nor is it essential for grasping the parikrama’s effects. What should be understood is that according to the degree one’s heart has been purified, to that degree a person will, by the process of parikrama, remember Krsna. That remembrance — along with the other devotional activities it inspires — further purifies the heart, giving one a progressively expanding spiritual response to the sacred places of the Lord’s pastimes.

The consequence of remembrance is that spiritual emotions awaken in the heart. The awakening of emotion is essential in order for devotees to understand the effects of parikrama. Why? Because as sentient beings, eternally related to Krsna, the Supreme Sentient Being, the awakening of spiritual emotion is our goal.

**Remembrance Awakens Emotion**

Remembrance of Krsna produces spiritual emotion corresponding to the level of a devotee’s spiritual purity. At the stages of purity freed from all material influences, known as bhava and prema, such emotions are called “particular ecstasies” (vibhava). And at the stages in which the devotee is still conditioned by matter, the emotions arising from devotional remembrance are called the “shadow of particular ecstasies” (vibhava-abhasa)

One difference between the two kinds of emotional ecstasies is that pure spiritual emotion appears after devotees are re-established in their eternal spiritual identities (sthayi-bhava), having fully purified their hearts. As long as devotees still identify with their material bodies, however, the shadow of particular ecstasies inspires them to continue their pursuit of pure devotion. In other words, whereas genuine spiritual emotion is the cause of tasting love for Krsna, the shadow of such emotion gives one a renewed enthusiasm for devotional activity that in time invokes the descent of divine mercy and fully matures into love for Krsna.

For devotees on the platform of transcendence, spiritual emotion increases the taste derived from devotional service, which in turn intensifies their attraction to that loving service and ultimately reawakens their pure love for Krsna. For devotees still conditioned by the modes of nature, the shadow of transcendental emotion inspires them to pursue their devotional practices further. This inspired devotional practice purifies devotees of bad habits, makes them steadier, and increases their taste for bhakti and their attachment to Krsna.

Let us now examine the ways in which the places of the Lord’s pastimes awaken emotion. The dynamics of this awakening are important, for such knowledge can guide devotees to the activities and consciousness that gradually awaken the shadow of spiritual emotions and eventually pure emotional ecstasy. This knowledge is also essential for the caretakers of holy places, who must ensure that the visual appearance of the places of Krsna’s pastimes (lila-sthanas) helps to invoke the kind of emotion they are meant to invoke — specifically in conditioned souls.

**How the Places of Krsna’s Pastimes Awaken Spiritual Emotion**

Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu has the following to say about vibhava:

*tatra jneya vibhavas tu raty-asvadana-hetavah*  
*te dvidhalambana eke tathaivoddipanah pare*

**“The cause bringing about the tasting of love for Krsna is called vibhava. Vibhava is divided into two categories — alambana (support) and uddipana (awakening).”**

Both varieties of spiritual emotion — pure and shadow — have two components: alambana and uddipana. The first (alambana) is the component in which love appears, also known as the foundation or support. The second (uddipana) is the component by which love appears, also known as the awakening or impetus.

The foundation of love (alambana) also has two features: the object (visaya), or Krsna, and the shelter (asraya), or Krsna’s devotee. Rupa Gosvami describes these two foundational aspects of love:
