**“The object of love is Krsna, and the container of that love is the devotee of Krsna. Learned scholars call them alambana — the foundations.”**

Simply put, uddipana are those things that awaken ecstatic love. Rupa Gosvami gives several examples of uddipana: Krsna’s qualities, activities, decoration, smile, fragrance, flute, bugle, ankle bells, conch, devotees, place of residence, favourite plant (tulasi), the marks on His feet, and the observance of fasts and vows that are associated with devotion to Him.

This appendix is meant to show how pure or shadow emotional ecstasy is awakened in Krsna’s devotee — the asraya-alambana— by the specific impetus of Krsna’s places of residence or pastimes. Or, in other words, how the dhama causes spiritual emotion in Krsna’s devotees. Understanding this enhances devotional practices and enables devotees to more greatly benefit from their parikrama of New Vraja-dhama.

An impetus (uddipana) awakens devotional attachment by stimulating remembrance of Krsna, the object of that attachment (visaya-alambana). If a devotee has attained bhava, the preliminary rays of love for the Lord, then remembrance of Krsna will naturally awaken fullblown love (prema) and its attendant symptoms, known as subordinate ecstasies (anubhavas). For a person in love the symptoms of love are naturally stirred by anything that reminds the lover of the beloved.

Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami tells the story of Maharaja Prataparudra’s son who was always dressed in yellow cloth and ornamented with jewels. The prince’s appearance stimulated Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s remembrance of Krsna, giving the Lord spiritual pleasure. This is an example of how something not even directly connected with Krsna can arouse ecstatic love in someone who loves the Lord.

Lord Caitanya’s visit to Agravana and the other forests of Vrndavana, as described by Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami, provides further examples for our present discussion. When the Lord first arrived in Vraja, He listened to the parrots glorify Radha and Krsna and then watched the dancing of peacocks. The sight of the bluish necks of the peacocks awakened His remembrance of Krsna and in ecstatic love Caitanya Mahaprabhu fell to the ground. The author then describes how the name of Vrndavana — not to mention Vrndavana itself—acted as an impetus for Caitanya Mahaprabhu, awakening the Lord’s ecstatic symptoms of love.

Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami writes:

**“Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s mind was absorbed in ecstatic love at Jagannatha Puri, but when He passed along the road on the way to Vrndavana, that love increased a hundred times. The Lord’s ecstatic love increased a thousand times when He visited Mathura, but it increased a hundred thousand times when He wandered in the forests of Vrndavana. When Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu was elsewhere, the very name of Vrndavana was sufficient to increase His ecstatic love. Now, when He was actually travelling in the Vrndavana forest, His mind was absorbed in great ecstatic love day and night. He ate and bathed simply out of habit. Thus I have written a description of the ecstatic love Lord Caitanya manifested in one of the places He visited while walking through the twelve forests of Vrndavana. To describe what He experienced everywhere would be impossible.”**

In addition to the name of Vrndavana, the animals of Vrndavana, and the forests of Vrndavana, Sri Caitanya-caritamrta indicates other things that stirred love for Krsna in Caitanya Mahaprabhu: seeing herds of cows, discovering Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda, seeing Govardhana Hill, seeing Deities like Harideva, visiting the place where Krsna felled the twin arjuna trees, visiting the site of the rasa dance, chanting japa in a solitary place in Vraja, and jumping into the waters of Akrura-kunda.

Because of Lord Caitanya’s intense love for Krsna, almost anything associated with Krsna or the places of Krsna’s pastimes would plunge Him into an ocean of ecstasy. This extraordinary sensitivity to spiritual stimulus is to be expected of great devotees, and so Caitanya Mahaprabhu, playing the role of a devotee, displayed it to the highest degree. The Lord explained this elevated condition to Ramananda Raya, after revealing His combined form of Radha and Krsna to Ramananda. Caitanya Mahaprabhu said:

*sthavara-jangama dekhe, na dekhe tara muni*  
*sarvatra haya nija ista-deva-sphurti*

**“The maha-bhagavata, the advanced devotee, certainly sees everything mobile and immobile, but he does not exactly see their forms. Rather, everywhere he immediately sees manifest the form of the Supreme Lord.”**

It should be emphasised that this heightened sensitivity to spiritual stimuli is possible only for highly elevated devotees because their perception of and response to such stimuli are completely spiritual.

Less advanced devotees are neither as sensitive nor as emotionally responsive to spiritual stimuli because they are still handicapped by the contaminating influence of the modes of nature, which covers their mind and senses. This prevents them from recognising spiritual stimuli and from responding to them transcendentally. Even if devotees are firmly situated in the mode of goodness, their response is far from being purely spiritual. It is still a shadow of emotional ecstasy, albeit a high-grade shadow.

The relationship between the intensity of emotion the devotee tastes and a devotee’s sensitivity to a given stimulus — for example, a holy place in Vraja—can be compared to the amount of electricity induced in a wire as it passes through a magnetic field.

The motion of a magnetic field around a copper wire induces electricity to flow through the wire. However, the strength of the electrical current flowing through the wire will vary according to the wire’s ability to conduct electricity — the more impurities there are in the wire, the less it will be able to conduct electricity, and the weaker will be the electrical current passing through it. Similarly, the nature of an impetus is to stir remembrance of Krsna, resulting in emotional ecstasy. However, the intensity of spiritual emotion induced will vary in proportion to the purity of the devotee’s heart: the fewer the impurities, the higher the grade of emotion induced. Therefore at the stages of ecstasy and love (bhava and prema), pure spiritual emotion will appear in a devotee’s heart. But from the beginning of anartha-nivrtti through the stage of attachment (asakti), the most one can hope for is varying grades of shadow emotion.

When a group of devotees travel together on parikrama, their individual spiritual advancement determines the type and intensity of the spiritual emotions that awaken within their hearts. Some devotees taste varying degrees of shadow emotion, and some rare souls will taste real spiritual ecstasy. In this way, according to the maturity of their love for Krsna, devotees will taste the nectar afforded by parikrama.

Raghava Pandita, Srinivasa Acarya, and Narottama Dasa Thakura toured Vrndavana in constant ecstasy because all three were perfected Vaisnavas. For example, simply by seeing Kisori-kunda in Umrao — where Lokanatha Gosvami discovered Radha-Vinoda and where Sri Radhika was once crowned queen of Vrndavana — Raghava Pandita floated in his own tears and his disciples rolled in the dust. These are some of the symptoms displayed by devotees who experience genuine ecstatic emotion while visiting Vrndavana.

**Cultivating an Appreciation for Spiritual Stimuli**

Devotees still under the influence of the modes of nature should not try to taste spiritual ecstasy or simulate its symptoms, both of which are accessible only to fully purified souls. However, practising devotees can cultivate remembrance of the Lord and sensitivity to spiritual stimuli in two ways: by learning to see how Krsna is present in His creation, and by attentively observing the disciplines of regulated devotion.

In the seventh and tenth chapters of the Bhagavad-gita, Krsna describes the process of learning to see Him in His creation. The process itself is very simple, requiring only a little knowledge and some self-discipline. For example, when a devotee drinks a glass of water and thinks, “Krsna is the taste in this water,” the taste of water (uddipana) can awaken awe of the Lord’s all-pervasiveness (vibhava-abhasa). This kind of cultivation will help make devotees sensitive to Krsna’s presence and will teach them to remember the Lord. Both of these skills are very helpful in tasting shadow emotions and can enrich devotees’ visits to the holy dhama.

The second way to awaken the shadow of spiritual emotions is to be attentive to the daily regimen of regulated devotional service, such as offering prayers, singing in kirtana, reading scriptures, and seeing the Deity of the Lord. These different limbs of regulated practice, done attentively and without offence, are themselves potent stimuli that naturally awaken remembrance of Krsna and a taste for devotional mellows. The devotional acts of inattentive devotees, however, cannot manifest their full potency nor awaken remembrance of Krsna. This is because their minds are fixed on other things, things which awaken emotions not connected to Krsna. In this way negligent Vaisnavas awaken the taste for sensual gratification. To highlight the importance of devotional attentiveness, Srimad-Bhagavatam says at its outset, *sadyo hrdy avarudhyate ’tra krtibhih susrusubhis tat-ksanat*: “As soon as one attentively and submissively hears the message of Bhagavatam, by this culture of knowledge the Supreme Lord is established within his heart.” By careful attention to the devotional practices themselves, which are by nature impetuses for spiritual emotion, remembrance of Krsna naturally awakens within the heart.

The concerted effort to remember Krsna’s form, qualities, and pastimes while engaging in such practices, especially while chanting Hare Krsna, will also be helpful at the maturing stages of the devotee’s spiritual development.

Whether on pilgrimage or while executing regulated devotional service, the aforementioned techniques help devotees to become more sensitive to the things in the world around them that stimulate Krsna consciousness. It is the development of such spiritual perception that deepens one’s understanding of the phrase “Krsna consciousness.” Krsna Himself encourages such vision:

*yo mam pasyati sarvatra sarvam ca mayi pasyati*  
*tasyaham na pranasyami sa ca me na pranasyati*

**“For one who sees Me everywhere and sees everything in Me, I am never lost, nor is he ever lost to Me.”**

In summary, devotees who practise remembering Krsna, and who are attentive in their regular devotional activities, will derive the greatest benefit from making pilgrimages to holy places — and they will best relish the taste of the devotional service they render.

**The Taste of Pure and Shadow Spiritual Emotion**

As already explained, vibhava is the special ecstasy devotees relish at the stage of love of God. It is a transcendental emotion that dwarfs all types of material happiness, even that available on Satyaloka and other heavenly realms. Because genuine emotional ecstasy is purely spiritual, it defies comparison with things of this world and so cannot be adequately expressed in words. Only devotees with the proper qualification can taste it. Others may hear about it with reverence in the hope that at some future time they will also be able to taste it.

The shadow of ecstatic emotion, however, is within reach of the practising devotee. It is a pleasure gleaned from devotional service in practise. Such pleasure is distinct from the true bliss of transcendental emotion. It is primarily a mixture of relief from sinful reactions and the comfort derived from knowing that one is doing something auspicious.

Devotional service in the mode of goodness starts at the stage of steadiness (nistha) at which devotees are freed of most unwanted habits. Lord Krsna says that such devotional service, when guided by transcendental knowledge, makes devotees “very happy” (su-sukham). Even at lower stages of devotional service devotees are happy because they know that their success in achieving the final objective of their lives is guaranteed if they just continue doing their prescribed duties in devotional service. Thus the varying stages of pleasure devotees feel as they move through the beginning stages of pure devotional service fall within the category of vibhava-abhasa. And although ultimately pure devotees do not serve Krsna in order to be happy, the happiness neophytes taste from devotional service gives them the strength required to resist the allurements of happiness in its more mundane forms.

When devotees see the places where Krsna enacted His pastimes or hear the narration of those pastimes, when they see a tree under which Krsna rested, draw water from a well that He dug, or touch Krsna’s footprints in a rock, their remembrance of Krsna is awakened — like a spark on tinder — and then their attachment to Krsna is aroused — like tinder aflame. As a result devotees’ faces glow with happiness, their voices rise in chorus to sing the Lord’s holy names, and their dedication to the practices of devotional service increases.

An advanced devotee will, by the influence of the aforementioned spiritual stimuli, relish real emotional ecstasy. And although such first-class devotees may restrain themselves from displaying the symptoms of such ecstasy, outsiders may still sense the exalted position of these elevated souls by the special ecstasy felt in their presence. This type of ecstasy felt by neophytes is also in the category of shadow emotion, but it is superior to the emotions beginners are qualified to relish on their own.

Srila Prabhupada explains that the “moonlike rays” of love from the hearts of advanced devotees sometimes reflect on the hearts of those in their association, and cause in those fortunate souls an upsurge in attachment to Krsna, called “para attachment.” Indeed such superior shadow ecstasy may sometimes be awakened by the grace of the holy dhama as well. Nothing is impossible to achieve by the association of Vaisnavas and the mercy of holy places. Therefore devotees always pray for that mercy, knowing that without it their aspirations for perfection will be fruitless. In Rupa Gosvami's words:

*kva jario ‘yam ativa pamarah*  
*kva durapam rati'-bhagbhir apy adah*  
*iyam ullasayaty ajarjara*  
*gurur uttarsa-dhura tathapi mam*

**“Who am I, a mere fool? And where is that perfection even great devotees fail to attain? Still, a new and intense thirst for it now makes me tremble.”**

**Summary**

The ways in which devotees are influenced by the places of Krsna’s pastimes may be summarised as follows:

Remembrance of Krsna naturally leads to love for Him. Depending on a devotee’s level of advancement, the holy places of Vraja — acting as impetuses (uddipana) —awaken within that devotee either the shadow of emotion (vibhava-abhasa) or pure emotion (vibhava). To the degree a devotee has attachment to Krsna—the visaya, or object of affection — to that degree the places of Krsna’s pastimes (lila-sthanas) awaken a corresponding quality of spiritual emotion. When this attachment is weak, the association of more advanced Vaisnavas is essential for tasting even the shadow of emotion. Neophytes overcome such spiritual weakness by attentive devotional practices. These practices give relief from the influence of the material modes and enable one to think of Krsna. In addition, when practising devotees (sadhakas) have the association of advanced (siddha) Vaisnavas, the benefits of their parikrama are further enhanced by the effect of the “moon-rays of bliss” that amplify the devotion in the hearts of practitioners.

Devotees who are transcendentally situated will benefit fully from doing parikrama. They taste pure ecstatic emotion, which by its influence nourishes love for Krsna, plunging them into an ocean of bliss that has no shore.

When Uddhava visited Vrndavana, he felt great bliss in seeing the rivers, forests, mountains, valleys, and flowering trees that had been enjoyed by Krsna. As he wandered about, Uddhava asked the Vraja-vasis about the pastimes the Lord performed in those places, and in turn Uddhava spoke to the Vraja-vasis about his friend and cousin, Hari. In this way Uddhava and the Vraja-vasis enjoyed transcendental bliss in one another’s company.

If the residents and guests of New Vraja-dhama follow in the footsteps of Uddhava and the Vraja-vasis by constantly reciting the pastimes of Radha and Krsna and singing Their glories, then those devotees will taste the mellows of pure devotion and advance quickly to the ultimate goal of life, pure love of Godhead.

## Appendix 10

**Kamsa Becomes a Gopi**

(This appendix follows from the description of Kisori-kunda in chapter six. It narrates another pastime associated with that holy lake.)

Two furlongs northeast of Yavata is the large and beautiful lake known as Kisori-kunda. Surrounded by flower gardens and spacious, ornate ghatas, it is home to flocks of colourful, sweet-singing birds. In the predawn hours, the inhabitants collect its very sweet water and happily sing songs to Mother Durga.

On its southern bank is the kunda’s most treasured asset: a forest clearing, the scene of Radha’s and Krsna’s nocturnal rasa dance. The nectar churned from the ecstasy of Their singing and dancing has made the waters of Kisori-kunda as sweet as grape juice and as potent as amrta. The residents say that those waters fulfil all desires.

Let us see what they mean.

Krsna and Balarama were well known to Kamsa, the king of Mathura. Every wicked demon sent to Vrndavana by the king had met with death at the hands of the two brothers.

Recently, though, from expert spies Kamsa had come to hear of the blossoming girls of Yavata. Amongst them, one gopi was rumoured to be charming above all, a veritable fountainhead of youthful beauty. Her name was Srimati Radharani, or Sri Radha.

Intensely curious, the king wanted to see for himself if the stories of Her exquisite beauty were factual. If they were, perhaps he would kidnap Radha and make Her his queen.

On the pretext of surveying his kingdom, Kamsa mounted his chariot and, leading a division of well-armed horsemen, headed towards Yavata. Reaching the outskirts of town, the king of the Kiratas instructed his soldiers to rest in a small forest and continued alone.

Kamsa had the strength of ten thousand elephants and was equipped with both earthly and celestial weapons. His tall, handsome frame, shining like gold, was decorated with jewels, necklaces, and earrings. Proud of his strength, beauty, and wealth, Kamsa was evil. Driven by lust, he had persecuted brahmanas, and overwhelmed by greed, he had tried to kill Krsna.

As Kamsa urged his horses forward, Kisori-kunda withdrew in fear, the fragrant breezes retreated, the birds stopped singing, and the lotuses closed. With a desire to quench his thirst, the king stopped before a deserted ghata. Kamsa tied his horses to a tree, stepped down from his chariot, and approached the water’s edge. After removing his gloves and rinsing his hands, he drank the cool, sweet water.

No sooner had a few drops passed his lips than Kamsa heard the neighing of his steeds. Startled, he quickly stood up. No one was there but his restless horses, staring at him apparently alarmed. Something was wrong, but what?

Again he looked around, but his keen ksatriya senses told him there were no enemies around. Again he knelt. Looking into the crystal-clear water, Kamsa saw a young girl looking back at him.

Startled, he looked to either side — as before — there was no one. Perhaps it was a water nymph! Again Kamsa looked into the water and again he saw her. She appeared as startled as he. When he plunged his hand into the lake, he felt only water and saw that his own hand was delicate, graceful, adorned with bracelets, and feminine.

The horses neighed once more, causing Kamsa to turn. And once again they merely stared back at him — startled.

As Kamsa rose he felt different. His movements were light, fluid, and gracious; his steps small, nimble, and delicate; his clothing loose, long, and fluttering. But he felt constrained by his attire, and when he looked down he saw that he wore a skirt!

Examining his form, Kamsa found shapely arms, blossoming torso, a narrow waist, and tapering thighs, all nicely dressed and ornamented as a gopi! With delicate hands he touched his limbs to feel this newly acquired form, a form that was doubtlessly feminine.

The horses snorted, fluttering their lips as if to say they were enjoying the humour of the situation. But Kamsa was in no mood for humour.

Again Kamsa knelt by the ghata and looked into the water. Staring at him was a young wide-eyed gopi with pouting lips and a golden complexion.

“I have become a woman,” he murmured. Even his voice was feminine. “How has this happened?” he thought. “What spell has been cast upon me?”

As Kamsa looked for witnesses to his plight, a thousand thoughts raced through his mind. What would his soldiers think? What could he tell his wives? Who would believe he was king?

King? No! He was no longer a king, he was now a gopi, a woman! He felt like Kamsa but looked like a gopi. Then, what was he? What was she?

Suddenly, from the top of the stairs leading to the ghata came the sound of voices, “Whose chariot and horses are these? They look very regal.”

“Ask that girl by the lake. She must know.”

Two fearless girls descended the stairs as if floating on air. They were young, beautiful, and full of feminine charm. Standing beside Kamsa, one asked, “Sakhi! Who is the owner of this ornate chariot and these beautiful horses?”

Kamsa replied, amazed by the sweetness of his own voice, “They belong to me!”

The girls, who had come to collect Radharani’s bath water, looked at each other and smiled. Lalita-devi opened her eyes in mock disbelief and said, “Sakhi! Where did you come by such opulence?”

Kamsa had no choice but to reveal his true identity. Perhaps this girl would know how to free him from this curse. He leaned forward and replied, “Devi! Know me to be Kamsa, the king of Mathura, Surasena, and all land bordered by the seas.”

Unable to suppress their delight, the two girls began to giggle. Their eyes opened even wider as Lalita’s maidservant said incredulously, “Friend! You do not look like Kamsa. Kings rarely dress as gopis and never have enchanting forms like yours.”

“Listen!” Kamsa interrupted. “You must help me!” His dainty hand gestured towards the chariot. “I left Mathura to survey this area. My cavalry is resting in a nearby wood, and I came here to quench my thirst.”

Pointing to the water he continued, “As soon as I drank from this lake, I assumed a female body. I became a gopi like you, complete with clothes, ornaments, and bangles.” Rattling his bracelets he exclaimed, “How has this happened? I must regain my real form at once!”

Lalita looked back and forth at Kamsa-gopi and the chariot. Within a moment she could understand what had happened. Narrowing her eyes, Lalita spoke with a voice full of defiance, “O Kamsa! Why not bring your entire army to drink? Why leave them thirsty and out of sight?”

At the thought of being ridiculed by his soldiers, Kamsa shook his head.

Pointing to Kisori-kunda, Lalita said, “The water of this lake fulfils all desires. What was in your heart that you now have the body of a gopi? Perhaps you were thinking of gopis? Perhaps you wanted to abduct one?”

Lalita concluded, “Well, now you can take a gopi — your own self—with you wherever you go. You deserve just what you got, and we’re leaving.”

Kamsa, desperate without shelter, fell to his knees and implored Lalita, “Sakhi! What you say is true. I came to this place to see the famed beauties of Vraja. But I had no intention of abducting anyone. Please help me.”

Just as Lalita was about to rebuke Kamsa, Radha entered Lalita’s mind and said, “Lalita! Bring that rascal to Me. In such a humiliated state, perhaps he can serve Krsna. Bring him!”

Checking her anger, Lalita said, “King Kamsa! I cannot help you. The princess of this village is Sri Radha. If you can invoke Her mercy, anything is possible.”

Kamsa’s delicate eyebrows rose as he asked, “How can I meet Radha?”

“Leave the chariot here,” replied Lalita, “and follow me. After the princess has performed Her morning duties, when She is ready, She may grace you with Her presence.”

And so it was that Kamsa, in his newly-acquired body of a gopi, left his horses tethered to a tree and followed Lalita and her friend back to Radha’s house. Having no alternative than to surrender to Lalita, he carried Radha’s bathing water and, on the way, bowed to many Vraja-vasis. To them, as to Radha’s mother-in-law, Jatila, who opened the door, Kamsa was an unknown girl, from her dress most likely a visitor from nearby.

Kamsa entered Abhimanyu’s home and was led to a large room, nicely decorated with carpets, curtains, and furniture inlaid with gems. “How opulent is the life of a cowherd,” he thought.

Lalita pointed to an ornate seat and announced, “Please wait here. I shall return!” Then she left.

After Radha was bathed and dressed, Lalita-devi, as she held a mirror before her incomparably beautiful friend, said, “Radha! An uncommon gopi is waiting in the reception hall to see You.”

As if innocent of having communicated to Lalita from within her heart, Radha sweetly said, “What is unique about that gopi?”

With a mere hint from her eyebrows, Lalita dismissed her maidservants and replied, “Friend! This gopi claims to be King Kamsa.”

Amused, Radha turned to Lalita, who continued, “He says he came here to patrol the kingdom, and when he drank the water from Kisori-kunda his male body and dress were transformed. Now, in a gopi form, he awaits Your mercy, hoping that by some magic You will restore his male form.”

For a moment Radha considered what Lalita had said. Then without a word Radha rose and walked, head held high, toward the reception hall. Lalita and some elder gopis followed behind Her.

When Radha entered the room, it seemed to Kamsa that spring, adorned with the essence of all other seasons, had appeared. Never had he seen such incomparable beauty. Never had he seen every sign of auspiciousness embodied in one person. Radha — cordial but reserved — smiled as Kamsa rose from his seat.

Radha took a seat near Kamsa’s and, with a gesture of Her hand, offered Lalita a sitting place. Turning to Kamsa, She said, “O gopi! My friend tells a strange story about you. Please let Me hear from your own lips the reason for your visit.”

Kamsa sighed, “O Radha! I have heard that You possess limitless mystic potencies. Finding myself the victim of a curse, I have come to You for shelter.”

Radha nodded to acknowledge that She was willing to hear more. Kamsa continued, “I am Kamsa, the king and caretaker of this land!” Hearing this, the gopis surrounding Radha silently exchanged smiles.

“By drinking the water of Your lake, I have come under an enchanting spell that has transformed me into a gopi. For the welfare of our citizens, to save me from humiliation, please, free me from this curse. Make me king again.”

Radha replied, “O King! —if indeed you are a king — your condition is but a product of your own wickedness. Kisori-kunda does nothing but fulfil one’s desires. You must now be a gopi because of being absorbed in lusty thoughts of gopis. Why should we undo the reactions to your own sinful deeds?”

Kamsa joined his palms and implored, “O Princess! What you say is true. But as a ksatriya I am impelled by passion. How can I be punished for my thoughts?”

Radha knew that Kamsa was not inclined to right conduct or transcendental knowledge. Here he was, a gopi, and now he again wanted the body of a king.

She teased, “Kariisa! Great devotees of the Lord, self-controlled yogis, sadhakas, and siddhas alike, all undergo different austerities to become Vraja gopis. Why, then, do you not thank destiny for this rare good fortune?”

Tears flowed from Kamsa’s eyes as he cried out, “But I want to be king! I have subdued others, imprisoned my father, and transgressed religious principles to become emperor.

“I need my ksatriya body to rule my kingdom,” Kamsa sobbed, “I cannot rule as a gopi”

Radha winked at Lalita and asked in a sweet voice, “Very well. Are you willing to reimburse Me?”

“Reimburse You? Anything,” Kamsa snapped. “Ask me for anything.”

“Anything?” asked Radhika.

“Anything!” vowed Kamsa.

Seizing the opportunity, Radha, flushed with anger, lifted Her right hand, and in a raised voice exclaimed, “Kamsa! Since the day Krsna took birth, you have tried to kill Him. Everyone knows. Repeatedly, with one purpose in mind, you have sent your demonic friends to Vrndavana. Although they all meet the same fate — death — such demons disturb Krsna’s play and pollute the atmosphere of this divine land.”

With glaring eyes, Radha scowled, “Do not send any more of your ruffians here.”

Radha stopped, shook her finger menacingly at Kamsa, and repeated, “If you want to be Kamsa again, promise that you will not send any more rogues to Vrndavana.”

Without a moment’s hesitation, Kamsa said, “I promise!”

Radha: “Promise what?”

Kamsa: “I promise that I will never again send anyone to harass Krsna or the Vraja-vasis. I promise.”

But Radha was not convinced. Casting a suspicious glance at Kamsa, She warned, “O King, be careful! Should you break your promise, you will again have the body of a gopi.” And for emphasis, She added, “Forever!”

Kamsa gasped, expectantly glancing around him for relief. Radhika composed Herself and said, “Go to the southern bank of Kisori-kunda. With Lalita as your witness make your vow before the sun. Once again drink the water of the lake and you will be a gopi no more. As you wish, you will again become a king.”

Kamsa hurriedly thanked Radha and rose to his feet, anxious to become a man again as soon as possible. Radhika smiled after the foolish king. Perhaps one day, by some inconceivable grace, he would become qualified to be a gopi at heart. Kamsa’s present appearance as a gopi was merely external — a form without substance, a shadow of transcendental reality.

Radhika watched from Her window, as Lalita and Kamsa-gopi faded from view. She mused, “In the future, others will want to achieve gopi-bhava by merely dressing as a gopi.” She shook Her lovely head in disapproval. “Imitation will never do. Gauranga and His nama-sankirtana would be the only way — there will be no other.”

## Appendix 11

**Meeting and Separation in Unwedded Love**

(This appendix follows from the pastimes at Prema-sarovara narrated in chapter six, expanding upon the many varieties of meeting and separation connected with transcendental love.)

At the level of pure devotional service, meeting with Krsna and separation from Krsna comprise two transcendental poles, eternally opposed and eternally alternating from one to the other. The dynamic relationship between these two states adds variety to the pastimes of the Lord and His devotees by constantly intensifying their love for one another. This appendix will elaborate on the many categories and subcategories of meeting and separation. It will also explain how these two states continually nourish one another by their interdependence.

**Defining Separation and Meeting**

Loving devotional service to Sri Krsna is performed in one of two conditions: while in the Lord’s association (sambhoga) or while separated from Him (vipralambha). Although these conditions enrich all five primary devotional mellows, the ecstasies induced by meeting and separation, particularly in the mellow of unwedded romantic love, surpass and encompass the ecstasies induced in all other mellows. Therefore, in order to present the most complete understanding of meeting and separation in devotional service, this appendix will specifically focus on how these conditions affect the Vraja gopis.

To reiterate, sambhoga (meeting) and vipralambha (separation) are two different conditions in which the gopis engage in loving service to Krsna. The two terms, sambhoga and vipralambha, can also refer to the loving service performed or the love experienced while under those conditions.

The word vipralambha is a compound of two words: vipra (“distance”) and lambha (“attainment”). According to Bhaktivinoda Thakura vipralambha is synonymous with “parting” (viraha) or “separation” (viyoga). Rupa Gosvami has this to say about love in separation:

**“When the lover and the beloved meet, they are called yukta (connected). Previous to their meeting, they are called ayukta (not connected). Whether connected or not connected, the ecstatic emotion arising due to not being able to embrace and kiss each other as desired is called vipralambha. This vipralambha helps nourish emotions at the time of meeting.”**

And the love felt upon meeting or becoming united with the beloved is defined by Srila Rupa Gosvami like this:

**“Meeting each other and embracing each other are aimed at bringing about the happiness of the lover and the beloved. When this stage becomes increasingly jubilant, the resultant ecstatic emotion is called sambhoga”**

In addition, Jiva Gosvami refers to sambhoga as the love that awakens “when two lovers enjoy (sambhoga) together.”

**The Connection Between Separation and Meeting**

It is essential for devotees to understand the relationship between separation and meeting in order to perform devotional service in the proper mood, regardless of whether their service is at the level of practice, ecstasy, or perfection. Therefore, before describing the different categories of meeting and separation, we will elaborate on that transcendental relationship.

From the beginning of their contact with Krsna consciousness, devotees are taught that material suffering is the result of distancing oneself from Krsna. Consequently, that suffering will come to an end when one again unites with the Lord by going “back to Godhead.” This clearly suggests that true happiness is found in being reunited with Krsna.

“Why then,” devotees wonder, “will we again suffer separation from Krsna after having achieved perfection? And why, even before reaching perfection, should we ‘cultivate a mood of separation’?”

Srila Prabhupada and the acaryas have elaborately answered these questions. And devotees must fully grasp those answers in order to avoid the pitfalls that appear when one neglects to understand the essential role separation plays in arriving at his eventual reunion with Krsna.

Let us begin by understanding how separation and meeting are interrelated.

When devotees love Krsna, they naturally want to express that love to Him with sweet words, loving gestures, and other forms of direct service. But this type of expression is not possible when devotees are separated from the Lord. In these instances, the desire to directly please Krsna remains unfulfilled. When left unfulfilled that desire continually grows stronger, increasing one’s love for Krsna in turn. This potent combination of heightened desire and increased love produces an intensified emotional state that makes the happiness felt at the time of meeting all the greater. In Srila Prabhupada’s words, “vipralambha serves as a nourishing element for future meetings.”

One might think that happiness is increased at the time of meeting because the desire to serve Krsna has been fulfilled. But this assumption is only partially correct. The primary reason for this increased happiness is that separation deepens one’s appreciation for meeting, just as it deepens one’s appreciation for Krsna. In the words of Bhaktivinoda Thakura, “The pleasure in sambhoga \[meeting\] is not properly appreciated without vipralambha \[separation\].” The Thakura concludes, “That is vipralambha’s function.” In other words, the ecstatic tension of separation from Krsna not only increases one’s desire to serve the Lord, it also increases one’s appreciation for the reunion, not to mention one’s love for the Lord Himself.

Therefore the state of being separated from Krsna and the sentiments that accompany that separation are both essential for devotees to fully appreciate their reunion with the Lord.

Rupa Gosvami gives an example to illustrate how this principle works in practice:

*na vina vipralambhena sambhogah pustim asnute*  
*na kasayite hi vastrada bhuyan rago vivardhate*

**“Just as one cannot redden cloth or some other object without the use of red dye, in the same way the happiness of amorous love cannot reach its fullness without there having been separation of the lovers.”**

In this example, plain cloth is synonymous with meeting, while red cloth symbolises a meeting with one’s beloved that has been intensified by the ecstasy of separation. Finally, red dye represents the emotionally heightening effects of separation. Red dye is as essential for producing red cloth as feelings of separation are for truly enjoying the happiness of meeting.

During the rasa dance Krsna abandoned the gopis, driving them nearly to madness with feelings of separation, before He again appeared and relieved their distress. In the confidential talks that followed, Krsna affirmed the interrelated nature of meeting and separation:

**“But the reason I do not immediately reciprocate the affection of living beings even when they worship Me, O gopis, is that I want to intensify their loving devotion. They then become like a poor man who has gained some wealth and then lost it, and who thus becomes so anxious about it that he can think of nothing else.”**

Having heard this explanation of the interrelated nature of separation and meeting, devotees are often eager to know why, at the advanced stage of love for Krsna, there is apparent suffering due to separation. For instance, a devotee once asked: “If Vaikuntha means ‘the place of no anxiety,’ then how can devotees — either in Vaikuntha (the spiritual sky) or in Goloka Vrndavana itself—suffer in anxiety when they are separated from Krsna?”

In answer to this question, Bhaktivinoda Thakura cites a statement made by Ramananda Raya to Caitanya Mahaprabhu in the Sri Caitanya-caritamrta in which Ramananda explains that the “suffering” of separation is actually a heightened stage of ecstasy. In other words, in the spiritual world there is no suffering as we understand it in the mundane sense of the word, there is only transcendental bliss appearing in unlimited forms, even forms that apparently resemble what we may presently recognise as suffering. In his explanation Ramananda Raya quotes Srimati Radharani:

**“Alas, before We met there was an initial attachment between Us brought about by an exchange of glances. In this way attachment evolved. That attachment has gradually grown, and there is no limit to it. Now that attachment has become a natural sequence between Ourselves. It is not that it is due to Krsna, the enjoyer, nor is it due to Me, for I am the enjoyed. It is not like that. This attachment was made possible by mutual meeting. This mutual exchange of attraction is known as manobhava, or Cupid. Krsna’s mind and My mind have merged together. Now, during this time of separation, it is very difficult to explain these loving affairs. My dear friend, though Krsna might have forgotten all these things, you can understand and bring this message to Him. But during Our first meeting there was no messenger between Us, nor did I request anyone to see Him. Indeed, Cupid’s five arrows were Our via media. Now, during this separation, that attraction has increased to another ecstatic state. My dear friend, please act as a messenger on My behalf, because if one is in love with a beautiful person, this is the consequence.”**

From the words of Ramananda Raya we can understand that what seems to be suffering, according to our limited material vision, is in truth the highest and most wonderful spiritual bliss (vivarta).

Lord Ramacandra also describes the purpose of separation, and the intense ecstasy that it generates, during a conversation with Sita-devi.

When she heard of the Lord’s incarnation as Caitanya Mahaprabhu, and how He takes the renounced order of life, Sita-devi asked, “O lotus-eyed one! Why would You give up Your wife and mother to take sannyasa. What happiness would You gain by giving sorrow to those who love You?”

Sri Rama replied, “My dear wife! There is nothing unknown to you, and yet you feign ignorance for the benefit of all living beings. Listen Sita! There are two ways My devotees feel love for Me: in union with Me (sambhoga), and in separation from Me (vipralambha). Although My eternal associates — like My wife and mother — desire sambhoga, I mercifully give them vipralambha, because the distress due to separation from Me is actually the topmost bliss and the union that follows it yields a happiness a million times greater than before. Therefore, by taking sannyasa and leaving home, through separation, I will give My wife and mother the highest happiness.”

**\* \* \* \***

The evidence presented by Ramananda Raya and Lord Ramacandra makes it clear that the sorrow a devotee apparently feels in being separated from Krsna is actually a form of spiritual ecstasy. That ecstasy “strengthens the ties of love and enhances the desire of the lover and beloved to meet.” Therefore — and Srila Prabhupada repeated this many times—the ecstasy of separation is more relishable than the happiness of the actual meeting because of the greatly enhanced feelings of anticipation aroused by that separation. In short, the ecstasy of separation is actually more pleasurable and more intense than that of meeting. Srila Prabhupada writes, “A person who is advanced in Krsna consciousness always feels separation from Krsna because such a feeling of separation excels the feeling of meeting Krsna.”

Not only does separation generate ecstasies that are both qualitatively and quantitatively superior to the ecstasies of meeting but the most intense ecstasies of separation also bring about different kinds of meeting. These feelings of love in separation are so powerful that they make devotees see Krsna in a variety of ways: in His instructions (vani), by visualising His presence (sphurti), through direct audience of Him (saksad-darsana), or by actually meeting with Him (sambhoga). Therefore, in one sense, devotees experience more association with Krsna by being separated from Him than by being united with Him.

This truth is further accentuated by the phenomenon of premavaicittya, in which, due to fear of impending separation from Krsna, devotees lose sight of the Lord even while in His company. This is because when they are with Krsna devotees already feel His absence, and when they are separated from Krsna their intense thoughts of Him mean that they always feel His presence. It is for this reason that Krsna decided that it would be kinder of Him not to return and live in Vrndavana.

The transcendental superiority of separation over meeting is beautifully described in the following verse by an unknown author:

**“Separation from Krsna is better than meeting Him. When I meet Him there is only one Krsna, but when I am separated from Him the three worlds become filled with Krsnas.”**

Not only did Srila Prabhupada direct his followers to cultivate the mood of separation, but he also cautioned them not to covet the mood of meeting. Meeting arises naturally, as the mature fruit of separation. If artificially invoked, it degrades devotees as well as the quality of their devotion.

Consider, for example, Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Lord Caitanya is Krsna Himself, but He adopted the mood of Srimati Radharani. Because Radharani is always feeling separation from Krsna, Caitanya Mahaprabhu was always feeling that separation as well. Srila Prabhupada therefore writes,

**“Those who follow the teachings of Lord Caitanya should experience and relish the feelings of scparation, not of meeting.”**

A true Gaudiya Vaisnava’s mood of devotion, both in practice and in perfection, is thus one of separation — a separation that will undoubtedly result in meeting Krsna face to face. This is not only the path faithful to Lord Caitanya’s teachings, but it is also the path that works.

But practically speaking, what is the mood of separation, and how can devotees incorporate that mood into their daily devotional activities?

By having entered conditional life in this material world, living entities have voluntarily distanced themselves from the Lord. When one becomes a devotee, regrets his past acts of rebellion against Krsna, and desires to be reinstated in his original postion as the Lord’s servant, he has adopted the mood of separation as described by Srila Prabhupada.

The following song of Narrotama Dasa Thakura — which Srila Prabhupada referred to as one of his favourite bhajanas — is representative of the mood of separation.

**“O Lord Hari, Lord Hari! I have spent my life uselessly. Although I have obtained a human birth, I have refused to worship Radha and Krsna, and in this way I have knowingly drunk poison.**

**“The treasure of love of God has descended from the spiritual world of Goloka Vrndavana, appearing in this world as the sankirtana movement of the chanting of Lord Hari’s holy names. Why am I not attracted to it? Day and night I burn from the poison of material existence, but still I refuse to take the antidote.**

**“Lord Krsna, the prince of Vraja has appeared as Lord Caitanya, the son of Saci-devi, and Lord Balarama has appeared as Lord Nityananda. Those who were most fallen and degraded have been lifted from their fallen condition by the chanting of the holy name of Lord Hari. The story of Jagai and Madhai testifies to the truth of this.**

**“O Lord Krsna! O son of Maharaja Nanda! O companion of Srimati Radharani, the daughter of Maharaja Vrsabhanu, please he merciful to me. Now that I have finally attained Your reddish lotus feet, please do not push me away. Except for You, who is my shelter?”**

The feelings of separation, so nicely illustrated in this song, become integral to a Vaisnava’s mood of service to Krsna when he accepts the responsibility to serve Lord Caitanya’s mission by awakening Krsna consciousness in conditioned souls. Such preaching necessitates that devotees constantly remind themselves, as well as others, that they have fallen from their original spiritual position. It also demands that they extol the merits of going back to Godhead. And when, by such service in the mood of separation, devotees please Lord Caitanya, He then accepts them into His eternal association. In that way, through service in separation, devotees achieve the perfection of meeting.

By contrast, those devotees who constantly think of Krsna’s pastimes, trying to artificially inject themselves into Krsna’s association by mental effort, are in the opinion of our acaryas devotional upstarts at best, and pretenders or cheaters at worst. Because such Vaisnavas want Krsna’s association without having first qualified themselves through service in the mood of separation, they have been labelled sahajiyas — devotees who want an incalculably valuable jewel, sambhoga, without having paid the appropriate price, vipralambha.

Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura decries those sadhakas who reject Lord Caitanya’s mood of service in separation in favour of service in meeting. Our predecessor acarya notes that the symptoms of ecstasy displayed by Lord Caitanya after receiving initiation from Isvara Puri are indicative of the mellow of separation (vipralambha-rasa). Although, as Krsna Himself, the Lord was always with Krsna, He felt that He had not achieved Krsna, and thus — in the mood of Srimati Radharani — “shedding tears \[of separation\] became His principal activity.” Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura writes, “Feelings of separation alone are the means and nourishment for sambhoga, or union.”

Sarasvati Thakura goes on to explain that Lord Caitanya descended from the spiritual world to reveal the glories of the mood of separation, and to rectify those who accept the mood of meeting as the guiding principle of their sadhana. Considering Himself a surrendered servant afflicted with separation from Krsna, Lord Caitanya achieved His aims by exemplifying them. Unfortunately for the sahajiyd-sampradayas, they have not understood the purpose of the Lord’s descent as Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Instead they display aversion to the principles of pure devotion by adopting the sadhana of sambhuga, fuelling the fire of mundane enjoyment in the hearts of conditioned souls, and thus ruining their chances for perfection.

Srila Prabhupada summarised these thoughts in his own words:

**“So-called devotees like the sahajiyas cheaply imagine they are meeting Krsna in Vrndavana. Such thinking may be useful, but actually meeting Krsna is possible through the attitude of separation taught by Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu.”**

**\* \* \* \***

What follows is a general description of the different categories and subcategories of separation and meeting that take place between Krsna and the milkmaids of Vraja. We will list four kinds of separation and their corresponding types of meeting. Under each of these eight main headings we will also list the eight ways in which each are invoked, and in the following section cite examples of some of the more prominent transcendental mellows listed. These examples are largely taken from Rupa Gosvami's *Sri Ujjvala-nilamani* and Jiva Gosvami's *Priti-sandarbha.* Since there is unlimited variety in the divine mellows of separation and meeting, only a few examples of each are given here, enabling the reader to at least sample the variety of tastes available in the vast ocean of transcendental loving exchange. Devotees inspired to study these topics in more detail are advised to consult those books mentioned in this paragraph.

**The Varieties of Separation, Their Corresponding Varieties of Meeting, and the Causes of Both Varieties of Separation (Vipralambha)**

1\. Purva-raga — feelings of separation caused by the couple falling in love, although they have never met:

a. Saksad darsana — direct vision of the beloved

b. Citra-pate darsana — seeing a painting of the beloved

c. Svapne darsana — seeing a vision of the beloved in a dream

d. Bhata mukhe sravana — hearing about the beloved from professional reciters

e. Duti mukhe sravana — hearing about the beloved from a messenger

f. Sakhi mukhe sravana — hearing about the beloved from a girlfriend

g. Gita hoite sravana — hearing a song about the beloved

h. Vamsi-dvani sravana — hearing the sound of the beloved’s flute

2\. Mana — feelings of separation distinguished by the lover’s pouting caused by:

a. Sakhi mukhe sravana — hearing about the beloved from a girlfriend

b. Suka mukhe sravana — hearing evidence about the beloved through the mimicry of a parrot

c. Murali dvani sravana — hearing the sound of the beloved’s flute

d. Vipaksa-gatre bhoga chihna darsana — seeing signs of enjoyment, caused by contact with the beloved, on a rival’s body

e. Priyatama gatre bhoga chihna darsana — seeing signs of enjoyment, caused by contact with a rival, on the beloved’s body

f. Gotra skhalana — accidentally being addressed by the beloved with a rival’s name

g. Svapne darsana — seeing something unfavourable in a dream

h. Anya nayikara sahge darsana—seeing the beloved with a rival

3\. Prema-vaicittya — feelings of separation while in the presence of the beloved expressed by:

a. Krsner prati aksepa — grieving to the beloved

b. Nija prati aksepa — grieving to oneself

c. Sakhi prati aksepa — grieving to a girlfriend

d. Duti prati aksepa — grieving to a messenger

e. Murali prati aksepa — grieving to the flute

f. Vidhata prati aksepa — grieving to the creator

g. Kandarpa prati aksepa — grieving to Cupid

h. Guru-jana prati aksepa — grieving to one’s superiors

4\. Pravasa — feelings of separation when distanced from the beloved caused by:

a. Bhavi viraha — anticipation of impending separation from the beloved

b. Mathura gamana — the beloved going to Mathura

c. Dvaraka gamana— the beloved going to Dvaraka

d. Kaliya naga damanartha jale pravesa — the beloved entering the Yamuna to chastise the Kaliya snake

e. Gosthe gamana — the beloved going to tend cows in the pasture

f. Nanda moksanartha varuna loke gamana — the beloved going to Varuna’s underwater world to rescue Nanda

g. Karyopalakse sthanantara gamana — the beloved going to any other place to do anything else

h. Rase antardhana— the beloved’s disappearance during the rasa dance

**Varieties of Meeting (Sambhoga)**

Meeting is of two kinds, direct and indirect. Direct meeting takes place in the waking state and indirect meeting takes place in dreams. The following divisions apply to both kinds of meeting.

1\. Samksipa sambhoga (or purva-raga-anantara sambhoga)—a brief meeting that follows purva-raga enjoyed by:

a. Balyavasthaya milana — meeting as children

b. Gosthe milana—meeting in the pasturelands

c. Go-dohana kale milana—meeting at the time of milking cows

d. Akasmat milana — meeting suddenly and unexpectedly

e. Hastakarsana rupa milana — meeting by grasping the hand

f. Vastrakarsana rupa milana — meeting by tugging on the clothes

g. Vartma-rodha rupa milana — meeting by blocking the path

h. Rati-bhoga rupa milana — meeting by enjoyment of sensual caressing

2\. Sankirna-sambhoga (or mana-anantara sambhoga) —a meeting following an exchange that was intruded upon by mana enjoyed by:

a. Maha-rase milana — meeting during the maha-rasa dance

b. Jala-kridaya milana — meeting during water sports

c. Kunja-lilaya milana — meeting during pastimes in the forest groves

d. Dana lilaya milana—meeting while disputing over paying a toll

e. Vamsi churite milana—meeting during the stealing of the flute

f. Nauka vilase milana—meeting during the enjoyment of boating

g. Madhu-pane milana — meeting while drinking intoxicating honey wine

h. Surya pujaya milana—meeting while worshipping the sun god

3\. Sampanna-sambhoga (kincid-dura-pravasa-anantara sambhoga) —a mature type of meeting that follows the separation caused by a short journey (pravasa) and enjoyed by:

a. Su-dura darsane milana — viewing each other from a long distance

b. Jhulana yatraya milana — meeting during the swing festival

c. Holi lilaya milana — meeting while throwing colours

d. Prahelikaya milana — meeting to play riddles

e. Pasa khelaya milana — meeting to gamble with dice

f. Narttana rase milana — meeting while engaged in dancing

g. Rasalase milana — meeting while lounging about

h. Kapata nidraya milana — meeting during pastimes of feigned sleep

4\. Samrddhimana-sambhoga (or sudura-pravasa-anantara sambhoga)—a fully enriched type of meeting that follows a very long journey (pravasa) and is enjoyed by:

a. Svapne milana — meeting in dreams

b. Kuruksetre milana — meeting at Kuruksetra

c. Bhavollasa milana — the blossoming of ecstatic emotions

d. Dvaraka hoite vrndavane pratyagamane milana — meeting in Vrndavana after returning from Dvaraka

e. Viparita sambhoge milana — meeting while engaged in various pastimes (lilaya milana), but with the roles of lover and beloved reversed

f. Bhojane milana — meeting while eating

g. Ekatre nidraya milana — meeting while resting together

h. Svadhina-bharttrka-bhave milana — meeting while the lover assumes the domineering mood

Feelings of Separation in the Stage of Preliminary Attraction (Purva-raga) and the Relief of a Subsequent Brief Meeting (Samksipa-sambhoga): Purva-raga, which Srila Prabhupada calls “preliminary attraction,” refers to the feelings of separation that develop in the lover and beloved even before they have first met. It is defined by Rupa Gosvami:

*ratir ya sangamat purvam darsana-sravanadi-ja*  
*tayor unmilati prajnaih purva-ragah sa ucyate*

**“When attachment produced in the lover and beloved — before their meeting — by seeing, hearing, and so on, becomes very palatable by the mixture of four ingredients, such as vibhava and anubhava, this is called purva-raga.”**

One example of preliminary attraction (purva-raga) is seen in Princess Rukmini. Simply by hearing about Krsna’s extraordinary form, qualities, and pastimes she decided that He would make the ideal husband.

A more substantial example of purva-raga can be seen in the Venu-gita of Srimad-Bhagavatam. After hearing the flute-song of Lord Krsna, the gopis began to describe their feelings for Him in a secluded place where they would not be overheard by their elders. Jiva Gosvami explains that because the gopis spoke of their feelings only, not of any personal contact with Krsna, they had not yet become His lovers and thus their affection was in the category of purva-raga.

The gopis had seen Krsna and His friends enter the forest each morning, they had frequently heard the song of His flute, and they had spoken about Him with each other. In these ways their dormant love was awakened, and on the noteworthy morning described by

Sukadeva Gosvami, the strength of this preliminary attraction forced them to reveal the long-cherished love for Krsna they had previously kept hidden, even from each other.

An even more confidential example of preliminary attraction is found in the chapter following the Venu-gita — Canto Ten, chapter twenty-two — in which Sukadeva Gosvami narrates how Krsna stole the clothes of the young, unmarried gopis (gopi-vastra-harana-lila). The unmarried young gopis, headed by Dhanya, had developed such an intense attachment to Krsna that they were willing to perform severe austerities in the cold of winter so that they could become His wives. This desire was awakened in them by seeing Krsna and specifically by hearing about Him from the gopis who were already associating with Him.

The gopis described in these two chapters of the Bhagavatam were finally able to enjoy with Krsna the following autumn when, on the full moon night, He called them with His flute into the forest. That these girls had not yet enjoyed with Krsna is revealed by their own words; they cite Laksmi-devi as the example they will follow in receiving Krsna by first touching His lotus feet.

Jiva Gosvami gives two further pieces of evidence to affirm that this meeting with Krsna was the gopis' first opportunity to enjoy with Him: When the gopis first heard Krsna’s flute, they tried to suppress their ecstasy due to shyness, not having yet met Krsna in person. And when they finally answered His call, Krsna initially rejected them, which He would not have done if they had previously been in His association.

This autumn meeting of the gopis with Krsna is an example of a brief meeting that follows feelings of separation brought on by preliminary attraction (sanksipta-sambhoga or purva-raga-anantara-sambhoga).

Srila Rupa Gosvami cites another example of Srimati Radharani’s preliminary attraction for Krsna, quoting Sri Jayanta:

**“My dear friend! I was just going to the bank of the Yamuna, and all of a sudden a very nice boy whose complexion is like a dark blue cloud became visible in front of My eyes. He glanced over Me in a way that I cannot describe. But since this has occurred, I am sorry that I can no longer engage My mind in the duties of My household affairs.”**

With only slight variations in their accounts, great authorities have spoken of the bodily transformations that result from feelings of separation caused by preliminary attraction. Rupa Gosvami gives the following list of bodily symptoms in Sri Ujjvala-nilamani:

*cintatra jagarodvegau tanavam malinangata*  
*pralapo vyadhir unmado moho mrtyur dasa dasa*

**“The ten bodily transformations resulting from separation from Krsna are anxiety, wakefulness, mental agitation, thinness, uncleanliness, talking like a madman, disease, madness, illusion and death.”**

When symptoms like these are found in conditioned souls they indicate poor health, and they certainly make the afflicted person unattractive to others. But when these symptoms are manifest in the gopis during the transcendental stage of purva-raga, the beauty of the doc-eyed milkmaids of Vraja paradoxically increases. This is but one example of the difference between material and spiritual life.

And although the tenth symptom of separation is referred to as “death,” it is not a symptom devotees display in a literal sense, as that would be inauspicious in relation to Krsna. The death of a gopi would not give Krsna ecstasy but sadness, and the gopis would never want to be the cause of Krsna’s sorrow. They only want Him to be happy — even if Krsna’s happiness requires that they must suffer.

Nevertheless, while suffering from the pain of separation, Srimati Radharani once revealed to Visakha Her desire to die:

**“My dear friend, if Krsna is unkind to Me, there will be no need for you to cry, for it will not be due to any fault of yours. I shall then have to die, but afterwards please do one thing for Me. To observe My funeral ceremony, place My body with its arms wrapped like creepers, embracing a tamala tree, so that I may remain forever in Vrndavana undisturbed. That is My last request.”**

Lord Krsna’s pastimes display numberless symptoms of loving emotion caused both by preliminary attraction and the brief meetings that follow. Of that endless variety, the ten symptoms already listed are the most prominent. According to a devotee’s constitutional state (sthayi-bhava), his or her love for Krsna is classified — in increasing degrees of selflessness — as ordinary, proper, or perfect. The more selfless one’s love for Krsna is, the more intense and more varied are the symptoms that result from preliminary attraction. And because the gopis’ love is perfect, or most developed (praudha), their attraction shows the greatest variety of symptoms. Therefore, the most extreme symptom, the yearning for death, appears only in the gopis.

Rupa Gosvami concludes his description of preliminary attraction by citing the single best example to illustrate the topic — the symptoms of purva-raga exhibited by Krsna after falling in love with Radha.

Once Vrnda-devi told Radharani:

**“Overwhelmed with bliss from tasting the fragrance of His own sweet flute-music, Krsna has now stopped playing His flute. He has forgotten to decorate Himself with many kinds of flower ornaments. He no longer thirsts to enjoy charming pastimes with an army of gopis. My friend, He is now simply allowing the restless wind of His heart to be drunk by the restless serpent of Your eyebrows.”**

Feelings of Separation in Jealous Anger (Mana) and the Types of Meetings that Follow (Sankirna-sambhoga): This section will examine the separation that arises from jealous anger (mana) and the corresponding meetings (sankirna-sambhoga or mana-anantara-sambhoga) that take place. Mana (“jealous anger”) is explained in Sri Ujjvala-nilamani:

*dam-patyor bhava ekatra sator apy anuraktayoh*  
*svabhistaslesa-viksadi-nirodhi mana ucyate*

**“The word mana indicates the mood a lover and beloved go through, whether in one place or in different places, which obstructs their looking at each other and embracing each other, despite the fact that they are attached to each other.”**

Also in the same text, Srila Rupa Gosvami describes maria in another way:

**“When the lover feels novel sweetness by exchanging hearty loving words but wishes to hide her feelings by crooked means, mana is endured.”**

Or, to put it in yet another way, maria is jealous anger that enhances loving affairs between young couples. It is, by nature, crooked like the movements of a snake, and is said to be of two kinds — with cause and without cause.

Mana and its symptomatic emotions should not be mistaken for mundane jealousy, which is born of envy. Mana is a purely spiritual emotion that arises when a beloved of Krsna fears she will lose Him or His love to a rival. When this fear arises in her heart it quickly leads to jealousy, and from jealousy to anger. The fear, jealousy, and anger that the gopis feel are all consequences of pure love for Krsna. Therefore, mana is completely transcendental.

The two types of jealous anger — with cause and without — have been explained by Srila Rupa Gosvami in Sri Ujjvala-nilamani. When Krsna shows affection for a rival, giving her special attention, then the jealous anger of His lover is justified because it has a cause (haituka- mana). When, however, there is no legitimate reason for the lover to think that Krsna’s affections have been directed elsewhere, but she still believes that He has been unfaithful and consequently rejects His advances, that jealous anger is said to be without cause (ahaituka- mana). This is the more intense of the two types of jealous anger.

An example of jealous anger with cause took place on the full moon evening of autumn: Having pleased the gopis during the rasa dance, Krsna left their company with Sri Radha. After some time He returned. The gopis welcomed Krsna with smiles, playful glances, and dancing eyebrows, and massaged His hands and feet as they held them in their laps. Still, Sukadeva Gosvami says, the gopis felt somewhat angry (kupitah). They felt angry because, even at their first intimate meeting with Krsna, they had been slighted for another.

Krsna pacified the gopis by speaking sweet words of praise that quickly won their hearts. Krsna said:

“My dear girls, understanding that simply for My sake you had rejected the authority of worldly opinion, of the Vedas, and of your relatives, I acted as I did only to increase your attachment to Me. Even when I removed Myself from your sight by suddenly disappearing, I never stopped loving you. Therefore, My beloved gopis, please do not harbour any bad feelings toward Me, your beloved. I am not able to repay My debt for your spotless service, even within a lifetime of Brahma. Your connection with Me is beyond reproach. You have worshipped Me, cutting off all domestic ties, which are difficult to break. Therefore please let your own glorious deeds be your compensation.”

Jiva Gosvami explains that jealous anger can also be a cause of pride. When pride and jealous anger are “without cause” (ahaituka-mana) they are born merely from love for the Lord. Jiva Gosvami gives an example of this kind of pride without cause: the pride the gopis felt at being the exclusive objects of Krsna’s attention.  And Srimad-Bhagavatam states:

**“Lord Kesava, seeing the gopis too proud of their good fortune, wanted to relieve them of this pride and show them further mercy. Thus He immediately disappeared.”**

In this incident the gopis’ pride had no external cause: it was born solely from their love for Krsna. The gopis became proud that Krsna loved them, and their display of pride became a further impetus for loving exchange. A similar sort of pride was then displayed by Srimati Radharani. As Radha and Krsna wandered together through Vrndavana forest, She thought, “My beloved has rejected all the other gopis and has chosen to reciprocate with Me alone.” Feeling proud of Herself, Radha told Lord Kesava, “I cannot walk any further. Please carry Me wherever You want to go.”

When both kinds of pride were concluded — pride without cause and pride with cause — then Krsna and the gopis were able to enjoy the pleasures of meeting (sambhoga) as is confirmed in Srimad-Bhagavatam:

**“When the cowherd girls heard the Supreme Personality of Godhead speak these most charming words, they forgot their distress caused by separation from Him. Touching His transcendental limbs, they felt all their desires fulfilled.”**

The eight causes for jealous anger, already listed on page 439, appear in many places throughout the narrations of Krsna’s pastimes. Let us now examine a few examples.

Once, a gopi falsely informed Sasimukhi — one of Radha’s closest friends — that Krsna had been unfaithful to her. In anger, deep within Vrndavana forest Sasimukhi abandoned innocent Krsna, leaving Him pained in the absence of His beloved.

At another time Krsna was trying to win Candravali’s favour when He accidentally addressed her as “Radharani,’’ thereby angering His sweetheart. Hearing this slip of the lip, Padma — Candravali’s intimate friend and advisor — rebuked Krsna with these words, “O cheater! This is not the sixteenth star Radha seated before You, but the host of moons, Candravali. O black one! You should now leave here because my friend has turned red and is not about to give up her glistening anger.” Indeed, it took much time and effort, especially in light of Krsna’s considerable gift for amorous persuasion, to again win Candravali’s heart.

At yet another time Krsna hastily left Candravali, arousing Padma’s suspicions. Padma became angry when she followed Krsna and saw Him meet RadharanI by the Yamuna’s shore.

The examples of anger with a cause just given may be compared to the examples of anger without a cause described in the following section.

Once, as Krsna returned from the forest with His friends and cows, He passed by Abhimanyu’s house, as He often did. But when Radha saw Him approaching She was suddenly overwhelmed by jealous anger, without rhyme or reason. Radha completely ignored Krsna, even though He was practically on Her doorstep. Hoping to talk some sense into her irrational friend, Syama gopi said to Radha, “O friend! Krsna gazes at You from the corner of His eyes with amorous love and You simply stare at Him as if He were not even present. Crush the jealousy in Your heart and go outside to greet Him.”

At another time, Radha and Krsna sat in a grove on the bank of the Yamuna, laughing and joking with each other. Before long Radha had Krsna completely under Her control, and so She ordered Him, just as She would order a menial servant, to collect flowers with which to decorate Her. Krsna wandered happily through the gardens along the riverside eager to fulfil His appointed task, but when He returned He found Radha frozen over in a fit of jealous anger for no apparent reason. Krsna was stunned by Her cold-hearted reception. With folded hands He pleaded, “O Manini! You ordered Me to pick flowers and now You are cold and silent for no good reason. Please give up this trick and tell Me which flower I should place on Your ear.”

At yet another time, while in the company of Their girlfriends, both Radha and Krsna became angry with each other for no reason the gopis could discern. Krsna hung His head in strubborn silence while Radha refused to acknowledge His presence. The gopis were perplexed by this aimless ill will, but Vrnda took the courage to speak up: “My dear friends. You have robbed each other of Your smiles for absolutely no reason at all. Please therefore resume Your usually cheerful demeanour and give up this senseless quarrel that has saddened us all.”

Just as there are many ways in which jealous anger is caused, there are also many ways in which it is quelled. Naturally, jealous anger that has a cause (haituka-mana) is more easily mollified than anger without a cause, because there is a discernible chain of events that has led to it, a chain that is absent in jealous anger without a cause (ahaituka-mana). Jealous anger with a cause can therefore be effectively appeased by logic, conciliatory words, or actions, but jealous anger without a cause is only appeased in time, through the natural course of loving affairs.

In whichever way jealous anger is quelled, it results in an enjoyable reunion between Krsna and His beloved that perpetually increases the unlimited ocean of transcendental bliss. Krsna has thus invented an endless variety of ways to pacify angry gopis, which include speaking sweet words, speaking sarcastic words, or having another person appease His lover on His behalf.

Sometimes Krsna ends a quarrel by giving the angry gopi a gift (dana), by falling at her feet (nati), or by refusing to speak to her (upeksa) out of apparent neglect. Sometimes He ends a lover’s quarrel by a fearful situation (rasantaram) like the sudden appearance of a dangerous snake, by an unexpected accident (yadrcchikam) like a thunder clash, or by a deliberately and carefully executed plan (buddhi-purvam) like pretending to be bitten by a snake. Sometimes the influence of a particular place, a special time, or the sound of Krsna’s flute will pacify the jealous anger of the Vraja gopis.

The reunion of Radha and Krsna described below reveals the imaginative sweetness with which Krsna pacifies His angry sweetheart.

Once Radha became very angry with Krsna and left Him alone in the forest as She walked back to Her father’s palace. Krsna waited for Her to return, but His patience was for naught. In time He also went home, unhappy at having been separated from His beloved, and unhappier still at seeing little likelihood for a speedy reconcilliation. Some days later — still without having won His lover’s forgiveness— Krsna accompanied His parents to a festival, where they happened to meet the monarchs of Varsana, accompanied by their daughter, Sri Radha. Sri Hara writes:

**“In the presence of His elders, Krsna moved His head so its shadow appeared at Radha’s feet. Radha responded by moving the vines of Her arms so their shadows embraced Krsna. In this way, Radha and Krsna ended Their playful lover’s quarrel and became again pleased with each other as Their unsuspecting elders looked on. All glories to Radha and Krsna again becoming pleased.”**

The Apparent Separation of Prema-vaicittya: The type separation called prema-vaicittya is explained in Sri Ujjvala-nilamani,

*priyasya sannikarse ’pi premotkarsa-svabhavatah*  
*ya visesa-dhiyartis tat prema-vaicittyam ucyate*

**"Prema-vaicittya is a word used to indicate an abundance of love that brings about grief from fear of separation, although the lover is present.”**

During prema-vaicittya the devotee is not actually separated from Krsna, and thus little has been written about the “meeting” that dispels this kind of separation. But the pastime with the bee at Premavana and the formation of Prema-sarovara (narrated in chapter six) does describe in detail the symptoms of prema-vaicittya and the feelings that follow it. In fact, Rupa Gosvami cites the behaviour of Sri Radha at Prema-sarovara as a clear example of prema-vaicittya.

Some acaryas, like Srila Jiva Gosvami and Krsnadasa Kaviraja, cite the queens of Dvaraka — the vaibhava-prakasa expansions of Sri Radha — as another prominent example of prema-vaicittya. As the Caitanya-caritamrta states: “In Dvaraka, amongst the queens, feelings of prema-vaicittya are very prominent.”

Even when Krsna’s queens were in His presence, by the influence of their intense loving attachment to Him (anuraga), they would appear deranged and speak or behave as if under the influence of intoxicating substances. This transcendental madness (unmada) filled the queens with such ecstasy that, according to Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura, they “saw their own mood reflected in everyone and everything else.” While in the midst of their delirium, the queens spoke ten verses that are very famous amongst Vaisnavas. Even though Krsna had expanded Himself into as many forms as there were queens and was sleeping soundly in each of their beds simultaneously, the queens denied Krsna’s presence and spoke with the conviction that He was elsewhere. Their collective soliloquy begins with the following verse:

*kurari vilapasi tvam vita-nidra na sese*  
*svapiti jagati ratryam isvaro gupta-bodhah*  
*vayam iva sakhi kaccid gadha-nirviddha-ceta*  
*nalina-nayana-hasodara-lileksitena*

**“My dear friend kurari, it is now night, and Lord Sri Krsna is sleeping. You yourself are not asleep or resting but are lamenting. Should I presume that you, like us, are affected by the smiling, liberal, playful glances of lotus-eyed Krsna? If so, your heart is deeply pierced. Is that why you are showing these signs of sleepless lamentation?”**

The purport to this verse, composed by the followers of Srila Prabhupada, paraphrases the great acarya Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura:

**“Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti explains that the transcendental madness (unmada) of the queens filled them with such ecstasy that they saw their own mood reflected in everyone and everything else. Here they point out to the kurari bird, whom they take to be sorrowing over separation from Lord Krsna, that if the Lord actually had any concern for her or themselves, He would not be sleeping comfortably at that moment. They warn the kurari not to expect Krsna to hear her lamentation and show some mercy. In case the kurari might think that Krsna is sleeping with His queens, they deny this by saying that He is gupta-bodha: His whereabouts are unknown to them. He is out in the world somewhere this night, but they have no idea where to go looking for Him. ‘Ah, dear bird,’ they cry, ‘even though you are a simple creature, your heart has been deeply pierced, just like ours. You must have had some contact, then, with our Krsna. What keeps you from giving up your hopeless attachment to Him?’”**

The queens’ monologue is then directed at, amongst other things, the ocean, the moon, and a cloud, concluding with the following address to a swan:

**“Welcome, swan. Please sit here and drink some milk. Give us some news of the descendant of Sura, dear one. We know you are His messenger. Is that invincible Lord doing well, and does that unreliable friend of ours still remember the words He spoke to us long ago? Why should we go and worship Him? O servant of a petty master, go tell Him who fulfills our desires to come here without the goddess of fortune. Is she the only woman exclusively devoted to Him?”**

In his commentary, Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti relates the following conversation between the queens and the swan, reproduced here in full, which reflects the deep emotions and contrary psychological characteristic of prema-vaicittya:

The queens ask, “Is the unconquerable Lord doing well?”

The swan replies, “How can Lord Krsna be doing well without you, His beloved consorts?”

Queens: “But does He even remember what He once told one of us, Srimati Rukmini? Does He recall that He said, ‘In all My palaces I can find no other wife as loving as you’?”

Swan: “He does indeed remember this, and that is just why He sent me here. You should all go to Him and engage in His devotional service.”

Queens: “Why should we go worship Him if He refuses to come here to be with us?”

Swan: “But my dear oceans of compassion, He is suffering so much from your absence! How can He be saved from this distress?” Queens: “Just listen, O servant of a petty master: tell Him to come here, as He should. If He is suffering from lusty desires, He has only Himself to blame, since He Himself is the creator of Cupid’s power. We self-respecting ladies are not going to yield to His demand that we go seek Him out.”

Swan: “So be it. Then I will take my leave.”

Queens: “No, one minute, dear swan. Ask Him to come to us here, but without the goddess of fortune, who always cheats us by keeping Him all to herself.”

Swan: “Don’t you know that goddess Laksmi is devoted exclusively to the Lord? How could He give her up like that?”

Queens: “And is she the only woman in the world who is completely sold out to Him? What about us?”

Sri Jiva Gosvami describes the reunion that followed the queens’ ecstatic expressions of separation, *lebhire paramam gatim*: “\[they\] attained the ultimate goal.” In fact, the acarya asserts that the use of the present tense of the word kriyamanena (“behaving”) in that same verse means the queens were immediately transferred to Dvaraka in the spiritual world to enjoy the company of the Lord eternally.
