The queens’ display of prema-vaicittya is comparable to a similar display of prema-vaicittya found in Srimati Radharani. One day the flames of separation overcame Radha, and although Krsna was standing directly before Her, She completely lost sight of Him. In Her distress Radhika placed a piece of straw between Her teeth, and much to Krsna’s astonishment, begged a gopi friend, “Please show Me where My lover, Krsna, is.”

There are eight expressions of separation in prema-vaicittya, distinguished by whom they are addressed to, for instance an attendant, Cupid, the beloved’s flute, or the beloved Himself. However, in every case the beloved is there to witness these ecstatic utterances of the lover.

Although prema-vaicittya falls under the category of separation, that separation exists only in perception, for in reality the lover and beloved are still in close physical proximity. The type of separation that will now be described involves actual physical distance between Krsna and His associates. Known as pravasa, it has two divisions: separation by a small distance and separation by a great distance. Our discussion will begin by outlining the characteristics common to both.

Common Characteristics of Separation by Distance (Pravasa): Srila Rupa Gosvami describes *pravasa*,

*purva-sangatayor yunor bhaved desantaradibhih*  
*vyavadhanam tu yat prajnaih sa pravasa itiryate*

**"Pravasa is a word used to indicate the separation of lovers who were previously intimately associated. This separation is due to their being in different places.”**

The two types of pravasa — caused by separation from Krsna over a short distance or separation over a long distance — have some characteristics and effects that are unique to each and some that are common to both.

The following characteristics and effects are common to both types of pravasa. Pravasa brings about within the gopis the ten bodily transformations called dasa-dasa. External forces (abuddhi-purvaka), such as destiny or the demigods, are the cause of pravasa. And pravasa always ends in an enjoyable reunion (sambhoga) of lover and beloved.

The ten bodily transformations brought on by feelings of intense separation from Krsna have already been listed on page 444 of this appendix in the section dealing with purva-raga. Those symptoms are anxiety, wakefulness, mental agitation, thinness, uncleanliness, mad talking, disease, madness, illusion, and death.

In Sri Ujjvala-nilamani, Srila Rupa Gosvami gives examples of how Srimati Radharani suffered from each one of these ten symptoms. Those examples are reproduced here.

Cinta (“anxiety”)

“At Akrura’s request, Krsna and Balarama left the house of Nanda Maharaja for Mathura. At that time the mind of Srimati Radharani was disrupted, and She became almost mad because of extreme separation from Krsna. She experienced great mental pain and agitation, which caused Her to drown in mental speculation in the river of anxiety. She thought, ‘Now I am going to die, and when I die, Krsna will surely come back to see Me again. But when He hears of My death from the people of Vrndavana, He will certainly be very unhappy. Therefore I shall not die.’”

Jagara (“wakefulness”)

“Thinking Herself very unfortunate, Srimati Radharani addressed Her very dear friend Visakha, ‘My dear friend, if I could see Krsna in My dreams, I would certainly be glorified for My great fortune. But what can I do? Sleep also plays mischievously with Me. Indeed, it has become My enemy. Therefore I have not slept since the departure of Krsna.’”

Udvega (“mental agitation”)

“Srimati Radharani addressed Lalita, ‘My dear beautiful-faced Lalita, I cannot express how My heart is burning. It is a great, unfathomable ocean of anxiety. Still, I wish to offer My obeisances at your lotus feet. What shall I do? Please consider My condition and advise Me how I can become peaceful. That is My desire.’”

Tanava (“thinness”)

“When Uddhava returned to Mathura after visiting Vrndavana, Lord Krsna enquired from him about Radharani and Visakha. Uddhava replied as follows: ‘Consider the condition of the gopis! Srimati Radharani especially is in a very painful condition because of separation from You. She has grown skinny, and Her bodily lustre is almost gone. Her heart is immersed in pain, and because She has given up eating, Her breasts have become black, as if diseased. Because of separation from You, all the gopis, especially Radharani, appear like dried-up water holes under the scorching heat of the sun.’”

Malina-angata (“uncleanliness”)

“Uddhava said to Krsna, ‘O most auspicious Krsna, please hear me. The tribulation caused by Your absence has made Visakha languid. Her lips tremble like trees in a strong wind. Her beautiful face is like a lotus flower that has withered under the snow, and her eyes are like lotus petals scorched by the heat of the autumn sun.’”

Pralapa (“mad talking”)

“This is Srimati Radharani’s lamentation for Her beloved Krsna, who was away from home. A woman whose husband has left home and gone to a foreign land is called prosita-bhartrka. Lamenting for Krsna in the same way that such a woman laments for her husband, Srimati Radharani said, ‘My dear friend, where is the glory of the family of Maharaja Nanda, who wears a half-moon ornament on His head? Where is Krsna, whose hue is like that of the indranila jewel and who plays so nicely on His flute? Where is your friend, the best of all men, so expert in dancing in the circle of the rasa dance? Where is He who is the real medicine to save Me from dying of heart disease? I must condemn Providence, for he has caused Me so many tribulations by separating Me from Krsna.’”

Vyadhi (“disease”)

“Being greatly afflicted by the pain of separation from Krsna, Srimati Radharani said, ‘My dear Lalita, kindly hear Me. I cannot bear suffering the fever of separation from Krsna, nor can I explain it to you. It is something like gold melting in an earthen box. This fever produces more distress than poison, and it is more piercing than a thunderbolt. I suffer exactly like someone almost dead from cholera. To be giving Me so much pain, this fever must be very strong indeed.’”

Unmada (“madness”)

“Uddhava said to Krsna, ‘My dear Krsna, all the gopis are so afflicted by Your absence that they have become almost mad. O Murari, at home Srimati RadharanI laughs unnecessarily and, like a madwoman, enquires about You from every entity without distinction, even from the stones. She rolls on the ground, unable to bear the agony of Your absence.’”

Moha (“illusion”)

“Lalita wrote Krsna the following letter on Srimati Radharani’s behalf: ‘My dear Krsna, Srimati Radharani has fallen unconscious on the ground, Her mind greatly agitated by Her separation from You. O enemy of Kamsa, You have now become a first-class politician, and therefore You can supposedly give relief to everyone. Therefore please consider the plight of Srimati RadharanI, or very soon You will hear of Her death. Maybe at that time You will lament, although now You are jubilant.’”

Mrtyu (“death”)

“In the following letter, Lalita chastised Krsna for staying in Mathura: ‘Simply by dancing in the circle of the rasa dance, You attracted Srimati Radharani’s love. Why are You now so indifferent to my dear friend RadharanI? She is lying nearly unconscious, thinking of Your pastimes. I shall determine whether She is alive by putting a cotton swab under Her nostrils, and if She is still living, I shall chastise Her.’”

The gopis of Vraja are not the only ones to go through these ten states of separation. Krsna endures them as well. Krsna’s relationship with the gopis is reciprocal, so naturally He is as much a victim of the arrows of Cupid as are His beloveds. As a case in point, Uddhava once wrote a letter to Lalita describing Krsna’s indifference to the many beautiful and cultured princesses of Dvaraka. In spite of the fact that these royal damsels constantly attended to Krsna’s desires, Uddhava informed Lalita: “Continually remembering Radharani’s artisitic expertise in loving pastimes on a stone bed in a cave of Govardhana Hill, Krsna faints from moment to moment.”

There is nothing in the material or spiritual domains that is outside of Krsna’s ultimate control. Nevertheless, Krsna’s pastimes are specifically controlled by His internal pastime potency (lila-sakti), which has been deputed by the Lord to take on this responsibility. Whatever exists or occurs within the Lord’s creation is therefore under the direct supervision of the Lord Himself or is supervised indirectly by the Lord’s potencies.

However, in analysing the separation of Krsna and the gopis from the perspective of the Lord’s transcendental pastimes — in which Krsna and the gopis act as ordinary members of the cowherd community , not as the Supreme Lord and His associates — it can be said that separation is forced upon Krsna and the gopis by external forces. This forced separation is called abuddhi-purvaka.

An example of forced separation, in this case as a result of destiny, occurs in the pastime of the demon Sankhacuda. When Krsna heard that Sankhacuda had stolen Radharani, Krsna said:

**“Eager with hundreds of desires, I brought You to this place deep in Vrndavana forest, filled with the sweet fragances of this full-moon autumn night. O beautiful one, why has cruel fate, appearing as this tricky demon Sankhacuda, now carried You far away?”**

The very purpose of separation from Krsna is to heighten the love devotees feel at the time of being reunited with Him. Regardless of how far Krsna travels from Vrndavana or how long He stays away, He ultimately returns to Vraja, the land of loving pastimes, and always rewards the steadfast love of the Vraja-vasis, especially the gopis. As Jiva Gosvami says, “In the pravasa separation felt by the Vraja gopis, the gopis did meet Lord Krsna again.” The ecstasy of meeting depends upon the pain of separation, and the agony of separation is meaningless without the eventual bliss of a reunion.

The final sections of this appendix will describe the two respective kinds of meeting that come after Krsna’s brief and extensive travels.

Separation by a Short Distance or Span of Time (Kincid-dura-pravasa) and Its Subsequent Mature Type of Meeting (Sampanna-sambhoga): The outline on page 441 lists the occasions when the gopis feel separation from Krsna as a result of His brief absence. These occasions are when the gopis merely anticipate Krsna’s departure, when Krsna enters the Yamuna to fight with Kaliya, when He goes to tend the cows, when He leaves for Varuna’s kingdom to rescue His father, and when Krsna disappears during the rasa dance.

These kinds of separation are brought to an end by enjoyable meetings that include the eight listed on page 441, beginning with “viewing each other over a long distance” and ending with “meeting during pastimes of feigned sleep.”

The reunions that follow periods of short separation from the beloved occur in two ways: exclusively in one pastime (eka-lila-gata) or repeatedly, on some sort of regular basis (paramparantarala-gata), be it daily or otherwise. Examples of both can be found in Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Krsna’s disappearance from amidst the gopis during the rasa dance and His subsequent departure from Radha in the forest are both singular incidents involving separation of the gopis and Krsna.

But the gopis’ daily glorification of Krsna while He was absent in the pasturelands of Vraja tending His father’s cows serves as an example of a period of short separation that occurs on a regular basis (vipralambha-kincid-dura-pravasa-paramparantarala-gata), in this case, daily. Sukadeva Gosvami refers to the suffering the gopis feel on those occasions as resulting from this type of separation: “Whenever Krsna went to the forest, the minds of the gopis would run after Him, and thus the young girls sadly spent their days singing of His pastimes.”

Another example of this sort of short separation was once described to Krsna by one of Radha’s gopi friends. She said, “O Krsna, when You are in the pasture herding the cows, Radharani spends Her day longing to see You again. She fixes Her eyes on the path where You will eventually return with the surabhi cows, She engages Her tongue in repeating the two syllables Krs-na, Her ears yearn to hear the sound of Your flute, and Her heart is fixed in the happiness of thinking of You.”

But whether Krsna leaves the gopis at one particular time for some unique purpose, or He repeatedly leaves them as part of His eternal pastimes, He always returns to the young girls of Vraja. Sukadeva Gosvami tells Maharaja Pariksit how the songs of the gopis brought Krsna back into their fold to restore their lives:

**“O King, having thus sung and spoken their hearts out in various charming ways, the gopis began to weep loudly. They were very eager to see Lord Krsna. Then Lord Krsna, a smile on His lotus face, appeared before the gopis. Wearing a garland and a yellow garment, He directly appeared as one who can bewilder the mind of Cupid, who bewilders the minds of ordinary people. When the gopis saw that their dearmost Krsna had returned to them, they all stood up at once, and out of affection for Him their eyes bloomed wide. It was as if the air of life had re-entered their bodies.”**

Jiva Gosvami tells how in this instance the pain of separation felt by the gopis was mitigated in two phases. First, when Krsna reappeared, His personal presence made the gopis forget the misery they had been enduring. Second, after they had drunk in the vision of Krsna’s loveliness to their hearts’ content, the gopis’ heartache actually vanished, for their desires were then completely fulfilled. Clearly, meeting with Krsna is like diving into an ocean of divine enjoyment. Just seeing Krsna is an antidote to the pangs of separation from Him. In fact, it is so powerful that even from a distance a vision of His form is no less potent than associating with Him directly. For that reason, gazing at Krsna is considered a kind of meeting (sambhoga), one that the gopis describe from first-hand encounters:

**“As Krsna respectfully greets His well-wishing friends, His eyes roll slightly as if from intoxication. He wears a flower garland, and the beauty of His soft cheeks is accentuated by the brilliance of His golden earrings and the whiteness of His face, which has the color of a badara berry. With His cheerful face resembling the moon, lord of the night, the Lord of the Yadus moves with the grace of a regal elephant. Thus He returns in the evening, delivering the cows of Vraja from the heat of the day.”**

Separation by a Long Distance or Span of Time (Sudura-pravasa) and Its Subsequent Fully Enriched Meeting (Samrddhimana-sambhoga): Extraordinarily intense feelings of separation from Krsna — including the hopeless conviction that He will never return again — arise when Krsna has left Vrndavana for Mathura, Dvaraka, or any other distant place. Such feelings of separation occur in all three phases of time; that is to say, when Krsna’s departure is planned for the future (bhavi), when it is taking place in the present (bhavan), and when it is spoken of or thought of as an event in the past (bhuta). Examples of feelings of separation occurring in each of these three categories are given below, in relationship to Krsna and Balarama leaving for Mathura.

Bhavi

Kamsa sent Akrura to Vrndavana with an invitation for Krsna and Balarama to come to Mathura. When the young gopis heard of Akrura’s plan to take Krsna and His brother to the city, “they became extremely distressed.” Although Krsna was still in Vraja, His imminent departure awoke feelings of separation in the gopis, inciting them to damn Providence — and therefore Akrura — as “cruel.” In fact, the threat of separation from Krsna convinced them that they would gladly disclose their illicit relationship with the prince of Vraja, and face certain ignominy, if they could simply convince Krsna to stay.

Bhavan

Nevertheless, and much to the gopis’ dismay, Akrura began to drive the horses with Krsna and Balarama aboard his chariot. The sounds of the gopis’ loud crying mixed with the rumbling of the chariot wheels as they started to roll. Akrura and the two brothers were followed behind by the wagons of Nanda Maharaja and the other cowherd men, with the bewildered gopis following on foot. The gopis walked for some time until they stood frozen in place, like painted images, hoping that Krsna would give them some last message, some ray of hope. Moved by the gopis’ lamentation, Krsna sent this loving promise as a consolation: “I will return.”

Bhuta

As Akrura’s chariot and its two dearly beloved passengers receded into the distance, the gopis continued to stand motionless like statues, staring at Krsna as He slowly disappeared from view. The gopis remained there staring into the distance as long as they could see the flag atop Akrura’s chariot flapping in the wind. They remained there even until the cloud of dirt raised by the chariot wheels had again settled upon the dusty road leading to Mathura.

Sukadeva Gosvami says:

*ta nirasa nivavrtur govinda-vinivartane*  
*visoka ahani ninyur gayantyah priya-cestitam*

**“The gopis then turned back, without hope that Govinda would ever return to them. Full of sorrow, they began to spend their days and nights chanting about the pastimes of their beloved.”**

Srila Jiva Gosvami says the word visoka used in this verse indicates that now that Krsna had left them, the gopis suffered from intense sorrow, and their only relief was to chant Krsna’s glories. But because the gopis’ love for Krsna was so exalted, and now further amplified by their feelings of separation, as they sang about Krsna’s pastimes it was as if those pastimes were happening directly before their very eyes.

The pangs of separation one feels after (bhuta) Krsna’s departure are more intense than those felt before (bhavi) or during (bhavan) His departure. The future and the present are short-lived and both quickly give way to an agonisingly long-lived past that seems to stretch into eternity. As the time they were last together with Krsna recedes further into that eternal past, even a moment appears to the gopis like a millennium. Therefore, the feelings of separation that we find described in Krsna’s pastimes are dominated by examples of the type of separation that afflicts Krsna’s devotees after the Lord’s departure to a far distant place — vipralambha-bhuta sudura-pravasa.

The only real remedy for the suffering of the gopis is for Krsna to return to them, but the Lord achieves some small measure of success in comforting His devotees by sending them messages that relay the depth of His love for them. These messages are entrusted to the Lord’s confidential associates — like Lord Balarama, Uddhava, or others — in either oral or written form.

For instance, after Lord Krsna returned from His studies with Sandipani Muni He sent Uddhava to Vrndavana. The Lord’s plan was to bring Uddhava into contact with the Vraja-vasi’s incomparable love for Krsna, and to have Uddhava deliver letters from Krsna to His long-suffering kinsmen, friends, and lovers. Once again, some years later, after Krsna had moved with the residents of Mathura to Dvaraka, Lord Balarama returned to Vrndavana and also relayed messages from Krsna, narrating Krsna’s pastimes to the anguished Vraja-vasis.

Both transcendental messengers met privately with the gopis, who had abandoned their customary shyness to reveal feelings they had long kept secret. The gopis expressed love and anger, mocking Krsna with their words even as they glorified Him within their hearts.

The most revealing words were spoken by Srimati Radharani to a bumblebee, which She imagined to be a messenger from Krsna. In a fit of madness inspired by Her intense feelings of separation from Krsna, Radha conveyed Her torment in so many ways: She expressed Her anger at Krsna for His having abandoned Her and the other gopis; She criticised Her rival, the goddess of fortune; She rejected the praise of Herself that She heard in the humming of the bee; She denounced all discussion of Krsna as a source of misery, while simultaneously admitting that She could not stop talking about Him; She claimed that Krsna is black because of His misdeeds; and She asserted that Krsna should be rejected, because even though to talk about Him may be nectar for the ears, it is poison for the heart. After speaking in such wild and derogatory ways, Radha’s mood abruptly changed and She again glorified Krsna. She even gave Her blessings to the bee. These mad emotional talks (citra-jalpa) of Sri Radha confounded Uddhava, the great devotee and scholar. At a loss, he could only repeat Krsna’s message to the gopis and praise them as the topmost transcendentalists.

The gopis were elated to hear that Uddhava had brought letters for them from Krsna. They were not pleased, however, with the initial content of Krsna’s message, which described Him as the all-pervading Supreme Being and prescribed the path of jnana as the way the gopis could always be with Him. But when Krsna went on to explain His purpose for leaving Vraja, the gopis were somewhat pacified. Krsna wrote:

*yat tv aham bhavatinam vai dure varte priyo drsam*  
*manasah sannikarsartham mad-anudhyana-kamyaya*

**“But the actual reason why I, the beloved object of your sight, have stayed far away from you is that I wanted to intensify your meditation upon Me and thus draw your minds closer to Me.”**

And when Krsna assured the gopis that they would soon meet Him again, the girls of Vraja were exceedingly pleased, their fever of separation relieved. In happiness they worshipped Uddhava as nondifferent from Lord Krsna.

Thus, for some time after receiving Krsna’s message the gopis felt relief. That is, until the winds of divine fate again stirred the turbulent ocean of love in separation, causing them to exhibit all manner of sublime and terrible emotions, the likes of which bewilder the mind of even Lord Brahma. In their ecstasy they would cry, roll on the ground, or condemn Krsna as hard-hearted. The gopis’ moods continued to oscillate between happiness, which accompanied the hope they felt when they received Krsna’s occasional message, to the extreme distress that again set in when Krsna still had not returned. Thus, to the eye unfamiliar with the ways of ecstatic devotion, the gopis appeared like madwomen or souls possessed. But in truth, the gopis’ turbulent emotions served to purify their love for Krsna, not unlike how the roaring flames of the blacksmith’s fire remove impurities from even the purest gold. And the gopis would persist in such contradictory, unpredictable, and extreme behaviour until they could again sec Krsna face to face — which they ultimately did.

**\* \* \* \***

All separation ends in an eventual reunion. The more painful the separation is, the more pleasurable the reunion will be. This is how feelings of separation nourish the ecstasy of meeting. And the most intense kinds of separation — which achieve their intensity by greater distance and time — are brought to an end by meetings that include the eight listed on pages 441-442, beginning with “meeting in dreams” and ending with “meeting while the lover assumes a domineering mood.”

After many, many years of being separated, Krsna and the gopis were reunited in a well-known meeting at Kuruksetra. For the observance of a solar eclipse, Lord Krsna travelled to Kuruksetra, where He again met the Vraja-vasis. Krsna first greeted His parents and the other senior members of His family before joining the gopis in a garden on a nearby hilltop, where the gopis had been watching their Lord from a distance. Sukadeva Gosvami says,

**“The Supreme Lord approached the gopis in a secluded place as they stood in their ecstatic trance. After embracing each of them and enquiring about their well-being, He laughed and spoke as follows.”**

During that exchange of loving words, Krsna removed the gopis’ pain of separation by first agitating their hearts with love. He then pleaded that they forgive Him for having left, and finally pacified their hearts with transcendental knowledge. Over the three months that followed, Krsna and the gopis fully relished the ecstasy of being reunited by seeing, touching, and conversing with one another. Then Krsna and the gopis were briefly separated again, after which, Jiva Gosvami states, they came together once more, never to be separated thereafter.

But if separation is integral to union, how is it that the gopis and Krsna could come to be together forever?

Jiva Gosvami substantiates his claim by first quoting Srimad-Bhagavatam: “Thus Lord Krsna, the spiritual master of the gopis and the very purpose of their life, showed them His mercy.” Sri Jiva explains that by “showing them His mercy” the Lord saw to it that the gopis obtained their previously stated desire — Krsna Himself.

Jiva Gosvami further substantiates his argument by citing a statement in the Padma Purana that describes Krsna’s two-month return to Vrndavana, and the Vraja-vasis’ subsequent entrance into aprakata pastimes for “eternal samyoga with Him \[Krsna\].”

Sri Jiva also cites Krsna’s own words to Uddhava as evidence of the gopis’ attainment of eternal union:

**“My dear Uddhava, just as great sages in yoga trance merge into self-realisation, like rivers merging into the ocean, and are thus not aware of material names and forms, similarly, the gopis of Vrndavana were so completely attached to Me within their minds that they could not think of their own bodies, or of this world, or of their future lives. Their entire consciousness was simply bound up in Me. All those hundreds of thousands of gopis, understanding Me to he their most charming lover and ardently desiring Me in that way, were unaware of My actual position. Yet by intimately associating with Me, the gopis attained Me, the Supreme Absolute Truth.”**

The essence of Jiva Gosvami's argument is this: the gopis’ paramour relationship with Krsna in this world was a shadow of their paramour relationship (parakrya-abhasa) with Krsna in Goloka Vrndavana. It was a shadow in the sense that during Krsna’s earthly pastimes the gopis imagined they were mortal and that they would thus have only one life to spend with their beloved. But they are actually Krsna’s eternal associates, and after their brief sojourn in Bhauma Vrndavana they returned to the spiritual world to enjoy with Krsna eternally. The aprakata-lila they enjoyed in this world was merely a hint of the nitya-lila they eternally enjoy in the world beyond. When, by Krsna’s grace, the gopis’ misconception was corrected, then they were able to enjoy the full bliss of perfect union (sambhoga) with Him in Goloka Vrndavana.

In Goloka, Krsna and His associates live eternally in union with one another, and the appearance of separation is repeatedly super-imposed upon that union for the sake of enriching their mutual love. But separation never lasts forever. It lasts only until the next meeting. Thus it can be said that because Krsna and the gopis eternally live together in Goloka, they are eternally united (in sambhoga). In this way, by returning to Goloka, the gopis attained eternal enjoyment with the Lord.

Meeting is of two types: direct (mukhya) and indirect (gauna). Direct meeting takes place while lover and beloved are awake and indirect meeting takes place during dreams. Both types of meeting occur after the four kinds of separation, and can be classified within the four categories of sambhoga: brief, intruded upon, mature, and perfect.

Direct meeting offers the lovers a variety of amorous pastimes, which include cheating tricks and meditating on the beloved. All these activities are at once sweet and hot, like the palatable taste of boiled sugarcane.

Direct meetings arc further divided into two types: scheduled and expected (agati), or sudden and unexpected (pradurbhava). Because most meetings arc scheduled and expected, the two examples given here are of sudden and unexpected meetings. One example takes place after a short absence from Krsna, the other after a long absence.

During the rasa dance Krsna left the gopis, who had abandoned their families to heed the call of His flute-song. Naturally they became very morose. After searching for Krsna in the depths of the forest, the gopis sat down on the bank of the Yamuna and grieved that they had lost Him. All of a sudden — wearing yellow garments, a flower garland, and a mischievous smile — Krsna appeared in their midst.

On another occasion, after Krsna had left Vrndavana for Mathura, He suddenly appeared before Radharani like a vision or an hallucination. Sri Radha told Lalita exactly what happened:

“Listen My friend to what I have seen with My very eyes! And do not think that My mind is bewildered and that what I am about tell you is actually a dream: Your friend Krsna, unexpectedly and all of a sudden, entered the forest near Govardhana Hill and once again displayed His expertise in a lover’s quarrel with Me.”

The pain of separation is in direct proportion to the intensity of love the gopis feel for Krsna (and vice versa). The happiness of a subsequent meeting also increases in proportion to the intensity of the feelings it relieves, with the potential to be a veritable festival of unimaginable happiness.

Meeting the beloved in a dream is practically indistinguishable from meeting while awake, and therefore both types of meeting are equally pleasurable. While dreaming of Krsna, the gopis partake in all the adventures of separation and meeting, along with their concomitant ecstasies. These encounters are worlds apart from the dreams of conditioned souls, which are no more than another layer of illusion produced by material nature. But this is not the case for the gopis, who are situated in love of God. Indeed, the playful nature of divine love brings the gopis into actual contact with Krsna on the pretext of dreams.

Srimati Radharani reveals Her night-time visions to Visakha:

**“My dear friend! Every night in My dream a certain strong young man, whose bodily lustre mocks the splendour of a host of fresh lotus flowers, and who is the crest jewel of all men expert at enjoying amorous pastimes, enjoys with Me in the forest by the Yamuna’s shore and kisses My mouth.”**

And just as the gopis enjoy the pleasures of romantic love with Krsna while dreaming, they also suffer the pain of separation from Him. Radharani told Nava-Vrnda:

**“After repeated attempts over a long time to see Him, Govinda finally appeared before Me in a dream. But, alas, no sooner had He entered the courtyard of My eyes, than Akrura, the cruel messenger of King Kamsa at once appeared on a chariot to take Him away again.”**

Enjoyment in Sambhoga as the Conclusion of Separation:

After a period of separation has achieved its purpose — to increase the mutual love of Krsna and the gopis — lover and beloved meet again by divine arrangement and enjoy the heightened pleasure that follows the pain of separation. Refreshed love and the magnified ecstacies that go along with it make meeting appear new, even within the context of a familiar pastime, as if emotions and events are all happening for the first time. This aspect of Krsna’s pastimes, as perpetually new — whether perceived directly or through hearing of them — is called nitya-nava-navayamana.

When Krsna and the gopis meet, they enjoy an unlimited variety of romantic pastimes that charm the hearts of audience and participants alike, and which include seeing each other, talking to each other, touching each other, blocking each other’s paths, taking part in a rasa dance, enjoying pastimes in Vrndavana forest, enjoying water-sports in the Yamuna River or the other waterways of Vraja, boating, enjoying playful thievery, enacting a toll collecting pastime, disappearing into the forest or another secluded place, drinking honey-liquor, Krsna disguising Himself as a gopi, pretending to be asleep, gambling, pulling each other’s garments, kissing, embracing, scratching each other with the fingernails, drinking from each other’s bimba-fruit lips, and enjoying union.

I shall conclude this appendix by giving only one example of the list above — how Krsna pretends to be asleep.

Once, while enjoying the company of some gopis within a secluded forest grove, Krsna closed His eyes and pretended to be asleep so that He could secretly listen to their conversations. When Krsna heard the gopis’ speaking playfully about His own sweetness, those words were so pleasing to His ears that a gentle smile appeared upon His lips, and the hairs of His body stood on end.

## Appendix 12

**Qualities in the Gopis’ Forms That Invoke Ecstatic Love in Krsna**

(This appendix follows from the description of Krsna’s youthful attraction to the gopis, which began while Radharani and Her friends were residing in Varsana, as described in chapter seven.)

Srila Rupa Gosvami lists seven aspects of the gopis’ forms that inspire love in Krsna: youthfulness (tarunya), beauty (rupa), lustrous beauty (lavanya), mature beauty (saundarya), transforming beauty (abhirupata), sweetness (madhurya), and delicacy (mardhva). In the stanza under discussion, I include the four characteristics relating to beauty under one heading, reducing my list to four — youthfulness, beauty, sweetness, and delicacy.

We should not misunderstand this analysis of feminine qualities to be inspired by the mode of passion. The gopis, like Krsna, are completely transcendental, beyond even the mode of goodness, not to mention the mode of passion. To describe their charms is to delineate the various ways in which the spiritual pleasure potency manifests itself in the gopis in order to increase unlimitedly the bliss Krsna feels from exchanges of love. And because a unique feature of the gopis’ love is to offer even their bodies to Krsna in devotional service, to analyse how their physical features attract and nurture Krsna’s love for them, when done by a qualified person with proper understanding, is spiritually edifying.

Youth, more than any other physical quality, brings spontaneity and zest to loving relationships. According to Rupa Gosvami, the gopis go through four phases of youth: the junction between childhood and youth (vayah-sandhi), the beginning of youth (navya), pronounced youth (vyakta), and full-blown youth (purna).

Sri Ujjvala-nilamani describes how one day, while Srimati Radharani was between childhood and youth (vayah-sandhi), Krsna spotted Her from a distance, turned to Subala and said, “The king of youthfulness is now entering his kingdom in Radharani’s transcendental body. Recognising the king’s greatness, Radharani’s beautiful hips greet him with the tinkling sounds of a sash of bells; fearing destruction at the king’s hand, Radharani’s waist girds itself with three folds of skin. And to present a suitable gift, the two ripe fruits of Radharani’s breasts offer themselves to the king.”

To describe the change in the princess of Varsana’s behaviour at the junction of childhood and youth, Visakha spoke these joking words to Her: “The bumblebee of Your sidelong glance now desires to land on the blue lotus flower of Your eyes, the young swan of Your heart searches for the tender lotus-sprout of Your shyness, and a flood of honey-soaked joking words rises on the lotus flower of Your mouth. O beautiful Radharani! I think You are now preparing a great festival of happiness just to please Madhava.”

In the second phase, the beginning of youth (navya), childish qualities retreat. New emotions appear within the gopis’ hearts, along with certain bodily transformations: their breasts begin to develop, their eyes are a little restless, their smiles are enchantingly mild. Indeed, their entire personalities churn with emotions of amorous love.

Vrnda-devi glorifies the beauty of Srimati Radharani’s body at the beginning of youth: “Your breasts are now a little raised, Your words a little crooked, Your eyes a little restless, Your hips a little firm, Your waist a little slender. The line of hair between Your navel and breasts is just beginning to show. O beautiful-faced girl, Your fresh youthfulness is now perfectly fit to be engaged in the service of Hari.”

The psychological symptoms — restlessness and eagerness — of this second stage of youth in which divine lust has not yet matured, can be understood from the following words spoken by an older, experienced gopi to her young sister-in-law: “My child, as you wander about searching for Lord Krsna, you cast a host of furtive glances towards the path trod by the surabhi cows. And the breeze carrying Krsna’s bodily aroma agitates you. In your heart the fire of love for Krsna has been kindled, but still that fire is covered by smoke.”

The appearance and effects of the third phase — pronounced youth (vyakta) — bless the gopis with opulence, just as rain clouds shower beauty and fragrance upon a garden of freshly bloomed flowers. The gopis' flower-bud breasts develop, three charming folds of skin appear on their waists, and the limbs of their bodies glow with a golden lustre. The gopis' youthful beauty is compared to a lake of nectar, their breasts to a pair of cakravaka ducks in the lake, their eyes to saphari fish, and the folds of skin at their waists to languid waves.

When pronounced youth is present in the gopis' bodies, eagerness for Krsna’s company matures and the girls find ways to be with Him directly. One day, Syama-gopi noticed some flowers from Radha’s lotus garland scattered in a grove of Vrndavana. Later, when she met Srimati Radharani, Syama noticed scratch-marks on Her chest. Putting these two clues together, she asked Radha: “O doe-eyed Radharani! The handsome lion of Hari is like an ocean of chivalrous power. How were You able to capture Him with a glance and lead Him into the cage of Your breasts?”

The fourth phase of youth is called full-blown (purna). Although ripe and beautiful, such youth has a flaw. Just as the decay of a fully blossomed flower is inevitable, so is the decline of the beauty of mature youth. Such beauty is therefore not as attractive to Krsna as is the mystifying phase of pronounced youth that has yet to reveal its full bounty. The eternal age of Srimati Radharani’s competitor, Candravali, is that of a full-blown youth (purna), whereas Srimati Radharani’s eternal age is that of pronounced youth (vyakta). That is one reason Krsna is more attracted to Radha than to Candravali.

The symptoms of full-blown youth are marked by the gopis’ large breasts shaped like water pots, their slender waists delicate like the stems of elegant flowers, their broad hips as round as the moon is full, and their tapering thighs resembling the trunks of plantain trees. At this age, every part of the gopis’ bodies is both exceedingly lustrous and enchantingly beautiful.

Once, as Candravali was lamenting the good fortune of her rival Radha, Padma described the incomparable symptoms of full-blown youth present in Candravali. Padma said, “Who amongst your rivals is not afraid of the beauty of your face? What young gopi does not drown in the showers of rain pouring from the cloud of your love for Krsna? What girl in Vraja has not become your student in the various arts of love? O Candravali, expert at relishing transcendental mellows, you are the reigning queen of your kingdom — this forest grove of Vrndavana.”

Youth and beauty are inseparable. The influence of youth is to beautify everyone it touches, and the influence of beauty is to invoke the appearance of youth upon those whom it blesses. But one should note that beauty alone bestows the gifts of flawlessly formed limbs and fine facial features, gifts that may be given independent of youth. Because the blessings of both youth and beauty have been simultaneously showered upon the girls of Vraja, their beauty is beyond compare. And although all the Vraja gopis possess unsurpassed beauty, some gopis are even more beautiful than others.

As already mentioned, beauty—the second characteristic of the gopis’ forms that captivates Krsna — also has four divisions: beauty (rupa), lustrous beauty (lavanya), mature beauty (saundarya), and transforming beauty (abhirupata). These four manifestations of beauty are embodied in the youthful maidens of Vraja, and in no one else. Not all gopis display all four types of beauty — but some do. The gopis who display all four divisions of beauty are thus considered to be more beautiful than their peers.

The beauty called rupa is such that a girl’s unadorned limbs appear as if they are decorated with various ornaments. Once, as Krsna lost Himself in the marvel of Radha’s beauty, He remarked, “Your curling locks of hair make needless these tilaka decorations drawn in musk. Your eyes are so beautiful that the two lotus flowers on Your ears wilt in their presence. The waves of beauty flowing from Your smile have pulverised Your necklace into dust. O Radharani! Why should You bother wearing all these ornaments? You are already splendidly adorned by the beauty of Your limbs; these other decorations are simply useless.”

Beyond the kind of beauty in which the perfection of bodily limbs outshines the lustre of jewelled ornaments is the further stage of lustrous beauty (lavanya). In lustrous beauty, the limbs themselves appear as the central gems in the jewellery worn by the gopis. In other words, the stage of beauty known as lavanya makes the gopis’ limbs appear to be like prize gems on strings of pearls.

Krsna glorified this type of beauty in Sri Radha: “O doe-eyed Radharani! The beautiful lustre of Your limbs mocks the splendour of jewelled mirrors. Were Lord Brahma to search the entire universe, he would be unable to find anything as beautiful as You.”

The third kind of beauty (saundarya) describes a type in which each part of the body is perfectly proportioned to complement the whole. Krsna Himself describes this kind of beauty: “O Radha! Your face is as beautiful as the full moon. Your chest is splendidly decorated with beautiful large breasts. Your arms are nicely connected to Your shoulders. Your waist is the size of My fist. Your hips are exquisitely broad, and Your tapering thighs are very lovely. Nothing in this creation is as beautiful as Your charming transcendental form.”

And finally, when the gopis’ beauty exerts its influence upon nearby objects, making those objects even more beautiful than they normally are — indeed as beautiful (abhirupa) as the gopis themselves— such transforming beauty is called abhirupata. In this regard Krsna once said to Radha: “O Radharani! When this white lotus bud rests against Your large water-pot-like breasts, it appears golden. The same bud held in Your beautiful palm decorated with sindura appears red. And when placed in Your curling locks of hair it at once appears blue. In this way, this white lotus bud, the friend of the bumblebees, appears like three different flowers.”

Transcendental beauty has no limit. In Krsna and His beloved gopis, it eternally increases by the force of their constantly expanding happiness and love for one another. When Lord Krsna sees the gopis He feels great joy, and although His beauty is unparalleled, by their influence He becomes even more beautiful. In the same way, when the gopis see Krsna each one thinks, “Krsna has obtained so much pleasure by seeing me,” and that thought increases the beauty and fullness of their faces and bodies. Then, at the sight of the gopis’ increased beauty, Lord Krsna’s beauty increases still further. And again, the more the gopis see Lord Krsna’s steadily increasing beauty, their beauty increases even more. In this way, amongst the gopis and Krsna there is a transcendental competition of ever-increasing beauty in which no one acknowledges defeat.

This brings us to the third characteristic of the gopis’ forms that captivates Krsna’s mind and increases the bliss He feels in their company: the quality of sweetness (madhurya). Rupa Gosvami defines such sweetness as a fifth kind of beauty, which is paramount and inexpressible by words.

Visakha-devi once described to Radharani, who is always determined to hear about Krsna’s sweetness, the effects that sweetness has upon Radha’s gopi friends. Visakha said, “The chest of Krsna, the enemy of Bakasura, eclipses the splendour of dark rain clouds, His graceful gestures captivate the eyes, and His ever-fresh sweetness makes pious girls falter in their vows of chastity.”

The fourth characteristic of the gopis’ forms that captivates Krsna’s mind and increases His transcendental bliss is the softness or delicacy (mardhva) of their bodies.

Rupa Gosvami defines delicacy (mardhva) as the quality by which the gopis cannot tolerate the touch of even the softest object. He divides this delicacy into three gradations: greatest, intermediate, and least. Examples of these three categories — from the most delicate to the least — can be seen in how Radha’s transcendental body is bruised by the jasmine flowers upon which she sleeps, in how Dhanistha’s breasts are bruised by the touch of her silk garments, and in how Padma’s face becomes sunburned by the slightest exposure to sunshine.

Krsna’s sense of sight helps Him relish the four kinds of youthful beauty found in the bodies of the gopis. His sense of taste helps Him relish the fifth kind of beauty, sweetness. And His sense of touch enables Him to fulfil His desire to further relish the gopis’ beauty by allowing Him to enjoy the delicacy of their limbs. We should always keep in mind, however, that each of Krsna’s individual senses can perform the functions of any other sense, or even of all of His senses simultaneously. Therefore, with either His eyes, ears, nose, tongue, or touch Krsna can enjoy the gopis’ youth, beauty, sweetness, and delicacy, one at a time, many together, or all at once. After all, He is the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

A lauded yet unfortunate poet — unfortunate for never having heard of the gopis — once wrote, “In youth and beauty, wisdom is but rare.” But in the girls of Vraja, their youth and beauty are but symptoms of their great wisdom — wisdom that leads one to realise that Krsna is the ultimate object of love. Furthermore, the gopis have put that wisdom into practise by their unparalleled attainment of love for Krsna, because through unfailing and complete surrender to Him they offer their youth and beauty as oblations for His divine enjoyment. Therefore, applied to the Vraja gopis, this quote should be amended to say, “In the youth and beauty of the gopis there is wisdom beyond compare.”

The gopis’ happiness is in Krsna’s happiness, and only for this purpose do they show concern for their own bodies by cleansing, decorating, and dressing themselves. Because Krsna is bound to reciprocate with His devotees, He accepts the gopis’ bodily youth, beauty, and so on as loving offerings, which in turn stimulate His own ecstasy, thus expanding the ocean of transcendental bliss. In this way, the sublime characteristics of the gopis’ forms are impetus (vibhava) for Krsna’s ecstatic love.

The gopis’ transcendental qualities are divided into three basic groups: those of the body, of speech, and of mind and intelligence. This appendix attempts to describe but a drop of one of these qualities— the characteristics of their transcendental bodies. The ultimate purpose of such descriptions is to highlight the fact that the glories of the gopis’ forms, as found in this book, are not mere revelries on feminine beauty, but elaborations on a prominent cause of happiness in Krsna. The happiness of Krsna is the goal of all Vaisnavas—whether they be practitioners or advanced transcendentalists.

## Appendix 13

**The Pastime at Pili-pokhara**

(This appendix follows from the description of pastime places in Varsana found in chapter seven.)

One day, the great Durvasa Muni visited King Vrsabhanu’s house, where the sage was nicely cared for by Sri Radha.

In return for Her pure service, the sage blessed Radha that whatever She cooked would taste like nectar and make whoever ate it immune to disease and danger. When Mother Yasoda heard of Radha’s newly acquired power, she naturally desired that her son benefit from the special potency of Radha’s cooking. And so it came to pass that even as an unmarried girl, Vrsabhanu-nandini would come daily to cook Govinda’s morning meal.

Radhika would leave Varsana after sunrise and, accompanied by Her young girlfriends, walk to Nandagrama to cook under the supervision of Mother Rohini. Who can describe the appreciation and love Rohini and Yasoda showed for Srimati Radharani? They thought it was by Her cooking that Krsna and Balarama enjoyed good health and continued victory over the demons.

Of the two mothers, Yasoda-devi is the supreme emblem of maternal love. No one within the fourteen worlds possesses even an atom of her affection for Krsna. And yet, although her heart is always overflowing with love for her son, she loves Balarama and Radhika no less than Krsna.

Every day Mother Yasoda reminded Balarama to protect Krsna from demons. And every day she reminded Radha that Krsna would make a most suitable husband. This was the heart’s desire of the queen of Vraja—to bring Radhika into her home as her beloved daughter-in-law.

Of course, the marriage of Radha and Krsna is not possible in Vrndavana. The mood of the milkmaids is that Hari is their coveted paramour. This mood is an eternal feature of His transcendental pastimes. Unwedded love (parakiya-bhava) is most pleasing to Krsna. Such forbidden love, which offers the widest variety of spiritual mellows, is continually refreshed by the challenges offered by furtively meeting others’ wives.

Tricked by maternal affection, Mother Yasoda is unaware of the course of the gopis' love. The compass of her heart points only to prema, unfailingly directing her every action towards the welfare of her son. She knows nothing else. Driven by such a mood, Yasoda can think of no better match for Krsna than Kirtida’s lovely daughter. With this goal in mind, she devises various plans.

She thinks, “Whose beauty other than Radharani’s matches my Krsna’s beauty? Who else besides Sri Radha possesses qualities that complement every one of my son’s countless virtues? If there is a more perfect match for my son, I have not seen her in Vrndavana. May the gods fulfil the desire of this unfortunate soul — let Radha’s and Krsna’s marriage ornament my family tree.”

One morning Mother Yasoda resolved to propose the match of Radha and Krsna to the queen of Varsana. While Radhika was preparing to go home, Yasoda held Her hand and said, “Radha! You are now of marriageable age. I know no one more suited for You than my son. Our families should meet to discuss this idea.”

Yasoda-devi sat on a jewelled dais. As she drew Radhika to her side, Mother Yasoda felt Radha’s butter-soft body melt into hers. By Radha’s touch Yasoda felt unlimited ecstasy. She thought, “Is this girl the goddess of fortune? Is She a devi descended from heaven? By Her mere touch I feel transported to Vaikuntha.”

Taking Radha’s small hands in hers, Mother Yasoda gazed at the auspicious signs on Radha’s palms. With tears in her eyes, Yasoda reached for a tray of turmeric and gently wiped Sri Radhika’s pinkish palms until they were coloured yellow. “Radha! Do you know what this means?”

Puzzled, Radhika shook Her head, Her golden earrings jingling. “No, mother! Why did you put haldi on My hands?”

Yasoda-devi stroked Radhika’s soft hair as she looked into Her beautiful face. She felt as if she had fallen into a great ocean of beauty, unable to reply until she managed to swim to its nearest shore. “Priya! When a young girl’s palms are painted with haldi, it means she is engaged. What do You think of that?”

Radhika’s doe-eyes looked down at Her palms. “I do not know what My mother will say, hut I like to wander here and there at will. If I am engaged, then I will lose My freedom.”

Yasoda replied, “Priya! Your mother is wise and magnanimous. Let her heart guide her. But You are blossoming in the sunlight of youth. You must, therefore, accept the responsibilities that accompany womanhood. You cannot play in the dust forever.”

Radhika glanced at Yasoda’s feet submissively and said, “Mother! Your every word is My order.”

Mother Yasoda embraced Her again. Affectionately smelling Radha’s forehead, she said, “Then go to Your home and let me know what Your mother decides.”

Touching Yasoda’s feet, Radha left the home of Nanda Maharaja, eager to rejoin Lalita and Her other friends. As Radha made Her way out of the palace Her ankle bells jingled like cooing swans. Absorbed in reverie, Yasoda watched Radha descend the palace’s winding stairs and thought, “When this girl matures, the demigods will be amazed at the sight of Her. In wonder they will fall from the heavens like rain.”

Radhika and Her companions were making their journey from Nandagrama back to Varsana. In the shelter of the forests on the outskirts of Vrsabhanu’s kingdom, Radhika stopped to ponder Her fate. Turning to Lalita, Radha said, “What is to be done now? The ropes of Mother Yasoda’s love are exceedingly strong. How can I untie Myself from this predicament?”

Tungavidya replied, “Friend! You have certainly been drawn into a delicate situation.”

Lalita said, “But it is easily remedied! Why should we be concerned?” “Radha!” Visakha interjected. “We are too young to be married. Should we marry, our freedom will be finished. Lalita! Quick! Find a solution to this problem.”

Lalita smiled. “Radha! Why are You so alarmed! Have Your hands been painted with indelible dye? Merely wash off the haldi and Your cares will at once dissolve. Then Mother Yasoda will have to find another way to realise her desires.”

Clapping her hands and pointing to the right, Visakha said, “Friend! Look at that nice lake. Quickly wash Your hands! Then let’s return home by the path Govinda takes with His cows.”

Many lotus flowers decorated the nearby pond, which resounded with songs of birds and humming bees as Radha approached. Numerous trees surrounded the lake, their branches weighed down with ripe fruit, the pollen of their flowers lazily falling upon its crystalline waters.

At each of its four sides beautiful bathing ghatas led into the water. Upon its banks deer and waterfowl played. This spot was a favourite of Hari, whose cows drank its sweet water before leaving for the pastures of Vraja.

Resembling the princess of swans, Sri Radha strolled to the shore. Raising Her skirt, She stepped ankle-deep into the lake. “Careful! Don’t slip!” warned Lalita.

Sudevi gave a naughty laugh, “Then we’ll really have a story to tell!” Radhika cast a bluish glance at Her lighthearted friends as their giggling echoed across the lake.

When Radha leaned forward to wash Her hands, She saw Her reflection in the water. Amazed by Her own unlimited beauty, Radha paused momentarily, stunned by the sight. “Wouldn’t it be nice to always be in Gopala’s company?” She thought. “Then I would never have to be separated from Him.”

As She rubbed Her hands together, the haldi dissolved into the water and spread out in golden circular waves. Absorbed in thoughts of Hari while gazing into the blue waters of the lake, Radha gently washed the turmeric from Her hands. Bewildered by remembering Krsna, Radhika did not notice when Her hands had become clean. In Her trance of love She continued to rub Her hands mechanically, like a washerwoman cleaning cloth, or a housewife grinding paste. As Gaurangi rubbed and rubbed, Her friends marvelled at the ever-new expressions of love Her face revealed.

Finally Citra giggled, “Radha! Look what You’ve done! The whole lake is now yellow. I think You washed Your heart along with Your hands. Quick! Let’s go, before someone comes.”

Radhika stared wide-eyed at the lake, which had become golden. It was not only the haldi but also Radha’s ecstatic love for Krsna that had tinted the pond yellow. “What will they say?” cried the gopis’ echo. Radha stepped out of the water and quickly rejoined Her friends.

Lalita said, “Radhika! I think that haldi was the river of Your heart’s affection overflowing its banks. You’d better control Yourself before those yellow waves flow through the streets of Vrndavana.”

Embarrassed, Radha lowered Her head and whispered, “I’m not the only golden-complexioned gopi in Vraja.”

Lalita happily replied, “That may be true! But we became golden by Your influence. Anyway, let’s leave this place before someone accuses us of contaminating this kunda”

Placing Radhika in front, the young gopis hurried on towards Varsana. They appeared like a group of colourful clouds, their golden complexions like flashing lightning, their uncontrolled giggling like the playful rumble of distant thunder, and their love for Krsna like an imminent monsoon shower.

Not long after the gopis had left the lake, Sri Krsna appeared at that very spot. Searching intently for a stray cow, He had hardly noticed the change of scenery. Entering the forest, Gopala continued His search, until He had absentmindedly passed the lake, unawares. Suddenly He realised He had noticed something unusual about the place He had just passed. Krsna stopped to think for a moment and then carefully retraced His steps.

When He returned to the clearing, Krsna looked left and right—and left again. “What is different about this place?” He wondered.

Then His eyes caught sight of the lake. Startled, He whispered to Himself, “That pool has turned yellow! It appears as if the water has been dyed a golden colour. What’s happened here?”

Gopala looked to the animals and trees for a clue, but they were silent. Absorbed in their activities, they carried on as usual. Krsna rubbed His eyes. Still, the water retained its golden tint. “I must investigate this,” He thought.

Krsna walked apprehensively down the steps to the water. Perhaps this was the work of some demon. Perhaps the demigods were playing tricks again. Cautiously He looked into the pond and, naturally, He saw His reflection upon its golden surface. There He was, possessed of the same turban, the same peacock feather, the same flute, the same cowherd dress. But there was one difference — His black complexion was now a golden hue. “I look like a golden Krsna,” He thought. “How strange.”

The birds, deer, trees, and flowers all turned to watch as Krsna reached out and touched His reflection. Suddenly He felt a surge of ecstasy run through His body. It was as if a bolt of lightning had struck Him. He drew hack and cried, “Visnu! Visnu! What was that?”

“What is this mystical yellow substance?” He wondered. But all He could hear in response to His question was the echo of His own voice.

His curiosity piqued, Krsna once again reached towards the water, and once again the animals and plants craned their necks to watch. Once again Govinda touched the water, and once again His whole body quivered with ecstasy. As intense feelings of love surged through Krsna, His body reacted in a variety of unique ways, and His mind was filled with extraordinary feelings of love.

Hari was amazed at this strange phenomenon. He was even more amazed by the prema coursing through His heart. He thought, “The love I am feeling is something new and remarkable. I no longer feel like an outside observer of prema; rather, this feeling seems to have Me as its object.”

Krsna looked across the lake as He pondered the situation. “Usually I reciprocate the love of the Vraja-vasis. They are the object of My love. That is natural. But if I examine My heart, I find that when I touch this lake, I feel love for Myself. How extraordinary!”

Krsna’s mind raced to grasp this newfound wonder. “Only the gopis possess the sort of intense and extraordinary love I now feel, and amongst the gopis, only Srimati Radharani’s love is quite this marvelous and exalted. The golden colour of this lake is the complexion of Her body, and the electrifying touch of its water is the mood of Her love. Radhika must have empowered this lake with Her prema. What other explanation could there be? How very wonderful! How very wonderful!”

Krsna’s appetite for tasting Radha’s love was now fully aroused. Why was this lake different from others? What would happen if He put His arms in it? No! Better yet! What if He fully immersed Himself? Looking left and right, Krsna entered the water up to His calves, then His knees, then His waist, and finally up to His neck. Casting a blissful glance at the trees and animals, He smiled playfully while dropping below the surface. The golden waters happily embraced Krsna’s transcendental form, and for some time He disappeared , almost completely.

The peacock feather atop His turban alone visible above the surface, Krsna moved here and there underwater, His course plotted by the movements of the feather. Then, after some time, Govinda stopped in one place. Only His protruding feather, quivering in the breeze like a colourful buoy, marked His location.

While all of this was going on, the flowers, trees, birds, and bees held their breath in suspense. Just when they were on the verge of losing consciousness, Krsna gradually reappeared from the yellow waters — but in quite an unusual state.

Still dressed as a cowherd, Sri Krsna’s sapphire lustre had disappeared— His complexion was now completely golden. If one were to smelt gold one thousand times it might resemble the lustrous brilliance that emanated from Krsna’s body.

But the change in Krsna was not merely external. There was also a change in His inner disposition — His very conception of Himself. Coming onto dry land, He looked about as if He were searching for someone, while He constantly called out His own names, “Krsna! Krsna! Krsna!”

Looking left and right, golden-coloured Krsna was absorbed in the mood, happiness, and ecstasy of Sri Radha — in a form known as Gauranga. He thought, “Where is Sri Krsna, whose form is curved in three places? Where is the sweet song of the flute, and where is the dancing, singing, and laughing of the rasa dance?”

Dipping in the water dyed by Radha’s haldi and surcharged with Her bhava had caused various ecstatic emotions to evolve within Gauranga. In separation from Himself, He was filled with anxiety. And intensely desiring to be reunited with Himself, He had almost completely lost His patience. In this condition He recited the following verse:

*amuny adhanyani dinantarani*  
*hare tvad-alokanam antarena*  
*anatha-bandho karunaika-sindho*  
*ha hanta ha hanta katham nayami*

**“O My Lord! O friend of the helpless! You are the only ocean of mercy! Because I have not met You, My inauspicious days and nights have become unbearable. I do not know how I shall pass the time.”**

Standing on the jewelled ghata, golden water still dripping from His hair and clothes, Gaura (“golden”) Krsna searched for a glimpse of Himself. He prayed to Govinda, whom He considered to be an ocean of mercy, to bestow His audience upon Him — this was the only means by which He could be freed from anxiety.

Thinking Sri Hari might be hiding in the nearby gardens, Gaura Krsna began to move with the grace of a gopi. His arms uplifted in divine supplication, He moved about the lakefront path, singing His own names in the sweetest of voices.

Sometimes He would look into a forest grove, sometimes He would search behind a tree, sometimes He would enquire from a forest creature. At all times He was anxious to know Sri Krsna’s whereabouts. Within His heart, Gaura Krsna had forgotten His identity and conceived of Himself as none other than Srimati Radharani.

Unable to meet Sri Krsna, Gaura’s mind was restless and agitated. Taking shelter of the object of His love, He stretched out His arms and, as the animals looked on in wonder, asked Krsna for a means by which to achieve Him:

*tvac-chaisavam tri-bhuvanadbhutam ity avehi*  
*mac-capalam ca tava va mama vadhigamyam*  
*tat kim karomi viralam murali-vilasi*  
*mugdham mukhambujam udiksitum iksanabhyam*

**“O Krsna! O player of the flute! In these three worlds nothing excels the sweetness of Your early age. You know My unsteadiness, and I know Yours. No one else knows about this. O My beloved! I want to see Your beautiful attractive face somewhere in a solitary place, but how can this be accomplished?”**

Gaura Krsna was anxious to behold the beautiful features of Hari. This aspiration unhinged His mind and made Him unsteady. Bewildered, He turned to the trees for direction, but they were silent, agitated with worry by His extraordinary condition.

Rooted in a plethora of ecstatic emotions, Gaura appeared like a tree, decorated by contradictory ecstasies and overburdened by the maddened fruits of His own love. Anxiety, impotence, humility, anger, and impatience all struggled with each other for supremacy. In that condition, His body and mind despondent, He opened His heart for all to see:

*he deva he dayita he bhuvanaika-bandho*  
*he krsna he capala he karunaika-sindho*  
*he natha he ramana he nayanabhirama*  
*ha ha kada nu bhavitasi padam drsor me*

**“O My Lord! O dearest! O sole friend of the universe! O Krsna! O restless one! O only ocean of mercy! O My Lord! O My enjoyer! O beloved of My eyes! Alas, when will You be visible to Me again?”**

Bathing in the waters stained yellow by Srimati Radharani's hands, Hari became bewildered by Her prema and displayed the form of Gaura Krsna. Roaming through the lakeside gardens He sang His own names, yearned to hear His own flute, and cried in hopes of seeing His own form. Gaura Krsna’s mood was identical to that of Sri Radha in separation from Him. Even the animals of Vraja cried upon seeing His pitiful condition. As a mystical potion gradually loses its influence, slowly Gaura Krsna remembered who He was, resumed His blackish form as Syamasundara, and again took up the search for His stray cow.

Even today, learned Vaisnavas call this lake Pili-pokhara—the yellow pond.

## Appendix 14

**Who Is Candrakanti and What Were Her Teachings?**

(This appendix follows from Krsna’s recollection of Candrakanti in chapter eight.)

The Padma Purana glorifies a girl named Candrakanti for attaining ecstatic love for Krsna simply by dancing and singing before a Deity of the Lord. The Nectar of Devotion states that Candrakanti was a “celebrated fair-faced girl” who observed strict celibacy, rejecting marriage, determined to obtain Krsna as her husband. She also faithfully maintained a daily spiritual regimen, constantly meditating on the transcendental form of the Lord and chanting His glories.

Jiva Gosvami elaborates on these statements by noting that the transcendental form of the Lord upon which Candrakanti meditated was a Deity of Lord Krsna. Sri Rupa Gosvami also gives details about Candrakanti in Stavamala. There he notes that Candrakanti attained perfection by dancing for the Deity of Krsna from dusk until dawn on the Prahodhani night.

Moreover, Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu cites Candrakanti as an example of a sadhaka who cultivated spontaneous attachment for Krsna, first awakening ecstatic devotion (bhava) and then loving devotion (prema) for the Lord. Again, in Stavamala, Sri Rupa asserts that Candrakanti was an expansion of Srimati Radharani who appeared on a mission to teach others, in particular the Gandharvas, how to love Krsna.

**Who is Candrakanti?**

According to the Padma Purana, Candrakanti was an Apsara, although our Gaudiya Vaisnava acaryas consistently refer to her as a Gandharva. The technical difference between Apsaras and Gandharvas is negligible: both known for their musical talents, the former usually refers to the female gender, the latter to the male. Regardless, since the acaryas refer to her as a Gandharva, we shall do so as well.

The works of Gosvamis Rupa, Raghunatha, and Jiva identify Candrakanti as Sri Radha. These acaryas specifically say that Radha appeared, or expanded Herself, as Candrakanti. However, unlike Caitanya Mahaprabhu, who is directly and fully Krsna, it appears that Candrakanti is only a partial expansion of Radharani. This understanding is based upon two authoritative statements.

The first statement comes from Rupa Gosvami, who in referring to Candrakanti uses the very specific and unambiguous term nijamsaka, meaning “direct and partial expansion.”

The second statement is made by Srila Prabhupada in his translation of verses 154-155 from Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika. There he states that Candrakanti appeared as Gadadhara Dasa in Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. These verses begin “*radha-vibhuti-rupa ya candrakantih*,” which means “Candrakanti, who is the embodiment of Sri Radha’s potency.” Srila Prabhupada’s translation is even more specific. He writes, “Candrakanti, who is the effulgence of Srimati Radharani.”

While these statements clearly confirm Candrakanti’s identity as Radha’s expansion, they also clarify the differences between her and Caitanya Mahaprabhu. She is only a part of Radha while He is fully Krsna — a difference that should always be borne in mind.

In addition to this ontological analysis, we will continue our study of Candrakanti by looking at the nature and details of her devotional service in order to understand her identity more fully.

The available history of Candrakanti does not describe how she developed spontaneous attachment to Krsna; whenever mentioned she is introduced as a raganuga-sadhaka with no further details given. In conditioned souls, spontaneous attachment generally arises through hearing Krsna’s pastimes from advanced devotees. But since Candrakanti was not a conditioned soul but the expanded potency of Radharani, it appears that her attachment to Krsna, like Lord Caitanya’s, did not begin at a specific point in material time.

However, Candrakanti’s evolution from spontaneous attraction to ecstasy and then to love is clearly attributed to her worship of the Deity form of Lord Krsna. Srila Prabhupada explains how the awakening of such spontaneous attachment, and its further elevation to the platform of amorous love for the Lord, is achieved through service to the Deity:

**“This development of conjugal love can be possible only with those who are already engaged in following the regulative principles of devotional service, specifically in the worship of Radha and Krsna in the temple. Such devotees gradually develop a spontaneous love for the Deity, and by hearing of the Lord’s exchange of loving affairs with the gopis they gradually become attracted to these pastimes. After this spontaneous attraction becomes highly developed, the devotee is placed in either of the above-mentioned categories \[of eternal loving service to Krsna.\]”**

There is more to be said about the mood of Candrakanti’s devotion. But first a few more details about her identity should be mentioned. One very interesting fact, found in Sri Madhava-mahotsava, comes from the mouth of Vrnda-devi, the goddess of Vrndavana. She cites a famous statement of the Bahv-rk-paricista—the Rg Veda—stating that Lord Madhava \[Krsna\] is glorious because Radha stands by His side.

*radhaya madhavo devo madhavenaiva radhika*

Elaborating on this verse, Vrnda-devi declares that the word “Radhika” refers to Candrakanti. Since the verse itself does not refer to Candrakanti by name, Vrnda-devi’s interpretation implies that, in addition to the pastime recorded in the Padma Purana, scriptures outside the Gaudiya Vaisnava canon also acknowledge the existence of Candrakanti.

Another point relating to Candrakanti's identity is her role in Krsna’s pastimes. By examining Jiva Gosvami’s Durgama-sangamani and Kavi Karnapura’s Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika, it is evident that Candrakanti attained the eternal perfection of becoming a gopi associate of Sri Radha in Goloka Vrndavana. Indeed, Jiva Gosvami says that Sri Radha personally bestowed this svarupa on Candrakanti. In her eternal form she continues to be known by the same name: Candrakanti-gopi.

The final item to be discussed regarding Candrakanti's identity is her role in Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. Kavi Karnapura writes:

**“Srila Gadadhara Dasa is considered to be a united form of Candrakanti, who is the effulgence of Srimati Radharani, and Purnananda, who is the foremost of Lord Balarama’s very dear girlfriends. Thus Srila Gadadhara Dasa Prabhu was one of the associates of both Caitanya Mahaprabhu and Nityananda Prabhu.”**

Srila Prabhupada calls Gadadhara Dasa the lustre of Sri Radha’s body but differentiates him from Lord Caitanya, who is characterised as having not only the bodily lustre of Sri Radha, but Her emotions as well. Once again, this testimony confirms that neither Gadadhara Dasa nor Candrakanti are fully-fledged incarnations of Sri Radha.

Finally, Gadadhara Dasa, like Candrakanti, was famous for teaching others how to learn to love Krsna by dancing for the Lord and chanting His names. And although Gadadhara was an associate of Lord Nityananda, he was also, like Candrakanti, absorbed in the mellow of amorous love.

From the information already presented, it is clear that Candrakanti is Radharani’s personified effulgence, appearing in three forms: as a Gandharva, as a gopi, and as Gadadhara Dasa. Since Radha’s effulgence is one of Her eternal features, the identities of both Candrakanti-gopi and Gadadhara Dasa should be likewise accepted as eternal. It is not clear, however, whether the Gandharva Candrakanti is an eternal form of Radha who appears and disappears to re-enact her mission in the heavenly planetary system, or whether that appearance is a singular one. Further research would be required to conclusively resolve that quandary. A fuller picture of Candrakanti’s character can, however, be gained by studying her mission, which is the focus of the following section.

**What Were Her Teachings?**

Rupa Gosvami is the only acarya to refer directly to the purpose of Candrakanti’s mission, but he does so with words of unmistakable clarity. Glorifying the names of Sri Radha he writes:

*sada-kisorika-gostha-yuva-raja-vilasini*  
*govinda-prema-siksartha-nati-krta-nijamsaka*

**“\[Srimati Radharani\] is eternally young, She enjoys transcendental pastimes with Vraja’s prince and She expanded \[to become the Gandharva, Candrakanti\] to teach others how to love Lord Govinda by dancing for Him.”**

To say that the purpose of expanding herself (nijamsaka) was to teach others (siksartha) is to say that Candrakanti had a particular mission. And the words govinda-prema clearly specify that mission: teaching others how to love Krsna. Using the words nati-krta, or dance, Sri Rupa also hints at the means by which Candrakanti taught others: sankirtana. And although this description is brief, it is a clear indication of Candrakanti’s mission, which becomes even more explicit in light of the verses of Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu already mentioned that describe her.

What is lacking in the above verse, however, is a description of Candrakanti’s style of teaching. Did she personally instruct others, the way Caitanya Mahaprabhu did? Or is the example of her life the means by which her mission was spread? There is not much information available to provide these details. But Jiva Gosvami does speak about at least one class of persons touched by Candrakanti, namely some Gandharva girls.

Sri Jiva narrates that these girls appeared during Radha’s abhiseka. Seeing Candrakanti in Radharani, upon the order of Paurnamasi they sang songs about Radha and Krsna. In these songs the Gandharvas revealed their knowledge of Radha and Krsna, describing the mutual love of the divine couple and the service the gopis render to Them. Thus, through Candrakanti’s teachings they had become well versed, or spiritually realised, or both.

Since that is the only reference to Candrakanti’s audience, we will elaborate on the nature of Candrakanti’s teachings solely by citing the verses of Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu that describe her accomplishments.

First mention of Candrakanti is made in the section on bhava-bhakti. There Sri Rupa explains how ecstatic devotion arises from spontaneous practice by quoting the Padma Purana:

**“A young girl, having great joy in the heart and being very enthusiastic to dance, spent the whole night \[dancing\] in order to please the Lord.”**

The second mention of Candrakanti is made in the section on prema-bhakti in relation to the development of loving devotion arising from raganuga-sadhana:

**“In this manvantara period, the beautiful-faced Candrakanti observed continuous celibacy, continuously meditating on the form of Krsna and thinking, ‘One should not desire anyone else as a husband.’ With hairs standing on end, she sang songs about Him. And she developed complete affection for Krsna by hearing stories about Him.”**

Jiva Gosvami’s commentaries on both verses are also very useful. The essential elements of these verses and their corresponding commentaries give us the following picture of Candrakanti.

With spontaneous attraction to Krsna in her heart, Candrakanti would dance and sing songs before the Deity of Krsna. She would also hear about His pastimes. She thus advanced to the stage of strong attachment (asakti), and one night, by dancing before the Lord, she attained the stage of ecstasy (bhava). Her mood of attachment to Krsna was similar to that of a gopi-heroine; indeed she would sometimes think that she was Krsna’s favourite. Of course, it is known that Radha is Krsna’s favorite gopi. But Candrakanti thought as she did, because in fact she was an expansion of Radha. By her continued devotions to Krsna’s Deity, she invoked Radha’s mercy and was thus awarded full love for Krsna and attained the position of a sakhi.

Let us list the lessons that Candrakanti taught through her example:

1\. Devotional service in practice, if at first regulated, must become spontaneous in order to attain bhakti on the level of the Vraja gopis.

2\. Dancing and chanting before the Deity form of the Lord and hearing from saints about Krsna’s pastimes are the main practices of spontaneous devotion.

3\. When sadhana is performed with enthusiasm and determination, a practising devotee receives the mercy of a resident of Vraja, by which ecstasy and love awaken and one is awarded an eternal position in Goloka Vrndavana.

4\. Whether situated in practice, in ecstasy, or in perfection, a devotee is always engaged in the sankirtana of Krsna’s names, qualities, and pastimes.

5\. Of all kinds of devotion, vraja-bhakti is the topmost.

Volumes can be written elaborating on each of these points. Since we have already discussed them at length in an earlier book, Suddha- bhakti-cintamani, we will not do so again here. What should he noted, however, is the similarity of Candrakanti’s teachings to those of Caitanya Mahaprabhu. All five points above arc included in the teachings of Lord Caitanya. Therefore, it may be said that their missions were very similar. Any possible differences are a result of details lacking in regard to Candrakanti’s life. For instance: what songs did she sing about Krsna? Did she sing the maha-mantra? Were the pastimes of Krsna she heard from Srimad-Bhagavatam? If she received initiation, into which sampradaya, and from whom did she receive it?

The remaining questions about Candrakanti are numerous. There is good reason for that: her example is not the basis for how people on this planet should take up Krsna consciousness. Caitanya Mahaprabhu and His followers are the teachers of pure devotion for living entities on this planet. The history of Lord Caitanya and His associates and its corresponding literary legacy are extremely rich, leaving no question unanswered.

In reviewing the lives and missions of Candrakanti and Lord Caitanya — Radha teaching us how to love Krsna, and Krsna in the mood of Radha teaching us how to love Krsna — it is found that there are many similarities, but also significant differences. The comparison is valuable, however, as it illustrates how kind both Radha and Krsna are to come to different planets of this universe to teach conditioned souls about sankirtana, the only means for attaining the topmost goal of life, vraja-prema. This raises an interesting question: Who else has come to this universe to teach vraja-bhakti, and where do the scriptures speak of him or her? This question opens the door to further interesting research that some learned Vaisnava may in the future consider undertaking.

In conclusion, while Candrakanti’s story is appreciated by Vaisnavas as a source of inspiration, it appears from Rupa Gosvami’s statement that her teachings were directed to the Gandharvas, not the residents of earth. Thus we offer our respects to her as a great Vaisnavi and as an expansion of Sri Radha, and we pray for her mercy to he strict followers in the line of Rupa and Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami.

## About the Author

Sivarama Swami was bom in Budapest, Hungary in 1949, and his family emigrated to Canada during the failed 1956 Hungarian revolution. He became a disciple of A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the fo under-acarya of ISKCON, in 1973 and entered the renounced order of life (sannyasa) in 1979. Sivarama Swami is well known for his deep knowledge of Vaisnava literature and has written many books about Gaudiya Vaisnavism, including the “Krsna in Vrndavana” series. He is a member of iskcon’s Governing Body Commission and is responsible for projects in Hungary, Turkey, and Romania. Although he travels and speaks internationally, he spends as much time as possible at New Vraja-dhama (Krishna Valley), a replica of Vrndavana and self-sufficient farming project in Hungary’s countryside.
